# The Cold War and International Relations - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It teaches how an alliance that had just destroyed Nazi Germany turned, inside three years, into two armed camps that spent the next four decades preparing to fight each other and never did. It begins where the wartime coalition begins to fail - at Yalta in February 1945 and at Potsdam in July and August of the same year, over Poland, over Germany, over reparations and over the question of who would govern the states the Red Army had liberated - and it insists on the distinction that makes the whole topic intelligible: the Cold War was not peace, and it was not war. It was organised hostility conducted with propaganda, economic instruments, alliance-building, intelligence operations, client states and limited wars fought by other people, under a nuclear ceiling that neither side was willing to break. It teaches the five causes the sources record - the incompatibility of a system built on collective ownership and central planning with one built on private ownership and profit; Stalin's determination to convert military position into a protective belt; the hardening of American and British policy after Roosevelt's death in April 1945; the atomic asymmetry of 1945 to 1949; and a wartime legacy of suspicion running from the delayed Second Front to the concealment of the bomb - and it teaches the mechanism that binds them, which is that each side read its own precautions as defensive and the other's as aggressive. It teaches the blame argument at full strength, in the three-way form the advanced material sets out: the orthodox case associated with George Kennan, the revisionist case associated with William Appleman Williams, and the archivally-informed post-revisionist settlement that distributes responsibility without erasing the difference between the two systems. It teaches containment as a set of instruments rather than a slogan: the Truman Doctrine of March 1947 and the aid to Greece and Turkey; the European Recovery Programme announced in June 1947 and the argument among historians about how much it actually did; the Cominform, the Molotov Plan and Comecon as the answer to it; the Czechoslovak coup of February 1948; the Berlin blockade of June 1948 and the airlift that defeated it; NATO in April 1949; the two German states of 1949; the Soviet bomb of September 1949 and NSC-68 of April 1950. It teaches Korea from 1950 to 1953 as the moment the rivalry acquired armies, and it teaches the four competing accounts of who started it without choosing between them on preference. It teaches the thaw after Stalin's death in March 1953 exactly as far as the evidence permits - the Panmunjom armistice, the Austrian State Treaty of May 1955, the concessions of that year, Khrushchev's formulation that there are only two ways, peaceful coexistence or the most destructive war in history - and it teaches the limits of that thaw in the same breath: the Warsaw Pact of May 1955, Hungary in 1956, the U-2 affair of 1960, the Berlin Wall of August 1961 and the missiles in Cuba in October 1962. It teaches the arms race as a technical sequence with a political logic, from the atomic bomb to the hydrogen bomb to intercontinental and submarine-launched missiles, to anti-ballistic missiles, to multiple independently targetable warheads, to the SS-20 and Cruise, to the Strategic Defence Initiative - and it teaches why the danger the sources identify is not the weapons themselves but the temptation to strike first. It teaches the parts of the topic that a Europe-only script loses: the Sino-Soviet quarrel that began after 1956 and ended in a war between communist states in February 1979; the refusal of many newly independent states to enter either bloc, and what that refusal bought them; Vietnam across two wars and three American presidencies; Chile, Laos, Cambodia, Thailand, Iran and Iraq; and the Middle East from 1948 to the Gulf War of 1990-91, where the sources are explicit that superpower rivalry internationalised conflicts it did not create. It teaches détente as risk management rather than reconciliation - SALT I in 1972, the opening to China in the same year, the Helsinki Agreement of 1975, Ostpolitik - and it teaches why Afghanistan in December 1979, the SS-20 and Pershing deployments, and the failure of SALT II turned it into what the advanced material calls a second Cold War. It closes at the boundary rather than through it: Gorbachev from March 1985, Geneva, Chernobyl, Reykjavik, the INF Treaty of December 1987, the Afghan withdrawal from May 1988, and the collapse of 1989-91 stated as the outcome that the end-of-Cold-War owner explains in detail. Responsibility throughout is stated about governments, parties, agencies, alliances and named individuals - never about nations or peoples. India appears where the sources place it, as one of the states that refused the bloc choice, and its own strategic vocabulary is left to the file that owns it.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is not the order of a textbook chapter, and the difference is deliberate. A chapter narrates the origins, then the crises, then détente, then the collapse, and arrives at a verdict about who was to blame. That structure produces the standard failing script: a chronicle of episodes with no mechanism, followed by a moral judgement that nothing in the answer was ever used to test. Here the definition comes first, because a large share of the marks in this topic are lost by candidates who never establish what kind of thing they are explaining. The causes are separated from the blame historiography, because they are different questions and the second is not settled by the first. Containment is taught as instruments before it is taught as crises, because the crises are what the instruments produced. Korea comes after the European sequence, because it is the moment the European pattern is exported. The thaw is taught before Cuba, because Cuba is unintelligible as an isolated drama and entirely intelligible as the point at which a partial thaw ran into the limits of deterrence. The Sino-Soviet split is taught before détente, because it is what made the American opening to China possible. Non-alignment is taught before Vietnam, because the first thing Vietnam demonstrates is what happened to a decolonising state that could not stay out. The Middle East and the intervention record come next, because they are the two places where a proxy-war reading is most tempting and most wrong. Détente, renewed confrontation and the ending phase come last among the narrative lessons, and the thematic and conversion lessons follow them.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Two labels run through every lesson, and keeping them apart is the habit that separates a confident answer from an over-confident one.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the two canonical owners held for this topic, the folder's master chronology, the official syllabus mapping, the folder README and answer-worthiness audit, the one core world-history book read for this session, or a verified question ledger | Assert it. Dates, institutions, treaty contents, figures the sources record, and recorded scholarly positions can be stated flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |

One further habit belongs with those labels. Where a standard connecting date or institutional name is needed to hold a sequence together, this session supplies it rather than retreating into vague formulations such as "a treaty was signed" or "a crisis followed". Every such item is verified, and its provenance sits in the final ledger rather than in the teaching. Where the source base is internally inconsistent, or where a page of the core text used here is unreadable, the gap is displayed at the point of use instead of being smoothed over.

Nine disciplines are specific to this topic. Each exists because this is a part of the syllabus where a fluent answer can be badly wrong.

**The definition discipline.** ✅ The core formulation held here is that the Cold War names a period in which the United States and the Soviet Union did not fight each other directly in an open general war, but confronted each other through propaganda, arms competition, alliance-building, economic pressure and limited wars. ⚠️ Two errors follow from ignoring it. The first is writing as though the Cold War were a long peace, which cannot account for Korea, Vietnam or Afghanistan. The second is writing as though it were a war, which cannot account for the fact that the two principals never exchanged fire. ✅ The exam sentence the owner supplies is exact: the Cold War was not peace; it was organised hostility under the shadow of nuclear weapons.

**The security-dilemma discipline.** ✅ The decisive framing the sources give is that each side interpreted its own acts as defensive and the other's as aggressive, and that the two powers created an atmosphere in which every international act could be interpreted in two ways. ⚠️ This is not a way of avoiding the blame question; it is the answer to it that the evidence best supports, and it must be *demonstrated* from cases rather than asserted as a formula. Every crisis lesson in this session therefore closes by stating what the crisis looked like from each capital.

**The proxy discipline.** ⚠️ "Proxy war" is a useful description of the superpowers' involvement and a false description of the conflicts' causes. ✅ The sources are explicit at the point where it matters most: in Angola, outside interference and the extension of the Cold War made a conflict much worse that would have produced fighting and bloodshed anyway. ✅ The Middle Eastern sequence carries the same rule, because local nationalism, territory, refugees, state security and oil politics had autonomous importance. ⚠️ The examinable operation is therefore to treat superpower involvement as a **modifier** of intensity, duration and armament, and not as the cause.

**The agency discipline.** ⚠️ Newly independent states are not pawns in this session. ✅ The sources record that after the Second World War many newly emerging states wanted to remain neutral or non-aligned in the struggle between communism and capitalism; that the leaders of many of these nations met in conference at Algiers in 1973 and made it clear that they regarded themselves as a Third World; and that when the United States built SEATO in 1954 only three Asian states joined, because it was obvious that many states wanted to keep clear of the Cold War and remain uncommitted. ⚠️ Non-alignment was therefore a strategy with a purpose, not indecision, and the evidence for it is a refusal that can be counted.

**The bloc discipline.** ⚠️ "The communist bloc" is a phrase that describes the Western alliance's problem, not the communist world's condition. ✅ Sino-Soviet relations deteriorated steadily after 1956; China accused the Soviet Union of revisionism; Soviet economic aid to China was reduced; a territorial claim north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang remained unsettled; both powers were at the end of the 1970s vying for American support against each other; and in February 1979 one communist state attacked another. ⚠️ An answer that treats containment as facing a single adversary has misdescribed the object it is analysing.

**The evidentiary discipline for numbers.** The rule is stated positively rather than as a count: **no quantity appears in this session unless a source used here carries it, and every quantity is used in the sense its source gives it.** The principal figures the sources carry are: the sixteen participating nations and the figure of over 13 billion dollars of Marshall Aid over four years, with the observation that this averaged only about 2.5 per cent of those countries' total national income; the Czechoslovak communists' 38 per cent of the votes and 114 of 300 seats in 1946; the two million tons airlifted into Berlin over ten months for 2.5 million West Berliners; the 60 million dollars of aid to Turkey; the refugee flow from East Germany of about 200 000 a year and over three million since 1945, and the 28-mile Wall; the Cuban invasion force of about 1400 men, the missile range of up to 2000 miles and the 25 Soviet ships; the Chinese counter-offensive of over 300 000 troops and the four million Korean dead with five million homeless; the Vietnam figures for troop numbers in early 1969, the American servicemen's deaths and the 300 000 wounded, and the proportion of the South severely damaged; the SS-20's three warheads against the MIRV's fourteen; the INF Treaty's 436 American and 1575 Soviet warheads and its four per cent of existing stocks; the 150 SS-20s and over 500 Pershing and Cruise missiles; the 100 000 Soviet troops in Afghanistan; the Allende election margin of 36 per cent against 35 and the Chilean repression figures of around 3000 killed or disappeared; the Suez Assembly vote of 64 to 5 and the UN force of 5000 from ten countries; and the intervention figures for Laos and Thailand that the account itself states.

⚠️ Three qualifications belong with that rule, and each is enforced throughout. ⚠️ First, **dates, treaty ranges, parallels of latitude and the ordinal counts this session uses to organise its own teaching** - five causes, three schools, four pressures, seven demand families - are structural devices rather than evidential claims, and they are not covered by the list above. ⚠️ Second, where the standard literature has series these sources do not carry - warhead totals by year, aid disbursements by country, casualty figures for the interventions in Laos, Cambodia, Thailand, Afghanistan and Angola, and defence budgets as a share of output - the session says so and supplies nothing. ⚠️ Third, **where a held figure differs from the verified published record, both are displayed at the point of use with the object each one counts named, and neither is averaged away**; the Vietnam fatality figure in Lesson 15 is the one place where this arises.

**The instrument discipline.** Every doctrine, plan and alliance in this topic is taught by what it actually did. The Truman Doctrine is taught by the commitment it created and the aid that immediately followed it; the Marshall Plan by the recovery it financed and the political purpose the sources attribute to it; the Cominform by the conformity it enforced on states that were already communist; NATO by the clause that converted an attack on one into an attack on all and by the American tradition it abandoned; the Warsaw Pact by its timing against West German entry into NATO; SALT I by the categories of weapon it counted and the category it left out; the Helsinki Agreement by the two things it traded, frontier recognition against human-rights undertakings; the INF Treaty by the fact that it destroyed a class of weapons rather than capping it. ⚠️ A script that names institutions without stating their powers is describing letterheads.

**The attribution discipline.** Responsibility is stated about governments, parties, ministries, intelligence agencies, alliances, congresses and named individuals - never about nations or peoples. ⚠️ Formulations such as "the Americans wanted world domination", "the Russians were expansionist by nature" or "the Arabs refused peace" are treated in this session as errors of fact, and they appear in the remediation set. ✅ The sources themselves model the correction: the European Recovery Programme is analysed as a decision of the American government with mixed motives, and Soviet policy in eastern Europe as a decision of Stalin and the Soviet leadership whose *methods*, the account says explicitly, were what upset the West.

**The boundary discipline.** Five neighbouring topics own material this one constantly touches. The military course of the Second World War and the immediate emergence of the superpowers belong to the Second World War owner; this session uses only the postwar position those events created. The structure, mandate and institutional performance of the United Nations belong to the United Nations owner; this session uses UN action only where it is an instrument or a casualty of the rivalry - in Korea, at Suez and over Hungary. China's revolution and domestic transformation belong to the China owner; this session uses only the international consequences and the Sino-Soviet chain. Decolonisation as a process belongs to the decolonisation owner; this session uses only the Cold War's interaction with it. The focused late-Soviet and Eastern European transition and the post-1991 order belong to the end-of-Cold-War owner; this session teaches the ending phase to the point of collapse and hands over. ⚠️ A script on Cold War origins that opens with the Nuremberg trials, or closes on the Maastricht Treaty, is answering somebody else's question.

*The middle box is the topic. Everything in the lessons that follow is either an instrument listed inside it, a crisis that tested its edges, or an attempt to negotiate its terms.*

### 1. The problem a definition solves

Start with the difficulty that costs marks. A candidate who has read about Korea, Vietnam and Afghanistan knows that people were killed in very large numbers during the Cold War. A candidate who has read about deterrence knows that the two principals never fired at each other. Put those two pieces of knowledge side by side without a definition and the script wobbles: it calls the period a war when it needs drama and a peace when it needs restraint, and the examiner can see that the candidate has no stable object of analysis.

✅ **Fact:** the definition held by the owner for this topic fixes the object precisely. The Cold War names a period in which the United States and the Soviet Union did not fight each other directly in an open general war, but confronted each other through propaganda, arms competition, alliance-building, economic pressure and limited wars.

✅ **Fact:** the core account states the same thing from the other direction. Relations between Soviet Russia and the West became so difficult that, although no actual fighting took place directly between the two opposing camps, the decade after 1945 saw the first phase of what became known as the Cold War; instead of allowing their mutual hostility to express itself in open fighting, the rival powers attacked each other with propaganda and economic measures, and with a general policy of non-cooperation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice that both formulations define the Cold War by an *absence* plus a *list of substitutes*. That is the structure of the definition, and it is worth reproducing in an introduction because it does two jobs in one sentence: it concedes that no general war occurred, and it immediately explains why the period was nevertheless an organised conflict. A candidate who writes "the Cold War was a period of tension between the USA and the USSR" has written a sentence that would equally describe 1935, and has told the examiner nothing.

### 2. Six words that must be fixed before the first crisis is described

| Word | ✅ What the sources make it mean here | ⚠️ The error it prevents |
|---|---|---|
| **Cold War** | Hostility without direct general war between the principals, conducted through the listed substitutes | Using it loosely for any period of great-power tension |
| **Superpower** | ✅ The USA and the USSR, who emerged as the two most powerful nations in the world and were no longer isolated; the USA had the world's largest navy and air force and controlled the atomic bomb, and the USSR, though severely weakened, still had the largest army in the world | Treating Britain or France as superpowers after 1945 |
| **Containment** | ✅ The policy Kennan advised, of containing the USSR by political, economic and diplomatic means; ✅ after March 1947 an American commitment to containing communism not just in Europe but throughout the world | Reading it as a plan to destroy communism, which is what its critics inside the USA wanted and did not get |
| **Bloc** | The two alliance systems and the states drawn into their orbits | Assuming either bloc acted as a single will - see Lesson 13 |
| **Détente** | ✅ A permanent relaxation of tensions between East and West, whose first real signs appear in the early 1970s | Reading it as friendship, alliance, or the end of the rivalry |
| **Proxy war** | A local conflict in which the superpowers supply, arm or fight through others | Treating superpower involvement as the *cause* of the local conflict |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second column of that table is not vocabulary revision. It is the answer to a whole class of questions. A stem asking whether the Cold War was "a global conflict" is testing the first two rows; a stem on détente is testing the fifth; a stem on the decolonised world is testing the sixth. Fixing the words early is therefore not preliminary work - it is most of the analysis, done in advance.

### 3. Where this topic sits in the official syllabus, and what it does not own

✅ **Fact:** the official General Studies Paper I clause held in this repository reads: "History of the world will include events from 18th century such as industrial revolution, world wars, redrawal of national boundaries, colonization, decolonization, political philosophies like communism, capitalism, socialism etc.- their forms and effect on the society."

✅ **Fact:** the folder's syllabus mapping divides that clause into named sub-clauses with single owners. This topic is listed among the direct owners of the clause as a whole, but the sub-clause on "political philosophies like communism, capitalism, socialism etc. - their forms and effect on the society" has a different Core owner: the Enlightenment-and-revolutions overview file, which holds the doctrines themselves.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is a genuine trap and not a bureaucratic detail. A stem that says "communism and capitalism" is not automatically a Cold War stem. If the demand is *what these doctrines are, how they developed and what they did to societies*, the answer belongs to the doctrine owner and a Cold War narrative is off-target. If the demand is *how the confrontation between states claiming those doctrines organised world politics after 1945*, it belongs here. ✅ The owner's own factual-risk control is blunt about it: do not define communism, capitalism or socialism in this file; this file owns the geopolitical record.

✅ **Fact:** the mapping also records that no separate World History clause appears in the verbatim Prelims syllabus held here, and that this folder's General Studies Paper II utility is support only - historical background to international institutions, not the live institutions themselves.

### 4. The five boundaries, stated once

```text
   TOPIC 14  SECOND WORLD WAR          -> owns the military course of the war and
                                          the immediate emergence of the superpowers
   TOPIC 16  UNITED NATIONS            -> owns UN structure, mandate and institutional
                                          performance
   TOPIC 17  CHINA AND ASIAN COMMUNISM -> owns the Chinese revolution and China's
                                          domestic transformation
   TOPIC 18  DECOLONISATION            -> owns decolonisation as a process, and the
                                          named post-independence cases
   TOPIC 21  END OF THE COLD WAR       -> owns the focused late-Soviet and eastern
                                          European transition and the post-1991 order
   ----------------------------------------------------------------------------
   TOPIC 15  THIS SESSION              -> owns the INTERNATIONAL-RELATIONS
                                          INTERACTION among all of the above:
                                          origins, bipolar structure, containment,
                                          alliances, deterrence, crises, theatres,
                                          non-alignment, détente, renewed tension
                                          and the ending phase
```

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the operative word in the bottom box is *interaction*. This session is allowed to use Korea, but not to become a history of Korea; allowed to use the UN's authorisation in 1950 and its ceasefire pressure at Suez in 1956, but not to assess the UN; allowed to use the Chinese revolution of 1949 as an input to American threat perception, but not to explain it. ⚠️ The practical test is a simple one: if a paragraph would still be needed in an answer where the Cold War did not exist, it belongs to another owner.

### 5. The period markers, and why the dates are the argument

| Marker | ✅ Date | ⚠️ What changes |
|---|---|---|
| Wartime harmony wears thin | 1945 | The coalition's shared enemy is removed; postwar planning begins on both sides |
| Containment declared and financed | 1947 | Hostility acquires instruments and a budget |
| Stalin dies; armistice in Korea | 1953 | A thaw becomes possible without becoming settled |
| Missiles in Cuba | 1962 | Deterrence is tested to its edge and both sides recoil |
| SALT I and the opening to China | 1972 | Rivalry acquires negotiated rules and a third pole |
| Soviet forces enter Afghanistan | 1979 | Détente is shown to be reversible |
| Communist systems collapse | 1989-91 | The rivalry ends, not by victory in war but by the withdrawal of one side's system |

✅ **Fact:** the advanced owner converts these into an explicit periodisation: hardening blocs from 1945 to 1953; partial thaw with Berlin and Cuba keeping confrontation alive, 1953-62; détente and triangular diplomacy in the 1970s; a second Cold War from 1979 to 1985 after Afghanistan and the missile tensions; and the Gorbachev years from 1985 to 1991.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and the point the advanced owner attaches to it is the one that upgrades an answer: answers become stronger when they show that "Cold War" was not one frozen condition but a sequence of differently structured confrontations. ⚠️ That single move converts a chronicle into an analysis, because each phase then requires a separate mechanism rather than the same adjective repeated.

### 6. The four demand families this material has to serve

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four kinds of question can be built from this topic, and each has a different centre of gravity. Recognising which one has been set is worth more than any amount of extra content.

1. **Causal.** *What caused the Cold War? Assess the relative role of ideology and power politics.* The centre is weighting, not listing.
2. **Episodic.** *Discuss the significance of a named crisis.* The centre is what the crisis revealed about how the system worked, not what happened in it.
3. **Structural.** *Was the Cold War a European conflict? Did the communist bloc act as one? Explain détente.* The centre is the architecture of the rivalry.
4. **Terminal.** *Why did the Cold War end?* The centre is multi-causality, and the boundary with the end-of-Cold-War owner.

✅ **Fact:** the basic owner's directive map states the corresponding prohibitions directly: for the causal family, do not write a blame verdict; for "assess the relative role", do not write "both mattered"; for a crisis question, do not write a crisis narrative; for détente, do not write "tensions reduced"; for the ending, do not write "Gorbachev ended it"; for the decolonised world, do not treat new states as pawns.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** this is a great deal of definitional apparatus before a single event has been described. History is made of events; the categories can be inferred from them afterwards.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection would be right for a topic with one clear storyline, and it is wrong for this one, for a reason internal to the evidence. ✅ The sources themselves record that the two powers created an atmosphere in which every international act could be interpreted in two ways, and that what was claimed as necessary for self-defence by one side was taken by the other as evidence of aggressive intent. ⚠️ A topic whose central mechanism is *double interpretation* cannot be built up from uninterpreted events, because there are none: the Marshall Plan is humanitarian relief or dollar imperialism depending on the frame, and the frame is exactly what the definitional work supplies. ⚠️ Teaching the categories first is therefore not a detour; it is the only way to keep the events from silently choosing a side.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ What was the Cold War? Explain why it did not become a general war between its principals.
> ⚠️ "The Cold War was a conflict without a battlefield." Comment.
> ⚠️ Examine the proposition that the Cold War structured international relations after 1945.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For any of these, open with the absence-plus-substitutes definition in one sentence, then give the restraint mechanism in one more - the American reluctance to use the bomb again unless attacked directly, and the Soviet unwillingness to risk such an attack. ⚠️ Only then begin the evidence. The commonest weak opening in this topic is a paragraph of atmosphere about "tension and mistrust"; the strongest is a definition that has already done analytical work.

### Traps

- Writing that the Cold War "began in 1945" or "began in 1947" as though the date were a fact rather than a periodisation choice. ⚠️ State which marker is being used and why.
- Calling the Cold War a war. ✅ The definition turns on the absence of direct general war between the principals.
- Calling it a peace. ⚠️ Korea, Vietnam and Afghanistan are inside the period, not exceptions to it.
- Defining communism and capitalism at length. ✅ The doctrine owner is a different file; this one owns the geopolitical record.
- Treating "superpower" as a compliment rather than a description of navy, air force, army and the bomb.
- Importing the collapse of the Soviet Union into an origins answer. ⚠️ The end-of-Cold-War owner holds it, and this session hands over at the collapse.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Definition: no direct general war between the principals, but propaganda, arms competition, alliance-building, economic pressure and limited wars.
2. ✅ The core account's version: no actual fighting directly between the camps; hostility expressed through propaganda, economic measures and a general policy of non-cooperation.
3. ✅ The restraint mechanism: the USA was reluctant to use the atomic bomb again unless attacked directly; the USSR dared not risk such an attack.
4. ✅ Superpower content: the USA had the world's largest navy and air force and controlled the atomic bomb; the USSR, severely weakened, still had the largest army in the world.
5. ✅ Containment as Kennan advised it: political, economic and diplomatic means.
6. ✅ The syllabus clause is the General Studies Paper I world-history clause; the doctrines sub-clause is owned elsewhere.
7. ✅ No separate Prelims world-history clause is held; Paper II use is historical support only.
8. ✅ Five boundaries: the Second World War, the United Nations, China, decolonisation and the end of the Cold War.
9. ✅ Period markers: 1945, 1947, 1953, 1962, 1972, 1979, 1989-91.
10. ✅ The advanced owner's five phases: 1945-53, 1953-62, the 1970s, 1979-85, 1985-91.
11. ⚠️ The upgrade sentence: the Cold War was not one frozen condition but a sequence of differently structured confrontations.
12. ⚠️ Four demand families: causal, episodic, structural, terminal - each with a different centre of gravity.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is the definition of the Cold War as "no direct general war between the principals, plus a list of substitutes" more useful in an answer than a definition built on "tension and mistrust"?

**Model answer:** Because it is falsifiable and it does analytical work in advance. ✅ The absence clause commits the answer to the fact that the two principals never fought each other directly in an open general war, which immediately raises the question the whole topic exists to answer - why not - and the sources supply the reply in one clause: the USA was reluctant to use the atomic bomb again unless attacked directly and the USSR dared not risk such an attack. ✅ The substitutes clause commits the answer to a definite list - propaganda, arms competition, alliance-building, economic pressure, a general policy of non-cooperation and limited wars - and every later section of the answer can then be filed under one of those headings, which is why the definition also supplies the structure. ⚠️ "Tension and mistrust", by contrast, is compatible with any great-power rivalry in any century; it excludes nothing, predicts nothing and cannot be tested against a case, so it leaves the examiner with no way of telling whether the candidate has understood this rivalry or merely heard of it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the definition as an introduction to be written and then abandoned. The substitutes list is the file structure of the whole answer, and a script that defines the Cold War by its instruments and then narrates it as a series of dates has discarded the only piece of apparatus it had.

---

## Lesson 2 - Why did the wartime alliance fail? The five causes, and the mechanism that binds them

Progress: 2/22 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the differences of principle between collective and private ownership; the long pre-history of mistrust from 1917 and the interventions of 1918; Stalin's foreign policy aims and what he took; the hardening of American and British policy after April 1945; the atomic asymmetry and what it did to every negotiation; the wartime legacy including the Second Front and the concealment of the bomb; and the security-dilemma mechanism that converts five causes into one process

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - the core account on the causes of the Cold War: the differences of principle between communist and capitalist states, the 1917 origin of capitalist mistrust and the 1918 interventions, Stalin's aims, the change of American policy after Roosevelt's death, the Second Front grievance, the concealment of the bomb and the refusal to share the occupation of Japan
🔍 CA search: not run for this lesson - the single topic-level query is recorded in Lesson 1 and its one result is used in Lesson 6
📰 CA found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five causes, two readings, one process

```text
                    THE FIVE CAUSES THE SOURCES RECORD
   +----------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | 1  DIFFERENCES OF PRINCIPLE                                           |
   |    collective ownership, central planning, state social policy        |
   |            vs                                                          |
   |    private ownership, private enterprise, pursuit of profit           |
   +----------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | 2  SOVIET SECURITY AIMS                                               |
   |    a protective belt in eastern Europe after vast wartime losses      |
   +----------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | 3  WESTERN READING OF SOVIET MOVES                                    |
   |    Truman and many western leaders saw them as expansionist           |
   +----------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | 4  ATOMIC ASYMMETRY                                                   |
   |    the USA had the bomb first; mistrust deepened sharply              |
   +----------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | 5  WARTIME LEGACY                                                     |
   |    delayed Second Front, occupation disputes, postwar planning        |
   +----------------------------------------------------------------------+
                                |
             EACH CAUSE IS READ TWICE - ONCE FROM EACH CAPITAL
                                |
   +---------------------------------+----------------------------------+
   | FROM MOSCOW                     | FROM WASHINGTON                  |
   | invaded in 1941 across the same | a state that has just absorbed   |
   | plain as in 1918; a belt of     | half a continent is not building |
   | friendly states is the minimum  | a fence, it is building a base   |
   | precaution                      |                                  |
   +---------------------------------+----------------------------------+
                                |
                      THE PRODUCT OF THE DOUBLE READING
   "every international act could be interpreted in two ways: what was
    claimed as necessary for self-defence by one side was taken by the
    other as evidence of aggressive intent"
                                |
                    A SECURITY DILEMMA, NOT A PLAN
```

*Read the diagram downwards, not across. The five causes are inputs; the double reading is the operation performed on them; the security dilemma is the output. Candidates who list the causes and stop have shown the examiner the top of the diagram only.*

### 1. The oldest cause: a quarrel that predates the alliance by a quarter of a century

✅ **Fact:** the core account locates the basic cause of conflict in the differences of principle between the communist states and the capitalist or liberal-democratic states. ✅ The communist system of organising state and society was based on the ideas of Karl Marx: the wealth of a country should be collectively owned and shared by everybody, the economy should be centrally planned, and the interests and well-being of the working classes should be safeguarded by state social policies. ✅ The capitalist system operates on the basis of private ownership of a country's wealth, and its driving forces are private enterprise in the pursuit of making profits and the preservation of the power of private wealth.

✅ **Fact:** the antagonism is dated from 1917. Ever since the world's first communist government was set up in Russia in 1917, the governments of most capitalist states viewed it with mistrust and were afraid of communism spreading to their countries, because that would mean the end of private ownership of wealth and the loss of political power by the wealthy classes.

✅ **Fact:** the mistrust was acted on. When civil war broke out in Russia in 1918, several capitalist states - the USA, Britain, France and Japan - sent troops to Russia to help the anti-communist forces. ✅ The communists won that war, and Stalin, who became Russian leader in 1929, was convinced that there would be another attempt by the capitalist powers to destroy communism in Russia; ✅ the German invasion of 1941, the account says, proved him right.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that last sentence is the most useful single item in the lesson, and it is easy to skate over. It tells us that by 1945 the Soviet leadership had a *tested hypothesis*, not a paranoid suspicion: it had predicted a capitalist attack and had been attacked. ⚠️ An answer that presents Soviet postwar suspicion as irrational has ignored the evidence the sources themselves supply, and an answer that treats it as fully justified has forgotten that the attacker in 1941 was Nazi Germany and not a capitalist democracy. ⚠️ The defensible formulation is the narrow one: Stalin's expectation that hostile powers would move against the Soviet system had been confirmed once, and confirmation of a general expectation by a particular event is exactly the kind of evidence that produces overconfident inference.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice also what the 1917-1941 background does to the phrase "wartime alliance". ✅ The account states that the need for self-preservation against Germany and Japan caused the USSR, the USA and Britain to forget their differences and work together. ⚠️ *Forget*, not *resolve*. The alliance suspended a quarrel; it did not settle one. That is why the question "why did the alliance break down?" is slightly misleading, and why the better framing is: why did the suspension end when it did?

### 2. Stalin's policy, stated as the sources state it

✅ **Fact:** Stalin's aim, in the account's words, was to take advantage of the military situation to strengthen Russian influence in Europe. ✅ As the Nazi armies collapsed he tried to occupy as much German territory as he could, and to acquire as much land as he could get away with from countries such as Finland, Poland and Romania. ✅ In this he was highly successful.

✅ **Fact:** the Western response is recorded in the same sentence: the West was alarmed at what they took to be Soviet aggression, and believed that he was committed to spreading communism over as much of the globe as possible.

✅ **Fact:** the owner's own weighting of this cause is that Stalin wanted a protective belt in eastern Europe after vast wartime losses, and that this was a genuine security rationale rather than merely a pretext - while the means, imposed governments, created the very hostility it guarded against.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "as much land as he could get away with" is worth pausing on, because it is not the language of a master plan. It describes an opportunist working an advantage, which is precisely the reading the post-revisionist historians reached from the archives, as Lesson 3 shows. ⚠️ The distinction matters for a "was there a Soviet blueprint?" stem: the evidence held here supports *expansion where the opportunity existed*, and does not support *a prior programme of global revolution executed on schedule*.

### 3. The change in Washington, and why a death in April 1945 belongs in a causal answer

✅ **Fact:** during the war the USA under President Roosevelt sent war materials of all kinds to Russia under Lend-Lease, and Roosevelt was inclined to trust Stalin. ✅ After Roosevelt died in April 1945, his successor Harry S. Truman was more suspicious and toughened his attitude towards the communists.

✅ **Fact:** the account records a contested claim about the bomb and attaches it to named historians rather than asserting it: some historians believe that Truman's main motive for dropping the atomic bombs on Japan was not simply to defeat Japan, which was ready to surrender anyway, but to show Stalin what might happen to Russia if he dared go too far.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** reproduce that claim the way the source does - as a position held by some historians - and it strengthens an answer; reproduce it as a fact and it weakens one. ⚠️ This is the first place in the topic where the fact/inference discipline earns marks directly, because the claim is about *motive*, and motive is exactly the category where archives support competing readings. ⚠️ The safe formulation is: the bomb's use had at minimum a demonstrative dimension in the reading of some historians, and its *effect* on Soviet calculations is not in dispute even where its intended purpose is.

### 4. The wartime grievances, listed from the Soviet side

✅ **Fact:** Stalin suspected that the USA and Britain were still keen to destroy communism. ✅ He felt that their delay in launching the invasion of France - the Second Front, which did not take place until June 1944 - was deliberately calculated to keep most of the pressure on the Russians and bring them to the point of exhaustion.

✅ **Fact:** two further grievances are recorded. The Western allies did not tell Stalin about the existence of the atomic bomb until shortly before its use on Japan. ✅ And they rejected his request that Russia should share in the occupation of Japan.

✅ **Fact:** the account then delivers the summarising clause of the whole section: above all, the West had the atomic bomb and the USSR did not.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the three grievances are not equally weighted, and an answer that lists them flat has missed the structure. The Second Front grievance is retrospective and concerns a war already won. The Japan grievance is procedural and concerns status. ⚠️ The atomic asymmetry is neither: it is a live, continuing condition that altered every subsequent negotiation, because it meant that any Soviet concession on territory or influence was a concession made by the weaker party to the only party holding an unanswerable weapon. ✅ The owner's phrasing of the consequence is exact: it made every conventional concession look like a strategic surrender.

### 5. The five causes, weighted rather than listed

| Cause | ✅ Evidence | ⚠️ Analytical weight | ⚠️ Counter-consideration |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Ideological incompatibility** | ✅ Collective ownership and central planning against private ownership and profit; capitalist mistrust from 1917; interventions in 1918 | Determined *interpretation*: it supplied the lens through which each side read the other's defensive acts | ⚠️ Ideology had not prevented the wartime alliance, so it cannot be a sufficient cause on its own |
| **Soviet security aims** | ✅ A protective belt after vast wartime losses; occupying as much German territory as possible; acquisitions from Finland, Poland and Romania | A genuine rationale, tested by the invasion of 1941 | ⚠️ The chosen means - imposed governments - manufactured the hostility they were meant to guard against |
| **Western reading of Soviet moves** | ✅ Truman and many western leaders saw the moves as expansionist; Truman toughened policy after April 1945 | Explains why containment felt defensive in Washington | ⚠️ Marshall aid also served American economic and political interests, which the revisionists press hard |
| **Atomic asymmetry** | ✅ The USA had the bomb first; the West had it and the USSR did not; mistrust deepened sharply | Converted every negotiation into a bargain between unequals | ✅ The Soviet bomb of September 1949 removed the asymmetry and began the arms race in earnest |
| **Wartime legacy** | ✅ The Second Front delayed to June 1944; the bomb concealed until shortly before use; the refusal to share the occupation of Japan | Explains why 1945 opened with a deficit of trust rather than a neutral slate | — |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the table is designed to be used, not memorised. For a 10-mark "what caused the Cold War" stem, take two rows and their counter-considerations. For 15 marks, take four. For 20, take all five and add the mechanism in section 6. ⚠️ The counter-consideration column is what converts a list into an assessment, because it forces the candidate to state what each cause fails to explain.

### 6. The mechanism: why five causes behave like one process

✅ **Fact:** the account's summarising sentence is the mechanism, and it is worth reproducing almost verbatim: with their entrenched positions and deep suspicions of each other, the USA and the USSR created an atmosphere in which every international act could be interpreted in two ways; what was claimed as necessary for self-defence by one side was taken by the other as evidence of aggressive intent.

✅ **Fact:** the advanced owner states the same point as the safest available exam line: the Cold War emerged from genuine ideological conflict, mutual insecurity and repeated acts that each side interpreted through worst-case assumptions.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is a security dilemma in the technical sense - a situation in which measures a state takes to make itself safer make its rival less safe, so that the rival's counter-measures in turn confirm the first state's fears. ⚠️ Its analytical value is that it is *self-sustaining*: once the double reading is established, no further cause is required to keep the conflict going, which is why the Cold War outlasted every individual grievance that began it, including Stalin himself.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and the limit of the mechanism must be stated too, or the answer becomes a formula. A security dilemma explains escalation; it does not explain the *content* of what each side did. It does not explain why the American instrument of choice was economic aid while the Soviet instrument of choice was governmental control, and that difference is a fact about the two systems rather than about the dilemma. ⚠️ The strongest formulation therefore keeps both halves: ideology determined the content and the interpretation; the security dilemma determined the dynamic.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the security-dilemma account is a way of avoiding the question. Somebody imposed governments on eight countries and somebody did not; treating both sides as trapped in a symmetrical misunderstanding flattens a real moral difference.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is right that symmetry can be overdone, and the evidence held here does not support full symmetry. ✅ The account states plainly that Soviet methods of forcing communist governments on the states of eastern Europe were bound to lend proof to claims that Stalin's aims were expansionist, and that it was Stalin's *methods* of gaining control which upset the West. ⚠️ That is an asymmetric finding and it should be stated. ⚠️ But the dilemma is not a claim about moral equivalence; it is a claim about causation. It says that the sequence of moves was self-reinforcing regardless of who was more culpable - and the proof is that the sequence continued unchanged after 1953, when the man who imposed those governments was dead. ⚠️ The correct answer keeps both: an asymmetry of method, inside a symmetry of dynamic.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ What caused the Cold War? Assess the relative role of ideology and power politics.
> ⚠️ "The Cold War was the product of mutual insecurity rather than of aggressive design." Examine.
> ⚠️ How far did the experience of the Second World War itself make the Cold War likely?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, use the owner's weighting sentence as the thesis - ideology supplied the interpretation, power supplied the stakes - and then prove each half from a different cause: ideology from the 1917-1918 pre-history, power from the atomic asymmetry. ⚠️ For the second, the danger is a formulaic answer; defeat it by stating the asymmetry of method in the same paragraph as the symmetry of dynamic. ⚠️ For the third, the Second World War owner holds the war itself, so confine the answer to the four recorded wartime residues: the Second Front, the concealed bomb, the refused Japanese occupation, and the territorial position the Red Army's advance created.

### Traps

- Listing five causes without weighting them. ⚠️ The directive in this family is almost always "assess" or "how far", and a list scores as narration.
- Dating the ideological quarrel from 1945. ✅ The sources date capitalist mistrust from 1917 and record the interventions of 1918.
- Asserting that Truman dropped the bomb to intimidate Stalin. ✅ The account attributes that to some historians; reproduce the attribution.
- Writing that Stalin had a blueprint for world revolution. ⚠️ The evidence held here describes taking as much as he could get away with, which is opportunism.
- Presenting the security dilemma as a verdict of moral equivalence. ⚠️ It is a causal claim, and the sources separately record an asymmetry of method.
- Forgetting that the alliance suspended rather than resolved the quarrel. ✅ The account says the partners *forgot* their differences for the duration.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Basic cause: differences of principle - collective ownership, central planning and state social policy against private ownership, private enterprise and profit.
2. ✅ Capitalist mistrust dates from the first communist government in Russia in 1917.
3. ✅ In 1918 the USA, Britain, France and Japan sent troops to help anti-communist forces in the Russian civil war.
4. ✅ Stalin expected another capitalist attempt to destroy communism; the German invasion of 1941 is recorded as proving him right.
5. ✅ The alliance was formed because self-preservation against Germany and Japan caused the partners to forget their differences.
6. ✅ Stalin's aim: to use the military situation to strengthen Russian influence; he took as much German territory as he could and land from Finland, Poland and Romania.
7. ✅ Roosevelt was inclined to trust Stalin; Truman, from April 1945, was more suspicious and toughened policy.
8. ✅ Some historians hold that the bombs on Japan were also meant to warn Stalin - an attributed position, not a settled fact.
9. ✅ Soviet grievances: the Second Front delayed to June 1944; the bomb concealed until shortly before use; no share in the occupation of Japan.
10. ✅ The summarising clause: above all, the West had the atomic bomb and the USSR did not.
11. ✅ The mechanism: every international act could be interpreted in two ways; self-defence to one side was aggressive intent to the other.
12. ⚠️ The formulation to use: ideology determined content and interpretation; the security dilemma determined the dynamic.
13. ✅ The asymmetry that must still be stated: it was Stalin's *methods* of gaining control which upset the West.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why the atomic asymmetry of 1945-49 belongs in a different causal category from the other four causes of the Cold War.

**Model answer:** Because the other four are dispositions or grievances, while the asymmetry was a continuing structural condition that altered the terms of every negotiation conducted under it. ✅ Ideological incompatibility, Soviet security aims and the Western reading of them are standing orientations, and the wartime legacy - the Second Front delayed to June 1944, the concealment of the bomb until shortly before its use, the refusal to let Russia share the occupation of Japan - is a set of completed grievances about the past. ✅ The atomic monopoly was neither: the West had the bomb and the USSR did not, and the account makes this its "above all" clause precisely because it operated continuously rather than episodically. ⚠️ The consequence is that between 1945 and September 1949 no Soviet concession could be read in Moscow as a normal diplomatic trade, because every concession was being made to a party holding an unanswerable weapon; the owner's formulation is that it made every conventional concession look like a strategic surrender. ⚠️ That also explains the timing of the arms race: once the Soviet bomb removed the asymmetry in 1949, the competition shifted from monopoly to matching, which is a different and more expensive kind of contest.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the atomic monopoly as merely the first item in an arms race. Between 1945 and 1949 there was no race, because only one side had the weapon; the causal work done in those four years was done by the *imbalance*, and it is a different mechanism from the competitive accumulation that followed.

---

## Lesson 3 - Who was to blame? Three schools, and how to use them instead of listing them

Progress: 3/22 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the orthodox case and George Kennan's position and career; the revisionist case, its American political context and William Appleman Williams; the post-revisionist settlement and the archival access that produced it; the disputed Soviet claim about the reporting of Stalin's February 1946 speech; the declared unreadable passage in the core text; and the procedure for converting a historiographical map into an argued verdict

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - the core account's section "Which side was to blame?", covering Kennan and the 1950s western consensus, the Soviet and 1960s-70s American revisionist position with Williams named, the political context of the Vietnam War, and the post-revisionist reading built on newly opened archives; the immediately following passage naming later historians is illegible in the copy held here and is declared below
🔍 CA search: not run for this lesson - the single topic-level query is recorded in Lesson 1
📰 CA found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the argument as three claims about the same evidence

```text
   THE SAME EVIDENCE BASE
   Soviet control of eastern Europe · the Truman Doctrine · Marshall Aid
   · the atomic monopoly · the Berlin blockade · NATO · Korea
                                |
   +----------------+-----------------+------------------+
   |                |                 |                  |
   ORTHODOX         REVISIONIST       POST-REVISIONIST
   (1950s west)     (USSR; US 1960s-  (US, popular in
                     early 1970s)      the 1980s)
   +----------------+-----------------+------------------+
   | Stalin's       | Soviet moves    | Both sides share |
   | motives were   | were defensive  | responsibility;  |
   | sinister; he   | after enormous  | US economic      |
   | intended to    | wartime losses; | policy sought    |
   | spread         | US atomic and   | political        |
   | communism as   | economic power  | influence, AND   |
   | widely as      | aggravated the  | Stalin was an    |
   | possible       | conflict        | opportunist      |
   +----------------+-----------------+------------------+
   | KENNAN         | WILLIAMS        | archive-based    |
   | containment by | "drive for      | middle view      |
   | political,     | world hegemony" |                  |
   | economic and   | via atomic      |                  |
   | diplomatic     | monopoly and    |                  |
   | means          | industrial      |                  |
   |                | strength        |                  |
   +----------------+-----------------+------------------+
        |                  |                    |
        |   WHAT DRIVES THE DIFFERENCE?         |
        |   not new facts alone, but            |
        |   (a) what counts as evidence of intent
        |   (b) the political climate of the writing decade
        |   (c) how much archive was open
        |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
   | THE SAFE EXAM LINE                                          |
   | "genuine ideological conflict, mutual insecurity, and       |
   |  repeated acts that each side interpreted through           |
   |  worst-case assumptions"                                    |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------+
```

*The diagram's value is the middle band. Most candidates can reproduce the three positions; very few can say what produces the disagreement, and that is the part an "assess" directive is actually testing.*

### 1. The orthodox case, and what Kennan's biography contributes to it

✅ **Fact:** during the 1950s, most western historians, and the account names the American George Kennan and his *Memoirs, 1925-50*, blamed Stalin. ✅ Kennan argued that Stalin's motives were sinister, and that he intended to spread communism as widely as possible through Europe and Asia, thus destroying capitalism.

✅ **Fact:** Kennan's position was not that of a distant observer. During the mid-1940s he had worked at the US embassy in Moscow, and later, in 1952-53, he was US Ambassador in Moscow.

✅ **Fact:** the policy conclusion he drew is the origin of the topic's central term: Kennan advised a policy of containment of the USSR by political, economic and diplomatic means.

✅ **Fact:** the account records what the orthodox reading made of two later events: the formation of NATO and the American entry into the Korean War in 1950 were the West's self-defence against communist aggression.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the biography cuts both ways and a good answer says so. Kennan's Moscow service gives his judgement the authority of proximity; it also means that the orthodox interpretation was written by a participant in the policy it justifies. ⚠️ That is not a refutation - participants often understand what observers miss - but it does explain why the position needed testing against material Kennan himself never saw, which is exactly what happened.

### 2. The revisionist case, and the political weather that produced it

✅ **Fact:** Soviet historians, and during the 1960s and early 1970s some American historians, argued that the Cold War ought not to be blamed on Stalin and the Russians. ✅ Their theory was that Russia had suffered enormous losses during the war, so it was only to be expected that Stalin would try to make sure neighbouring states were friendly, given Russia's weakness in 1945; ✅ that Stalin's motives were purely defensive; and ✅ that there was no real threat to the West from the USSR.

✅ **Fact:** some Americans claim that the USA should have been more understanding and should not have challenged the idea of a Soviet sphere of influence in eastern Europe, and that the actions of American politicians, especially Truman, provoked Russian hostility unnecessarily.

✅ **Fact:** the account names the leading proponent and his thesis: William Appleman Williams believed that the Cold War was mainly caused by the USA's determination to make the most of its atomic monopoly and its industrial strength in its drive for world hegemony.

✅ **Fact:** the account is unusually explicit about the origin of this view, and the point is examinable. The main reason behind the new view was that during the late 1960s many people in the USA became critical of American foreign policy, especially American involvement in the Vietnam War. ✅ This caused some historians to reconsider the American attitude towards communism in general; they felt that American governments had become obsessed with hostility towards communist states, and they were ready to take a more sympathetic view of the difficulties Stalin had found himself in at the end of the Second World War.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the single most useful historiographical fact in the topic, because it lets a candidate say something about *why* interpretations change without speculating. ⚠️ The revisionist turn was not produced by a new document; it was produced by a contemporary war that made a standing interpretation of American conduct look less plausible to the historians living through it. ⚠️ Used carefully, that observation earns marks in any "assess the historiography" stem: interpretations of the Cold War's origins have tracked the perceived character of American power at the time of writing.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the corresponding caution is equally important. The fact that a view arose in a particular political climate does not make it false. Williams's claim is about the leverage of the atomic monopoly and industrial strength, and that leverage is independently documented - the asymmetry of Lesson 2 is not a revisionist invention. ⚠️ The correct treatment is therefore to accept the mechanism the revisionists identified and to question the weight they gave it.

### 3. The post-revisionist settlement, and what made it possible

✅ **Fact:** a third view, known as the post-revisionist interpretation, was put forward by some American historians and became popular in the 1980s. ✅ They had the benefit of being able to look at many new documents and to visit archives which had not been open to earlier historians.

✅ **Fact:** the new evidence suggested that the situation at the end of the war was far more complicated than earlier historians had realised, and this led them to take a middle view, arguing that both sides should take some blame for the Cold War.

✅ **Fact:** the two halves of the post-revisionist claim are stated separately and must be reproduced separately. ✅ They believe that American economic policies such as Marshall Aid were deliberately designed to increase US political influence in Europe. ✅ And they also believe that although Stalin had no long-term plans to spread communism, he was an opportunist who would take advantage of any weakness in the West to expand Soviet influence.

✅ **Fact:** the account closes the section with the asymmetric finding already met in Lesson 2 - Soviet methods of forcing communist governments on the states of eastern Europe were bound to lend proof to claims that Stalin's aims were expansionist - and then with the mechanism: entrenched positions, deep suspicion, and an atmosphere in which every international act could be interpreted in two ways.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the post-revisionist position is often reported in scripts as "both sides were to blame", which drains it of content. ✅ Its actual content is two specific and testable claims: that American aid had a deliberate political purpose, and that Soviet expansion was opportunistic rather than programmatic. ⚠️ Those two claims are what a candidate should write, because each of them contradicts one of the earlier schools at a precise point: the first contradicts the orthodox picture of purely defensive containment, the second contradicts the revisionist picture of purely defensive Soviet conduct.

### 4. A declared gap, and a disputed claim

⚠️ **Declared evidence gap.** The passage of the core text that immediately follows the post-revisionist paragraph - which names later historians writing in the same year on opposite sides of the question, one crediting American policy and the atomic monopoly with the outcome and one locating the collapse of communism in Chinese economic choices - is heavily degraded in the copy held here. Individual words are legible; the sentences are not. **No position, name, date, book or argument from that passage is asserted anywhere in this session**, and no attempt is made to reconstruct it. Where a later reading of the Cold War's outcome is needed, this session uses only what the readable text and the two canonical owners supply.

✅ **Fact, with its dispute displayed:** the account records that Stalin frightened the West by a widely reported speech in February 1946, in which he said that communism and capitalism could never live peacefully together and that future wars were inevitable until the final victory of communism was achieved. ✅ It then records the counter-claim in the same breath: Russian historians have claimed that the speech was reported in the West in a misleading and biased way, especially by George Kennan, who was the US chargé d'affaires in Moscow.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** display that pairing rather than resolving it. It is a small case with a large lesson: the same document generated a Western perception of Soviet intent and a Soviet allegation of Western distortion, and the man at the centre of the allegation is the same man who supplied the orthodox interpretation and the containment doctrine. ⚠️ A candidate who can state that in two sentences has demonstrated exactly the kind of source-criticism the directive "critically examine" asks for.

### 5. How to use the three schools instead of listing them

| Step | ⚠️ What to do | Why it earns marks |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Name the three positions in one sentence each, with the named proponent where the sources supply one | Compression: three schools in three lines leaves room for the analysis |
| 2 | Identify what each school treats as decisive evidence of intent | This is the actual disagreement, and most scripts never reach it |
| 3 | State the archival asymmetry - the post-revisionists had material the first two did not | It explains the sequence without patronising the earlier writers |
| 4 | State the political-climate point about the revisionist turn | A rare, sourced observation about why interpretation moved |
| 5 | Give a graded verdict, not a compromise | "Both sides" is a non-answer; the two post-revisionist sub-claims are an answer |
| 6 | Add the asymmetry of method as the qualification | It prevents the verdict from collapsing into equivalence |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** step 5 deserves emphasis because it is where most answers fail. A graded verdict says which cause carried more weight *and for which phase*. ⚠️ A defensible example: for the period to 1947, the Soviet method of installing governments did more to create the confrontation than any American act, because it broke an undertaking given at Yalta; for the period after 1947, American instruments - aid conditioned on political alignment, a permanent alliance, a rearmament programme - did more to make the division permanent. ⚠️ That is a verdict with a time-signature, and it is far stronger than a single global judgement.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** historiography is a distraction in a General Studies answer. The examiner wants the history, not a survey of what historians have said about it.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is right about proportion and wrong about relevance. ⚠️ Proportion first: three schools in three sentences is correct; three paragraphs is self-indulgent, and a script that spends a third of its length on historians has not answered a question about the Cold War. ⚠️ But relevance is not in doubt, for a reason specific to this topic. The commonest stem in the causal family asks *who was more responsible*, and that stem presupposes that responsibility is contestable. ✅ The three-school map is the evidence that it is contestable, and the archival point is the evidence that the contest has moved. ⚠️ Without them, a candidate asserting a verdict has no basis for the assertion; with them, the verdict is the conclusion of an argument. ⚠️ The correct use is therefore instrumental: historiography earns marks when it is the *reason* for the verdict, and loses them when it is a substitute for one.

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Which side was more responsible for the Cold War? Critically examine.
> ⚠️ "The Cold War was a failure of interpretation rather than a conflict of interests." Assess.
> ⚠️ Examine how interpretations of the origins of the Cold War have changed, and why.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, do not open with the historiography; open with the verdict and use the schools as evidence for it. ⚠️ For the second, the trap is to agree too quickly: interests were real - a protective belt in eastern Europe and an open European market are material interests, not misreadings - so the correct answer concedes the interests and locates the failure of interpretation in the reading of each side's precautions. ⚠️ For the third, the political-climate observation about the late 1960s and the archival-access observation about the 1980s are the two sourced explanations available, and they are enough.

### Traps

- Reporting post-revisionism as "both sides were to blame". ✅ Its content is two specific claims: deliberate political purpose in American aid, and opportunism rather than programme on the Soviet side.
- Attributing the revisionist view only to Soviet historians. ✅ Some American historians of the 1960s and early 1970s held it, and Williams is the named proponent.
- Treating Kennan as a historian only. ✅ He was an embassy official in the mid-1940s and ambassador in 1952-53, and the author of the containment prescription.
- Asserting that Stalin's February 1946 speech proves his intentions. ✅ The Soviet counter-claim of biased Western reporting is recorded alongside it, with Kennan named.
- Reconstructing the illegible passage of the core text from general knowledge. ⚠️ It is declared here and used nowhere.
- Giving an ungraded verdict. ⚠️ A verdict with a time-signature - before and after 1947 - is stronger than a global one.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Orthodox: most western historians of the 1950s, Kennan named, blamed Stalin; his motives were sinister and he intended to spread communism through Europe and Asia.
2. ✅ Kennan's positions: US embassy Moscow in the mid-1940s; ambassador 1952-53; author of the containment prescription by political, economic and diplomatic means.
3. ✅ Orthodox reading of later events: NATO and the American entry into Korea in 1950 were self-defence against communist aggression.
4. ✅ Revisionist: Soviet historians and some American historians of the 1960s and early 1970s; enormous wartime losses; Stalin's motives purely defensive; no real threat to the West.
5. ✅ Williams: the Cold War was mainly caused by the USA's determination to exploit its atomic monopoly and industrial strength in a drive for world hegemony.
6. ✅ Political context: criticism of American foreign policy during the Vietnam War reopened the question in the late 1960s.
7. ✅ Post-revisionist: popular in the 1980s; new documents and newly opened archives; both sides share blame.
8. ✅ Its two claims: Marshall Aid was deliberately designed to increase American political influence in Europe; Stalin had no long-term plan to spread communism but was an opportunist.
9. ✅ The asymmetric finding: Soviet methods of forcing communist governments were bound to lend proof to claims of expansionism.
10. ✅ The disputed speech: February 1946, communism and capitalism could never live peacefully together; Russian historians allege biased Western reporting, naming Kennan.
11. ⚠️ Declared gap: the passage naming later historians on the outcome of the Cold War is illegible here and is used nowhere.
12. ⚠️ The safest line: genuine ideological conflict, mutual insecurity, and repeated acts interpreted through worst-case assumptions.

### Concept check

**Question:** What exactly changed between the orthodox and the post-revisionist interpretations, and why is "new archives" only half of the explanation?

**Model answer:** What changed was the treatment of *intent* on both sides, and the archives explain only one direction of the change. ✅ The orthodox reading, written in the 1950s and associated with Kennan, inferred sinister intent from Soviet conduct and read American policy as self-defence, citing NATO and the entry into Korea. ✅ The post-revisionists, writing in the 1980s with access to documents and archives earlier historians could not use, concluded that the end of the war had been far more complicated than had been realised, and split intent in two: Marshall Aid was deliberately designed to increase American political influence, while Stalin had no long-term plan to spread communism and acted as an opportunist. ⚠️ The archival point explains the second half - a judgement that Soviet policy lacked a programme requires access to the material that would show a programme if one existed. ⚠️ It does not explain the first half, because the political purpose of American aid was always visible in the public record; what changed there was willingness to name it, and the account supplies the reason: criticism of American foreign policy during the Vietnam War led historians to reconsider the American attitude towards communism generally. ⚠️ So the movement had two engines, evidence and climate, and an answer that cites only the archives has explained the Soviet half of the revision and left the American half unexplained.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the three schools as a chronological improvement in which the latest is simply correct. The post-revisionist view is better evidenced on Soviet intentions because of archival access, but on American motives it restates a claim the revisionists had already made; and the account still records an asymmetry of method that a purely symmetrical reading would lose.

---

## Lesson 4 - 1945-47: what actually happened at Yalta, at Potsdam and in eastern Europe

Progress: 4/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Yalta agreements and the Polish disagreement; Potsdam's cooler atmosphere, the German questions, reparations and the Oder-Neisse dispute; the establishment of communist governments across eastern Europe and the Hungarian example; the Yugoslav exception and Tito's legitimacy; Stalin's February 1946 speech and Churchill's Fulton reply; the percentages point about Churchill's 1944 agreement; and why the West's surprise is itself evidence worth using

📚 Book context: queried - the core account on the Yalta agreements and the Polish problem, the Potsdam conference and its disagreements over Poland and reparations, the establishment of pro-communist governments in eastern Europe with the Hungarian case, Tito's elected government in Yugoslavia, the Fulton speech and the iron-curtain phrase, and the record of Churchill's 1944 agreement with Stalin on spheres of influence; the summary of the Yalta conference's opening paragraph is illegible in the copy held here and the agreed points are taken from the readable list that follows it
🔍 CA search: not run for this lesson - the single topic-level query is recorded in Lesson 1

### Visual: what was agreed, what was left open, and what happened to each

   |   and Neisse; no agreement    |      |   the pro-communist Polish    |

   |   compromise: some non-       |      | WITHHELD                      |
   |   communist members to join   |      | · Truman did not inform Stalin|
