*The right-hand column of the Yalta box is the origin of the whole quarrel. Poland is where an alliance that had agreed on the United Nations, on zones and on Japan could not agree on a frontier - and the frontier question was already being settled on the ground by the army that held it.*

### 1. What Yalta settled, and the one thing it did not

✅ **Fact:** at Yalta the agreed points were that a new organisation, to be called the United Nations, should be set up to replace the failed League of Nations; that Germany was to be divided into zones - Russian, American and British, with a French zone included later - while Berlin, which happened to be in the middle of the Russian zone, would also be split into corresponding zones, with similar arrangements for Austria; that free elections would be allowed in the states of eastern Europe; and that Stalin promised to join the war against Japan on condition that Russia received the whole of Sakhalin Island and some territory in Manchuria.

⚠️ **Declared evidence gap.** The opening paragraph of the Yalta section in the copy of the core text held here - the sentences introducing the conference, its location and its attendance - is illegible. The four agreed points above are taken from the readable list that follows. **No date, place description or attendance detail is asserted from the illegible paragraph**; the standard conference dates used for sequencing in this session come from the declared connecting-fact pass recorded in the final ledger.

✅ **Fact:** there were ominous signs of trouble over what was to be done with Poland. ✅ When the Russian armies swept through Poland, driving the Germans back, they had set up a communist government in Lublin, even though there was already a Polish government-in-exile in London. ✅ It was agreed at Yalta that some non-communist members of the London-based government should be allowed to join the Lublin government, while in return Russia would be allowed to keep a strip of eastern Poland which she had annexed in 1939. ✅ However, Roosevelt and Churchill were not happy about Stalin's demand that Poland should be given all German territory east of the rivers Oder and Neisse, and no agreement was reached on this point.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two structural facts about the Polish settlement decide everything that follows. First, the compromise was *additive*: non-communist ministers were to join a communist government, not to replace it, so the communist core was conceded in advance. Second, the frontier question was left open while the territory in question was already under the control of one party to the dispute. ⚠️ A negotiation in which one side holds the object being negotiated is not a negotiation about possession; it is a negotiation about recognition. That is why Poland recurs at Potsdam not as an unsettled question but as an accomplished fact.

### 2. Potsdam: a cooler room and a shorter list

✅ **Fact:** the atmosphere at Potsdam was distinctly cooler. ✅ The three leaders at the beginning were Stalin, Truman - replacing Roosevelt, who had died in April - and Churchill, but Churchill was replaced by Clement Attlee, the new British Labour prime minister, after Labour's election victory.

✅ **Fact:** the war with Germany was over, but no agreement was reached about her long-term future. ✅ The big questions were whether, or when, the four zones would be allowed to join together to form a united country again. ✅ Germany was to be disarmed, the Nazi party would be disbanded and its leaders tried as war criminals. ✅ It was agreed that the Germans should pay something towards repairing the damage they had caused; most of these reparations were to go to the USSR, which would be allowed to take non-food goods from its own zone and from the other zones as well, provided the Russians sent food supplies to the western zones of Germany in return.

✅ **Fact:** it was over Poland that the main disagreement occurred. ✅ Truman and Churchill were annoyed because Germany east of the Oder-Neisse Line had been occupied by Russian troops and was being run by the pro-communist Polish government, which expelled some five million Germans living in the area; this had not been agreed at Yalta.

✅ **Fact:** Truman did not inform Stalin about the exact nature of the atomic bomb, though Churchill was told about it. ✅ A few days after the conference closed, the two atomic bombs were dropped on Japan; ✅ the Russians had declared war on Japan on 8 August and invaded Manchuria; ✅ and the fighting was brought to an end without the need for Russian help. ✅ They annexed south Sakhalin as agreed at Yalta, but they were allowed no part in the occupation of Japan.

⚠️ **Correction to the held wording, declared.** The account writes that "the war ended quickly on 10 August". ⚠️ That compresses three distinct moments and this session separates them: **10 August 1945** is the date of Japan's conditional offer to surrender; the Allied acceptance was announced and Japanese capitulation made public on **14-15 August 1945**; and the **Instrument of Surrender was signed on 2 September 1945**. ⚠️ Nothing in this topic's argument depends on which of the three is used - the point is that the war ended without a Soviet contribution that would have earned an occupation share - but an answer that writes "the war ended on 10 August" has stated an offer as a conclusion, and the distinction is exactly the kind an examiner tests.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the reparations formula is worth a sentence in any answer on why Germany could not be reunified, because it is a mechanism rather than a grievance. It required a *continuing exchange* between zones under different administrations - industrial goods flowing east, food flowing west - which meant that the economic management of the four zones had to remain coordinated indefinitely. ⚠️ Once either side concluded that the other was failing to perform, the reparations clause became a standing reason to treat the zones as separate economies, which is exactly what happened.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and notice the sequence of the last paragraph. Stalin was not told the nature of the bomb; Churchill was; the bombs were used; the war ended without Soviet help; and the Soviet request to share the occupation of Japan was refused. ⚠️ That is four items in a fortnight, each individually defensible on Allied grounds, and collectively a demonstration to Moscow that the alliance's most important decisions would now be taken by two of its three members. ⚠️ This is the clearest early instance of the double reading of Lesson 2, and it is worth using as the example when the mechanism has to be demonstrated rather than asserted.

### 3. Eastern Europe: what "systematic interference" consisted of

✅ **Fact:** in the months following Potsdam, the Russians systematically interfered in the countries of eastern Europe to set up pro-communist governments. ✅ This happened in Poland, Hungary, Bulgaria, Albania and Romania. ✅ In some cases their opponents were imprisoned or murdered.

✅ **Fact:** the Hungarian case is the account's worked example, and it is the most useful one because it does not involve a coup. In Hungary the Russians allowed free elections; but although the communists won less than 20 per cent of the votes, they saw to it that a majority of the cabinet were communists.

✅ **Fact:** by the end of 1947 every state in the area with the exception of Czechoslovakia had a fully communist government. ✅ Elections were rigged; non-communist members of coalition governments were expelled; many were arrested and executed; and eventually all other political parties were dissolved. ✅ All this took place under the watchful eyes of secret police and Russian troops.

✅ **Fact:** in addition, Stalin treated the Russian zone of Germany as if it were Russian territory, allowing only the Communist Party and draining it of vital resources.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Hungarian example deserves the space because it isolates the mechanism. An election was held, it was lost, and the outcome was produced anyway through control of the cabinet. ⚠️ That tells a candidate what "Sovietisation" actually means in this period: not primarily the cancellation of elections, but the systematic detachment of executive power from electoral results, backed by police and troops. ⚠️ It is also the most economical reply to the revisionist claim that Soviet conduct was purely defensive, because a purely defensive requirement - governments that would not join a hostile alliance - did not require a cabinet majority after a 20 per cent vote.

### 4. Yugoslavia: the exception that defines the rule

✅ **Fact:** only Yugoslavia did not fit the pattern. ✅ Here the communist government of Marshal Tito had been legally elected in 1945. ✅ Tito had won the election because of his immense prestige as leader of the anti-German resistance; ✅ it was Tito's forces, not the Russians, who had liberated Yugoslavia from German occupation; ✅ and Tito resented Stalin's attempts to interfere.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Yugoslav case is the control experiment for the whole of eastern Europe. It isolates the variable: where a communist movement had won its own war and therefore had its own legitimacy, it obtained power by election and then refused Soviet direction. ⚠️ Where communist parties depended on the Red Army for their position, they obtained power by manipulation and accepted direction. ⚠️ That single contrast supports two conclusions an examiner rewards: Soviet control in eastern Europe rested on the presence of Soviet force rather than on communism as such; and the later Yugoslav divergence, which Lesson 13 and the end-of-Cold-War owner develop, was visible in 1945 in the manner of Tito's arrival.

### 5. February 1946 and March 1946: two speeches that named the division

✅ **Fact:** Stalin frightened the West further by a widely reported speech in February 1946 in which he said that communism and capitalism could never live peacefully together and that future wars were inevitable until the final victory of communism was achieved. ✅ Russian historians have claimed that the speech was reported in the West in a misleading and biased way, especially by George Kennan, then US chargé d'affaires in Moscow.

✅ **Fact:** Churchill responded at Fulton, Missouri, in March 1946, repeating a phrase he had used earlier: "From Stettin in the Baltic to Trieste in the Adriatic, an iron curtain has descended across the continent." ✅ Claiming that the Russians were bent on "indefinite expansion of their power and doctrines", he called for a Western alliance which would stand firm against the communist threat.

✅ **Fact:** the speech drew a sharp response from Stalin, who revealed his fears about Germany and the need to strengthen Soviet security, and who was able to denounce Churchill as a "warmonger".

✅ **Fact:** the account records that not everybody in the West agreed with Churchill: over a hundred British Labour MPs signed a motion criticising the Conservative leader for his attitude.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Labour MPs' motion is a small fact that does disproportionate work. It shows that the hardening of Western policy was a contested political choice inside Western democracies, not an automatic response to Soviet conduct. ⚠️ A candidate who knows it can avoid the two lazy formulations - "the West responded" and "public opinion demanded" - and write instead that a particular reading of Soviet intentions won a political argument in the West during 1946-47, over identifiable opposition.

### 6. The uncomfortable fact: the West had already conceded a sphere

✅ **Fact:** the account states that the West was profoundly irritated by Russia's treatment of eastern Europe, which disregarded Stalin's promise of free elections made at Yalta - and yet that they ought not to have been surprised at what was happening: even Churchill had agreed with Stalin in 1944 that much of eastern Europe should be a Russian sphere of influence.

✅ **Fact:** the account then lists the arguments available to Stalin: that friendly governments in neighbouring states were necessary for self-defence; that these states had never had democratic governments anyway; and that communism would bring much-needed progress to backward countries. ✅ And it delivers the verdict that decides how this material should be used: it was Stalin's *methods* of gaining control which upset the West, and they gave rise to the next major developments.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this paragraph is the most examinable in the lesson because it refuses both easy answers. It does not say that the West had no grievance; it says that the grievance was about method rather than about the existence of a sphere, because the existence of a sphere had already been conceded in 1944. ⚠️ That distinction is precisely the kind of qualification that converts a "how far was Stalin to blame" answer from assertion into argument, and it also supplies the transition to Lesson 5: because the objection was to methods rather than to the sphere itself, the Western response took the form of building a counter-sphere by different methods - aid, recovery and alliance - rather than of contesting the existing one.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the eastern European sequence is simply Soviet conquest dressed up as politics. Rigged elections, expelled ministers, executions, secret police and Russian troops are not a "method of gaining control"; they are annexation, and the analytical language here is too neutral.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is right that the language must not soften the record, and this session states the record without softening: opponents imprisoned or murdered, elections rigged, ministers expelled, parties dissolved, all under secret police and Russian troops. ⚠️ But the objection over-reads the conclusion it wants. Three pieces of evidence in the same pages prevent "annexation" from being the whole account. ✅ Yugoslavia took the communist road without Soviet imposition and then refused Soviet direction. ✅ Hungary held a free election, which the communists lost, before the cabinet was captured - so the process ran *through* electoral institutions rather than simply abolishing them. ✅ And Czechoslovakia remained a functioning democracy with a freely elected coalition until February 1948, which means the pattern was neither instantaneous nor uniform. ⚠️ The precise finding is therefore harsher in one respect and more exact in another: Soviet control was imposed, and it was imposed by varied political means whose variation is itself evidence about what Moscow thought it needed in each case.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How far did the wartime conferences themselves make the Cold War likely?
> ⚠️ "Soviet policy in eastern Europe between 1945 and 1948 was defensive." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Examine the significance of the Polish question in the breakdown of the wartime alliance.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, the structure is agreed/unagreed rather than narrative: list what Yalta and Potsdam settled, then show that the two items left open - the German future and the Polish frontier - were exactly the two that could not be settled later, and explain why the reparations formula made German unity harder rather than easier. ⚠️ For the second, use Hungary and Yugoslavia as the two decisive cases and close with the method/sphere distinction. ⚠️ For the third, the strongest line is the one in section 1: the frontier was being negotiated while one party already held the ground, so the dispute was about recognition rather than possession.

### Traps

- Describing Yalta as a failure. ✅ It agreed on the United Nations, on zones for Germany and Austria, on free elections in eastern Europe and on Soviet entry against Japan; it failed on Poland's western frontier.
- Writing that Churchill attended all of Potsdam. ✅ He was replaced by Attlee after the British election result.
- Saying that Stalin was told nothing about the bomb. ✅ He was not told its exact nature; Churchill was told.
- Writing that the war with Japan "ended on 10 August". ✅ 10 August is the surrender offer; the acceptance was announced on 14-15 August and the Instrument of Surrender was signed on 2 September 1945.
- Treating eastern European Sovietisation as uniform. ✅ Hungary's free election, Yugoslavia's elected communist government and Czechoslovakia's survival until 1948 are three distinct cases.
- Forgetting Churchill's own 1944 agreement on spheres. ✅ The account uses it to explain why the West ought not to have been surprised.
- Presenting the Fulton speech as official Western policy. ✅ Churchill was then the leader of the opposition in Britain, and over a hundred Labour MPs signed a motion criticising him.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Yalta agreements: the United Nations; German and Austrian zones with Berlin split inside the Russian zone; free elections in eastern Europe; Soviet entry against Japan for Sakhalin and Manchurian territory.
2. ✅ Yalta's Polish compromise: non-communist ministers to join the Lublin government; Russia keeps the strip annexed in 1939; no agreement on the Oder-Neisse frontier.
3. ✅ Potsdam: a distinctly cooler atmosphere; Truman for Roosevelt; Attlee for Churchill mid-conference.
4. ✅ Potsdam agreed: disarmament, the disbanding of the Nazi party, war-crimes trials, and reparations mostly to the USSR with a food-for-goods exchange.
5. ✅ Potsdam did not agree: whether or when the four zones would rejoin; and the Oder-Neisse position, where five million Germans were expelled.
6. ✅ Truman withheld the bomb's exact nature from Stalin; Churchill was told; the USSR declared war on 8 August and invaded Manchuria; the war with Japan ended without Russian help; it annexed south Sakhalin but got no part in the occupation. ⚠️ The account's "ended quickly on 10 August" is corrected: 10 August is the surrender offer, 14-15 August the acceptance and announcement, 2 September the signed Instrument of Surrender.
7. ✅ Pro-communist governments were installed in Poland, Hungary, Bulgaria, Albania and Romania; opponents were imprisoned or murdered.
8. ✅ Hungary: free elections, communists under 20 per cent of the vote, a communist majority in the cabinet anyway.
9. ✅ By the end of 1947 every state in the area except Czechoslovakia had a fully communist government.
10. ✅ Yugoslavia: Tito legally elected in 1945 on resistance prestige; his forces, not the Russians, liberated the country; he resented Soviet interference.
11. ✅ February 1946: Stalin's speech on the impossibility of peaceful coexistence; Russian historians allege biased Western reporting by Kennan.
12. ✅ March 1946: Fulton, the iron curtain from Stettin to Trieste, the call for a Western alliance; Stalin's "warmonger" reply; over a hundred Labour MPs object.
13. ✅ Churchill had agreed with Stalin in 1944 that much of eastern Europe should be a Russian sphere; the objection was to Stalin's methods.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using Hungary and Yugoslavia, explain what Soviet control in eastern Europe actually depended on.

**Model answer:** On the presence of Soviet power, not on communism as a shared doctrine. ✅ In Hungary the Russians allowed free elections, the communists won less than 20 per cent of the votes, and they nonetheless saw to it that a majority of the cabinet were communists - so the electoral institution survived while its consequence was detached from it, backed, as across the region, by secret police and Russian troops. ✅ In Yugoslavia the communist government of Tito was legally elected in 1945 on the prestige of having led the anti-German resistance, and it was Tito's forces rather than the Russians who had liberated the country - with the result that Tito resented Stalin's attempts to interfere. ⚠️ Placing the two cases side by side isolates the variable: where a communist party owed its position to the Red Army, Moscow could convert an adverse electoral result into a favourable cabinet; where a communist party owed its position to its own war, Moscow could not direct it at all. ⚠️ The conclusion is that the eastern bloc was held together by the distribution of force rather than by ideological solidarity, which is also why the bloc's first open defection was Yugoslav and why the later Sino-Soviet quarrel was possible at all.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the eastern European takeovers as a single event in 1945. The process ran from 1945 to 1948, used different means in different states, and left Czechoslovakia with a freely elected coalition until February 1948 - which is precisely why the Czechoslovak coup, when it came, had the shock value it did.

## Lesson 5 - Containment built: the Truman Doctrine, Marshall Aid, the Cominform and Czechoslovakia

Progress: 5/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Greece and the British appeal; the exact Truman formula of March 1947 and the aid that followed; the European Recovery Programme, its stated purpose and its probable political aim; the sixteen participants and the 13-billion-dollar figure with the 2.5-per-cent qualification; the historians' dispute over what the Plan actually achieved; Molotov's "dollar imperialism" and the Soviet refusal; the Cominform, the Molotov Plan and Comecon; the Yugoslav expulsion of 1948; and the Czechoslovak coup of February 1948 with its arithmetic and its consequence

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - the core account on the Truman Doctrine and its Greek origin, the Marshall Plan with Marshall's own statement of purpose and the debate between American and European historians over its effect, the Cominform and Comecon, and the Czechoslovak coup of February 1948 including the election arithmetic of 1946 and the death of Jan Masaryk
🔍 CA search: not run for this lesson - the single topic-level query is recorded in Lesson 1

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four instruments and their four answers, in sequence

```text
   TRIGGER
   Greece: communists trying to overthrow the monarchy, helped from
   Albania, Bulgaria and Yugoslavia; British troops, who had restored
   the monarchy in 1944, are feeling the strain; Bevin appeals to the USA
        |
        v
   INSTRUMENT 1 - THE TRUMAN DOCTRINE (March 1947)
   the USA "would support free peoples who are resisting subjugation by
   armed minorities or by outside pressures"
   -> Greece receives massive arms and supplies; by 1949 the communists
      are defeated
   -> Turkey receives aid worth about $60 million
   -> MEANING: no return to isolation; containment of communism not just
      in Europe but throughout the world, including Korea and Vietnam
        |
        v
   INSTRUMENT 2 - THE MARSHALL PLAN / ERP (announced June 1947)
   "our policy is directed not against any country or doctrine, but
   against hunger, poverty, desperation and chaos"
   -> 16 nations draw up a joint plan by September
   -> over $13 billion over four years
   -> BUT: aid averaged only about 2.5% of those countries' total
      national income
        |
        +------------------ SOVIET READING -----------------+
        |  Molotov: "dollar imperialism" - a device for      |
        |  gaining control of western Europe and interfering |
        |  in eastern Europe. The USSR rejects it; satellites|
        |  and interested Czechoslovakia are not allowed to  |
        |  take it                                           |
        +----------------------------------------------------+
        |
        v
   ANSWER 1 - THE COMINFORM (September 1947)
   draws together European communist parties; to be communist is not
   enough - it must be Russian-style communism; eastern Europe to be
   industrialised, collectivised, centralised; trade primarily inside
   the bloc; Yugoslavia expelled 1948 but stays communist
   ANSWER 2 - THE MOLOTOV PLAN (1947): Russian aid to the satellites
   ANSWER 3 - COMECON: co-ordination of their economic policies
        |
        v
   INSTRUMENT 3 (by force) - CZECHOSLOVAKIA, February 1948
   the last democratic state in eastern Europe; coalition freely elected
   1946; communists 38% of votes, 114 of 300 seats, one third of cabinet
   -> May elections due; communists expected to lose ground, blamed for
      the rejection of Marshall Aid
   -> armed coup by a party already controlling unions and police
   -> the iron curtain is complete
```

*Read the arrows and the topic's first mechanism appears: an instrument on one side produces a counter-instrument on the other, and each counter-instrument then justifies the next instrument. Nothing in this sequence requires a new cause after 1946.*

### 1. Greece, and why the trigger matters more than it looks

✅ **Fact:** the Truman Doctrine sprang from events in Greece, where communists were trying to overthrow the monarchy. ✅ British troops, who had helped liberate Greece from the Germans in 1944, had restored the monarchy, but they were now feeling the strain of supporting it against the communists, who were receiving help from Albania, Bulgaria and Yugoslavia. ✅ Ernest Bevin, the British Foreign Minister, appealed to the USA.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of this trigger shape the doctrine that came out of it, and they are worth stating because they explain the doctrine's odd wording. First, the threat was internal - a civil conflict, not an invasion - which is why the formula speaks of "armed minorities" as well as "outside pressures". Second, the communists were being helped by neighbouring communist states but not, on this evidence, directly by Moscow, which is why the doctrine had to be framed against communism in general rather than against the USSR by name. Third, the immediate cause of American involvement was British incapacity. ⚠️ The Truman Doctrine is therefore, among other things, a document recording the transfer of a security commitment from a declining power to an ascending one - and that transfer is a theme this session meets again at Suez in 1956.

### 2. The doctrine, its exact formula, and what followed within two years

✅ **Fact:** Truman announced in March 1947 that the USA "would support free peoples who are resisting subjugation by armed minorities or by outside pressures".

✅ **Fact:** Greece immediately received massive amounts of arms and other supplies, and by 1949 the communists were defeated. ✅ Turkey, which also seemed under threat, received aid worth about $60 million.

✅ **Fact:** the account states the doctrine's meaning in two clauses that should be memorised together. The Truman Doctrine made it clear that the USA had no intention of returning to isolation as she had after the First World War; and she was committed to a policy of containing communism, not just in Europe, but throughout the world, including Korea and Vietnam.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that second clause is the hinge of the entire topic and is routinely underused. A commitment defined by the *type* of threat rather than by a *region* has no geographical stopping point, and the account says so by naming Korea and Vietnam in the same sentence. ⚠️ This is the precise mechanism by which a European dispute became a global one: not because the superpowers chose to globalise it, but because the doctrine that justified the first intervention contained no principle that could exclude the next. ⚠️ Any answer on "was the Cold War a European conflict?" should use this sentence, because it converts the globalisation of the rivalry from a description into an explanation.

### 3. Marshall Aid: the stated purpose, the probable aim, and the numbers

✅ **Fact:** announced in June 1947, the Marshall Plan was an economic extension of the Truman Doctrine. ✅ American Secretary of State George Marshall produced his European Recovery Programme, which offered economic and financial help wherever it was needed. ✅ "Our policy", he declared, "is directed not against any country or doctrine, but against hunger, poverty, desperation and chaos."

✅ **Fact:** western Europe was certainly suffering from all of these problems, exacerbated by the coldest winter for almost seventy years in 1947-48.

✅ **Fact:** the account then separates purposes carefully, and the separation should be reproduced. One of the aims of the ERP was to promote the economic recovery of Europe, but there was more behind it than humanitarian feeling: a prosperous Europe would provide lucrative markets for American exports; and its main aim was probably political, since communism was less likely to gain control in a flourishing western Europe.

✅ **Fact:** by September, sixteen nations - Britain, France, Italy, Belgium, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, Portugal, Austria, Greece, Turkey, Iceland, Norway, Sweden, Denmark, Switzerland and the western zones of Germany - had drawn up a joint plan for using American aid. ✅ During the next four years over $13 billion of Marshall Aid flowed into western Europe, fostering the recovery of agriculture and industry, which in many countries were in chaos because of war devastation. ✅ During the same period the communist parties in western Europe suffered electoral defeat, most notably in France and Italy, which had seemed the most likely states to go communist.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "its main aim was probably political" is the account's own hedge, and it should be carried across intact. A candidate who writes that the Marshall Plan *was* a political instrument has overstated a source that says *probably*; a candidate who writes that it was humanitarian relief has ignored the sentence entirely. ⚠️ The accurate formulation is that the Plan had a declared humanitarian purpose, a visible commercial benefit and a probable political aim, and that the three are compatible - which is exactly why the Plan is such good evidence for the double-reading mechanism of Lesson 2.

### 4. The historians' dispute, and the number that decides it

✅ **Fact:** most American historians have claimed that Europe's rapid recovery from impending economic and political disaster was due entirely to the Marshall Plan, which was held up as a perfect example of humanitarian intervention. ✅ Greg Behrman, in a history of the Plan published in 2008, follows the same line: Marshall and his assistants were heroes and America saved Europe from economic disaster and a communist takeover. ✅ In another 2008 publication, Nicolaus Mills also sees the Plan as a model of how to help states struggling with exhaustion, poverty and economic chaos, though he admits that European leaders themselves played an important part in their countries' recovery.

✅ **Fact:** European historians have rejected the view that Europe was saved solely by the Marshall Plan. ✅ They point out that European economies recovered so quickly after 1947 that the conditions for recovery must already have been in place. ✅ And although $13 billion sounds an enormous sum, Marshall Aid averaged only about 2.5 per cent of the total national income of the sixteen countries involved.

✅ **Fact:** the account then poses the counterfactual and answers it. If Marshall Aid had not been available, would western Europe have turned communist, either from electoral choice or by Soviet invasion? ✅ The overwhelming evidence suggests that the communists' popularity was already in decline before American aid began to arrive. ✅ And most historians agree that Stalin was more concerned to protect Soviet security than to start launching wholesale invasions of western Europe.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 2.5 per cent figure is the most valuable quantity in this lesson, because it converts a debate about motives into a testable claim about magnitude. An intervention worth one-fortieth of national income can plausibly have been decisive at the margin - filling a dollar shortage, financing imports of capital goods, stabilising a currency - and cannot plausibly have been the sole cause of an entire continent's recovery. ⚠️ The defensible verdict is therefore *catalytic rather than causal*: the Plan accelerated and secured a recovery whose conditions were already present, and its political effect - the containment of communist parties that were in any case losing ground - was similarly reinforcing rather than originating.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and there is a second-order point worth one sentence in a strong answer. Both halves of the counterfactual answer weaken the *orthodox* case rather than the Plan: if communist popularity was already declining and Stalin was not planning invasions, then the danger against which containment was built was smaller than containment's architects believed. ⚠️ That does not make containment irrational - decisions are made under uncertainty - but it does mean that a "how necessary was the Marshall Plan?" stem has a genuinely open answer, which is why examiners set it.

### 5. The Soviet reading, and the three answers it produced

✅ **Fact:** the Russians were well aware that there was more to Marshall Aid than pure benevolence. ✅ Although in theory aid was available for eastern Europe, Russian Foreign Minister Molotov denounced the whole idea as "dollar imperialism". ✅ He saw it as a blatant American device for gaining control of western Europe, and worse still, for interfering in eastern Europe, which Stalin considered to be Russia's sphere of influence. ✅ The USSR rejected the offer, and neither her satellite states nor Czechoslovakia, which was showing interest, were allowed to take advantage of it.

✅ **Fact:** the Cominform - the Communist Information Bureau - was the Soviet response to the Marshall Plan. ✅ Set up by Stalin in September 1947, it was an organisation to draw together the various European communist parties. ✅ All the satellite states were members, and the French and Italian communist parties were represented. ✅ Stalin's aim was to tighten his grip on the satellites: to be communist was not enough - it must be Russian-style communism. ✅ Eastern Europe was to be industrialised, collectivised and centralised; states were expected to trade primarily with Cominform members; and all contacts with non-communist countries were discouraged.

✅ **Fact:** when Yugoslavia objected she was expelled from the Cominform in 1948, though she remained communist.

✅ **Fact:** in 1947 the Molotov Plan was introduced, offering Russian aid to the satellites, and another organisation, Comecon - the Council of Mutual Economic Assistance - was set up to co-ordinate their economic policies.

⚠️ **Open gap, declared.** The sources used here give **no founding date for Comecon**, and none is supplied. ⚠️ The Molotov Plan is dated 1947 by the account; Comecon is introduced in the same sentence without a year, which is not the same thing as being dated to 1947, and this session does not date it. ⚠️ An answer should write "Comecon, set up to co-ordinate the satellites' economic policies" and give no year unless the candidate holds one from a source of their own.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Cominform's defining clause - *to be communist was not enough; it must be Russian-style communism* - is the single best piece of evidence in the early period for the bloc discipline of Lesson 13, and it should be used there as well as here. ⚠️ It establishes that the requirement of conformity was recognised as a problem inside the bloc in 1947, which is eleven years before the Sino-Soviet quarrel and immediately produced the Yugoslav expulsion. ⚠️ The bloc's internal diversity is therefore not a late development; it is present at the moment the bloc is organised.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice also the asymmetry between the two economic systems that emerges here and lasts the whole period. The Western instrument was a transfer of resources to states that then spent them under their own governments; the Eastern instrument combined a smaller transfer with a requirement to restructure the domestic economy and to reorient trade. ⚠️ Lesson 21 develops the consequence, but the seed is planted in September 1947.

### 6. Czechoslovakia, February 1948: the arithmetic, the coup and the consequence

✅ **Fact:** the communist takeover of Czechoslovakia came as a great blow to the Western bloc, because it was the only remaining democratic state in eastern Europe.

✅ **Fact:** there was a coalition government of communists and other left-wing parties, freely elected in 1946. ✅ The communists had won 38 per cent of the votes and 114 seats in the 300-seat parliament, and they held a third of the cabinet posts. ✅ The prime minister, Klement Gottwald, was a communist; President Benes and the foreign minister, Jan Masaryk, were not. ✅ They hoped that Czechoslovakia, with its highly developed industries, would remain as a bridge between east and west.

✅ **Fact:** a crisis arose early in 1948. ✅ Elections were due in May, and all the signs were that the communists would lose ground; they were blamed for the Czech rejection of Marshall Aid, which might have eased the continuing food shortages. ✅ The communists decided to act before the elections; already in control of the unions and the police, they seized power in an armed coup. ✅ All non-communist ministers with the exception of Benes and Masaryk resigned. ✅ A few days later Masaryk's body was found under the windows of his offices; his death was officially described as suicide, but when the archives were opened after the collapse of communism in 1989, documents were found which proved beyond doubt that he had been murdered. ✅ The elections were held in May but there was only a single list of candidates, all communists; Benes resigned and Gottwald became president.

✅ **Fact:** the western powers and the UN protested but felt unable to take any action because they could not prove Russian involvement - the coup was purely an internal affair. ✅ However, the account records that there can be little doubt that Stalin, disapproving of Czech connections with the West and of the interest in Marshall Aid, had prodded the Czech communists into action; ✅ nor was it just coincidence that several of the Russian divisions occupying Austria were moved up to the Czech frontier. ✅ The bridge between East and West was gone; the iron curtain was complete.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Czechoslovak case is the cleanest demonstration in the topic of how the two instrument-sets interacted, and it repays being written out as a chain. Marshall Aid was offered; Czechoslovakia showed interest; Moscow forbade acceptance; the refusal prolonged food shortages; the shortages were blamed on the communists; the communists faced electoral defeat in May; and they therefore seized power in February using instruments - the unions and the police - that they already held. ⚠️ Every link is sourced, and the chain shows an intervention producing exactly the outcome it was intended to prevent in one bloc while producing its mirror image in the other.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the account's treatment of Soviet involvement is also a model of evidential discipline worth imitating. It records that the West could not prove Russian involvement; it records the judgement that there can be little doubt Stalin prodded the Czech communists; and it records the corroborating fact of the troop movements from Austria. ⚠️ That is three different epistemic statuses in three sentences - unproven, judged highly likely, and independently observed - and reproducing the distinction is worth more than asserting a conclusion.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the Marshall Plan cannot be treated as a Cold War instrument, because it was formally open to the Soviet Union and its neighbours. An offer that the other side refuses is not an act of hostility.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection has real force and must be conceded first: ✅ in theory aid was available for eastern Europe, and it was Molotov who denounced it and the USSR that rejected it, with the satellites and an interested Czechoslovakia forbidden to accept. ⚠️ But three considerations qualify the concession. ✅ First, the account itself records that the Plan's main aim was probably political, and that a flourishing western Europe would be less likely to fall to communism - an aim that does not disappear because the offer was formally universal. ⚠️ Second, acceptance carried conditions that no Soviet-aligned government could meet without exposing its economy to external scrutiny and reorienting its trade westwards, so an offer that is formally open can be substantively closed. ✅ Third, the timing is decisive: the Cominform followed in September 1947 and the Czechoslovak coup in February 1948, both of them responses to the Plan, which is precisely how contemporaries on both sides understood it. ⚠️ The defensible position is therefore that the Plan was a genuine recovery programme, a genuine commercial opportunity and a genuine Cold War instrument at the same time - and that insisting on only one of the three is what produces a weak answer.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the role of economic instruments in the early Cold War.
> ⚠️ "The Truman Doctrine and the Marshall Plan were two halves of a single policy." Discuss.
> ⚠️ How important was the Marshall Plan in the recovery of western Europe?
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the Czechoslovak coup of February 1948.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first two, the structure writes itself from the visual: trigger, doctrine, economic extension, Soviet reading, three counter-instruments, and the coup that completed the division; the analytical payload is the "no geographical stopping point" clause. ⚠️ For the third, the 2.5-per-cent figure and the two counterfactual findings - communist popularity already declining, Stalin not planning invasions - give a graded verdict of catalytic rather than sole causation. ⚠️ For the fourth, do not narrate the coup: state the arithmetic of 1946, the expected loss in May, the aid refusal that produced the blame, and the instruments already held - and close with the unprovable-but-judged-likely status of Soviet involvement.

### Traps

- Dating the Truman Doctrine to the Marshall Plan or vice versa. ✅ The Doctrine is March 1947; the Plan was announced in June 1947.
- Quoting the Truman formula loosely. ✅ It is support for free peoples resisting subjugation by armed minorities or by outside pressures.
- Writing that the Marshall Plan saved western Europe. ✅ European historians reject sole causation, and the aid averaged about 2.5 per cent of national income.
- Treating the Cominform as an aid organisation. ✅ Aid was the Molotov Plan; co-ordination was Comecon; the Cominform enforced Russian-style communism.
- Saying Yugoslavia left the communist bloc in 1948. ✅ She was expelled from the Cominform and remained communist.
- Describing the Czechoslovak coup as a Soviet invasion. ✅ It was an internal armed seizure by a party controlling the unions and the police, with Russian divisions moved to the frontier and Soviet involvement judged likely but not proven at the time.
- Forgetting Masaryk. ✅ The death was officially called suicide; documents found after 1989 proved murder.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Trigger: Greek communists, helped from Albania, Bulgaria and Yugoslavia; British strain; Bevin's appeal.
2. ✅ Truman Doctrine, March 1947: support for free peoples resisting subjugation by armed minorities or outside pressures.
3. ✅ Results: massive supplies to Greece and communist defeat by 1949; about $60 million to Turkey.
4. ✅ Meaning: no return to isolation; containment of communism worldwide, including Korea and Vietnam.
5. ✅ Marshall Plan, June 1947: the European Recovery Programme; Marshall's declared target was hunger, poverty, desperation and chaos.
6. ✅ Mixed purposes: European recovery, lucrative markets for American exports, and a probable main political aim.
7. ✅ Sixteen nations by September; over $13 billion over four years; western European communist parties defeated electorally, notably in France and Italy.
8. ✅ The qualification: aid averaged only about 2.5 per cent of the sixteen countries' total national income.
9. ✅ The counterfactual findings: communist popularity was already declining, and Stalin was more concerned with Soviet security than with invading western Europe.
10. ✅ Molotov: "dollar imperialism"; the USSR refused, and forbade the satellites and Czechoslovakia to accept.
11. ✅ Cominform, September 1947: Russian-style communism required; industrialisation, collectivisation, centralisation, intra-bloc trade; Yugoslavia expelled 1948 but still communist.
12. ✅ Molotov Plan 1947 for aid; Comecon for economic co-ordination - ⚠️ undated in the sources used here, and left undated.
13. ✅ Czechoslovakia: 1946 coalition freely elected; communists 38 per cent and 114 of 300 seats; Gottwald communist premier, Benes president, Masaryk foreign minister.
14. ✅ February 1948: coup before the May elections; unions and police already held; Masaryk's death, later proved a murder; single-list elections in May; Gottwald president.
15. ✅ Consequence: the bridge between East and West gone; the iron curtain complete.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why does the Truman Doctrine's wording, rather than the aid that followed it, explain the globalisation of the Cold War?

**Model answer:** Because the wording defined the commitment by the type of threat rather than by a place, and a commitment of that shape has no boundary. ✅ Truman announced in March 1947 that the USA would support free peoples who are resisting subjugation by armed minorities or by outside pressures - a formula generated by a Greek civil conflict in which the insurgents were helped by neighbouring states rather than invaded by a great power, which is why both an internal and an external source of pressure had to be covered. ✅ The aid that followed was geographically bounded: supplies to Greece, where the communists were defeated by 1949, and about $60 million to Turkey. ⚠️ The doctrine was not, and the account draws the conclusion itself: the USA was committed to containing communism not just in Europe but throughout the world, including Korea and Vietnam. ⚠️ That sentence is the mechanism by which a European quarrel about Poland and Germany became a set of commitments in east Asia within three years, because nothing in the formula supplied a principle for refusing the next case that resembled Greece.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating globalisation as a deliberate decision to extend the rivalry worldwide. The evidence supports something less intentional and more binding - a general criterion adopted for a particular emergency, which then applied itself wherever the criterion was met.
