### Visual: the technical ladder and the political problem at the top of it

```text
   1945  ATOMIC BOMB, US MONOPOLY
         first use on Hiroshima and Nagasaki demonstrates "horrifying
         powers of destruction"; the world is left under the threat of
         a nuclear war that might destroy the entire planet
              |
              |   THE DETERRENT ARGUMENT (as the sources state it)
              |   "some people argue that this acted as a deterrent,
              |    making both sides in the Cold War so frightened of
              |    the consequences that they were deterred or
              |    discouraged from fighting each other"
              v
   Sept 1949  THE SOVIET ATOMIC BOMB -> "an arms race began to develop"
              |
   1949-50    Truman gives the go-ahead for a US hydrogen bomb "many
              times more powerful"; NSC-68 (April 1950) regards the
              Russians as fanatics who would stop at nothing, and
              proposes to more than triple armaments expenditure
              |
   Aug 1953   the Russians as well as the Americans have a hydrogen bomb
              -> "the two sides were now so finely balanced that
                 international tensions had to be relaxed if nuclear war
                 was to be avoided"
              |
   late 1950s SATELLITE AND MISSILE COMPETITION
              (the detail of this paragraph is DECLARED ILLEGIBLE here;
               only the recorded consequence is used: the Americans
               within a few months launched an earth satellite of
               their own)
              |
   1960s      ICBMs and SLBMs (Polaris, later Poseidon) - submarine
              launch removes the value of the Turkish bases
              |
   early 70s  the USSR overtakes the USA and her allies in numbers of
              ICBMs and SLBMs, and brings out the ABM
              SOVIET MOTIVE: not only security - "if they could get
              somewhere near equality with the Americans, there would
              be a good chance of persuading them to limit and reduce
              the arms build-up"
              |
   1970s      the US MIRV: one missile, as many as 14 warheads, each
              programmed to a different target
   1977       the Soviet SS-20: their version, three warheads, targeted
              on western Europe, less sophisticated
   late 70s   US Cruise missiles based in Europe, flying low to
              penetrate under Russian radar
              |
              v
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | THE DANGER, NAMED BY THE SOURCES                                  |
   | "both sides had enough of this horrifying weaponry to destroy the |
   |  world many times over. The main danger was that one side or the  |
   |  other might be tempted to try and win a nuclear war by striking  |
   |  first and destroying all the other side's weapons before they    |
   |  had time to retaliate."                                          |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
```

*The ladder is not the point; the box at the bottom is. Every technical step above it is intelligible only as an attempt to make a first strike less attractive - or, in the case of the MIRV and the ABM, as a step that made it more so.*

### 1. The deterrent argument, and the care the sources take with it

✅ **Fact:** the account records that the first ever use of nuclear weapons, on Hiroshima and Nagasaki, demonstrated their horrifying powers of destruction, and that the world was left under the threat of a nuclear war that might well have destroyed the entire planet. ✅ It then states the deterrent claim with an attribution rather than as a finding: some people argue that this acted as a deterrent, making both sides in the Cold War so frightened of the consequences that they were deterred or discouraged from fighting each other.

✅ **Fact:** a separate and more definite statement appears in the causes section, and it is the one to rely on: open war was avoided because the Americans were reluctant to use the atomic bomb again unless attacked directly, while the Russians dared not risk such an attack.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the difference between those two formulations is examinable. The first is a general theory - weapons so terrible that they prevent war. The second is a specific historical claim about two governments' calculations in a particular period. ⚠️ A candidate should prefer the second, because it can be tested against cases and the first cannot. ⚠️ And when it is tested, it holds in a qualified form: the principals never fought each other directly, but they fought through others in Korea, Vietnam and Afghanistan, which means nuclear weapons did not prevent war - they relocated it. ⚠️ That is the sentence to write, because it concedes the deterrent's real effect while refusing the claim that the period was peaceful.

### 2. 1949 to 1950: the end of the monopoly and the American response

✅ **Fact:** when it became known in September 1949 that the USSR had successfully exploded an atomic bomb, an arms race began to develop. ✅ Truman responded by giving the go-ahead for the USA to produce a hydrogen bomb many times more powerful than the atomic bomb.

✅ **Fact:** his defence advisers produced a secret document, known as NSC-68, in April 1950, which shows that they had come to regard the Russians as fanatics who would stop at nothing to spread communism all over the world. ✅ They suggested that expenditure on armaments should be more than tripled in an attempt to defeat communism.

✅ **Fact:** it was not only the Russians who alarmed the Americans: a communist government was proclaimed in China in October 1949 after Mao Zedong had defeated Chiang Kai-shek, who had been supported by the USA and was now forced to flee to Taiwan. ✅ When the USSR and communist China signed a treaty of alliance in February 1950, American fears of an advancing tide of communism seemed about to be realised. ✅ It was in this atmosphere of American anxiety that the Cold War spotlight shifted to Korea.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the eight months from September 1949 to April 1950 are the densest in the topic and should be taught as a single causal block rather than as separate items. The monopoly ends; a communist government takes power in the world's most populous country; the two are formally allied; a secret assessment concludes that the adversary will stop at nothing; and armaments expenditure is proposed to be more than tripled. ⚠️ The Korean invasion of June 1950 then arrives into a Washington that has already reached those conclusions - which is precisely why, as Lesson 9 shows, Truman was convinced the attack was Stalin's doing. ⚠️ The sequence explains an interpretation, and explaining interpretations is what this topic rewards.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** NSC-68 also deserves a note on its status. It is an internal assessment by defence advisers, and the account uses it as evidence of *how they had come to regard* the adversary. ⚠️ It is therefore excellent evidence about American threat perception and weak evidence about Soviet intentions - and using it for the second purpose is a common error.

### 3. A declared gap in the arms-race section

⚠️ **Declared evidence gap.** The page of the core text carrying the opening of the nuclear-arms-race section - including the paragraph on the first artificial satellite and the American reaction to it - is largely illegible in the copy used here, as is the page carrying the photograph and caption on the Berlin Wall. **No detail of the satellite episode is asserted in this session beyond the one legible clause**, namely that the Americans again felt that they dared not be left behind and within a few months had launched an earth satellite of their own. **No date, name, designation or technical specification for any satellite is stated anywhere in this session**, and none is reconstructed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the legible clause is nevertheless worth using, because it contains the whole psychology of the arms race in nine words - *they dared not be left behind*. ⚠️ An arms race driven by the fear of falling behind is self-propelling in a way that one driven by a calculated requirement is not: a state that needs a specific capability stops when it has it, while a state that needs not to be behind cannot stop until its rival does.

### 4. The technical sequence, taught by what each system did to the balance

| System | ✅ What it is, as the account describes it | ⚠️ Its effect on the first-strike problem |
|---|---|---|
| **ICBM** | Intercontinental ballistic missile | Compresses warning time; makes fixed launch sites targets |
| **SLBM** | Submarine-launched ballistic missile; ✅ the American Polaris, later Poseidon, launched from submarines in the eastern Mediterranean | *Reduces* the temptation: submarines at sea cannot be destroyed in a first strike, so retaliation survives |
| **ABM** | ✅ The anti-ballistic missile, brought out by the Russians, which could destroy incoming enemy missiles before they reached their targets | *Increases* the temptation: a side that can intercept the survivors of its own first strike has less to fear from retaliating |
| **MIRV** | ✅ The multiple independently targetable re-entry vehicle: a missile which could carry as many as fourteen separate warheads, each programmed to hit a different target | *Increases* it sharply: one missile can destroy many of the enemy's, so striking first becomes arithmetically attractive |
| **SS-20** | ✅ The Soviet version of the MIRV, 1977, targeted on western Europe, less sophisticated, carrying three warheads | Regionalises the problem: a European theatre weapon rather than an intercontinental one |
| **Cruise** | ✅ Developed by the Americans at the end of the 1970s and based in Europe; the new refinement was that they flew in at low altitudes and so were able to penetrate under Russian radar | Compresses warning further and complicates detection |

✅ **Fact:** the account records the Soviet motive for the catch-up of the 1960s and it is not the obvious one. The Russians decided to go all out to catch up with the American stockpile of ICBMs and SLBMs; their motive was not just to increase their own security - they hoped that if they could get somewhere near equality with the Americans, there would be a good chance of persuading them to limit and reduce the arms build-up.

✅ **Fact:** the account also records the opportunity that made it possible: as the Americans became more deeply involved in the war in Vietnam from 1961 to 1975, they had less to spend on nuclear weapons, and slowly but surely the Russians began to catch up; by the early 1970s they had overtaken the USA and her allies in numbers of ICBMs and SLBMs.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Soviet motive as the account states it is the direct link between this lesson and Lesson 18, and it should be used there. It means that SALT I in 1972 was not an interruption of the arms race but its intended destination for one of the parties: the Soviet leadership built up in order to negotiate down. ⚠️ It also supplies the least obvious cost of Vietnam. The war did not only fail in its own terms; by absorbing American resources it permitted a strategic shift that the United States had not chosen, and the negotiations of the 1970s were conducted from a position that the 1960s had altered.

### 5. Why the arms race was dangerous, stated precisely

✅ **Fact:** the account's summary is blunt: by this time both sides had enough of this horrifying weaponry to destroy the world many times over; the main danger was that one side or the other might be tempted to try and win a nuclear war by striking first and destroying all the other side's weapons before they had time to retaliate.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most important sentence in the lesson, because it identifies the danger as *instability* rather than *quantity*. Large arsenals that can survive an attack are stabilising, because neither side can profit from going first. Arsenals that are vulnerable, or that can multiply their effect through MIRVs and be defended by ABMs, are destabilising, because the advantage of going first becomes calculable. ⚠️ That is why the MIRV and the ABM are the two most dangerous items in the table above, and why they are precisely the systems that arms control tried to address: ✅ SALT I decided how many ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs each side could have, and there was no agreement about MIRVs. ⚠️ The agreement covered the categories that could be counted and left uncovered the one that mattered most, which is the single sharpest criticism of détente's first achievement.

### 6. The protest movements, and an honest verdict on them

✅ **Fact:** people in many countries were worried at the way the major powers continued to pile up nuclear weapons and failed to make any progress towards controlling them, and movements were set up to try to persuade governments to abolish nuclear weapons.

✅ **Fact:** in Britain the Campaign for Nuclear Disarmament, started in 1958, put pressure on the government to take the lead so that Britain would be the first nation to abandon nuclear weapons - unilateral disarmament - in the hope that the USA and the USSR would follow. ✅ They held mass demonstrations and rallies, and every year at Easter they held a protest march from London to Aldermaston, where there was an atomic weapons research base, and back.

✅ **Fact:** no British government dared take the risk. ✅ They believed that unilateral disarmament would leave Britain vulnerable to a nuclear attack from the USSR, and would only consider abandoning their weapons as part of a general agreement by all the major powers - multilateral disarmament.

✅ **Fact:** during the 1980s there were protest demonstrations in many European countries, including West Germany and Holland, and also in the USA. ✅ In Britain many women protested by camping around the American base at Greenham Common in Berkshire, where the Cruise missiles were positioned; the fear was that if the Americans ever fired any of these missiles, Britain could be almost destroyed by Russian nuclear retaliation.

✅ **Fact:** the account's verdict is carefully hedged and should be reproduced hedged: in the long run, perhaps the enormity of it all and the protest movements did play a part in bringing both sides to the negotiating table.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the protest material is worth including in an answer for two reasons that have nothing to do with sympathy. First, it is evidence that the Cold War had a domestic political dimension inside Western democracies - governments had to justify deterrence to electorates, and the eastern bloc faced no equivalent requirement, which is a real asymmetry. ⚠️ Second, the unilateral/multilateral distinction is a genuine analytical distinction that scripts frequently blur: unilateral means disarmament by one state alone, multilateral means disarmament by general agreement, and the British governments' position was that only the second was safe. ⚠️ The correct verdict is the account's own - a possible contribution, stated as a perhaps - and a candidate who claims more has overstated the source.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the deterrence explanation is unfalsifiable. Every year without a superpower war is counted as a success for deterrence, and no possible evidence could count against it. It is therefore not an explanation at all.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is a serious one and it is partly right, which is why the sources themselves attribute the general deterrent claim rather than asserting it. ⚠️ But three considerations rescue a narrower version. ✅ First, there is direct evidence of the calculation in a specific case: at Cuba in 1962, the account records that both sides realised how easily a nuclear war could have started and how terrible the results would have been, that it seemed to bring them both to their senses, and that a marked relaxation of tension followed with a hotline and a test-ban treaty. ⚠️ That is behaviour changing in response to the danger, which is evidence rather than assumption. ✅ Second, the account attributes the restraint to a particular asymmetry - American reluctance to use the bomb again *unless attacked directly*, Soviet unwillingness to risk such an attack - which is a testable claim about conditions rather than a general law. ⚠️ Third, the deterrence claim is not that war was impossible but that direct general war between the principals was avoided, and the proxy wars of Lessons 9, 15 and 19 are exactly what a theory of relocated rather than abolished conflict predicts. ⚠️ The defensible position is therefore modest: nuclear weapons raised the threshold for direct conflict between the principals and lowered the cost of conflict conducted through others.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "Nuclear weapons kept the Cold War cold." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Examine the causes and consequences of the nuclear arms race.
> ⚠️ Assess the view that arms control during the Cold War addressed the symptoms rather than the causes of insecurity.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, do not simply agree. Concede the specific mechanism, then use the relocation argument - Korea, Vietnam, Afghanistan - and close with the Cuban evidence of behaviour actually changing. ⚠️ For the second, use the ladder, but organise it by the first-strike problem rather than chronologically, because that converts a list of acronyms into an argument. ⚠️ For the third, the decisive fact is that SALT I counted ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs and left MIRVs uncovered - the destabilising system was the one not addressed.

### Traps

- Asserting that nuclear weapons prevented war. ✅ The account attributes the general claim to "some people"; the specific claim is about direct general war between the principals only.
- Dating the arms race from 1945. ✅ The account dates its development from the Soviet bomb of September 1949.
- Using NSC-68 as evidence of Soviet intentions. ✅ It is evidence of how American defence advisers had come to regard the Russians.
- Reconstructing the satellite episode. ⚠️ That passage is illegible here and nothing beyond the recorded American response is stated.
- Treating all weapons systems as equally destabilising. ⚠️ SLBMs reduced the first-strike temptation; ABMs and MIRVs increased it.
- Confusing the MIRV and the SS-20. ✅ Up to fourteen warheads against three, and the SS-20 was targeted on western Europe.
- Confusing unilateral with multilateral disarmament. ✅ One state alone against general agreement among the major powers.
- Claiming the protest movements produced arms control. ✅ The account offers only a "perhaps" about a part played.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Hiroshima and Nagasaki demonstrated horrifying powers of destruction; the world was left under the threat of a war that might have destroyed the planet.
2. ✅ The deterrent claim is attributed, not asserted; the specific claim is that the USA was reluctant to use the bomb again unless attacked directly and the USSR dared not risk such an attack.
3. ✅ September 1949: the Soviet atomic test; an arms race began to develop.
4. ✅ Truman authorised a hydrogen bomb many times more powerful; by August 1953 both sides had one.
5. ✅ NSC-68, April 1950: the Russians regarded as fanatics who would stop at nothing; armaments expenditure to be more than tripled.
6. ✅ October 1949 the Chinese communist government; February 1950 the Sino-Soviet treaty of alliance; then Korea in June 1950.
7. ✅ SLBMs: Polaris, later Poseidon, launched from submarines in the eastern Mediterranean.
8. ✅ ABM: destroys incoming missiles before they reach their targets.
9. ✅ MIRV: as many as fourteen warheads on one missile, each programmed to a different target.
10. ✅ SS-20, 1977: the Soviet version, three warheads, targeted on western Europe, less sophisticated.
11. ✅ Cruise, end of the 1970s: based in Europe, flying low to penetrate under Russian radar.
12. ✅ The Soviet motive for catching up: near-equality as the route to persuading the Americans to limit and reduce the build-up.
13. ✅ The opportunity: American spending absorbed by Vietnam, so that by the early 1970s the USSR had overtaken the USA and her allies in ICBMs and SLBMs.
14. ✅ The danger: enough weaponry to destroy the world many times over, and the temptation to strike first and destroy the other side's weapons before they could retaliate.
15. ✅ CND from 1958, the Aldermaston marches, the unilateral/multilateral distinction, the 1980s demonstrations and Greenham Common; the account's verdict is a hedged "perhaps".

### Concept check

**Question:** Why were the ABM and the MIRV more dangerous than the increase in the total number of warheads?

**Model answer:** Because they changed the incentive to strike first, which is the danger the account actually names. ✅ Its summary is that both sides had enough weaponry to destroy the world many times over, and that the main danger was that one side might be tempted to try to win a nuclear war by striking first and destroying all the other side's weapons before they had time to retaliate. ⚠️ Totals do not affect that temptation much once both arsenals are large, because the deciding question is not how many weapons exist but how many would survive an attack. ✅ The MIRV made one missile capable of carrying as many as fourteen separate warheads, each programmed to a different target, so a single attacking missile could destroy several of the defender's - which makes striking first arithmetically profitable in a way it had not been. ✅ The ABM could destroy incoming missiles before they reached their targets, which means an attacker who had already reduced the defender's arsenal might expect to intercept whatever retaliation survived. ⚠️ Together they made a first strike look calculable, and that is a change of kind rather than of degree - which is also why SALT I's failure to cover MIRVs, while counting ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs, is the most substantial criticism of it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that every new weapon made the world more dangerous in the same way. The submarine-launched missile did the opposite: a retaliatory force hidden at sea cannot be destroyed in a first strike, so it removed rather than created the incentive that the account identifies as the central danger.

---

## Lesson 9 - Korea, 1950-53: the war that globalised containment

Progress: 9/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the origins of the division at the 38th parallel and the two states of 1948; the four competing explanations of who launched the invasion and the most widely accepted view; Truman's four recorded reasons for intervening; the UN authorisation obtained in the Soviet delegation's absence and the fifteen contributing states - the USA and fourteen others; the course of the war from Pusan to Inchon to the Yalu and the Chinese counter-offensive; MacArthur's removal and the choice for containment over rollback; and the five recorded results

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - the core account on the war in Korea and its effects on international relations: the Japanese occupation since 1910, the division at the 38th parallel, the two republics of 1948, the four suggested origins of the invasion, Truman's reasons for acting, the Security Council decision in the Russian delegation's absence, the campaign from Pusan and Inchon to the Yalu, the Chinese intervention, MacArthur's removal, Panmunjom, and the five results including SEATO and the peaceful-coexistence agreements with India and Burma
🔍 CA search: not run for this lesson - the single topic-level query is recorded in Lesson 1
📰 CA found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a war in four movements, with the line it began and ended on

```text
   BEFORE
   Korea under Japanese occupation since 1910
   August 1945: the USA and the USSR agree to divide the country along
   the 38th parallel to organise the Japanese surrender and withdrawal -
   Russia in the north (which has a frontier with the USSR), the
   Americans in the south. NOT INTENDED, as far as the Americans were
   concerned, to be permanent
   The UN wants free elections for the whole country; the Americans
   agree, believing their zone's two-thirds of the population would
   outvote the communist north. No agreement is reached; the artificial
   division continues
   August 1948  Republic of Korea (South), Syngman Rhee, capital Seoul
   September 1948  Democratic People's Republic of Korea (North),
                   Kim Il Sung, capital Pyongyang
   1949  Russian and American troops withdraw
   Both leaders claim the right to rule the whole country; most Koreans
   bitterly resent the division forced on them by outsiders

   THE WAR
   MOVEMENT 1  June 1950  North invades without warning
               September: communists hold all but the south-east
               around Pusan
   MOVEMENT 2  15 September  American marines land at Inchon, near
               Seoul, 200 miles behind the communist front lines;
               by the end of September UN troops have entered Seoul
               and cleared the south
               >>> THE DECISION POINT: instead of calling for a
                   ceasefire now that the original UN objective had
                   been achieved, Truman orders an invasion of the
                   North, with UN approval, to unite the country and
                   hold free elections
   MOVEMENT 3  October  Pyongyang captured; two-thirds of the North
               occupied; UN troops reach the River Yalu, the frontier
               with China. Zhou Enlai's warning that China would resist
               had been ignored
               November: a Chinese counter-offensive with over 300 000
               troops described as "volunteers"; by mid-January 1951
               UN troops are out of the North, across the 38th parallel,
               and Seoul is captured again
   MOVEMENT 4  MacArthur argues for attacking Manchuria, with atomic
               bombs if necessary; Truman judges this would provoke a
               large-scale war the USA did not want, settles for
               containing communism, and removes MacArthur
               June 1951  UN troops clear the South again and fortify
               the frontier
               Peace talks at Panmunjom last two years, ending July 1953
               with the frontier roughly along the 38th parallel, where
               it had been before the war began

   AFTER: the line is where it started. About four million Korean
   soldiers and civilians killed; five million homeless; the division
   seemingly permanent; both states intensely suspicious, heavily armed,
   with constant ceasefire violations
```

*The shape of the diagram is the argument. A war that begins and ends on the same line is not explained by its military course; it is explained by the two decision points inside it - the choice to cross the parallel in October 1950, and the choice not to cross the Yalu in 1951.*

### 1. How the division happened, and why it hardened

✅ **Fact:** the origins of the war lay in the fact that Korea had been under Japanese occupation since 1910. ✅ When the Japanese were defeated in August 1945, the USA and the USSR agreed to divide the country into two zones along the 38th parallel, so that they could jointly organise the Japanese surrender and withdrawal - Russia in the north, which had a frontier with the USSR, and the Americans in the south. ✅ As far as the Americans were concerned, it was not intended to be a permanent division.

✅ **Fact:** the United Nations wanted free elections for the whole country and the Americans agreed, believing that since their zone contained two-thirds of the population, the communist north would be outvoted. ✅ However, the unification of Korea, like that of Germany, soon became part of Cold War rivalry: no agreement could be reached, and the artificial division continued.

✅ **Fact:** elections were held in the south, supervised by the UN, and the independent Republic of Korea was set up with Syngman Rhee as president and its capital at Seoul in August 1948. ✅ The following month the Russians created the Democratic People's Republic of Korea under the communist government of Kim Il Sung, with its capital at Pyongyang. ✅ In 1949 Russian and American troops were withdrawn, leaving a potentially dangerous situation: most Koreans bitterly resented the artificial division forced on their country by outsiders, but both leaders claimed the right to rule the whole country. ✅ Before very long it was clear that Syngman Rhee was a ruthless authoritarian, while Kim Il Sung was even worse: he seemed to be modelling himself on Stalin, arresting and executing many of his critics.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the German parallel is drawn by the account itself and it is the fastest way into the topic, but the differences matter more than the similarity. In Germany the occupying powers remained; in Korea both withdrew in 1949, leaving two governments each claiming the whole and neither restrained by a great-power garrison. ⚠️ In Germany the division ran between two zones of a defeated aggressor; in Korea it ran through a country that had itself been occupied since 1910 and whose population resented the line. ⚠️ The combination - a resented line, two irredentist governments and no external garrison - is why the Korean division produced a war and the German division produced a blockade.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and the American expectation about elections is worth a sentence, because it shows how the division hardened. Washington agreed to all-Korea elections precisely because it expected to win them on population. ⚠️ Once agreement proved impossible, the arrangement that survived was the one neither side had designed: a temporary administrative line converted into a frontier by the failure of the political process meant to remove it. ⚠️ That is the same mechanism as Germany, and it is the general lesson of both: occupation lines become frontiers when the politics of reunification fails, and they do so without anyone deciding that they should.

### 2. Who started it? Four accounts, and the one most widely accepted

✅ **Fact:** without warning, North Korean troops invaded South Korea in June 1950. ✅ The account then states plainly that even now it is still not clear how the attack originated, or whose idea it was, and offers four suggestions.

| # | ✅ The suggestion as recorded | ⚠️ What it would imply |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | It was Kim Il Sung's own idea, possibly encouraged by a statement by Dean Acheson, the American Secretary of State, earlier in 1950: talking about which areas around the Pacific the USA intended to defend, Acheson for some reason did not include Korea | A local initiative encouraged by an inadvertent Western signal - which would make the war a failure of deterrence by ambiguity |
| 2 | Kim may have been encouraged by the new Chinese communist government, who were at the same time massing troops in Fukien province facing Taiwan, as if about to attack Chiang Kai-shek | A Chinese-sponsored initiative - which would make the Sino-American hostility of Lesson 13 prior to the war rather than produced by it |
| 3 | Perhaps Stalin and the Russians were responsible, wanting to test Truman's determination; they had supplied the North Koreans with tanks and other equipment. A communist takeover of the south would strengthen Russia's position in the Pacific and be a splendid gesture against the Americans, to make up for Stalin's failure in West Berlin | The reading Washington adopted - and the one that justified treating the invasion as a global challenge |
| 4 | The communists claimed that South Korea had started the war, when troops of the "bandit traitor" Syngman Rhee had crossed the 38th parallel | A claim recorded as a claim; the account does not endorse it |

✅ **Fact:** the account then gives the balance of opinion: probably the most widely accepted view nowadays is that Kim Il Sung himself pressed the idea of a campaign to unify the peninsula, and that both the USSR and China approved the plan and promised help in the way of war materials, but made it clear that they had no desire themselves to become directly involved.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that settlement is the single most useful item in the lesson, and it should be quoted in substance rather than summarised, because it resolves the Korea question in the exact way the owner's factual-risk control requires - Korea was both a Korean conflict and a Cold War crisis. ⚠️ Initiative was local; approval and materials were external; direct involvement was withheld. ⚠️ A candidate who writes that Stalin ordered the invasion has adopted one of four suggestions as fact; a candidate who writes that it was a purely Korean civil war has ignored the approval and the tanks. ⚠️ The accurate sentence carries all three elements, and it also models the proxy discipline of this session: the patrons enabled a war they did not originate and declined to fight themselves.

### 3. Truman's four reasons, and what they reveal

✅ **Fact:** the account lists four reasons for Truman's decision to intervene. ✅ He was convinced that the attack was Stalin's doing; he took it as a deliberate challenge and saw it as part of a vast Russian plan to spread communism as widely as possible. ✅ Some Americans saw the invasion as similar to Hitler's policies during the 1930s: appeasement of the aggressors had failed then, and therefore it was essential not to make the same mistake again. ✅ Truman thought it important to support the United Nations Organization, which had replaced the League of Nations; the League had failed to preserve peace because the great powers, and especially the USA, had not been prepared to back it, and Truman was determined that the USA should not repeat that fatal mistake. ✅ And Truman was a Democrat president under severe criticism from Republicans for failing to act against what they saw as the dangerous spread of world communism - Senator Joseph McCarthy had claimed that the State Department was "infested" with communists working in effect for the USSR, and Truman was anxious to show that this claim was preposterous.

✅ **Fact:** American policy therefore changed decisively: instead of just economic help and promises of support, Truman decided it was essential for the West to take a stand by supporting South Korea. ✅ American troops in Japan were ordered to Korea even before the UN had decided what action to take.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the four reasons are of four different kinds, and separating them is what converts a list into analysis. The first is an assessment of the adversary, and Lesson 8 has already shown that NSC-68 had reached it two months earlier. The second is an analogy with the 1930s - a historical argument, and one whose accuracy depends entirely on whether the first assessment was right. The third is institutional: a commitment to the credibility of the UN, explicitly framed as learning from the League's failure. ⚠️ The fourth is domestic, and it is the one candidates usually omit: a president exposed to a McCarthyite charge of communist infiltration had a political incentive to act that was independent of the strategic case.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the detail that American troops were ordered from Japan *before* the UN had decided is the sharpest single fact in this section. ⚠️ It establishes that the UN's authorisation ratified an American decision rather than producing one - which is the necessary qualification to any claim that Korea was a United Nations action, and which belongs in any answer that touches the UN's Cold War role while leaving the UN's own assessment to its owner.

### 4. The UN authorisation, and the absence that made it possible

✅ **Fact:** the UN Security Council called on North Korea to withdraw her troops, and when this was ignored, asked member states to send help to South Korea. ✅ This decision was reached in the absence of the Russian delegation, who were boycotting meetings in protest against the UN refusal to allow Mao's new Chinese regime to be represented, and who would certainly have vetoed such a decision.

✅ **Fact:** in the event, the USA and fourteen other countries - Australia, Canada, New Zealand, Nationalist China, France, the Netherlands, Belgium, Colombia, Greece, Turkey, Panama, the Philippines, Thailand and Britain - sent troops, making **fifteen troop-contributing states in all**, though the vast majority were Americans. ✅ All forces were under the command of American General MacArthur.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Soviet boycott is one of the great contingencies of the period and it is worth stating as one. The Security Council authorisation that gave the intervention its legal character was obtained only because the state that would have vetoed it was absent, and absent for a reason unconnected with Korea - a protest about Chinese representation. ⚠️ Two consequences follow. First, the "UN action" in Korea was legally possible by accident, and the Soviet delegation returned to the Council promptly afterwards, which is why no comparable authorisation was available in any later Cold War crisis. ⚠️ Second, the composition of the force - the vast majority American, under an American commander - meant that the UN flag covered what was in practice an American operation with allied contingents, which is exactly how the communist world described it and why, as the results show, it denounced the organisation as a tool of the capitalists.

### 5. The two decision points

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** everything analytically interesting in this war is concentrated in two choices, and an answer that reaches them has already outperformed a narrative.

**Decision point one: crossing the 38th parallel, October 1950.** ✅ By the end of September UN troops had entered Seoul and cleared the south of communists. ✅ Instead of calling for a ceasefire, now that the original UN objective had been achieved, Truman ordered an invasion of North Korea, with UN approval, aiming to unite the country and hold free elections. ✅ The Chinese Foreign Minister Zhou Enlai warned that China would resist if UN troops entered North Korea, but the warning was ignored. ✅ By the end of October UN troops had captured Pyongyang, occupied two-thirds of North Korea and reached the River Yalu, the frontier with China.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the moment the war changes category. Up to September the operation was the reversal of an aggression - containment in its literal sense. After September it was an attempt to unify Korea by force, which is rollback, and the objective had been changed without the mandate being reconsidered. ⚠️ The cost was immediate and had been announced in advance: ✅ the Chinese government was seriously alarmed, because the Americans had already placed a fleet between Taiwan and the mainland to prevent an attack on Chiang, and there seemed every chance that they would now invade Manchuria. ⚠️ A candidate who can state that the Chinese intervention followed an ignored warning and a change of war aims has explained the war's second half rather than described it.

**Decision point two: not crossing the Yalu, 1951.** ✅ In November the Chinese launched a massive counter-offensive with over 300 000 troops described as "volunteers"; by mid-January 1951 they had driven the UN troops out of North Korea, crossed the 38th parallel and captured Seoul again. ✅ MacArthur was shocked at the strength of the Chinese forces and argued that the best way to defeat communism was to attack Manchuria, with atomic bombs if necessary. ✅ However, Truman thought this would provoke a large-scale war which the USA did not want, so he decided to settle for merely containing communism; MacArthur was removed from his command. ✅ In June UN troops cleared the communists out of South Korea again and fortified the frontier. ✅ Peace talks opened at Panmunjom, lasted two years, and ended in July 1953 with an agreement that the frontier should be roughly along the 38th parallel, where it had been before the war began.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the removal of MacArthur is the Cold War's clearest demonstration of the nuclear ceiling described in Lesson 8, and it is more informative than any statement of doctrine. A theatre commander proposed attacking the territory of a nuclear-armed adversary's ally with atomic weapons; the president judged that this would produce the large-scale war the United States did not want; and the commander, not the policy, was removed. ⚠️ The word the account uses for what Truman settled for - *merely* containing communism - is the vocabulary of a disappointed choice, and it captures exactly what containment meant in practice: accepting an unsatisfactory outcome because the alternative was general war.

### 6. The five results, and which of them matters most

✅ **Fact:** the account lists five results. ✅ For Korea itself it was a disaster: the country was devastated, about four million Korean soldiers and civilians had been killed and five million people were homeless; the division seemed permanent; both states remained intensely suspicious of each other and heavily armed, and there were constant ceasefire violations. ✅ Truman could take some satisfaction from having contained communism and could claim that this success, plus American rearmament, dissuaded world communism from further aggression - though many Republicans felt the USA had lost an opportunity to destroy communism in China, and this feeling contributed towards some of the later excesses of McCarthyism. ✅ The UN had exerted its authority and reversed an act of aggression, but the communist world denounced it as a tool of the capitalists. ✅ The military performance of communist China was impressive: she had prevented the unification of Korea under American influence and was now clearly a world power, and the fact that she was still not allowed a seat in the UN seemed even more unreasonable. ✅ And the conflict brought a new dimension to the Cold War: American relations were now permanently strained with China as well as with Russia, and the familiar pattern of both sides trying to build up alliances appeared in Asia as well as Europe.

✅ **Fact:** the account then gives the content of that fifth result on both sides. ✅ China supported the Indo-Chinese communists in their struggle for independence from France, and at the same time offered friendship and aid to under-developed Third World countries in Asia, Africa and Latin America; peaceful coexistence agreements were signed with India and Burma in 1954. ✅ Meanwhile the Americans tried to encircle China with bases: defensive agreements with Australia and New Zealand in 1951, and SEATO in 1954 - with only three Asian states joining. ✅ Relations between the USA and China were also poor because of Taiwan: the communists still hoped to capture the island and destroy Chiang Kai-shek's Nationalist Party for good, but the Americans were committed to defending Chiang and wanted to keep Taiwan as a military base.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the fifth result is the one that earns marks, because it is the only one that changes the structure of the rivalry rather than its scoreboard. Before Korea, containment was a European policy with a global formula; after Korea it was a global policy with an Asian institutional apparatus, an additional permanent adversary in Beijing, and a competing Chinese offer of friendship and aid to the decolonising world. ⚠️ The 1954 agreements with India and Burma belong to that last strand and are the point at which this lesson hands to Lesson 14: the states declining SEATO in 1954 were, in the same year, being offered a different kind of relationship by a communist power that was not the Soviet Union.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third result also deserves a careful sentence, because it is where the UN boundary sits. The organisation had reversed an aggression and had been denounced by half the world for doing so; both statements are in the account, and both are about the UN's *position in the rivalry* rather than about its institutional performance, which belongs to its own owner.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** Korea proves that containment worked. The aggression was reversed, the line was restored, the communist advance in Asia was halted, and the West paid a price it could afford. The war is a vindication, not a problem.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection states one half of the record accurately and omits the parts that qualify it. ⚠️ Three qualifications are required. ✅ First, the price was not paid mainly by the West: about four million Korean soldiers and civilians were killed and five million were made homeless, in a country divided by outsiders in 1945 along a line most Koreans resented. ⚠️ A verdict on containment that counts only the containing powers' costs is an incomplete verdict. ✅ Second, the intervening decision to cross the parallel produced a Chinese entry that cost the UN forces everything they had gained and permanently strained American relations with China - so the war's most durable consequence was created by an objective that went beyond containment. ✅ Third, the account itself records the contrary American reading: many Republicans felt the USA had lost an opportunity to destroy communism in China, and this contributed to the later excesses of McCarthyism. ⚠️ Korea therefore vindicated containment only in the narrow sense that the line held; in the wider sense it demonstrated the doctrine's costs, its domestic political fragility and its capacity to generate new adversaries.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Explain why war broke out in Korea in June 1950 and why the USA became involved.
> ⚠️ What were the outcomes and the effects of the war in Korea?
> ⚠️ "The Korean War was a civil war that the Cold War took over." Critically examine.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, give the four suggested origins in one compressed sentence each, then the most widely accepted view, then Truman's four reasons sorted by kind - strategic assessment, historical analogy, institutional commitment, domestic politics. ⚠️ For the second, use the five results but give the fifth the analytical weight and show both halves of it, the American alliance-building and the Chinese counter-offer. ⚠️ For the third, the answer is the most widely accepted view itself: local initiative, external approval and materials, no direct involvement by the patrons - which makes the proposition half true and locates the other half precisely.

### Traps

- Writing that Stalin ordered the invasion. ✅ That is one of four suggestions; the most widely accepted view credits Kim Il Sung with pressing the idea and the USSR and China with approving it.
- Treating Korea as purely a civil war. ✅ Approval, war materials and the later Chinese intervention are all recorded.
- Saying the UN authorised the American deployment. ✅ American troops in Japan were ordered to Korea even before the UN had decided what action to take.
- Forgetting the Soviet boycott. ✅ The Council decision was reached in the Russian delegation's absence; they were protesting at the refusal to seat Mao's government and would certainly have vetoed it.
- Describing the war as an American operation with no allies, or as a broadly international one. ✅ The USA and fourteen other countries sent troops - fifteen contributing states in all - and the vast majority of the force was American.
- Missing the change of war aims in October 1950. ✅ The original UN objective had been achieved by the end of September.
- Presenting MacArthur's removal as a personality clash. ✅ He proposed attacking Manchuria with atomic bombs if necessary; Truman judged this would provoke a large-scale war and settled for containment.
- Omitting the human cost. ✅ About four million killed and five million homeless.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Korea under Japanese occupation since 1910; divided at the 38th parallel in August 1945 for the Japanese surrender, not intended by the Americans to be permanent.
2. ✅ All-Korea elections proposed; the Americans expected their zone's two-thirds of the population to outvote the north; no agreement was reached.
3. ✅ August 1948 the Republic of Korea under Syngman Rhee at Seoul; September 1948 the Democratic People's Republic under Kim Il Sung at Pyongyang; both troops withdrawn in 1949.
4. ✅ June 1950: the North invades without warning.
5. ✅ Four suggested origins: Kim's own idea, possibly encouraged by Acheson's omission of Korea; Chinese encouragement; Stalin testing Truman, with Soviet tanks supplied; and the communist claim that the South began it.
6. ✅ The most widely accepted view: Kim pressed the idea; the USSR and China approved and promised materials but did not want direct involvement.
7. ✅ Truman's four reasons: conviction that it was Stalin's doing; the 1930s appeasement analogy; support for the UN where the League had failed; and the domestic pressure of McCarthy's charge.
8. ✅ American troops were ordered from Japan before the UN decided; the Security Council acted in the Soviet delegation's absence, during their boycott over Chinese representation.
9. ✅ Fourteen other countries sent troops alongside the USA - fifteen contributing states in all; the vast majority were American; MacArthur commanded.
10. ✅ September 1950: Inchon, 200 miles behind the lines; Seoul retaken; the south cleared.
11. ✅ October 1950: Truman orders the invasion of the North with UN approval; Zhou Enlai's warning ignored; Pyongyang taken; the Yalu reached.
12. ✅ November 1950: over 300 000 Chinese "volunteers"; by mid-January 1951 the UN is out of the North and Seoul has fallen again.
13. ✅ MacArthur proposes attacking Manchuria, with atomic bombs if necessary; Truman refuses and removes him; June 1951 the frontier is fortified; Panmunjom talks run two years to July 1953, restoring roughly the 38th parallel.
14. ✅ Results: about four million Korean dead and five million homeless; containment claimed as a success and rollback mourned by Republicans, feeding McCarthyism; the UN's authority exerted and denounced as a capitalist tool; China established as a world power still excluded from the UN; and the rivalry given an Asian alliance structure on one side and a Chinese friendship-and-aid offer on the other, with peaceful-coexistence agreements signed with India and Burma in 1954.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Korean War ended on the line it began on. What, then, did it change?

**Model answer:** It changed the geography, the membership and the instruments of the rivalry, none of which appears on a map of the front. ✅ Geographically, it converted containment from a European policy into a global one in practice as well as in the Truman Doctrine's wording: the familiar pattern of both sides building alliances now appeared in Asia as well as Europe, with defensive agreements with Australia and New Zealand in 1951 and SEATO in 1954. ✅ In membership, it added a second permanent adversary: the military performance of communist China was impressive, she had prevented the unification of Korea under American influence, she was now clearly a world power, and American relations were permanently strained with China as well as with Russia. ✅ In instruments, it opened a competition for the decolonising world that had not previously existed, because China simultaneously offered friendship and aid to under-developed countries in Asia, Africa and Latin America and signed peaceful-coexistence agreements with India and Burma in 1954, while the American alliance built for the same region recruited only three Asian members. ⚠️ The unchanged line is therefore the least informative fact about the war: its consequences were structural, and they set the terms for the next twenty years.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that because the frontier was restored, the war was a draw without consequences. A war can be militarily inconclusive and strategically decisive, and Korea is the clearest case in this topic - it is the event that made the Cold War a genuinely global system rather than a European confrontation with a global formula.

---

## Lesson 10 - After Stalin: how far was the thaw real?

Progress: 10/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the two recorded reasons for the thaw, the death of Stalin and the discrediting of McCarthy; the hydrogen-bomb balance of August 1953 as the underlying condition; Khrushchev's February 1956 formulation of peaceful coexistence and what it did and did not concede; the first signs at Panmunjom and in Indo-China; the four concessions of 1955; the Austrian State Treaty as the most important development and its exact terms; and the four recorded limits that made the thaw partial

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - the core account's section "To what extent was there a thaw after 1953?", covering the death of Stalin and the new leaders, the hydrogen-bomb balance, Khrushchev's February 1956 speech with its quoted formulation, the discrediting of McCarthy, the first signs, the 1955 concessions, the Austrian State Treaty and its terms, and the four listed limits including Hungary, the Warsaw Pact, continued nuclear build-up, Berlin and Cuba
🔍 CA search: not run for this lesson - the single topic-level query is recorded in Lesson 1
📰 CA found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a ledger with two columns that both belong to 1953-62

| ✅ Evidence that the thaw was real | ✅ Evidence that it was partial |
|---|---|
| Stalin dies March 1953; new leaders - Malenkov, Bulganin and Khrushchev - want to improve relations with the USA | The Warsaw Pact is signed in 1955, shortly after West Germany enters NATO |
| By August 1953 the Russians as well as the Americans have a hydrogen bomb: the two sides are so finely balanced that tensions have to be relaxed if nuclear war is to be avoided | The USSR crushes the Hungarian rising in Budapest in 1956 with tanks |
| Khrushchev, February 1956: "there are only two ways - either peaceful coexistence or the most destructive war in history. There is no third way" | The Russians continue to build up their nuclear armaments |
| McCarthy discredited in 1954; the Senate condemns him; Eisenhower announces that the American people want to be friendly with the Soviet people | Berlin causes more tension - 1958, 1960, 1961 |
| Peace at Panmunjom ends the Korean War in July 1953; the war in Indo-China ends the following year | The most provocative action of all: Soviet missiles installed in Cuba, less than a hundred miles from the American coast, in 1962 |
| 1955 concessions: military bases in Finland given up; the veto lifted on sixteen new UN member states; the quarrel with Yugoslavia healed by Khrushchev's visit to Tito; the Cominform abandoned | Khrushchev's policy is "a curious mixture" that western leaders often found difficult to understand; sometimes he seemed to be seeing how far he could push the Americans before they stood up to him |
| **The Austrian State Treaty, May 1955 - the most important development in the thaw** | — |

```text
   THE QUESTION THE LEDGER FORCES
   Both columns are sourced. So the exam answer is never "yes" or "no";
   it is: WHAT KIND of relaxation was available, and WHERE did it stop?

   THE ANSWER THE EVIDENCE SUPPORTS
   Relaxation was available wherever an issue could be SETTLED AND
   CLOSED without altering the distribution of control
      -> Austria: occupation ended, neutrality accepted, treaty signed
      -> Korea and Indo-China: fighting stopped, lines fixed
      -> Finland bases, UN admissions, Yugoslavia, Cominform: conceded
   Relaxation was NOT available wherever an issue was about WHO CONTROLS
   WHAT
      -> Hungary: control contested -> tanks
      -> Berlin and Germany: control contested -> three crises
      -> Cuba: control of a new client contested -> the worst crisis
```

*The code block is the lesson's argument in five lines. The thaw was not shallow; it was bounded, and the boundary is drawn in exactly the same place in every case.*

### 1. The two reasons, and the condition underneath them

✅ **Fact:** the account opens the section by conceding the point and qualifying it in one sentence: there is no doubt that in some ways East-West relations did begin to improve during 1953, though there were still areas of disagreement and the thaw was not a consistent development.

✅ **Fact:** the death of Stalin was probably the starting point of the thaw, because it brought to the forefront new Russian leaders - Malenkov, Bulganin and Khrushchev - who wanted to improve relations with the USA.

✅ **Fact:** their reasons were possibly connected with the fact that by August 1953 the Russians as well as the Americans had developed a hydrogen bomb: the two sides were now so finely balanced that international tensions had to be relaxed if nuclear war was to be avoided.

✅ **Fact:** the second reason concerns the United States. Anti-communist feelings in the USA, which had been stirred up by Senator Joseph McCarthy, began to moderate when McCarthy was discredited in 1954. ✅ It had gradually become clear that McCarthy himself was something of a fanatic, and when he began to accuse leading generals of having communist sympathies, he had gone too far. ✅ The Senate condemned him by a large majority and he foolishly attacked the new Republican President Eisenhower for supporting the Senate. ✅ Soon afterwards Eisenhower announced that the American people wanted to be friendly with the Soviet people.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the structure of this explanation is worth noticing because it is unusual and examinable. The thaw is explained by *one change of leadership on each side of the domestic political spectrum* - a new Soviet leadership that wanted better relations, and the removal of the American politician whose campaign had made better relations politically impossible. ⚠️ Underneath both sits a structural condition, the hydrogen-bomb balance of August 1953, which neither leadership created. ⚠️ A strong answer states the structural condition first and the two political changes as what allowed the condition to be acted on, because that ordering explains why the thaw came in 1953-54 rather than earlier, when the condition did not yet exist, or later, when it still did.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and the McCarthy material carries a second point that belongs in Lesson 21. The Cold War had a domestic front inside the United States - an accusation that the State Department was infested with communists working in effect for the USSR, which Lesson 9 showed influencing a presidential decision on Korea - and the account records the mechanism by which it ended: a Senate condemnation by a large majority after the accusations reached the generals. ⚠️ That is evidence about the internal political costs of the rivalry, and about the institutional limits that eventually contained them.

### 2. Peaceful coexistence: the formulation and its two halves

✅ **Fact:** Nikita Khrushchev explained the new policy in a famous speech in February 1956 in which he criticised Stalin and said that peaceful coexistence with the West was not only possible but essential: "there are only two ways - either peaceful coexistence or the most destructive war in history. There is no third way."

✅ **Fact:** the account immediately supplies the qualification, and it is the more important half. This did not mean that Khrushchev had given up the idea of a communist-dominated world; this would still come, but it would be achieved when the western powers recognised the superiority of the Soviet economic system, not when they were defeated in war. ✅ In the same way, he hoped to win neutral states over to communism by lavish economic aid.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** peaceful coexistence is therefore a change of *instrument*, not of *objective* - and stating it that way is worth a mark or two in any stem on the thaw. The goal remained a communist-dominated world; the route shifted from military confrontation to economic demonstration and aid competition. ⚠️ That has three consequences a good answer draws. ⚠️ First, it explains why the thaw was compatible with an intensified arms build-up: parity was the precondition of coexistence, not its casualty. ⚠️ Second, it explains why the competition for the decolonising world intensified in exactly this period, because "lavish economic aid" to neutral states is the operational content of the new doctrine - the link to Lesson 14. ⚠️ Third, it explains why Western leaders found Khrushchev's policy, in the account's phrase, a curious mixture difficult to understand: conciliation and pressure were not alternating moods but the two halves of a single strategy.

### 3. The first signs, and the four concessions of 1955

✅ **Fact:** the first signs were the signing of the peace agreement at Panmunjom, which ended the Korean War in July 1953, and the end of the war in Indo-China the following year.

✅ **Fact:** the Russians made important concessions in 1955. ✅ They agreed to give up their military bases in Finland. ✅ They lifted their veto on the admission of sixteen new member states to the UN. ✅ The quarrel with Yugoslavia was healed when Khrushchev paid a visit to Tito. ✅ And the Cominform was abandoned, suggesting more freedom for the satellite states.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the four concessions are not of equal weight and an answer that ranks them shows judgement. Giving up the Finnish bases is a territorial withdrawal; lifting the UN veto is a procedural concession with a large institutional effect; the visit to Tito is a reversal of the 1948 expulsion and an admission that the Cominform's demand for Russian-style communism had failed in its first test; and abandoning the Cominform itself is the formal retirement of that demand. ⚠️ The third and fourth therefore belong together and they belong in Lesson 13, because the Soviet leadership was in 1955 conceding the principle whose enforcement against China would destroy that relationship from 1956. ⚠️ The chronological irony is exact and worth one sentence in a strong answer.

### 4. The Austrian State Treaty: the thaw's one completed settlement

✅ **Fact:** the account calls this the most important development in the thaw. ✅ At the end of the war in 1945 Austria was divided into four zones of occupation, with the capital, Vienna, in the Russian zone. ✅ Unlike Germany, she was allowed her own government because she was viewed not as a defeated enemy but as a state liberated from the Nazis. ✅ The Austrian government had only limited powers, and the problem was similar to the one in Germany: whereas the three western occupying powers organised the recovery of their zones, the Russians insisted on squeezing reparations, mainly in the form of food supplies, from theirs.

✅ **Fact:** no permanent settlement seemed likely, but early in 1955 the Russians were persuaded, mainly by the Austrian government, to be more co-operative; they were also afraid of a merger between West Germany and western Austria.

✅ **Fact:** as a result of the agreement, all occupying troops were withdrawn and Austria became independent, with her 1937 frontiers. ✅ She was not to unite with Germany, her armed forces were strictly limited, and she was to remain neutral in any dispute between East and West, which meant that she could not join either NATO or the European Economic Community. ✅ One point the Austrians were unhappy about was the loss of the German-speaking area of the South Tyrol, which Italy was allowed to keep.

⚠️ **Signature detail, supplied because it is routinely misattributed.** The treaty was signed at Vienna on **15 May 1955**, and the Soviet signature was that of the Foreign Minister, **Vyacheslav Molotov**, not of Khrushchev. ⚠️ Khrushchev's role in the settlement was as the Soviet leader whose policy permitted it, which is the sense in which this session attributes it to him; the act of signature was Molotov's, and an answer should say that the Soviet government signed rather than naming the wrong individual.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Austria is the control case for Germany, and setting the two side by side is the strongest analytical move available in this lesson. ⚠️ The starting conditions were close to identical: four zones, a capital inside the Russian zone, Western reconstruction against Soviet extraction. ⚠️ Three things differed, and each explains part of the divergent outcome. Austria had her own government throughout, because she was treated as liberated rather than defeated, so there was a native authority capable of negotiating - and the account records that it was mainly the Austrian government that persuaded the Russians. ⚠️ Austria was strategically marginal in a way Germany was not: an Austria with strictly limited forces and treaty neutrality removed a Soviet worry without creating a Western asset, whereas no comparable formula existed for Germany's industrial and demographic weight. ⚠️ And the Soviet fear the account names - a merger between West Germany and western Austria - gave Moscow a positive reason to settle. ⚠️ The general finding is the one in the visual: settlement was available where an issue could be closed without shifting the balance of control, and Austrian neutrality was precisely such a closure.

### 5. The four limits, stated exactly

✅ **Fact:** the account records that Khrushchev's policy was a curious mixture which western leaders often found difficult to understand. ✅ While making the conciliatory moves described above, he was quick to respond to anything which seemed to be a threat to the East, and he had no intention of relaxing Russia's grip on the satellite states. ✅ The Hungarians discovered this to their cost in 1956, when a rising in Budapest against the communist government was ruthlessly crushed by Russian tanks. ✅ Sometimes he seemed to be prepared to see how far he could push the Americans before they stood up to him.

✅ **Fact:** four specific limits are listed. ✅ The Warsaw Pact of 1955 was signed between Russia and her satellite states shortly after West Germany was admitted to NATO; the Pact was a mutual defence agreement which the West took as a gesture against West Germany's membership. ✅ The Russians continued to build up their nuclear armaments. ✅ The situation in Berlin caused more tension. ✅ And the most provocative action of all was when Khrushchev installed Soviet missiles in Cuba, less than a hundred miles from the American coast, in 1962.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the four limits have a common structure and saying so is the payoff of the lesson. Each concerns control rather than settlement: control of the satellites (Hungary and, defensively, the Warsaw Pact), control of the strategic balance (the continued build-up), control of Berlin, and control over a new client in the western hemisphere. ⚠️ In every case where the issue was who controls what, the thaw stopped; in every case where the issue could be settled and closed, it proceeded. ⚠️ The answer to "how far was the thaw real?" is therefore not a percentage but a boundary, and the boundary is drawn in the same place four times.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the thaw was propaganda. A leadership that crushed Budapest with tanks in the same period as it spoke of peaceful coexistence was not relaxing anything; it was changing its language while keeping its practice, and the West was right to treat the concessions as tactical.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is correct about Hungary and wrong as a general account, and the evidence that refutes it is the Austrian treaty. ⚠️ Propaganda does not require withdrawing your troops from a country, accepting its independence within its 1937 frontiers, and giving up the reparations you had been extracting from your zone - all of which were done, irreversibly, in May 1955. ⚠️ Nor does it require lifting a veto that had blocked sixteen states from the United Nations, an institutional change that could not be undone. ⚠️ The correct reading is the one the account's own structure suggests: the thaw was genuine within a defined limit and absolutely rigid at that limit, and both halves of that sentence describe the same policy. ⚠️ The Hungarian rising and the Austrian treaty are eighteen months apart and are not inconsistent, because Austria was outside the Soviet security perimeter once neutrality was accepted, and Hungary was inside it.

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ How accurate is it to talk about a "thaw" in the Cold War in the years after 1953?
> ⚠️ Examine the meaning and limits of "peaceful coexistence".
> ⚠️ Why was the Austrian question settled in 1955 when the German question was not?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, the two-column ledger is the plan; the mark comes from the boundary argument in section 5, not from the length of either column. ⚠️ For the second, lead with the quoted formulation and then immediately with the qualification - a communist-dominated world still to come, through recognition of Soviet economic superiority and lavish aid to neutral states - because the qualification is what most scripts omit. ⚠️ For the third, use the three differences in section 4 and generalise them into the closure principle.

### Traps

- Treating the thaw as continuous improvement. ✅ The account says it was not a consistent development.
- Presenting peaceful coexistence as an abandonment of the communist objective. ✅ It was a change of route: recognition of Soviet economic superiority rather than defeat in war.
- Omitting McCarthy. ✅ His discrediting in 1954, the Senate's condemnation and Eisenhower's statement are the American half of the explanation.
- Forgetting the hydrogen-bomb balance of August 1953. ✅ It is the structural condition under both political changes.
- Listing the 1955 concessions without ranking them. ⚠️ The Tito visit and the abandonment of the Cominform are the two with bloc-wide implications.
- Describing Austria as neutral by choice alone. ✅ Neutrality was a treaty obligation that also barred her from NATO and from the European Economic Community.
- Naming Khrushchev as the signatory of the Austrian State Treaty. ✅ It was signed at Vienna on 15 May 1955, and Molotov signed for the Soviet Union.
- Writing that Hungary proves the thaw was fake. ⚠️ Austria in May 1955 was irreversible; the correct finding is a bounded thaw with a rigid limit.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The account's own framing: relations did begin to improve during 1953, but disagreements remained and the thaw was not a consistent development.
2. ✅ Stalin's death in March 1953 brought forward Malenkov, Bulganin and Khrushchev, who wanted better relations with the USA.
3. ✅ By August 1953 both sides had a hydrogen bomb; the balance was so fine that tensions had to be relaxed if nuclear war was to be avoided.
4. ✅ McCarthy discredited in 1954 after accusing leading generals; condemned by the Senate by a large majority; Eisenhower then spoke of friendship with the Soviet people.
5. ✅ Khrushchev, February 1956: only two ways, peaceful coexistence or the most destructive war in history; there is no third way.
6. ✅ The qualification: a communist-dominated world would still come, through Western recognition of Soviet economic superiority, and neutral states were to be won by lavish economic aid.
7. ✅ First signs: Panmunjom in July 1953; the end of the war in Indo-China the following year.
8. ✅ 1955 concessions: Finnish bases given up; the veto lifted on sixteen new UN members; the Yugoslav quarrel healed by a visit to Tito; the Cominform abandoned.
9. ✅ Austria: four zones, Vienna in the Russian zone, her own government because she was viewed as liberated rather than defeated; Western reconstruction against Soviet reparations in food.
10. ✅ Persuaded mainly by the Austrian government, and fearing a West German-Austrian merger, the Russians settled in May 1955.
11. ✅ Terms: all occupying troops withdrawn; independence within the 1937 frontiers; no union with Germany; strictly limited armed forces; neutrality, and therefore no NATO and no European Economic Community; the South Tyrol left with Italy. ✅ Signed at Vienna on 15 May 1955, with Molotov signing for the Soviet Union.
12. ✅ The four limits: the Warsaw Pact of 1955; Hungary crushed by tanks in 1956; the continued nuclear build-up; Berlin; and, most provocative of all, the missiles in Cuba in 1962.
13. ⚠️ The boundary principle: relaxation where an issue could be settled and closed; rigidity where the issue was who controls what.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Austrian State Treaty was signed in May 1955 and Russian tanks crushed the rising in Budapest in 1956. Show why these are not inconsistent.

**Model answer:** Because they fall on opposite sides of a single boundary, and the boundary is control rather than ideology. ✅ The Austrian settlement removed a Soviet liability without creating a Western asset: all occupying troops were withdrawn, Austria became independent within her 1937 frontiers, she was barred from uniting with Germany, her armed forces were strictly limited, and she was required to remain neutral, which meant she could join neither NATO nor the European Economic Community - and the Russians were additionally afraid of a merger between West Germany and western Austria, so settlement served their security rather than costing it. ✅ Hungary was inside the perimeter that settlement was designed to protect, and the account is explicit that Khrushchev had no intention of relaxing Russia's grip on the satellite states, with the rising in Budapest ruthlessly crushed by Russian tanks. ⚠️ The same policy produces both outcomes: an issue that can be closed on terms that do not shift the distribution of control is available for settlement, and an issue that *is* the distribution of control is not. ⚠️ That is also why the same leader could pursue peaceful coexistence and install missiles in Cuba, since the second concerned control over a new client rather than the closure of an old dispute.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the thaw as sincerity and the repression as hypocrisy. Both were instruments of one strategy whose objective, on the account's own evidence, had not changed - a communist-dominated world reached through demonstrated economic superiority and aid rather than through war.

---

## Lesson 11 - 1956: Suez and Hungary, two crises in one autumn

Progress: 11/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Nasser's provocations and the Czechoslovak arms deal that made Egypt part of the Cold War; the withdrawal of the Aswan offer and the nationalisation of the canal; Eden's reading and the secret plan with France and Israel; the course of the war and the joint American-Soviet demand for a ceasefire; the six recorded outcomes; the Hungarian rising crushed in the same weeks; the contrast the two crises draw; and what 1956 established about the limits of both blocs

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - the core account's section on the Suez War of 1956, covering the competing attributions of blame, Nasser's actions, the Czechoslovak arms deal, the cancellation of the Aswan grant, the nationalisation of the canal, Eden's position with the recorded contemporary remarks, the secret Anglo-French-Israeli plan, the course of the war, the joint American and Soviet demand at the UN, and the outcome; together with the account's separate comparison of the Suez and Hungarian crises in its United Nations chapter

### Visual: one autumn, two crises, one lesson drawn twice

   | the invasions 64 to 5 and calls|  | same resolution and set up a   |
   | for withdrawal                 |  | committee; the Russians refused|
   |                                |  | to co-operate and no progress  |

   +--------------------------------+  +--------------------------------+
