```text
   | both demanded an immediate     |  | is ruthlessly crushed by       |
   | ceasefire and prepared to send |  | Russian tanks                  |
   | a UN force                     |  |                                |
   +--------------------------------+  +--------------------------------+
   | Outcome: Britain, France and   |  | Outcome: Soviet control        |
   | Israel withdraw; a UN force of |  | restored; the West protests    |
   | 5000 from ten countries polices|  | and does not intervene         |
   | the frontier                   |  |                                |
   +--------------------------------+  +--------------------------------+
                    |                                |
                    v                                v
   THE CONTRAST THE ACCOUNT ITSELF DRAWS
   "Britain and France were willing to bow to international pressure;
    the Russians simply ignored the UN, and nothing could be done."
                                |
                    THE COLD WAR FINDING
   Each bloc's leader could discipline its own allies; neither could
   discipline the other. Suez shows the USA restraining its allies;
   Hungary shows the USSR coercing its satellite; and in neither case
   did the other superpower intervene militarily.
```

*The two panels are deliberately parallel because the events were simultaneous and the comparison is the analysis. Read the bottom two boxes and the year's meaning appears: 1956 established the boundaries of both spheres, in one case by restraint and in the other by force.*

### 1. Why Egypt entered the Cold War at all

✅ **Fact:** the account records that blame for the Suez War can be assigned differently depending on the point of view. ✅ The Arabs blamed the Israelis, who actually began hostilities by invading Egypt. ✅ The communist bloc and many Arab states blamed Britain and France, accusing them of imperialist tactics - trying to keep control in the Middle East against the wishes of the Arab nations - and accused the Americans of encouraging Britain to attack. ✅ The British, French and Israelis blamed Colonel Nasser of Egypt for being anti-Western; ✅ however, even the Americans thought that Britain and France had overreacted by using force, and most British historians agree.

✅ **Fact:** Nasser, the new ruler of Egypt, was aggressively in favour of Arab unity and independence, including the liberation of Palestine from the Jews, and almost everything he did irritated the British, Americans or French. ✅ He organised guerrilla fighters known as fedayeen to carry out sabotage and murder inside Israel, and Egyptian ships blockaded the Gulf of Aqaba leading to the port of Eilat, which the Israelis had taken from Egypt in 1949. ✅ In 1936 Britain had signed an agreement with Egypt allowing British troops to remain at Suez; the treaty was due to expire in 1956 and Britain wanted it renewed, but Nasser refused and insisted that all British troops should withdraw immediately the treaty ended. ✅ He sent help to the Algerian Arabs in their struggle against France, prodded the other Arab states into opposing the British-sponsored Baghdad Pact, and forced King Hussein of Jordan to dismiss his British army chief of staff.

✅ **Fact:** and then the decisive act: he signed an arms deal with Czechoslovakia in September 1955 for Russian fighters, bombers and tanks, and Russian military experts went to train the Egyptian army.

✅ **Fact:** the account states the American reaction and its logic without euphemism. The Americans were outraged, since it meant that the West no longer controlled arms supplies to Egypt. ✅ Egypt now became part of the Cold War: any country which was not part of the Western alliance and which bought arms from Eastern Europe was, in American eyes, just as bad as a communist country. ✅ It was seen as a sinister plot by the Russians to move into the Middle East.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that paragraph is the single most important passage in this session for the proxy discipline, and it should be read slowly. The causal sequence runs *from Cairo outward*: an Egyptian government pursuing Egyptian objectives - the end of a British base, support for Algerian independence, opposition to a Western-sponsored pact, pressure on Israel - purchased arms from the only supplier willing to sell without political conditions. ⚠️ Only at that point did Washington reclassify Egypt as a Cold War problem, and the criterion the account records for the reclassification is explicit and extraordinarily broad: not alliance with the USSR, not communism at home, but the purchase of arms from eastern Europe while outside the Western alliance. ⚠️ That criterion converts non-alignment itself into a suspect status, and it is the mechanism by which the Cold War reached states that were trying to stay out of it. ⚠️ Any answer on the decolonised world should use this sentence, because it shows the process happening rather than asserting it.

### 2. From the cancelled dam to the nationalised canal

✅ **Fact:** the Americans therefore cancelled a promised grant of $46 million towards the building of a dam at Aswan in July 1956; their intention was to force Nasser to abandon his new links with the communists.

✅ **Fact:** crisis point was reached when Nasser immediately retaliated by nationalising the Suez Canal, intending to use the income from it to finance the dam. ✅ Shareholders in the canal, the majority of whom were British and French, were promised compensation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two acts are causally linked and the link is usually lost in scripts that treat nationalisation as a free-standing act of defiance. Aid was withdrawn to punish an arms purchase; nationalisation was the announced method of replacing the withdrawn finance. ⚠️ Economic coercion intended to reverse a foreign-policy choice produced instead a larger and less reversible foreign-policy act - a pattern this session meets again in Cuba, where American economic pressure preceded Castro's turn to Moscow. ⚠️ The general finding is worth stating once and using twice: sanctions aimed at a weak state's alignment tend to force the alignment they are designed to prevent, because they remove the alternative that the state was relying on to stay uncommitted.

### 3. Eden's reading, the contemporary language, and the plan

✅ **Fact:** Anthony Eden, the British Conservative prime minister, took the lead at this point. ✅ He believed that Nasser was on the way to forming a united Arabia under Egyptian control and communist influence, which could cut off Europe's oil supplies at will. ✅ He viewed Nasser as another Hitler or Mussolini, and, according to the historian Hugh Thomas, "saw Egypt through a forest of Flanders poppies and gleaming jackboots".

✅ **Fact:** he was not alone. ✅ Churchill remarked: "We can't have this malicious swine sitting across our communications", and the new Labour leader, Hugh Gaitskell, agreed that Nasser must not be appeased in the way that Hitler and Mussolini had been appeased in the 1930s. ✅ The account records what this consensus ignored: everybody in Britain ignored the fact that Nasser had offered compensation to the shareholders and had promised that the ships of all nations, except Israel, would be able to use the canal.

✅ **Fact:** secret talks took place between the British, French and Israelis and a plan was hatched: Israel would invade Egypt across the Sinai peninsula, whereupon British and French troops would occupy the canal zone on the pretext that they were protecting it from damage in the fighting. ✅ Anglo-French control of the canal would be restored, and the defeat, it was hoped, would topple Nasser from power.

✅ **Fact:** the account adds a qualification from recent research that should not be dropped. The war could easily have been avoided, and Eden was more in favour of getting rid of Nasser by peaceful means; ✅ there was in fact a secret Anglo-American plan, Omega, to overthrow Nasser using political and economic pressures. ✅ In mid-October 1956 Eden was still willing to continue talks with Egypt; he had called off the military operation and there seemed a good chance of a compromise over control of the canal. ✅ However, Eden was under pressure from several directions to use force: MI6, the British Intelligence Service, and some members of the British government, including Harold Macmillan as chancellor of the exchequer, urged military action, and Macmillan assured Eden that the USA would not oppose a British use of force. ✅ In the end it was probably pressure from the French government which caused Eden to opt for a joint military operation with France and Israel.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three things in that paragraph repay a sentence each in a strong answer. ⚠️ First, the 1930s analogy is being used as an argument for action, exactly as it was in Washington over Korea in 1950 - which shows that the interwar experience functioned as a shared decision-rule across the Western alliance and was applied to a non-communist Egyptian nationalist as readily as to a communist invasion. ⚠️ Second, the existence of Omega establishes that the *objective* - removing Nasser - was common ground between London and Washington; the disagreement was about instruments, and it is the choice of instrument rather than the aim that produced the rupture. ⚠️ Third, Macmillan's assurance that the USA would not oppose force is the decisive misjudgement of the episode, and it is a misjudgement about the alliance rather than about Egypt.

### 4. The war, and the intervention that ended it

✅ **Fact:** the war began with the planned Israeli invasion of Egypt on 29 October. ✅ This was a brilliant success, and within a week the Israelis had captured the entire Sinai peninsula. ✅ Meanwhile the British and French bombed Egyptian airfields and landed troops at Port Said at the northern end of the canal.

✅ **Fact:** the attacks caused an outcry from the rest of the world, and the Americans, who were afraid of upsetting all the Arabs and forcing them into closer ties with the USSR, refused to support Britain, although they had earlier hinted that support would be forthcoming. ✅ At the United Nations, Americans and Russians for once agreed: they demanded an immediate ceasefire and prepared to send a UN force. ✅ With the pressure of world opinion against them, Britain, France and Israel agreed to withdraw, while UN troops moved in to police the frontier between Egypt and Israel.

✅ **Fact:** the account's separate treatment of the UN's role adds the procedural detail. A Security Council resolution condemning force was vetoed by Britain and France, whereupon the General Assembly, by a majority of 64 votes to 5, condemned the invasions and called for a withdrawal of troops; in view of the weight of opinion against them the aggressors agreed to withdraw, provided the UN ensured a reasonable settlement over the canal and kept the Arabs and Israelis from slaughtering each other; a UN force of 5000, made up of troops from ten different countries, moved in. ✅ American and Russian pressure was also important in bringing about the ceasefire.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the phrase "for once agreed" is the Cold War content of Suez and should be the centre of any answer on the crisis's place in this topic. ⚠️ The two superpowers had a shared interest in the outcome for opposite reasons: Washington needed to avoid driving the Arab states towards Moscow, and Moscow wished to appear as the champion of an Arab state against European imperialism, at a moment when its own tanks were in Budapest. ⚠️ Convergent interests, not cooperation, produced a joint demand - and that distinction is worth making, because "the superpowers cooperated at Suez" overstates what happened, while "the superpowers converged at Suez, each for its own reasons" is both accurate and more interesting.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second Cold War content is the reversal of the alliance's internal hierarchy. Britain and France acted against explicit American preference and were compelled to stop by the combination of American financial and diplomatic pressure and near-universal condemnation. ⚠️ That establishes, in public and in one week, that the Western alliance's members retained the formal capacity to act independently and had lost the practical capacity to do so against the leading member's opposition - a point that connects directly to the alliance asymmetry of Lesson 7.

### 5. The outcomes, and the two that belong to this topic

✅ **Fact:** the account calls the result a complete humiliation for Britain and France, who achieved none of their aims, and a triumph for President Nasser.

✅ **Fact:** the war failed to overthrow Nasser, and his prestige as the leader of Arab nationalism against interfering Europeans was greatly increased; for the ordinary Arab people, he was a hero. ✅ The Egyptians blocked the canal, the Arabs reduced oil supplies to western Europe, where petrol rationing was introduced for a time, and Russian aid replaced that from the USA. ✅ The British action soon lost them an ally in Iraq, where premier Nuri-es-Said came under increasing attack from other Arabs for his pro-British attitude and was murdered in 1958. ✅ Britain was now weak and unable to follow a foreign policy independently of the USA. ✅ The Algerians were encouraged in their struggle for independence from France, which they achieved in 1962.

✅ **Fact:** the war was not without success for Israel: although she had been compelled to hand back all territory captured from Egypt, she had inflicted heavy losses on the Egyptians in men and equipment, which would take years to make good; for the time being the fedayeen raids ceased and Israel had a breathing space in which to consolidate. ✅ Following Britain's humiliation, the Israelis now looked towards the USA as their chief supporter.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two of these outcomes are Cold War outcomes and the rest belong to the Middle East and decolonisation owners. ⚠️ The first is that Russian aid replaced that from the USA - the precise outcome American policy had been designed to prevent, achieved in four months by the sequence of arms deal, cancelled grant, nationalisation and war. ⚠️ The second is the Israeli reorientation towards Washington, which is the origin of the alignment that structures the region for the rest of the period and is developed in Lesson 16. ⚠️ Everything else - Nasser's prestige, the canal, Iraqi politics, Algeria - is owned elsewhere and should be used in one clause each, as evidence that the crisis had autonomous regional causes and effects.

### 6. Hungary, and the comparison

✅ **Fact:** in 1956 a rising in Budapest against the communist government was ruthlessly crushed by Russian tanks, and the account presents it as the clearest demonstration that Khrushchev had no intention of relaxing Russia's grip on the satellite states.

✅ **Fact:** the account's United Nations chapter records the institutional sequence and the comparison. The rising took place at the same time as the Suez Crisis and showed the UN at its most ineffective: when the Hungarians tried to exert their independence from Russian control, Soviet troops entered Hungary to crush the revolt; the Hungarian government appealed to the UN, but the Russians vetoed a Security Council resolution calling for a withdrawal of their forces; the General Assembly passed the same resolution and set up a committee to investigate the problem, but the Russians refused to co-operate with the committee and no progress could be made. ✅ The contrast with Suez was striking: there, Britain and France were willing to bow to international pressure; the Russians simply ignored the UN, and nothing could be done.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the contrast the account draws is about the UN, and its owner is the United Nations file. The Cold War inference that belongs here is different and more general, and it has three parts. ⚠️ First, both superpowers enforced discipline inside their own spheres in the same fortnight, one through financial and diplomatic pressure on its allies and the other through armour against a satellite's population. ⚠️ Second, neither intervened in the other's sphere: there was no Soviet military response to the attack on Egypt and no Western military response in Hungary, exactly as the owner's evidence bank records - the West would not contest the Soviet sphere militarily. ⚠️ Third, the *difference in method* is itself the evidence of the difference between the two alliance systems, and it is the strongest single illustration of the asymmetries set out in Lesson 7.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there is also a harder point that a strong answer can make in one sentence. The simultaneity was not coincidental in its effects even if it was in its timing: each crisis reduced the other's diplomatic cost. Moscow's ability to denounce Anglo-French imperialism at the United Nations was worth more in the week its tanks were in Budapest, and Western condemnation of the Soviet action carried less weight in the week British and French troops were at Port Said. ⚠️ State it as an effect rather than as a plan, because the sources record no coordination and none should be implied.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** Suez is not a Cold War episode at all. It was a colonial war fought by two European empires over a canal, ended by American financial pressure. Filing it under the Cold War imports a framework the events do not need.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is right that the *causes* were not Cold War causes, and this session says so: Egyptian nationalism, an expiring base agreement, the Algerian war, Arab politics and a canal. ⚠️ But three sourced elements make the episode part of this topic without reducing it to one. ✅ First, the trigger for the American cancellation of the Aswan grant was an arms deal with Czechoslovakia, and the account records the criterion explicitly - a country outside the Western alliance buying arms from eastern Europe was, in American eyes, just as bad as a communist country. ⚠️ That is a Cold War criterion applied to a non-Cold War act, and it converted the dispute. ✅ Second, the resolution was produced by the two superpowers demanding a ceasefire together, which is an event in the history of the rivalry and not of the canal. ✅ Third, the outcome was that Russian aid replaced American aid in Egypt, which realigned the region for a generation. ⚠️ The correct formulation is the one the owner supplies for the whole Middle Eastern sequence: the Cold War internationalised a conflict it did not create.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the causes and consequences of the Suez crisis of 1956.
> ⚠️ "The events of 1956 defined the limits of both blocs." Discuss.
> ⚠️ How far was the Suez crisis a Cold War episode?

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, keep the causes local and the consequences split between local and Cold War: the arms deal, the cancelled grant and the nationalisation are the chain, and Russian aid replacing American aid is the Cold War consequence. ⚠️ For the second, the two-panel visual is the plan, and the mark comes from the three-part inference in section 6 - simultaneous internal discipline, mutual non-intervention, and different methods revealing different alliances. ⚠️ For the third, concede the local causes first; a script that argues Suez was "really" about the Cold War will be marked down, and one that argues it had nothing to do with it cannot explain why Washington cancelled the dam.

### Traps

- Writing that Nasser nationalised the canal without compensation. ✅ Shareholders were promised compensation, and he promised access to the ships of all nations except Israel.
- Making the Israeli invasion a surprise to Britain and France. ✅ It was the first step of a plan agreed in secret talks between the three.
- Saying the Americans were neutral. ✅ They refused support although they had earlier hinted it would be forthcoming, and they were afraid of pushing the Arab states towards the USSR.
- Describing the joint superpower demand as cooperation. ⚠️ The account says they "for once agreed"; the interests were convergent, not coordinated.
- Forgetting Omega. ✅ A secret Anglo-American plan to remove Nasser by political and economic pressure already existed; the dispute was about method.
- Using Hungary as evidence about the UN in a Cold War answer. ⚠️ The UN comparison belongs to its own owner; what belongs here is the mutual non-intervention finding.
- Treating Suez as the end of British power in one stroke. ✅ The recorded finding is narrower: Britain was now weak and unable to follow a foreign policy independently of the USA.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Blame at Suez is attributed differently by different parties; even the Americans thought Britain and France overreacted, and most British historians agree.
2. ✅ Nasser's irritants: the fedayeen, the Aqaba blockade, refusal to renew the 1936 base agreement due to expire in 1956, help to the Algerians, opposition to the Baghdad Pact, and pressure on King Hussein.
3. ✅ September 1955: the Czechoslovak arms deal for Russian fighters, bombers and tanks, with Russian experts training the Egyptian army.
4. ✅ The American criterion: a country outside the Western alliance buying arms from eastern Europe was, in American eyes, just as bad as a communist country.
5. ✅ July 1956: the promised $46 million grant for the Aswan dam is cancelled to force Nasser away from the communists.
6. ✅ Nasser nationalises the canal to finance the dam, promising compensation to shareholders.
7. ✅ Eden's reading: a united Arabia under Egyptian control and communist influence able to cut Europe's oil; Nasser as another Hitler or Mussolini; Hugh Thomas's Flanders-poppies phrase; Churchill's and Gaitskell's remarks.
8. ✅ The secret plan: Israel invades across Sinai; Britain and France occupy the canal zone on the pretext of protecting it; Nasser to fall.
9. ✅ Recent research: Eden preferred peaceful removal; the Anglo-American Omega plan existed; he was still willing to talk in mid-October and had called off the operation; MI6 and Macmillan urged force, and Macmillan wrongly assured him of American acquiescence; French pressure was probably decisive.
10. ✅ 29 October: the Israeli invasion takes Sinai in a week; Britain and France bomb airfields and land at Port Said.
11. ✅ Americans and Russians "for once agreed" and demanded an immediate ceasefire; the Council resolution was vetoed by Britain and France; the Assembly condemned the invasions 64 to 5; a UN force of 5000 from ten countries moved in.
12. ✅ Outcomes: humiliation for Britain and France, triumph for Nasser; the canal blocked and oil supplies reduced with petrol rationing in western Europe; Russian aid replacing American aid; Nuri-es-Said murdered in 1958; Britain unable to act independently of the USA; the Algerians encouraged; Israel gaining a breathing space and turning to the USA.
13. ✅ Hungary, simultaneously: Soviet troops crushed the revolt; the Russians vetoed the Council resolution and refused to co-operate with the Assembly's committee; Britain and France bowed to international pressure while the Russians simply ignored the UN.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how an Egyptian arms purchase in September 1955 led to a war in October 1956, and identify the point at which the Cold War entered the sequence.

**Model answer:** The Cold War entered at the reclassification, not at the beginning. ✅ Nasser's objectives were Egyptian and regional - the end of the British base at Suez when the 1936 agreement expired in 1956, help to the Algerians, opposition to the Baghdad Pact, pressure on Israel - and he signed an arms deal with Czechoslovakia in September 1955 for Russian fighters, bombers and tanks, with Russian experts training the army. ✅ At that point the Americans were outraged because the West no longer controlled arms supplies to Egypt, and the account records the criterion that converted the dispute: any country which was not part of the Western alliance and which bought arms from eastern Europe was, in American eyes, just as bad as a communist country. ✅ The consequence was the cancellation in July 1956 of the promised $46 million towards the Aswan dam, intended to force Nasser to abandon his new links with the communists; ✅ he retaliated by nationalising the canal to finance the dam, promising compensation to shareholders; ✅ and Eden, under pressure from MI6, from Macmillan and finally from the French government, opted for the secret plan with France and Israel. ⚠️ Each step is a response to the previous one, and the step that made the quarrel a Cold War quarrel was an American classification rule rather than any Soviet action in Egypt.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Nasser as a Soviet client. He bought weapons from a communist state while remaining outside both alliances, and it was the purchase rather than any alignment that triggered the American response - which is precisely why the episode is evidence that non-alignment was treated as a suspect status rather than as a recognised position.

## Lesson 12 - Cuba, 1962: deterrence at its sharpest, and what it produced

Progress: 12/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: how Cuba entered the Cold War in 1959 and the sequence from nationalisation to the break in relations; the Bay of Pigs and Operation Mongoose; the five recorded reasons for Khrushchev's decision, including the Jupiter missiles in Turkey; Kennedy's choice of blockade over air strikes and invasion; U Thant's appeal and the compromise, including the undisclosed Turkish undertaking; Castro's anger and the cooling of Cuban-Soviet relations; the hotline and the Nuclear Test Ban Treaty; and the later historians' criticisms of Kennedy

### Visual: a crisis built in three years and resolved in a fortnight

```text
   1959  Castro seizes power from Batista, "the corrupt, American-backed
         dictator", and outrages the USA by nationalising American-owned
         estates and factories
              |
         as Cuba's relations with the USA worsen, those with the USSR
         improve
              |
   Jan 1961  the USA breaks off diplomatic relations with Cuba;
             the Russians increase their economic aid
              |
   Apr 1961  THE BAY OF PIGS. Kennedy approves a plan by Batista
             supporters to invade from American bases in Guatemala;
             the CIA is deeply involved; about 1400 men land; the
             operation is so badly planned and carried out that
             Castro's forces and his two jet planes have no difficulty
             crushing it
             -> later that year Castro announces that he is a Marxist
                and Cuba a socialist country
              |
   Nov 1961  OPERATION MONGOOSE. Kennedy gives the go-ahead for a secret
             CIA operation to "help Cuba overthrow the Communist regime";
             Cuban merchant ships are sunk, installations sabotaged,
             American invasion exercises carried out
              |
         Castro appeals to the USSR for military help
              |
   1962  Khrushchev decides to set up nuclear missile launchers in Cuba,
         with a range of up to 2000 miles - New York, Washington,
         Chicago and Boston under threat
              |
   Oct 1962  photographs from spy planes show a missile base under
             construction
              |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
   | KENNEDY'S OPTIONS                                             |
   | · air strikes against the bases (urged by military advisers)  |
   | · full-scale invasion (urged by General Maxwell Taylor)       |
   | · BLOCKADE + demand for dismantling  <- CHOSEN                |
   |   alerts American troops; blockades Cuba to keep out the 25   |
   |   Russian ships bringing missiles; demands the dismantling of |
   |   the sites and the removal of missiles already there         |
   +--------------------------------------------------------------+
              |
         U Thant, Secretary-General of the UN, appeals to both sides
         for restraint
              |
   THE COMPROMISE
   Khrushchev moves first: orders the ships to turn back; promises to
   remove the missiles and dismantle the sites
   Kennedy: promises the USA will not invade Cuba again, and undertakes
   to disarm the Jupiter missiles in Turkey - BUT WILL NOT ALLOW THIS
   TO BE ANNOUNCED PUBLICLY
              |
   RESULTS
   · Castro furious at being "deserted" apparently without consultation;
     Cuban-Soviet relations extremely cool for several years
   · both sides realise how easily nuclear war could have started
   · the hotline between Moscow and Washington
   · July 1963: the USSR, the USA and Britain sign a Nuclear Test Ban
     Treaty, agreeing to test only underground
```

*The diagram shows why "the Cuban missile crisis" is the wrong unit of analysis. The crisis lasted days; the confrontation that produced it lasted three years, and every step in the left-hand column is a response to the step above it.*

### 1. How Cuba entered the rivalry

✅ **Fact:** Cuba became involved in the Cold War in 1959 when Fidel Castro, who had just seized power from the corrupt, American-backed dictator Batista, outraged the USA by nationalising American-owned estates and factories.

✅ **Fact:** as Cuba's relations with the USA worsened, those with the USSR improved: in January 1961 the USA broke off diplomatic relations with Cuba, and the Russians increased their economic aid.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the order of events is the whole argument and it is frequently reversed in scripts. Castro seized power from an American-backed dictator, nationalised American property, and *then* the relationship with Moscow improved as the relationship with Washington deteriorated. ⚠️ The account underlines the point by recording that it was later in 1961, after the Bay of Pigs, that Castro announced he was now a Marxist and that Cuba was a socialist country. ⚠️ A candidate who writes that a communist Cuba invited Soviet missiles has inverted a sequence in which American hostility preceded the Cuban declaration of Marxism by two years. ⚠️ This is the Suez mechanism of Lesson 11 repeating in the western hemisphere, and stating it as a pattern rather than a coincidence is what distinguishes an analytical answer.

### 2. The Bay of Pigs and Mongoose: the provocation that produced the missiles

✅ **Fact:** convinced that Cuba was now a communist state in all but name, the new US president, John F. Kennedy, approved a plan by a group of Batista supporters to invade Cuba from American bases in Guatemala. ✅ The Central Intelligence Agency was deeply involved.

✅ **Fact:** the account then records the doctrine that made the operation thinkable, and it is worth quoting because it is the account's own generalisation: there was a general view in the USA at this time that it was quite permissible for them to interfere in the affairs of sovereign states and to overthrow any regimes which they felt were hostile and too close for comfort.

✅ **Fact:** the small invading force of about 1400 men landed at the Bay of Pigs in April 1961, but the operation was so badly planned and carried out that Castro's forces and his two jet planes had no difficulty crushing it. ✅ Later the same year, Castro announced that he was now a Marxist and that Cuba was a socialist country.

✅ **Fact:** Kennedy continued his campaign to destroy Castro in various ways: Cuban merchant ships were sunk, installations on the island were sabotaged and American troops carried out invasion exercises. ✅ Castro appealed to the USSR for military help. ✅ In November 1961 Kennedy gave the go-ahead for a secret CIA operation known as Operation Mongoose which aimed to "help Cuba overthrow the Communist regime".

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Bay of Pigs is usually taught as a fiasco, and its more important property is that it was an *instructive* fiasco for both sides. For Havana it demonstrated that the United States would use force and could be defeated when it used force badly, which made a Soviet guarantee both necessary and plausible. ⚠️ For Moscow it demonstrated that an American administration would attempt regime change in the western hemisphere and had just failed, which made a deterrent deployment look defensible rather than reckless. ⚠️ The strongest formulation for an answer is that the missiles were a response to a threat that was documented rather than imagined, and that saying so does not make the deployment prudent.

### 3. Khrushchev's five reasons, weighted

✅ **Fact:** Khrushchev decided to set up nuclear missile launchers in Cuba aimed at the USA, whose nearest point was less than a hundred miles from Cuba. ✅ He intended to install missiles with a range of up to 2000 miles, which meant that all the major cities of the central and eastern USA, such as New York, Washington, Chicago and Boston, would be under threat. ✅ This was a risky decision, and there was great consternation in the USA when, in October 1962, photographs taken from spy planes showed a missile base under construction.

✅ **Fact:** the account lists five reasons and explicitly refuses to place all the blame on one side.

| # | ✅ The reason as recorded | ⚠️ Weight |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | The Russians had lost the lead in ICBMs, so this was a way of trying to seize the initiative back from the USA - "but it would be wrong to put all the blame for the crisis on the USSR" | Strategic: a cheap way of offsetting an intercontinental deficit with intermediate-range weapons |
| 2 | In 1959 the Americans had signed an agreement with Turkey allowing them to deploy Jupiter nuclear missiles from bases in Turkey - and this was before any top-level contacts between Castro and the Russians had taken place. Khrushchev's memoirs: "the Americans had surrounded our country with military bases, now they would learn what it feels like to have enemy missiles pointing at you" | Reciprocal: the account's own dating makes the Turkish deployment prior, which is why it refuses to assign all blame |
| 3 | A gesture of solidarity with his ally Castro, who was under constant threat from the USA; although the Bay of Pigs had failed, Operation Mongoose followed in November 1961, and the missiles might dissuade such an operation or, failing that, be used against invading American troops | Alliance management: a patron demonstrating that its protection is real |
| 4 | It would test the resolve of the new, young American President Kennedy | Political probing - the "how far can I push them" pattern of Lesson 10 |
| 5 | Perhaps Khrushchev intended to use the missiles for bargaining with the West over the removal of American missiles from Europe, or a withdrawal from Berlin by the West | Bargaining: the missiles as a chip rather than as a capability |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** reason two is the one that changes an answer, and the reason it changes it is the date. ✅ The account states that the Turkish agreement was signed in 1959, before any top-level contacts between Castro and the Russians - which means the Cuban deployment was chronologically a response to an American deployment and not the reverse. ⚠️ That is why the account says it would be wrong to put all the blame on the USSR, and it is why the eventual settlement included an American undertaking on the Jupiters. ⚠️ A script that presents the crisis as an unprovoked Soviet gamble has omitted the fact the source uses to prevent exactly that reading.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** reasons four and five also deserve one sentence together, because they point to something uncomfortable. If the missiles were partly a test of a new president's resolve and partly a bargaining chip for Berlin, then a deployment capable of starting a nuclear war was being used as a diplomatic instrument. ⚠️ That is the strongest available criticism of Khrushchev, and it is also the best illustration in the topic of the danger Lesson 8 named: the value of a weapons system as leverage creates incentives to deploy it in ways that increase the chance of the war it is meant to prevent.

### 4. Kennedy's choice, and why the blockade was the decisive act

✅ **Fact:** Kennedy's military advisers urged him to launch air strikes against the bases; General Maxwell Taylor urged him to launch a full-scale invasion of Cuba. ✅ But he acted more cautiously: he alerted American troops, began a blockade of Cuba to keep out the 25 Russian ships which were bringing missiles to Cuba, and demanded the dismantling of the missile sites and the removal of those missiles already in Cuba. ✅ The situation was tense, and the world seemed to be on the verge of nuclear war. ✅ The Secretary-General of the UN, U Thant, appealed to both sides for restraint.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the blockade is the same structural choice as the Berlin airlift of 1948 and should be taught as its descendant. ⚠️ Air strikes would have killed Soviet personnel and destroyed Soviet equipment - an act of war with no further decision required from Moscow. ⚠️ An invasion would have done the same on a larger scale. ⚠️ A blockade placed the next move on the other side: Soviet ships could turn back, and it was their captains, on Moscow's instruction, who would have to decide whether to force a passage. ⚠️ In both 1948 and 1962 the side with the stronger local position chose the option that left the other side responsible for the first shot, which is the operating rule identified in Lesson 6 - and the fact that it held under this much greater pressure is the best evidence that it was a rule rather than a coincidence.

### 5. The settlement, and the part that was hidden

✅ **Fact:** Khrushchev made the first move: he ordered the Russian ships to turn back, and eventually a compromise solution was reached. ✅ Khrushchev promised to remove the missiles and dismantle the sites; in return Kennedy promised that the USA would not invade Cuba again, and undertook to disarm the Jupiter missiles in Turkey - though he would not allow this to be announced publicly.

✅ **Fact:** Castro was furious with Khrushchev for "deserting" him, apparently without consulting the Cubans, and Cuban-Soviet relations were extremely cool for several years.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the undisclosed Turkish undertaking is the most examinable detail in the lesson, because of what concealment did to the historical record. The public settlement looked like a Soviet retreat in exchange for a promise not to invade; the actual settlement was an exchange of two missile deployments plus a non-invasion pledge. ⚠️ Concealing half of the bargain produced an inflated contemporary reputation for American firmness - and Lesson 18 shows that reputation shaping American expectations of what pressure could achieve for the rest of the decade. ⚠️ It is also a reminder that the management of crises in this period included the management of how they were reported, which is a genuine finding rather than a cynical observation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Castro's fury is the item most often dropped and it belongs in any answer on the agency of smaller states. ⚠️ The Cuban government had accepted an enormous risk on its own territory and was not consulted about the settlement of it; the result was several years of cold relations with its patron. ⚠️ That is the same finding the Sino-Soviet material produces in Lesson 13 - patrons and clients had divergent interests, and superpower management of a crisis could be directly contrary to the client's preference.

### 6. What the crisis produced

✅ **Fact:** the crisis had only lasted a few days, but it was extremely tense and it had important results. ✅ Both sides could claim to have gained something, but most important was that both sides realised how easily a nuclear war could have started and how terrible the results would have been. ✅ It seemed to bring them both to their senses and produced a marked relaxation of tension. ✅ A telephone link, the hotline, was introduced between Moscow and Washington to allow swift consultations, and in July 1963 the USSR, the USA and Britain signed a Nuclear Test Ban Treaty, agreeing to carry out nuclear tests only underground to avoid polluting the atmosphere any further.

⚠️ **Note on dating.** ✅ The core account gives July 1963 for the signature of the Nuclear Test Ban Treaty. ⚠️ The standard record verified in the declared connecting-fact pass gives 25 July 1963 for the initialling in Moscow and 5 August 1963 for the signature. ⚠️ Both are displayed here rather than reconciled by preference; for examination purposes the year is what matters and the month should be given as the account gives it, or the distinction stated in a clause.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the hotline and the test ban are small agreements with a large significance, and the significance is the one that carries into Lesson 18. They are the first arrangements in the rivalry whose purpose is *risk management* rather than advantage: a telephone line reduces the chance of catastrophe by misunderstanding, and a ban on atmospheric testing addresses a shared harm. ⚠️ Neither reduces either side's capability by a single warhead. ⚠️ Détente, when it arrives, has exactly this character on a larger scale - which is why this session teaches Cuba as the origin of détente rather than as the climax of confrontation.

### 7. The historians' reassessment

✅ **Fact:** at first Kennedy's handling of the crisis was highly praised. ✅ Most American commentators argued that by standing up to the Russians and by resisting pressure from his own army Chiefs of Staff for a military response, Kennedy defused the crisis and achieved a peaceful settlement. ✅ The president's brother Robert was one of his chief supporters, particularly in his book *Thirteen Days* (1969). ✅ In order to lay all the blame for the crisis on the USSR, the Americans emphasised that Khrushchev and various Russian diplomats had repeatedly lied, insisting that they had no intention of building missile bases in Cuba.

✅ **Fact:** however, some later historians were more critical of Kennedy. ✅ A few accused him of missing a chance to solve the problem of Cuba once and for all - he ought to have called Khrushchev's bluff, attacked Cuba and overthrown Castro. ✅ Others criticised Kennedy for causing the crisis in the first place by placing nuclear missiles in Turkey and repeatedly trying to destabilise the Castro regime. ✅ It was also pointed out that since Soviet long-range missiles could already reach the USA from Russia itself, the missiles in Cuba did not exactly pose a new threat.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two lines of criticism run in opposite directions and that is precisely why they are useful. One says Kennedy was too cautious; the other says he was the author of the crisis. ⚠️ A candidate who reports both has demonstrated that the assessment of the crisis is contested from both flanks, which is a stronger position than endorsing either. ⚠️ The third criticism - that Soviet long-range missiles could already reach the United States, so the Cuban missiles did not pose a genuinely new threat - is the most analytically interesting, because it suggests the crisis was substantially about *perception and prestige* rather than about a change in the strategic balance. ⚠️ Stating that in one sentence, and noting that governments may go to the edge of war over perceived rather than actual shifts, is the highest-value observation available in this lesson.

✅ **Fact:** the account also records what happened after the crisis to the competition itself. Although in public the Russians claimed the outcome as a victory, in private they admitted that their main aim - to establish missile bases near the USA - had failed. ✅ Even the removal of American Thors and Jupiters from Turkey meant nothing, because the Americans now had another threat: ballistic missiles known as Polaris, later Poseidon, which could be launched from submarines in the eastern Mediterranean.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that last fact closes the loop with Lesson 8 and deserves to be used there and here. The concession that resolved the crisis was militarily worthless because a superior system had already made the conceded system redundant. ⚠️ Technology, in other words, had overtaken the object of the bargain before the bargain was struck - which is the clearest single illustration of why arms control that counts deployed systems rather than capabilities tends to lag behind the competition it is trying to limit.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ What were the causes of the Cuban missiles crisis? How was the crisis resolved and what were its consequences?
> ⚠️ Discuss the significance of the Cuban missiles crisis in the history of the Cold War.
> ⚠️ "The Cuban missiles crisis was a victory for American firmness." Critically examine.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, do not begin in October 1962: begin in 1959 and use the three-year chain, then the five reasons with the Turkish dating, then the blockade choice, then the compromise including the hidden Turkish undertaking. ⚠️ For the second, the significance is the hotline, the test ban and the origin of détente - a crisis whose main product was the machinery of risk management. ⚠️ For the third, the concealed Turkish undertaking and the observation that Soviet long-range missiles could already reach the United States are the two facts that dismantle the proposition without denying that Kennedy resisted pressure for a military response.

### Traps

- Starting the story in October 1962. ✅ The chain begins with Castro's seizure of power in 1959 and the nationalisations.
- Saying Castro was a communist when he took power. ✅ He announced that he was a Marxist and Cuba a socialist country later in 1961, after the Bay of Pigs.
- Omitting the Jupiter missiles in Turkey. ✅ Signed in 1959, before any top-level contacts between Castro and the Russians; the account uses this to refuse all blame to the USSR.
- Describing the settlement as an unconditional Soviet retreat. ✅ Kennedy promised not to invade again and undertook to disarm the Jupiters, but would not allow that to be announced publicly.
- Forgetting Castro's reaction. ✅ He was furious at being deserted apparently without consultation, and relations were extremely cool for several years.
- Treating the blockade as a minor detail. ⚠️ It was the decisive choice, and it left the next move with Moscow rather than taking it.
- Reporting only the praise of Kennedy. ✅ Later historians criticised him from both directions, and one criticism notes that Soviet long-range missiles could already reach the USA.
- Giving the test ban a confident month. ✅ The account says July 1963; the standard record dates the signature to 5 August 1963 after initialling on 25 July.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1959: Castro seizes power from Batista and nationalises American-owned estates and factories.
2. ✅ January 1961: the USA breaks off diplomatic relations; Soviet economic aid increases.
3. ✅ April 1961: the Bay of Pigs - about 1400 men from American bases in Guatemala, the CIA deeply involved, crushed by Castro's forces and two jet planes.
4. ✅ The recorded American doctrine of the time: a general view that it was permissible to interfere in sovereign states and overthrow hostile regimes too close for comfort.
5. ✅ Later in 1961 Castro announces he is a Marxist and Cuba a socialist country.
6. ✅ November 1961: Operation Mongoose; ships sunk, installations sabotaged, invasion exercises held.
7. ✅ Khrushchev's five reasons: lost lead in ICBMs; the Jupiter deployment agreed with Turkey in 1959 before any top-level Castro-Soviet contacts, with his memoir quotation; solidarity with Castro; testing Kennedy; and possible bargaining over American missiles in Europe or Berlin.
8. ✅ Range up to 2000 miles: New York, Washington, Chicago and Boston under threat; the base revealed by spy-plane photographs in October 1962.
9. ✅ Advisers urged air strikes; Maxwell Taylor urged invasion; Kennedy alerted troops, blockaded Cuba against 25 Russian ships, and demanded dismantling and removal.
10. ✅ U Thant appealed to both sides for restraint.
11. ✅ The compromise: Khrushchev turns the ships back and removes the missiles; Kennedy promises no further invasion and undertakes to disarm the Jupiters in Turkey, but will not allow this to be announced.
12. ✅ Castro furious at desertion without consultation; Cuban-Soviet relations cool for several years.
13. ✅ Results: both sides realised how easily nuclear war could have started; a marked relaxation; the Moscow-Washington hotline; and the Nuclear Test Ban Treaty with the USSR, USA and Britain agreeing to test only underground.
14. ✅ Later criticism from both flanks; and the observation that Soviet long-range missiles could already reach the USA, so the Cuban missiles were not exactly a new threat.
15. ✅ In private the Russians admitted their main aim had failed; and the Turkish concession meant little because Polaris, later Poseidon, could be launched from submarines in the eastern Mediterranean.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why is the Cuban missiles crisis better described as the origin of détente than as the climax of confrontation?

**Model answer:** Because what it produced was machinery for managing risk rather than any change in the balance of forces. ✅ The crisis itself was brief and extremely tense, and the account's own summary of its importance is that both sides realised how easily a nuclear war could have started and how terrible the results would have been, that this seemed to bring them to their senses, and that a marked relaxation of tension followed. ✅ The concrete products were a telephone link between Moscow and Washington allowing swift consultation, and a Nuclear Test Ban Treaty signed by the USSR, the USA and Britain limiting tests to underground. ⚠️ Neither removed a single warhead or altered either side's capability; both reduced the probability of catastrophe through misunderstanding or shared harm. ⚠️ That is exactly the character of the agreements of the 1970s - SALT I decided how many ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs each side could have without reducing armaments - so the crisis established the model that détente later scaled up. ⚠️ Treating it as the climax of confrontation also misreads the strategic outcome, because the concession that settled it, the Jupiters in Turkey, had already been made redundant by submarine-launched missiles in the eastern Mediterranean.

**Misconception to avoid:** Believing that the crisis proved firmness works. The public settlement omitted the American undertaking on the Turkish missiles, which Kennedy would not allow to be announced, so the reputation for successful firmness rested on an incomplete account of the bargain - and later historians additionally noted that the Cuban missiles added little to a Soviet capability that could already reach the United States.

```text

         ambassadors are exchanged
              |
   THE TRIANGLE AT ITS SHARPEST
   "At the end of the 1970s both Russia and China were vying for
    American support, against each other, for the leadership of world
    communism."
              |
   Feb 1979  CHINA ATTACKS VIETNAM
             partly in retaliation for Vietnam's December 1978 invasion
             of Kampuchea, which overthrew the Khmer Rouge government of
             Pol Pot, a protege of China; partly a frontier dispute.
             Vietnam supported Russia. China withdrew after three weeks,
             having, as Beijing put it, "taught the Vietnamese a lesson"
   1984  China's stated grievances against the USSR:
           · Russian troops in Afghanistan
           · Soviet backing of Vietnamese troops in Kampuchea
           · the Soviet build-up along the Mongolian and Manchurian
             frontiers
   1985  Gorbachev: five-year trade and economic co-operation
         agreements, July; regular contact between the governments
   1989  A FORMAL RECONCILIATION in May, when Gorbachev visits Beijing;
         Vietnam withdraws from Kampuchea the same year
```

*Read the two quarrel boxes together and the standard account of the split collapses. It was not only a doctrinal dispute that later acquired a territorial dimension; the account says that once the doctrinal ground shifted, the territorial claim became the principal one - which means classical state interest outlasted ideology inside an ideological alliance.*

### 1. The alliance, and how quickly it went wrong

✅ **Fact:** the USSR and China had signed a treaty of mutual assistance and friendship in 1950, and the account records the Western reaction in the same period: when the USSR and communist China signed that treaty in February 1950, American fears of an advancing tide of communism seemed about to be realised.

✅ **Fact:** relations between the USSR and China deteriorated steadily after 1956.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two dates together are the most economical way of making the point that the Western threat assessment of 1950 was accurate for six years and then wrong for the next thirty. ⚠️ The alliance that produced NSC-68's alarm, that helped shape the American response to Korea and that justified the encirclement strategy of SEATO had begun to dissolve within six years of its signature - and containment continued to be designed against a combined adversary for a further fifteen. ⚠️ That is a finding about the durability of threat assessments as much as about the bloc, and it is worth one sentence in any answer on containment's record.

### 2. The doctrinal quarrel, stated precisely

✅ **Fact:** the Chinese did not approve of Khrushchev's policies, particularly his belief in peaceful coexistence and his claim that it was possible to achieve communism by methods other than violent revolution. ✅ This went against the ideas of Lenin, leader of the 1917 Russian communist revolution, and so the Chinese accused the Russians of revisionism - revising or reinterpreting the teachings of Marx and Lenin to suit their own needs. ✅ They were angry at Khrushchev's soft line towards the USA. ✅ In retaliation the Russians reduced their economic aid to China.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice what the Chinese charge was actually about, because scripts often blur it into general rivalry. It was about two propositions announced in the same February 1956 speech that Lesson 10 examined: that peaceful coexistence with the West was not only possible but essential, and - implicitly in Khrushchev's claim that Western recognition of Soviet economic superiority rather than defeat in war would bring the communist world - that revolution need not be violent. ⚠️ Both are revisions of a doctrine in which the seizure of power is by revolution and the antagonism between systems is irreconcilable. ⚠️ So the same speech that produced the thaw in East-West relations produced the split in East-East relations, and saying so links Lessons 10 and 13 in a single clause that few candidates will write.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the retaliation is also more significant than its one line suggests. Reducing economic aid to an ally in order to win a doctrinal argument is the use of a material instrument in an ideological dispute - the same instrument, in miniature, that Washington used at Aswan in 1956 and against Cuba after 1959. ⚠️ The general observation is that both superpowers used aid as leverage against states whose alignment they wished to correct, and that in all three cases the leverage failed.

### 3. The territorial quarrel, and the sentence that reorders the topic

✅ **Fact:** the ideological argument was not the only source of trouble: there was also a frontier dispute. ✅ During the nineteenth century Russia had taken over large areas of Chinese territory north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang province, which the Chinese were now demanding back, so far without success.

✅ **Fact:** and the account adds the clause that matters most: now that China herself was following a softer policy towards the USA, it seemed that the territorial problem was the main bone of contention.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that sentence is the most quotable item in the lesson and the most analytically useful in the whole bloc argument. It says that when the doctrinal disagreement lost its force - because China was itself now dealing with the capitalist superpower - the dispute did not end; it reverted to a nineteenth-century territorial claim. ⚠️ Three inferences follow. ⚠️ First, the ideological quarrel was genuine but not fundamental: remove it and a quarrel remained. ⚠️ Second, communist states behaved as states, with inherited grievances about frontiers, exactly as non-communist states did - which is a direct refutation of the proposition that ideology determined the conduct of the eastern bloc. ⚠️ Third, this is the strongest evidence available for the owner's formulation that bipolarity described the distribution of weapons rather than the distribution of loyalty.

### 4. The triangle: how a quarrel inside the bloc produced the decade's biggest diplomatic move

✅ **Fact:** China and the USA had been extremely hostile towards each other since the Korean War and seemed likely to remain so while the Americans backed Chiang Kai-shek and the Nationalists in Taiwan, and while the Chinese backed Ho Chi Minh.

✅ **Fact:** however, in 1971 the Chinese unexpectedly invited an American table-tennis team to visit China. ✅ Following the success of that visit, the USA responded by calling off their veto of Chinese entry into the UN, and communist China was therefore allowed to become a member of the UN in October 1971.

✅ **Fact:** President Nixon, looking for a bold initiative for which his presidency would be remembered, decided he would visit China himself; Chairman Mao agreed to receive him and the visit took place in February 1972. ✅ Though Mao was reported to have told Zhou Enlai that the USA was "like an ape moving towards becoming a human being", the meeting led to a resumption of diplomatic relations. ✅ President Ford also paid a successful visit to Beijing in 1975.

✅ **Fact:** there was still the problem of Taiwan to sour the relationship: though Chiang himself died in 1975, his supporters still occupied the island, and the communists would not be happy until it was brought under their control. ✅ Relations improved further in 1978 when Democrat President Carter decided to withdraw recognition of Nationalist China; however, this caused a row in the USA, where Carter was accused of betraying his ally. ✅ The climax of détente between China and the USA came early in 1979 when Carter gave formal recognition to the People's Republic of China, and ambassadors were exchanged.

✅ **Fact:** and the account states the triangular position in one sentence: at the end of the 1970s both Russia and China were vying for American support, against each other, for the leadership of world communism.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the ordinary account of the 1972 opening presents it as American diplomatic skill. The sources support something less flattering and more interesting: the opening was *made available* by a quarrel the United States had not caused and for many years had not believed in. ⚠️ The Chinese invitation came first - a table-tennis team, not a diplomatic initiative - and the American response was to withdraw an obstruction rather than to offer anything. ⚠️ The clause about both communist powers vying for American support is the strategic inversion that makes the decade intelligible: the state whose containment had been designed against a communist bloc became the arbiter between the bloc's two largest members, and did so without conceding anything on Taiwan.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Mao's reported remark is worth including for a reason beyond colour. It establishes that the opening involved no ideological softening on the Chinese side whatever; it was an act of state policy conducted in an unchanged doctrinal vocabulary. ⚠️ That is useful evidence in any answer tempted to read the rapprochement as a thaw in belief rather than a realignment of interest.

### 5. When communist states fought each other

✅ **Fact:** to complicate matters further, Vietnam now supported Russia. ✅ When the Chinese attacked Vietnam in February 1979, relations reached rock bottom. ✅ The Chinese attack was partly in retaliation for Vietnam's invasion of Kampuchea, formerly Cambodia, in December 1978, which overthrew the Khmer Rouge government of Pol Pot, a protégé of China, and partly because of a frontier dispute. ✅ They withdrew after three weeks, having, as Beijing put it, "taught the Vietnamese a lesson".

✅ **Fact:** in 1984 the Chinese set out their grievances against the USSR: the presence of Russian troops in Afghanistan; Soviet backing of the Vietnamese troops in Kampuchea; and the Soviet troop build-up along the Chinese frontiers of Mongolia and Manchuria.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** February 1979 is the hardest single fact in this topic against a monolithic-bloc reading, and it should be stated with all its elements, because each one does separate work. ⚠️ A communist state invaded a second communist state in December 1978, removing a third communist government that was the client of a fourth communist state, which then attacked the invader while the invader was supported by the fifth and largest. ⚠️ No account of the Cold War as a contest between two ideological blocs can accommodate that sentence, and a candidate who can produce it in an examination has settled the question of bloc cohesion with one example.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 1984 grievance list is equally useful for a different purpose. Every one of the three items is a security complaint about Soviet military deployment - in Afghanistan, in Kampuchea by proxy, and on China's own frontiers. ⚠️ Not one of them is doctrinal. ⚠️ Twenty-eight years after a quarrel that began over revisionism, the agenda between the two largest communist powers had become entirely a matter of troops and borders, which is what the territorial sentence of section 3 predicted.

### 6. The reconciliation, and its timing

✅ **Fact:** Mikhail Gorbachev was determined to begin a new era in Sino-Russian relations. ✅ Five-year agreements on trade and economic co-operation were signed in July 1985, and regular contact took place between the two governments. ✅ A formal reconciliation took place in May 1989 when Gorbachev visited Beijing. ✅ Also in 1989 Vietnam withdrew its troops from Kampuchea, and so their relations with China improved.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the timing is the point, and the owner's own formulation captures it: the communist world reunited diplomatically in the very year it began to dissolve politically. ⚠️ Gorbachev arrived in Beijing in May 1989, four months before the process that Lesson 20 describes began in earnest in Poland and Hungary, and in the same weeks as the Chinese government's suppression of the demonstration in Tiananmen Square in June 1989, which the account records as bringing worldwide condemnation. ⚠️ Within thirty months the Soviet Union had ceased to exist. ⚠️ The reconciliation therefore settled a quarrel between two states one of which was about to disappear, which is a reminder that diplomatic achievements are dated by the durability of their parties.

### 7. What the split did to the Cold War

| ⚠️ Effect | ✅ Supporting evidence from this session |
|---|---|
| **It invalidated the premise of containment** | ✅ Containment was designed against communism as such, and NSC-68 regarded the Russians as fanatics who would stop at nothing; ✅ the encirclement of China through ANZUS and SEATO assumed a combined adversary; ✅ yet relations between the two largest communist powers deteriorated steadily after 1956 |
| **It made the 1972 opening possible** | ✅ The Chinese invitation of 1971, the UN seat in October 1971, Nixon's visit in February 1972, and the recorded position that both communist powers were vying for American support |
| **It gave the USA leverage in détente** | ✅ The account lists triangular pressure created by China-US rapprochement among the reasons for détente, and records the Russians as not wanting to be left out when Chinese-American relations began to improve in 1971 |
| **It produced war between communist states** | ✅ Vietnam invades Kampuchea, December 1978; China attacks Vietnam, February 1979, withdrawing after three weeks |
| **It converted ideology into geopolitics** | ✅ The frontier claims north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang; the 1984 grievances, all three of them military deployments |
| **It ended only as the system ended** | ✅ July 1985 agreements; formal reconciliation in Beijing in May 1989 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third row is the one that connects this lesson to Lesson 18, and it is the least obvious. The account explicitly lists among the reasons for détente that the Russians were on bad terms with China and did not want to be left out when relations between China and the USA began to improve in 1971. ⚠️ Soviet interest in negotiating with Washington was therefore increased by a Chinese initiative towards Washington - which means that one of the central achievements of détente was produced in part by a quarrel inside the adversary's camp rather than by the negotiating skill of either principal.

### 8. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the Sino-Soviet split is a specialist's point. For most of the period the two communist powers were on the same side against the West, and organising an answer around their quarrel exaggerates a dispute between two states that never fought each other.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection fails on two counts, one chronological and one factual. ⚠️ Chronologically, the alliance lasted from 1950 to 1956 - six years of a forty-five-year period - and the deterioration ran for the remaining thirty-three. ⚠️ Describing the quarrel as an interlude inverts the proportions. ⚠️ Factually, the claim that they never fought is true of the two principals and false of the bloc: ✅ China attacked Vietnam in February 1979, and Vietnam supported Russia. ⚠️ The objection does, however, contain a real warning that should be honoured: the split must not be used to suggest that the Western alliance faced no coherent adversary. ✅ Soviet power in eastern Europe was real, the Warsaw Pact existed, and Soviet deployments in Afghanistan and support for Vietnam were the subject of Chinese complaint precisely because they were effective. ⚠️ The precise finding is that there were two communist great powers pursuing divergent and at times hostile policies, and that Western strategy was built for one.

### 9. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The communist bloc acted as a single actor in the Cold War." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Examine the causes and consequences of the Sino-Soviet split.
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the American opening to China in 1971-72.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, the answer is a refusal built on four items: the deterioration after 1956, the revisionism charge, the territorial claim that outlasted it, and February 1979. ⚠️ For the second, separate the doctrinal and territorial causes and then use the account's own sentence about the territorial problem becoming the main bone of contention. ⚠️ For the third, resist crediting American diplomacy alone: the invitation came from Beijing, the American act was to withdraw a veto, and the opening's significance is triangular - it gave Washington leverage in détente and left Taiwan unresolved.

### Traps

- Dating the split from the 1960s without the trigger. ✅ Relations deteriorated steadily after 1956, and the doctrinal charge is aimed at Khrushchev's positions of that year.
- Explaining "revisionism" vaguely. ✅ It means revising or reinterpreting the teachings of Marx and Lenin to suit one's own needs, and the specific objects were peaceful coexistence and non-violent routes to communism.
- Omitting the territorial dispute. ✅ Areas north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang, and the account says the territorial problem became the main bone of contention.
- Crediting Nixon with initiating the opening. ✅ The Chinese unexpectedly invited an American table-tennis team in 1971, and the first American step was calling off the UN veto.
- Saying the opening settled Taiwan. ✅ Chiang died in 1975, his supporters still occupied the island, and Carter's withdrawal of recognition in 1978 caused a domestic row.
- Missing February 1979. ✅ China attacked Vietnam, withdrawing after three weeks; Vietnam supported Russia; the Kampuchean invasion of December 1978 had removed a Chinese protégé.
- Treating the 1989 reconciliation as a durable settlement. ⚠️ It occurred months before the collapse that Lesson 20 describes.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1950: the Sino-Soviet treaty of mutual assistance and friendship; February 1950 alarmed Washington as an advancing tide of communism.
2. ✅ After 1956 relations deteriorated steadily.
3. ✅ The doctrinal objections: peaceful coexistence, and the claim that communism could be achieved by methods other than violent revolution.
4. ✅ The charge: revisionism - revising or reinterpreting the teachings of Marx and Lenin to suit their own needs; also anger at the soft line towards the USA.
5. ✅ The Soviet retaliation: reduced economic aid to China.
6. ✅ The territorial claim: areas north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang, taken in the nineteenth century, demanded back without success.
7. ✅ Once China softened towards the USA, the territorial problem became the main bone of contention.
8. ✅ 1971: the table-tennis invitation; the American veto on UN entry called off; China admitted in October 1971.
9. ✅ February 1972: Nixon's visit; Mao's reported "ape" remark; diplomatic relations resumed; Ford visits in 1975.
10. ✅ 1978: Carter withdraws recognition of Nationalist China, causing a domestic row; 1979: formal recognition of the People's Republic and exchange of ambassadors.
11. ✅ At the end of the 1970s both Russia and China were vying for American support against each other for the leadership of world communism.
12. ✅ December 1978: Vietnam invades Kampuchea and removes Pol Pot, a Chinese protégé; February 1979: China attacks Vietnam and withdraws after three weeks.
13. ✅ 1984 grievances: Russian troops in Afghanistan; Soviet backing of Vietnamese troops in Kampuchea; the build-up on the Mongolian and Manchurian frontiers.
14. ✅ July 1985: five-year trade and economic co-operation agreements; May 1989: formal reconciliation on Gorbachev's visit to Beijing; Vietnam withdraws from Kampuchea the same year.

### Concept check

**Question:** Show how the Sino-Soviet split changed what containment was actually facing, and why Western policy was slow to reflect it.

**Model answer:** It replaced a single adversary with two hostile ones, and Western policy continued for fifteen years to be designed for the first. ✅ Containment was framed against communism as such - the Truman Doctrine covered free peoples resisting subjugation without naming the Soviet Union, NSC-68 in April 1950 described the Russians as fanatics who would stop at nothing to spread communism all over the world, and after Korea the Americans tried to encircle China with bases through agreements with Australia and New Zealand in 1951 and SEATO in 1954. ✅ Yet relations between the USSR and China deteriorated steadily after 1956, with the Chinese accusing the Russians of revisionism over peaceful coexistence and non-violent routes to communism, the Russians reducing economic aid in retaliation, and an unresolved territorial claim north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang which, once China softened towards the USA, became the main bone of contention. ⚠️ The slowness is explained by the quarrel's form: it was conducted in doctrinal vocabulary between two states that remained communist, so from outside it looked like a dispute within a family rather than a strategic realignment. ✅ The reversal, when it came, was rapid and initiated by Beijing - a table-tennis invitation in 1971, the UN seat in October 1971, Nixon's visit in February 1972 - and by the end of the decade both communist powers were vying for American support against each other.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the split as a disagreement that was eventually patched up in 1989 and therefore secondary. The quarrel produced a Chinese attack on Vietnam in February 1979 and a list of grievances in 1984 consisting entirely of Soviet military deployments; the 1989 reconciliation settled it only months before one of the two parties ceased to exist.

---

## Lesson 14 - Decolonisation, non-alignment and the agency of the new states

Progress: 14/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the recorded wish of newly emerging states to stay neutral or non-aligned; the Third World as a self-description at Algiers in 1973; the SEATO refusal counted; the peaceful-coexistence agreements with India and Burma in 1954 and the competing Chinese offer; the two bargaining advantages and the two exposures of an uncommitted position; the Angolan case as the clearest evidence of the proxy discipline; India's position and the cross-link that avoids duplication; and the difference between neutrality and non-alignment

📚 Book context: queried - the core account on decolonisation among the effects of the Second World War, including the Algiers conference of 1973 and the Third World self-description; the Korean-war results section on SEATO's poor Asian uptake and on China's friendship-and-aid offer with the 1954 agreements with India and Burma; and, for the cross-owned African evidence, the decolonisation owner's named-case bank on Angola with the account's own verdict on outside interference
🔍 CA search: not run for this lesson - the single topic-level query is recorded in Lesson 1

### Visual: the third position, and what it cost and bought

