```text
                                |
        +-----------------------+-----------------------+
        |                       |                       |
   JOIN THE WEST           JOIN THE EAST           JOIN NEITHER
   arms, aid, alliance     arms, aid, training     "wished to remain
   protection              and a development       neutral or non-aligned
                           model                   in the struggle between
                                                   the other two worlds -
                                                   communism and
                                                   capitalism"
        |                       |                       |
        |                       |            +----------+----------+
        |                       |            | WHAT IT BOUGHT      |
        |                       |            | · aid, arms and     |
        |                       |            |   diplomatic support|
        |                       |            |   sought from BOTH  |
        |                       |            | · competition among |
        |                       |            |   suitors raises the|
        |                       |            |   price of your     |
        |                       |            |   alignment         |
        |                       |            +---------------------+
        |                       |            | WHAT IT COST        |
        |                       |            | · pressure and      |
        |                       |            |   destabilisation   |
        |                       |            |   from both         |
        |                       |            | · reclassification: |
        |                       |            |   buying arms from  |
        |                       |            |   eastern Europe    |
        |                       |            |   while outside the |
        |                       |            |   Western alliance  |
        |                       |            |   was, in American  |
        |                       |            |   eyes, "just as bad|
        |                       |            |   as a communist    |
        |                       |            |   country"          |
        +-----------------------+------------+---------------------+
                                |
                   THE COUNTABLE EVIDENCE OF THE CHOICE
   SEATO, 1954: the USA "was disappointed when only three Asian states -
   Pakistan, Thailand and the Philippines - joined"
   -> "It was obvious that many states wanted to keep clear of the Cold
      War and remain uncommitted."
   ALGIERS, 1973: "the leaders of many of these newly emerging nations
   met in conference and made it clear that they regarded themselves as
   a Third World... they wished to remain neutral or non-aligned in the
   struggle between the other two worlds"
```

*The right-hand branch is a policy, not a gap. The two boxes beneath it explain why it was chosen and why it was dangerous, and the two dated items at the bottom are the evidence that it was chosen deliberately and collectively.*

### 1. The recorded position, in the sources' own words

✅ **Fact:** the account, surveying the effects of the Second World War, records that the war encouraged the movement towards decolonisation; that the defeats inflicted on Britain, Holland and France by Japan, and the Japanese occupation of their territories - Malaya, Singapore and Burma, French Indo-China and the Dutch East Indies - destroyed the tradition of European superiority and invincibility; and that it could hardly be expected that, having fought to get rid of the Japanese, the Asian peoples would willingly return to European rule. ✅ Gradually they achieved full independence, though not without a struggle in many cases, and this in turn intensified demands for independence among the peoples of Africa and the Middle East, so that in the 1960s the result was a large array of new states.

✅ **Fact:** the account then records the political self-definition: the leaders of many of these newly emerging nations met in conference at Algiers in 1973 and made it clear that they regarded themselves as a Third World. ✅ By this they meant that they wished to remain neutral or non-aligned in the struggle between the other two worlds - communism and capitalism. ✅ Usually poor and under-developed industrially, the new nations were often intensely suspicious of the motives of both communism and capitalism, and they resented their own economic dependence on the world's wealthy powers.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of that passage should be carried into an answer intact. ⚠️ First, the position is defined *negatively but deliberately* - neutral or non-aligned in the struggle between the other two worlds - which is a chosen relation to a conflict, not an absence of foreign policy. ⚠️ Second, the stated ground is suspicion of the motives of *both* systems, which rules out the common script in which non-alignment is a softer form of alignment with one side. ⚠️ Third, the passage names an economic grievance - resentment of economic dependence on the world's wealthy powers - which is not a Cold War grievance at all, and which explains why the Third World's agenda diverged from the superpowers' agenda even where its votes were sought by both.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the boundary must also be stated. Decolonisation as a process, its routes, its named post-independence cases and its regional variations belong to the decolonisation owner. ⚠️ What belongs here is the interaction: what the Cold War did to decolonising states, and what their choices did to the Cold War.

### 2. The evidence that the refusal was real: three dated items

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** assertions about non-alignment are cheap; the sources supply three items that can be counted or dated, and an answer built on them is substantially stronger than one built on the concept.

1. ✅ **SEATO, 1954.** The Americans tried to encircle China with bases; in 1951 defensive agreements were signed with Australia and New Zealand, and in 1954 these three states, together with Britain and France, set up the South East Asia Treaty Organization. The USA was disappointed when only three Asian states - Pakistan, Thailand and the Philippines - joined. ✅ The account's own conclusion: it was obvious that many states wanted to keep clear of the Cold War and remain uncommitted.
2. ✅ **The Chinese counter-offer, 1954.** In the same year, China offered friendship and aid to under-developed Third World countries in Asia, Africa and Latin America, and peaceful coexistence agreements were signed with India and Burma.
3. ✅ **Algiers, 1973.** The collective self-description as a Third World, with the stated wish to remain neutral or non-aligned.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** item two is the most interesting and the least used. In 1954 a decolonising Asian state could be offered a Western alliance, a Soviet relationship, or a Chinese offer of friendship and aid on a coexistence basis - three options, not two, and the third came from a communist power that was not the Soviet Union. ⚠️ The Cold War, seen from Delhi or Rangoon in 1954, was therefore not a binary choice at all, and the standard two-bloc diagram misrepresents the decision that was actually being taken. ⚠️ Item two also anticipates the split of Lesson 13 by two years: China was building an independent relationship with the decolonising world before its quarrel with Moscow became open.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the chronological spread of the three items matters too. 1954, 1954 and 1973 - which means the refusal to align was present at the moment of the first Asian alliance and was still being collectively affirmed nineteen years later, through the period of the Sino-Soviet split, Vietnam and détente. ⚠️ It was, in other words, durable, and durability is the property that distinguishes a policy from a posture.

### 3. What non-alignment bought, and what it cost

| ⚠️ Advantage | ✅ Evidence available in this session |
|---|---|
| **Aid and arms from more than one source** | ✅ Khrushchev hoped to win neutral states over to communism by lavish economic aid; ✅ China offered friendship and aid to under-developed Third World countries; ✅ Egypt obtained Russian fighters, bombers and tanks through the Czechoslovak deal of September 1955 while remaining outside both alliances |
| **A raised price for any eventual alignment** | ⚠️ A state that both blocs wish to attract can extract better terms than one already committed; ✅ the contest for neutral states is precisely what Khrushchev's aid policy was designed to win |
| **Freedom from a patron's wars** | ✅ Only three Asian states joined SEATO; the rest incurred no obligation in the wars that followed in Indo-China |

| ⚠️ Exposure | ✅ Evidence available in this session |
|---|---|
| **Reclassification as hostile** | ✅ Any country not part of the Western alliance which bought arms from eastern Europe was, in American eyes, just as bad as a communist country - the criterion applied to Egypt in 1955 |
| **Economic coercion** | ✅ The $46 million Aswan grant cancelled in July 1956 to force Nasser away from his new links with the communists |
| **Covert intervention** | ✅ The account's record of American activity in an astonishing number of states, including attempts at assassination, rigging of elections, organising and financing acts of terrorism, economic destabilisation and, in the last resort, full-scale military intervention - taught in Lesson 17 |
| **Internationalised civil conflict** | ✅ Angola after 1975, where the account's verdict is that outside interference and the extension of the Cold War certainly made the conflict much worse |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the two tables together answer the commonest stem in this family - was non-alignment effective? - and the answer they support is conditional rather than global. ⚠️ Non-alignment worked as a strategy where a state had something both blocs wanted and no internal conflict for either to exploit; it failed where an internal contest offered a point of entry. ⚠️ Egypt in 1955 obtained arms from one bloc and remained outside both; Angola in 1975 had a three-way internal contest and became a theatre. ⚠️ The difference is not the wisdom of the governments but the structure of the domestic situation, and saying so avoids both the celebratory and the dismissive account of the Third World's Cold War.

### 4. The Angolan case, as the decolonisation owner records it

✅ **Fact:** the decolonisation owner's named-case bank records that civil war broke out in Angola immediately after independence from Portugal in 1975, between three movements: the Marxist-style MPLA under Neto, appealing across tribal divisions; UNITA under Savimbi, based on the Ovimbundu; and the weaker FNLA on the Bakongo. ✅ The USA backed the FNLA with advisers, cash and armaments; Cuba sent troops for the MPLA; South African troops invaded via Namibia; and Mobutu sent troops from Zaire. ✅ The account's own verdict is reproduced there in full: no doubt there would have been fighting and bloodshed anyway, but outside interference and the extension of the Cold War to Angola certainly made the conflict much worse. ✅ Settlement came only when the international situation changed, with a UN-brokered deal in December 1988 trading South African withdrawal from Namibia against the departure of the 50 000 Cuban troops.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the single best-evidenced statement of the proxy discipline in the whole repository, and it should be quoted in substance rather than paraphrased into vagueness, because its structure is exactly right: a concession that local conflict would have occurred anyway, followed by a precise claim about what external involvement added - severity, not existence. ⚠️ Three further observations belong to this session rather than to the decolonisation owner. ⚠️ First, the intervening parties include two states that were not superpowers - Cuba and South Africa - and one African state, Zaire, which means "superpower proxy war" understates the number of autonomous actors. ⚠️ Second, Cuba is simultaneously a client in Lesson 12 and a patron here, which is a useful corrective to a hierarchy of great powers and dependants. ⚠️ Third, the settlement of December 1988 was produced by a change in the international situation rather than by exhaustion on the ground, which is the ending-phase mechanism of Lesson 20 appearing early in Africa.

### 5. India, and the cross-link that avoids duplication

✅ **Fact:** the core account records that China offered friendship and aid to under-developed Third World countries and that peaceful coexistence agreements were signed with India and Burma in 1954.

✅ **Fact:** the advanced owner records the two entry points it considers useful and then imposes a boundary: after the Second World War many newly emerging states wanted to remain neutral or non-aligned in the struggle between communism and capitalism; and in Asia, the Korean War and SEATO showed that many states wanted to stay uncommitted rather than enter bloc politics. ✅ It then instructs that this file should not re-explain India's non-alignment at length, and that the dedicated India foreign-policy material should be used instead.

✅ **Fact:** the material cross-linked there records that the 1954 India-China agreement concerning the Tibet region placed the five Panchsheel principles in its preamble - mutual respect for territorial integrity and sovereignty, non-aggression, non-interference, equality and mutual benefit, and peaceful coexistence - and that non-alignment meant independent judgement amid bloc rivalry, not legal neutrality, isolation or equal distance on every issue.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the boundary is a real one and worth respecting in an examination as well as in this session. ⚠️ In a General Studies Paper I world-history answer, India appears as one instance of a general phenomenon - a newly independent state that declined the bloc choice and helped give that choice a vocabulary - and the detail of Indian foreign policy, its institutions and its later evolution belongs to the Indian owners. ⚠️ The formulation that keeps an answer inside the boundary is: the 1954 agreements are evidence that the coexistence vocabulary was being written jointly by Asian states rather than received from either superpower.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the distinction the Indian material draws is also the sharpest conceptual point available in this lesson, and it belongs in any answer that uses the word. ⚠️ Non-alignment is not neutrality. ✅ Austrian neutrality, met in Lessons 7 and 10, was a treaty obligation: no union with Germany, strictly limited forces, no NATO and no European Economic Community. ⚠️ Non-alignment imposed no such legal disabilities and claimed the opposite - independent judgement, case by case, with relations of every kind available to both sides. ⚠️ A state that is neutral has contracted out of the conflict; a state that is non-aligned has reserved its judgement inside it, which is a far more demanding position and explains why it was so frequently misread as inconsistency.

### 6. Why the superpowers competed for the uncommitted at all

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** it is worth asking why states that were, in the account's words, usually poor and under-developed industrially mattered to powers with hydrogen bombs. Four answers are supported by material in this session.

1. ✅ **Doctrinal demonstration.** Khrushchev's peaceful coexistence held that the communist world would arrive when the western powers recognised the superiority of the Soviet economic system, and that neutral states would be won by lavish economic aid. Development outcomes in uncommitted states were therefore evidence in the central argument.
2. ✅ **Votes and legitimacy.** The UN's membership expanded - ✅ the Soviet veto was lifted on sixteen new members in 1955 - and the General Assembly's weight was demonstrated at Suez, where the invasions were condemned 64 to 5.
3. ✅ **Bases and access.** ✅ The American attempt to encircle China with bases, ✅ the Jupiter deployment in Turkey and ✅ the Soviet interest in the Middle East all depended on the alignment of states that were neither superpower.
4. ✅ **Denial.** ✅ The American criterion applied to Egypt - arms bought from eastern Europe by a state outside the Western alliance - shows that the competition was frequently about preventing the other side's access rather than about securing one's own.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the fourth is the most important for understanding why non-alignment was so often punished. A competition to *deny* access does not require the target to be a communist state; it only requires that it not be reliably closed to the other side. ⚠️ Under that logic a genuinely uncommitted state is, by definition, a problem - and the Egyptian case shows the reasoning being applied in exactly that form.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** non-alignment was largely rhetorical. States that called themselves non-aligned took arms and money from one side or the other; Egypt was armed by the eastern bloc, Angola's government was defended by Cuban troops, and the movement's conferences produced declarations rather than outcomes. The agency claimed for these states is generous rather than evidenced.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is right about individual cases and wrong about the general claim, and the distinction between the two is the answer. ⚠️ Non-alignment was never a claim to have no relations with either bloc; the Indian formulation the cross-link supplies is explicit that it meant independent judgement rather than legal neutrality or equal distance on every issue. ⚠️ Taking Soviet arms while refusing a Soviet alliance is therefore a case of the policy operating, not of it failing. ✅ The evidence that it operated is countable: only three Asian states joined SEATO, which the account itself treats as proof that many states wanted to keep clear of the Cold War and remain uncommitted. ⚠️ On outcomes, the objection is stronger, and it should be conceded: a state's ability to sustain an uncommitted position depended on its internal cohesion, and where that cohesion failed the position collapsed, as in Angola. ⚠️ The defensible verdict is that non-alignment was an effective instrument of sovereignty and a weak instrument of protection.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the impact of the Cold War on the newly independent states of Asia and Africa.
> ⚠️ "The states of the Third World were instruments of superpower rivalry." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Assess non-alignment as a response to bipolarity.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, structure by mechanism rather than by region: reclassification, economic coercion, covert intervention and internationalised civil conflict, each with one case. ⚠️ For the second, open with the SEATO count, because a refusal that can be counted defeats the proposition faster than any amount of argument, and close with the Angolan verdict, which concedes the local conflict and limits the external role to severity. ⚠️ For the third, the neutrality/non-alignment distinction is the conceptual centre, and Austria is the contrast case that makes it visible.

### Traps

- Using "neutral" and "non-aligned" interchangeably. ⚠️ Austrian neutrality was a treaty obligation with legal disabilities; non-alignment was independent judgement without them.
- Treating non-alignment as a single organised movement in this file. ✅ What the sources here record is a widespread wish to stay uncommitted, the SEATO refusal, and the collective self-description at Algiers in 1973.
- Duplicating India's foreign-policy material. ⚠️ The owners cross-link it; a world-history answer uses India as one instance.
- Presenting the choice as binary. ✅ China's 1954 offer of friendship and aid, with agreements signed with India and Burma, was a third option from a communist power that was not the USSR.
- Reducing African and Asian conflicts to proxy wars. ✅ The Angolan verdict concedes that there would have been fighting anyway and limits the external contribution to making it much worse.
- Forgetting the economic grievance. ✅ The new nations resented their economic dependence on the world's wealthy powers, which is not a Cold War grievance.
- Treating Cuba only as a client. ✅ In Angola, Cuba sent troops as a patron.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The war encouraged decolonisation: Japanese defeats of Britain, Holland and France destroyed the tradition of European superiority and invincibility; independence followed, often after a struggle; the 1960s produced a large array of new states.
2. ✅ Algiers, 1973: leaders of many newly emerging nations declared themselves a Third World, wishing to remain neutral or non-aligned in the struggle between communism and capitalism.
3. ✅ They were usually poor and under-developed industrially, intensely suspicious of the motives of both systems, and resentful of economic dependence on the world's wealthy powers.
4. ✅ SEATO, 1954: only Pakistan, Thailand and the Philippines joined; many states wanted to keep clear of the Cold War and remain uncommitted.
5. ✅ 1954: China offered friendship and aid to Third World countries and signed peaceful coexistence agreements with India and Burma.
6. ✅ Khrushchev hoped to win neutral states over to communism by lavish economic aid.
7. ✅ The American criterion applied to Egypt: a state outside the Western alliance buying arms from eastern Europe was just as bad as a communist country.
8. ✅ Economic coercion: the $46 million Aswan grant cancelled in July 1956.
9. ✅ Angola after 1975: the MPLA, UNITA and the FNLA; American backing for the FNLA; Cuban troops for the MPLA; South African and Zairean intervention; the verdict that outside interference made the conflict much worse; the December 1988 deal trading Namibian withdrawal against the departure of 50 000 Cuban troops.
10. ✅ The 1954 India-China agreement on the Tibet region placed the five Panchsheel principles in its preamble.
11. ⚠️ Non-alignment is independent judgement, not legal neutrality, isolation or equal distance on every issue; Austria's treaty neutrality is the contrast.
12. ⚠️ Why the uncommitted mattered: doctrinal demonstration, votes and legitimacy, bases and access, and denial of access to the other side.
13. ⚠️ The conditional verdict: an effective instrument of sovereignty, a weak instrument of protection.

### Concept check

**Question:** Distinguish neutrality from non-alignment, and explain why the difference made non-aligned states more exposed rather than less.

**Model answer:** Neutrality is a contracted-out legal status; non-alignment is a reserved political judgement inside the conflict. ✅ Austria's neutrality under the 1955 treaty came with enforceable disabilities - all occupying troops withdrawn, independence within the 1937 frontiers, no union with Germany, strictly limited armed forces, and a bar on joining either NATO or the European Economic Community - so both sides knew in advance what Austria could and could not do. ✅ Non-alignment, as the India-facing material puts it, meant independent judgement amid bloc rivalry rather than legal neutrality, isolation or equal distance on every issue, which left every individual decision open. ⚠️ That openness is precisely what created the exposure. A neutral state's behaviour is predictable and therefore uninteresting to both blocs; a non-aligned state's behaviour is contestable, and so becomes an object of competition. ✅ The Egyptian case shows the consequence: an arms purchase from Czechoslovakia in September 1955 produced an American classification under which any country outside the Western alliance buying arms from eastern Europe was just as bad as a communist country, and then the cancellation of the Aswan grant in July 1956. ⚠️ A neutral Austria buying arms would have breached a treaty; a non-aligned Egypt buying arms breached nothing and was punished anyway, because in a competition to deny access to the other side an uncommitted state is by definition unresolved.

**Misconception to avoid:** Assuming that non-alignment meant equidistance, so that any aid or arms from one bloc disproves it. The position claimed independent judgement case by case, which is compatible with taking arms from one side while refusing that side's alliance - and the countable proof that the judgement was real is that only three Asian states joined SEATO in 1954.

---

## Lesson 15 - Vietnam: two wars, three presidents, and the limits of military power

Progress: 15/22 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the first war against France, 1946-54, the Vietminh's composition and the decisive Chinese supply from 1950; the Geneva partition at the 17th parallel and the elections that were never held; the four causes of the civil war in the South; the domino theory and Eisenhower's application of it; Kennedy's safe-village failure; Johnson's bombing decision and the more sympathetic recent assessment; the Tet offensive and the American reversal of March 1968; Nixon's Vietnamisation and its failure; the revulsion factors; the recorded costs; and the effects on American policy and on the communist powers
