### Visual: one country, two wars, and a doctrine applied to both

```text
   WAR ONE  1946-54  INDEPENDENCE FROM FRANCE
   Indo-China (Vietnam, Laos, Cambodia) = part of the French empire,
   occupied by Japan during the war
   Resistance to both Japanese and French organised by the League for
   Vietnamese Independence (VIETMINH) under the communist Ho Chi Minh,
   who had spent many years in Russia learning how to organise
   revolutions
   >>> BUT: the Vietminh, though led by communists, was AN ALLIANCE OF
       ALL SHADES OF POLITICAL OPINION THAT WANTED AN END TO FOREIGN
       CONTROL
   1945  Ho declares the whole of Vietnam independent
   1946  the French show no intention of allowing full independence;
         hostilities begin
   WHY THE VIETMINH WON
     · masters of guerrilla tactics
     · massive support from the Vietnamese people
     · the French, still suffering the after-effects of the world war,
       failed to send enough troops
     · THE DECISIVE FACTOR: from 1950 the new Chinese communist
       government of Mao Zedong supplied the rebels with arms and
       equipment
   THE AMERICAN ROLE: seeing the struggle as part of the Cold War and
   the fight against communism, the Americans supplied the French with
   military and economic aid - but it was not enough
   May 1954  French defeat at Dien Bien Phu
   GENEVA AGREEMENT 1954
     · Laos and Cambodia independent
     · Vietnam TEMPORARILY divided at the 17TH PARALLEL
     · Ho's government recognised in the North; a separate government
       in the South for the time being
     · ELECTIONS FOR THE WHOLE COUNTRY IN JULY 1956, after which it
       would be united
   >>> THE ELECTIONS WERE NEVER HELD

   WAR TWO  1961-75  CIVIL WAR AND AMERICAN INTERVENTION
   1955  Ngo Dinh Diem chosen by national referendum in the South;
         refuses to prepare for the all-Vietnam elections. The USA,
         backing him, did not press him FOR FEAR OF A COMMUNIST
         VICTORY IF THE ELECTIONS WENT AHEAD
         >>> "the US attitude was a violation of the Geneva Agreement"
   1960  opposition groups, including many former communist members of
         the Vietminh, form the NATIONAL LIBERATION FRONT; a guerrilla
         campaign begins
   1963  Ngo overthrown and murdered in an army coup; generals follow;
         the guerrilla war continues
   1961-63 KENNEDY: the 'safe village' policy - peasants moved en masse
         into fortified villages to isolate the Vietcong. A FAILURE,
         because most of the Vietcong WERE peasants and simply
         continued to operate inside the villages
   1965  JOHNSON begins bombing North Vietnam, assuming the Vietcong
         were controlled by Ho. Over the next seven years a greater
         tonnage of bombs fell on North Vietnamese cities than fell on
         Germany during the Second World War; over half a million
         American troops arrive in the South
   Feb 1968  THE TET OFFENSIVE captures something like 80 per cent of
         all towns and villages. Much ground is lost later, but it
         convinces many Americans of the hopelessness of the struggle
   31 Mar 1968  Johnson suspends the bombing, freezes troop levels and
         seeks a negotiated peace; Paris talks open in May and run five
         more years
   1969-73  NIXON: 'Vietnamization' - rearm and train the South
         Vietnamese army and withdraw American troops (about half home
         by mid-1971) - while resuming heavy bombing of the North and
         bombing the Ho Chi Minh Trail through Laos and Cambodia
   end 1972  the Vietcong control the entire western half of the country
   1973  the Americans withdraw from the struggle
   1975  South Vietnamese forces rapidly collapse; the whole country is
         united under a communist government; before the end of the year
         Cambodia and Laos have also become communist
```

*The diagram is long because the misconception it corrects is durable. Vietnam is not one American war; it is an anti-colonial war that the United States financed, followed by a civil war that the United States entered, with the hinge between them an election that was cancelled because the wrong side would have won it.*

### 1. The first war, and the three facts that decide how the second should be read

✅ **Fact:** from 1946 until 1954 the Vietnamese were fighting for independence from France. ✅ Indo-China was occupied by the Japanese during the war, and resistance to both Japanese and French was organised by the League for Vietnamese Independence, the Vietminh, led by the communist Ho Chi Minh, who had spent many years in Russia learning how to organise revolutions.

✅ **Fact:** and then the qualification that the account supplies immediately, which changes everything: the Vietminh, though led by communists, was an alliance of all shades of political opinion that wanted an end to foreign control.

✅ **Fact:** at the end of the war in 1945 Ho Chi Minh declared the whole of Vietnam independent; when it became clear that the French had no intention of allowing full independence, hostilities broke out, beginning an eight-year struggle which ended with the French defeat at Dien Bien Phu in May 1954.

✅ **Fact:** the Vietminh were successful partly because they were masters of guerrilla tactics and had massive support from the Vietnamese people, and because the French, still suffering from the after-effects of the world war, failed to send enough troops. ✅ The decisive factor was probably that from 1950 the new Chinese communist government of Mao Zedong supplied the rebels with arms and equipment.

✅ **Fact:** the USA also became involved: seeing the struggle as part of the Cold War and the fight against communism, the Americans supplied the French with military and economic aid, but it was not enough. ✅ However, the Americans were determined to take France's place in order to prevent the spread of communism throughout south-east Asia.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three facts from that passage govern the rest of the lesson. ⚠️ First, the movement was a nationalist coalition under communist leadership, which means the American framing of the struggle as "the fight against communism" was a description of the leadership and not of the movement - a category error with enormous consequences, because a coalition for independence cannot be defeated by defeating communism. ⚠️ Second, the decisive external supply came from China in 1950, not from the Soviet Union, which places Vietnam inside the Asian communist story of Lesson 13 rather than inside the European superpower story. ⚠️ Third, American involvement began in the *first* war, as finance for a European empire's attempt to retain a colony - which is the fact that makes the later American position so difficult, because the power presenting itself as the defender of freedom in Vietnam had funded the colonial war there.

### 2. Geneva, and the election that was not held

✅ **Fact:** by the Geneva Agreement of 1954, Laos and Cambodia were to be independent; Vietnam was temporarily divided into two states at the 17th parallel; Ho Chi Minh's government was recognised in North Vietnam; South Vietnam was to have a separate government for the time being, but elections were to be held in July 1956 for the whole country, which would then become united. ✅ Ho Chi Minh was disappointed at the partition but was confident that the communists would win the national elections. ✅ As it turned out, the elections were never held, and a repeat performance of the Korean situation seemed likely.

✅ **Fact:** the South Vietnamese government under President Ngo Dinh Diem, chosen by a national referendum in 1955, refused to make preparations for the elections for the whole of Vietnam. ✅ The USA, which was backing his regime, did not press him for fear of a communist victory if the elections went ahead. ✅ The account states the consequence flatly: the US attitude was a violation of the Geneva Agreement.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the most important paragraph in the lesson and the one most often missing from scripts. The partition at the 17th parallel was explicitly temporary and was to be resolved by an all-Vietnam election in July 1956; the election was cancelled because the side backing the South expected to lose it; and the account describes the American position as a violation of the agreement. ⚠️ Every subsequent question about the legitimacy of the two governments and of the war traces to that cancellation. ⚠️ It is also the exact parallel with Korea that the account draws: in both cases a temporary administrative line became a frontier because the unification mechanism was abandoned when its outcome became predictable - and in both cases the abandoning party was the one that would have lost.

### 3. The four causes of the civil war in the South

✅ **Fact:** the account gives four. ✅ The first is the refusal to hold the elections, with American acquiescence, and the domino theory that underlay it: President Eisenhower was just as worried as Truman had been about the spread of communism, and he seemed to become obsessed with the domino theory - if there is a line of dominoes standing on end close to each other and one is pushed over, it will knock over the next one in the line, and so on; he thought this could be applied to countries, so that if one country in a region fell to communism, it would quickly knock over all its neighbours.

✅ **Fact:** the second is Ngo's loss of popularity. ✅ Although he began energetically, his government soon lost support: he came from a wealthy Roman Catholic family, whereas three-quarters of the population were Buddhist peasants who thought themselves discriminated against. ✅ They demanded land reform of the type carried out in China and North Vietnam, where land had been taken from wealthy landowners and redistributed among poorer people, and this did not happen in South Vietnam. ✅ Ngo also gained a reputation, perhaps not wholly deserved, for corruption, and he was unpopular with nationalists, who thought he was too much under American influence.

✅ **Fact:** the third is the formation of the opposition. ✅ In 1960 various opposition groups, which included many former communist members of the Vietminh, formed the National Liberation Front. ✅ They demanded a democratic national coalition government which would introduce reforms and negotiate peacefully for a united Vietnam. ✅ A guerrilla campaign began, attacking government officials and buildings; Buddhist monks had their own special brand of protest, committing suicide in public by setting fire to themselves. ✅ Ngo's credibility declined further when he dismissed all criticism, however reasonable, and all opposition as communist inspired - and the account adds the correction: in fact the communists were only one section of the NLF. ✅ He also introduced harsh security measures; he was overthrown and murdered in an army coup in November 1963, after which the country was ruled by a succession of generals, of whom President Nguyen Van Thieu lasted the longest, from 1967 to 1975. ✅ The removal of Ngo left the basic situation unchanged and the guerrilla war continued.

✅ **Fact:** the fourth is the American decision. ✅ When it became clear that Ngo could not cope, the USA decided to increase its military presence; under Eisenhower they had been supporting the regime since 1954 with economic aid and military advisers, and they accepted Ngo's claim that communists were behind all the trouble. ✅ Having failed to defeat communism in North Korea and Cuba, they felt a strong stand must be made, and both Kennedy and his successor Lyndon Johnson were prepared to go further.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second and third causes together constitute the strongest available criticism of the American analysis, and they come from the account rather than from any later polemic. ⚠️ The grievances were religious, agrarian and nationalist: a Catholic elite governing a Buddhist peasant majority that wanted the land reform its northern neighbour had carried out, under a president whom nationalists regarded as an American dependant. ⚠️ Ngo's response was to classify all criticism as communist-inspired, and the account corrects him - the communists were only one section of the National Liberation Front. ⚠️ The United States accepted his classification. ⚠️ That is the same error as in 1946, made larger: a political problem with local causes was diagnosed as a Cold War problem and treated with Cold War instruments, and the instruments could not address the causes.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the domino theory deserves its own sentence because it is the clearest statement in the whole topic of the reasoning that made containment indivisible. ⚠️ Its structure is what matters: dominoes fall *because they are adjacent*, not because of anything internal to them. ⚠️ A theory of that shape removes local conditions from the analysis entirely, which is precisely why it produced interventions in states whose politics had been misdescribed. ⚠️ It also, as the account notes elsewhere, delivered the outcome it feared: after the American withdrawal, Cambodia and Laos became communist before the end of 1975, though for reasons that Lesson 17 shows included American bombing and intervention in both.

### 4. Three presidents, three methods, one result

| President | ✅ Method | ✅ Recorded outcome |
|---|---|---|
| **Kennedy, 1961-63** | The "safe village" policy: local peasants moved en masse into fortified villages, leaving the Vietcong isolated outside | A failure, because most of the Vietcong were peasants, who simply continued to operate inside the villages |
| **Johnson, 1963-69** | Bombing of North Vietnam from 1965, on the assumption that the Vietcong were controlled by Ho Chi Minh; over seven years a greater tonnage of bombs fell on North Vietnamese cities than fell on Germany during the Second World War; over half a million American troops in the South | The Tet offensive of February 1968 captured something like 80 per cent of all towns and villages; though much ground was lost later, it convinced many Americans of the hopelessness of the struggle |
| **Nixon, 1969-74** | "Vietnamization": rearming and training the South Vietnamese army to allow a gradual American withdrawal - about half sent home by mid-1971 - while resuming heavy bombing of the North and bombing the Ho Chi Minh Trail through Laos and Cambodia | It was all to no avail: at the end of 1972 the Vietcong controlled the entire western half of the country |

✅ **Fact:** Johnson faced a situation in which, according to reports from American advisers in 1964, the Vietcong and the NLF controlled about 40 per cent of South Vietnamese villages and the peasant population seemed to support them.

✅ **Fact:** many historians have blamed Johnson for committing the USA so deeply in Vietnam, calling it "Johnson's War". ✅ Recent assessments have taken a more sympathetic view of his predicament: according to Kevin Ruane, "far from being the hawk of legend, historians now tend to see Johnson as a man wracked with uncertainty about which direction to take on Vietnam". ✅ He was afraid that American intervention on a large scale would bring China into the war; his real interest was his campaign for social reform, his "great society" programme; and he inherited the situation from decisions taken by the two previous presidents, being the unfortunate one who felt he had no alternative but to honour their commitments.

✅ **Fact:** on 31 March 1968 Johnson announced that he would suspend the bombing of North Vietnam, freeze troop levels and seek a negotiated peace. ✅ Great pressure had been put on the government by public opinion in the USA to withdraw, and some of his military experts told him that the USA could not win the war at any reasonable cost. ✅ In May, peace talks opened in Paris, but no quick compromise could be reached and the talks went on for another five years.

✅ **Fact:** early in 1969 there were half a million Americans, 50 000 South Koreans and 750 000 South Vietnamese against 450 000 Vietcong plus perhaps 70 000 North Vietnamese.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 1969 force comparison is the single most useful set of figures in this lesson because of what it shows about the relation between force and outcome. The side that lost had, on those numbers, an approximately two-to-one advantage in personnel, together with complete air superiority and the greatest bombing campaign in history to that date. ⚠️ Any answer on the limits of military power should use those numbers rather than the assertion, because the assertion is a commonplace and the arithmetic is evidence. ⚠️ The explanation the account supports is the safe-village failure written large: the opposing force was not separable from the population it came from, so instruments that measured success in territory, tonnage and body counts were measuring the wrong quantities.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Ruane reassessment deserves careful handling, because it is an invitation to a sophisticated point rather than a simple exculpation. ⚠️ A president who privately doubted the war, feared Chinese entry, cared most about domestic reform, and escalated anyway because he felt bound by his predecessors' commitments is evidence for a structural rather than a personal explanation of the war. ⚠️ That is the strongest way to use it: the escalation was produced by the accumulated commitments of a doctrine, not by the disposition of the man executing it.

### 5. The revulsion, and why it mattered

✅ **Fact:** by the end of 1972 Nixon was under pressure both at home and from world opinion to withdraw, and several factors caused a revulsion of feeling against the war. ✅ The terrible bombing of North Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia. ✅ The use of chemicals to destroy jungle foliage and of inflammable napalm jelly, which burned people alive; the after-effects of the chemicals caused many babies to be born deformed and handicapped. ✅ And the deaths of thousands of innocent civilians, the most notorious incident taking place in March 1968 when American soldiers rounded up the inhabitants of a village.

⚠️ **Declared evidence gap.** The pages of the core text carrying the remainder of that incident, the 1973 ceasefire and the 1975 collapse are blank in the copy held here. **No detail of the incident beyond the legible clause is asserted**, and **no ceasefire terms, dates or military details of the final collapse are stated**. For the end of the war this session uses only the account's own chapter summary: ✅ in 1973 the Americans withdrew from the struggle, following which the South Vietnamese forces rapidly collapsed, and the whole country became united under a communist government in 1975; ✅ before the end of that year, neighbouring Cambodia and Laos had also become communist.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the revulsion list is worth including in an answer for a structural reason. It records a war being lost politically in the intervening power's own society while it was still being fought - and the mechanism was the visibility of the methods. ⚠️ This is the sharpest available contrast with Lesson 11's Hungary and with the eastern bloc generally: a domestic constituency capable of ending a war is an asymmetry between the two systems, and it cuts both ways. It constrained American power, and it meant that the costs imposed on a distant population became a political fact in Washington only when they became visible there.

### 6. The costs and the consequences

✅ **Fact:** about 48 000 American servicemen lost their lives, with a further 300 000 wounded. ✅ Around a third of the South was severely damaged by explosives and defoliants. ✅ The problems of reconstruction were enormous, and the new government's policies had unpleasant aspects such as concentration camps for opponents and no freedom of speech.

⚠️ **Fatality figure: two counts, both displayed, neither averaged.** ✅ The core account gives **about 48 000** American servicemen's deaths. ✅ The verified official record - the U.S. Defense Casualty Analysis System file held by the National Archives - records **58 220 U.S. military fatal casualties** of the Vietnam conflict, of which **47 434 are recorded as hostile deaths and 10 786 as non-hostile deaths**. ⚠️ The two figures are therefore counting different objects rather than disagreeing: the account's approximately 48 000 corresponds closely to the hostile-death category, while the official total includes deaths from accidents, illness and other non-hostile causes within the conflict period. ⚠️ **The correct examination practice is to name the category.** Write "about 47 000 American combat deaths, and 58 220 U.S. military fatal casualties in all", or give the total alone; **do not write 48 000 as the total, and do not average or reconcile the two.** ⚠️ This is the single place in this session where a held figure and the verified published record differ, and the numbers discipline set out at the opening requires it to be displayed here rather than smoothed over.

✅ **Fact:** as well as being a blow to American prestige, the failure had a profound effect on American society: involvement in the war was seen in many circles as a terrible mistake, and this, together with the Watergate scandal which forced Nixon to resign, shook confidence in a political system that could allow such things to happen. ✅ War veterans, instead of being treated as heroes, often found themselves shunned. ✅ Future American governments would have to think very carefully before committing the country so deeply in any similar situation.

✅ **Fact:** the war was a victory for the communist world, though both the Russians and Chinese reacted with restraint and did not boast about it to any great extent. ✅ This perhaps indicated that they wished to relax international tensions, though they now had another powerful force on their side in the Vietnamese army.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the restraint of Moscow and Beijing in 1975 is the item that connects this lesson to Lesson 18 and is almost never used. Two communist powers watched the United States suffer its largest defeat of the Cold War and declined to exploit it publicly, because both were by then engaged in their own negotiations with Washington - the Soviet Union in SALT and Helsinki, China in the relationship opened in 1972. ⚠️ Détente was therefore strong enough by 1975 to restrain the rhetoric of both communist powers at the moment of their greatest shared success, which is better evidence for its reality than any treaty text.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and the account's closing clause - that they now had another powerful force on their side in the Vietnamese army - reads very differently once Lesson 13 is in place. Within four years that army invaded Kampuchea and was attacked by China, and Vietnam supported Russia in the ensuing quarrel. ⚠️ The "powerful force on their side" was on one side of a war inside the communist world, which is a reminder that victories are dated by what the victors do next.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the United States did not lose Vietnam; it withdrew from a war it had chosen not to escalate further, and the South fell two years later to a conventional invasion. Calling this a demonstration of the limits of military power confuses political will with military capacity.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is technically defensible and analytically beside the point, and the way to say so is to accept its premise. ⚠️ Yes, the United States retained enormous unused capacity, including the nuclear capacity MacArthur had wanted to use in Korea. ⚠️ But the whole meaning of the Cold War's central constraint is that capacity which cannot be used is not available: Johnson escalated while afraid that large-scale intervention would bring China into the war - the same fear that removed MacArthur in 1951 - which means the unused capacity was unusable for a reason internal to the conflict, not for want of resolve. ⚠️ Second, the objection cannot explain the record inside the usable range: ✅ the safe-village policy failed because the enemy were the peasants being resettled; ✅ a bombing campaign exceeding in tonnage what fell on Germany did not stop the supply or the insurgency; ✅ and at the end of 1972, after four years of Vietnamisation and resumed bombing, the Vietcong controlled the entire western half of the country. ⚠️ The defensible formulation is that military power was sufficient to prevent a communist victory for as long as it was applied and insufficient to produce a political outcome that could survive its withdrawal - which is exactly what "the limits of military power" means.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did the United States intervene in Vietnam, and why did it fail?
> ⚠️ "Vietnam demonstrated the limits of superpower military might." Examine.
> ⚠️ Assess the consequences of the Vietnam War for international relations.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, the two-war structure is the plan, and the hinge is the cancelled election of July 1956 with the account's own verdict that the American attitude was a violation of the Geneva Agreement. ⚠️ For the second, use the 1969 force comparison and the safe-village mechanism rather than the assertion, and concede the unused-capacity objection before answering it. ⚠️ For the third, sort the consequences: domestic - the blow to prestige, the effect on American society, the caution imposed on future governments; regional - Cambodia and Laos communist before the end of 1975; and systemic - the restraint of Moscow and Beijing, which is evidence about détente.

### Traps

- Treating Vietnam as one American war. ✅ There were two wars: independence from France, 1946-54, and the civil war with American intervention, 1961-75.
- Describing the Vietminh as a communist organisation. ✅ It was communist-led and an alliance of all shades of opinion that wanted an end to foreign control.
- Crediting Soviet supply with the French defeat. ✅ The decisive external factor was probably Chinese supply from 1950.
- Omitting the cancelled election. ✅ Geneva provided for all-Vietnam elections in July 1956; Ngo refused to prepare for them and the USA did not press him for fear of a communist victory; the account calls this a violation of the agreement.
- Saying the NLF was the communist party. ✅ It included many former communist Vietminh members, but the communists were only one section of it.
- Reporting Johnson as a simple hawk. ✅ Recent assessments, with Ruane quoted, see him as wracked with uncertainty, afraid of Chinese entry and primarily interested in domestic reform.
- Treating Tet as a military victory for the Vietcong. ✅ Much ground was lost later; its effect was to convince many Americans of the hopelessness of the struggle.
- Omitting the human cost, or giving the American fatality figure without its category. ✅ About four million Korean dead and five million homeless in Lesson 9; for Vietnam, the account's about 48 000 is the hostile-death count and 58 220 is the verified official total.
- Supplying details of the 1973 ceasefire or the 1975 collapse. ⚠️ Those pages are blank here; only the chapter summary's outline is used.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Indo-China - Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia - was French and was occupied by Japan; resistance was organised by the Vietminh under Ho Chi Minh, who had spent many years in Russia learning to organise revolutions, and it was communist-led but an alliance of all shades of opinion wanting an end to foreign control.
2. ✅ 1945 Ho declares independence; 1946 hostilities begin; May 1954 the French are defeated at Dien Bien Phu - through guerrilla mastery, massive popular support, insufficient French troops and, probably decisively, Chinese arms and equipment from 1950, while the USA financed the French but not enough.
3. ✅ Geneva 1954: Laos and Cambodia independent; Vietnam temporarily divided at the 17th parallel; all-Vietnam elections in July 1956 - which Ngo Dinh Diem, chosen by referendum in 1955, refused to prepare for, with the USA declining to press him for fear of a communist victory, in what the account calls a violation of the Geneva Agreement.
4. ✅ Eisenhower's domino theory: one country falling to communism would quickly knock over its neighbours.
5. ✅ Ngo's unpopularity - a wealthy Roman Catholic family against three-quarters Buddhist peasants, no land reform of the northern or Chinese kind, a reputation for corruption, nationalist resentment of American influence - produced the National Liberation Front of 1960, including many former communist Vietminh members, demanding a democratic coalition and peaceful negotiation for unity, with Buddhist monks protesting by public self-immolation; the communists were only one section of it.
6. ✅ November 1963: Ngo overthrown and murdered; generals rule, Nguyen Van Thieu longest, 1967-75; Kennedy's safe villages had already failed because most Vietcong were peasants.
7. ✅ 1964 advisers' report: the Vietcong and NLF controlled about 40 per cent of villages with apparent peasant support; Johnson bombs from 1965, and over seven years a greater tonnage falls on North Vietnamese cities than fell on Germany in the Second World War, with over half a million American troops in the South.
8. ✅ February 1968 Tet: something like 80 per cent of towns and villages captured; ground lost later; American opinion turns. 31 March 1968: bombing suspended, troop levels frozen, negotiation sought; Paris talks from May run five more years.
9. ✅ Early 1969: half a million Americans, 50 000 South Koreans and 750 000 South Vietnamese against 450 000 Vietcong and perhaps 70 000 North Vietnamese; Vietnamisation follows, with resumed bombing of the North and of the Ho Chi Minh Trail through Laos and Cambodia, and by the end of 1972 the Vietcong hold the entire western half of the country.
10. ✅ Revulsion factors: the bombing of North Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia; defoliants and napalm with birth defects among the after-effects; the deaths of thousands of civilians.
11. ✅ 1973 American withdrawal; 1975 the South collapses and the country is united under a communist government; Cambodia and Laos communist before the end of the year.
12. ✅ Costs: the account's about 48 000 American dead - which corresponds to the hostile-death category rather than the total - against the verified official record of 58 220 U.S. military fatal casualties, comprising 47 434 hostile and 10 786 non-hostile deaths; 300 000 wounded; about a third of the South severely damaged; concentration camps for opponents and no freedom of speech under the new government.
13. ✅ Consequences: a blow to prestige; a profound effect on American society, compounded by Watergate; veterans shunned; future governments cautious; a communist victory received with restraint by Moscow and Beijing.

### Concept check

**Question:** Identify the single decision that did most to determine the character of the second Vietnam war, and explain why.

**Model answer:** The cancellation of the all-Vietnam elections due in July 1956. ✅ The Geneva Agreement of 1954 had divided Vietnam only temporarily at the 17th parallel, recognising Ho Chi Minh's government in the North and providing a separate southern government for the time being, with elections for the whole country in July 1956 after which it would be united - and Ho accepted the partition because he was confident the communists would win those elections. ✅ Ngo Dinh Diem refused to prepare for them, and the USA, which was backing his regime, did not press him for fear of a communist victory if the elections went ahead; the account states that the American attitude was a violation of the Geneva Agreement. ⚠️ Three consequences flow from that single decision. It converted a temporary line into a contested frontier, exactly as in Korea. It removed the only agreed peaceful route to unification, so that the objective the North had accepted electoral means to achieve could afterwards be pursued only by force. ⚠️ And it placed the United States in the position of defending a government whose claim to represent the South had been protected from the test that the agreement prescribed - which is the origin of the legitimacy problem that no amount of aid, resettlement, bombing or Vietnamisation could repair.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the war as a straightforward case of communist aggression from the North against an independent South. The South's separate existence was a temporary arrangement under an agreement that the account says the American position violated, and the opposition that took up arms in 1960 formed a National Liberation Front of which the communists were only one section.

---

## Lesson 16 - The Middle East: why the proxy-war label is insufficient

Progress: 16/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the four explanatory layers and what each misses alone; the 1948-49 war and the refugee question; Suez as the Cold War entry point already taught; the build-up to 1967 and the Six-Day War's territorial transformation; the Yom Kippur War, superpower intervention and the oil weapon; Camp David and what it did not settle; the Iran-Iraq War and the Gulf War as the sequel that outlasted the Arab-Israeli cycle; the intifada; and the disciplined formulation for a Cold War answer

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - the core account's Middle East chapter on the Six-Day War of 1967 and its build-up and results, the Yom Kippur War of 1973 and the oil weapon, and Camp David and the Egyptian-Israeli peace of 1978-79 with its aftermath and the intifada; the Suez section was read for Lesson 11; the page carrying part of the Oslo material is degraded in the copy held here and is not used
🔍 CA search: not run for this lesson - the single topic-level query is recorded in Lesson 1
📰 CA found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four explanatory layers, and the sequence they have to explain

```text
   THE SEQUENCE
   1948-49  Arab-Israeli War: Israel survives; the Palestinian refugee
            problem and Arab-Israeli hostility become enduring regional
            issues; the Arab states do not sign a peace treaty and
            refuse recognition, regarding the war as the first round
   1956     SUEZ: canal nationalisation, the British-French-Israeli
            attack, US and USSR pressure (Lesson 11)
   1967     SIX-DAY WAR: Israel occupies Sinai, Gaza, the West Bank,
            East Jerusalem and the Golan Heights
   1973     YOM KIPPUR WAR: Egyptian and Syrian attack; Israel recovers
            and crosses the canal; both superpowers intervene
            diplomatically; the oil weapon and the OPEC price rise
   1978-79  CAMP DAVID: Egypt and Israel make peace; the Palestinian
            question remains unresolved
   1980-88  IRAN-IRAQ WAR
   1987     the INTIFADA begins in the refugee camps of Gaza and the
            West Bank
   1990-91  GULF WAR
                                |
   FOUR LAYERS OF EXPLANATION - AND WHAT EACH MISSES ALONE
   +------------------+-------------------------+---------------------+
   | LAYER            | WHAT IT CAPTURES        | WHAT IT MISSES      |
   +------------------+-------------------------+---------------------+
   | Cold War rivalry | arms, diplomacy and     | local actors made   |
   |                  | crisis management by    | choices and         |
   |                  | the USA and USSR        | sometimes           |
   |                  |                         | constrained their   |
   |                  |                         | patrons             |
   +------------------+-------------------------+---------------------+
   | Decolonisation / | Suez, anti-imperial     | does not by itself  |
   | Arab nationalism | sovereignty, Nasserism  | explain Israeli     |
   |                  |                         | security or         |
   |                  |                         | Palestinian claims  |
   +------------------+-------------------------+---------------------+
   | Competing        | Israeli state security  | must also include   |
   | national         | and Palestinian self-   | interstate Arab     |
   | projects         | determination           | politics and        |
   |                  |                         | territorial         |
   |                  |                         | occupation          |
   +------------------+-------------------------+---------------------+
   | Oil and          | the Suez Canal, Gulf    | economic strategy   |
   | strategic routes | security, the 1973 oil  | cannot replace      |
   |                  | shock                   | political causation |
   +------------------+-------------------------+---------------------+
                                |
   THE OWNER'S CONCLUSION, TO BE USED AS WRITTEN
   "the Cold War internationalized Middle Eastern conflicts, but did
    not create all of their local causes"
```

*The table is the lesson. A candidate who can name four layers and state what each one fails to explain has already produced the structure that a "critically examine" directive on this region is looking for.*

### 1. Why this material sits inside a Cold War session at all

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Middle East appears here for one reason and should be used for one reason: it is the region in which the temptation to write a proxy-war narrative is strongest and the evidence against it is clearest. ⚠️ The boundary is therefore tight. The region's own history - its state formation, its internal politics, its social and economic transformation - belongs elsewhere; what belongs here is the *interaction*, and the exact question is how much of the conflict the superpower rivalry explains.

✅ **Fact:** the advanced owner sets out the four layers reproduced in the visual, and its historiographical caution is the sentence to carry into an answer: avoid writing either that "ancient hatred" made conflict inevitable or that superpowers manufactured it from outside; the stronger explanation combines post-imperial state formation, nationalism, security dilemmas, territorial control and external intervention.

✅ **Fact:** the basic owner adds the causal diagram in its own terms: post-imperial borders and competing national projects, plus Palestinian dispossession and Israeli security fears, plus Arab nationalism, oil and superpower intervention, produce recurrent wars, partial settlements and unresolved statehood questions.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** notice the position of superpower intervention in that formula. It is one term in a three-term sum, and it appears alongside oil and Arab nationalism rather than above them. ⚠️ That placement is the argument: the Cold War is an input to the regional system, not the system itself.

### 2. 1948-49, and the two problems it created

✅ **Fact:** the owner records that in the Arab-Israeli War of 1948-49 Israel survived, and that the Palestinian refugee problem and Arab-Israeli hostility became enduring regional issues.

✅ **Fact:** the core account adds the political consequence that governs the next two decades: the Arab states did not sign a peace treaty at the end of the 1948-49 war and were still refusing to give Israel official recognition; they would not recognise the legality of Israel and regarded that war as only the first round in the struggle to destroy Israel and liberate Palestine.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** two things follow that are essential to a disciplined answer. ⚠️ First, the absence of a peace treaty means that the region's subsequent wars are not separate conflicts but resumptions, which is why the sequence in the visual runs continuously rather than as a set of episodes. ⚠️ Second, the refugee problem is generated at the outset and is not resolved by any of the settlements that follow, which is why the sequence ends with the intifada in the camps rather than with Camp David.

### 3. The build-up to 1967, and the war's territorial transformation

✅ **Fact:** in 1967 the Arab states joined together again in a determined attempt to destroy Israel, the lead being taken by Iraq, Syria and Egypt. ✅ In Iraq a new government came to power in 1963 influenced by the ideas of the Ba'ath Party in neighbouring Syria; supporters of the Ba'ath, meaning "resurrection", believed in Arab independence and unity and were left-wing in outlook, wanting social reform and better treatment for ordinary people; they were prepared to co-operate with Egypt, and in June 1967 their president, Aref, announced: "Our goal is clear - to wipe Israel off the map."

✅ **Fact:** in Syria political upheavals brought the Ba'ath Party to power in 1966; it supported El Fatah, the Palestinian Liberation Movement, a more effective guerrilla force than the fedayeen. ✅ Founded in 1957, Fatah eventually became the core section of the Palestine Liberation Organization, with Yasser Arafat as one of its leaders. ✅ The Syrians also began to bombard Jewish settlements from the Golan Heights, which overlooked the frontier.

✅ **Fact:** in Egypt, Nasser was immensely popular because of his leadership of the Arab world and his attempts to improve conditions at home with socialist policies, including limiting the size of farms to 100 acres and redistributing surplus land to peasants; attempts were made to industrialise, with over a thousand new factories built, almost all under government control; and the Aswan Dam project was vitally important, providing electricity and water for irrigating an extra million acres of land - after early delays at the time of the Suez War in 1956, work eventually got under way and the project was completed in 1971. ✅ With all going well at home and the prospect of effective help from Iraq and Syria, Nasser decided that the time was ripe for another attack on Israel; he began to move troops up to the frontier in Sinai and closed the Gulf of Aqaba.

✅ **Fact:** the Russians encouraged Egypt and Syria and kept up a flow of anti-Israeli propaganda, because Israel was being supported by the USA; their aim was to increase their influence in the Middle East at the expense of the Americans and Israelis, and they hinted that they would send help if war came. ✅ Syria, Jordan and Lebanon also massed troops along their frontiers with Israel, while contingents from Iraq, Saudi Arabia and Algeria joined them; Israel's situation seemed hopeless.

✅ **Fact:** the Israelis decided that the best policy was to attack first rather than wait to be defeated. ✅ They launched a series of devastating air strikes which destroyed most of the Egyptian air force on the ground on 5 June; Israeli troops moved with remarkable speed, capturing the Gaza Strip and the whole of Sinai from Egypt, the rest of Jerusalem and the West Bank from Jordan, and the Golan Heights from Syria; the Arabs had no choice but to accept a UN ceasefire order on 10 June, and it was all over in less than a week. ✅ The reasons for the spectacular Israeli success were the slow and ponderous Arab troop build-up which gave the Israelis plenty of warning, Israeli superiority in the air, and inadequate Arab preparations and communications.

✅ **Fact:** the results. ✅ For the Israelis it was a spectacular success: this time they ignored a UN order to return the captured territory, which acted as a series of buffer zones and meant that Israel would be much easier to defend; however, it brought a new problem - how to deal with about a million extra Arabs who now found themselves under Israeli rule, many of them living in the refugee camps set up in 1948 on the West Bank and in the Gaza Strip. ✅ It was a humiliation for the Arab states and especially for Nasser, who now realised that the Arabs needed outside help if they were ever to free Palestine; the Russians had been a disappointment and had sent no help, and to improve their relations with Egypt and Syria they began to supply them with modern weapons.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Soviet role in 1967 is the passage that most rewards close reading, because it contains both halves of the proxy question. ✅ Before the war Moscow encouraged, propagandised and hinted at help; ✅ during the war it sent none; ✅ after the war it re-armed its clients to repair the relationship. ⚠️ That is a patron whose influence was real in the phase where it cost nothing, absent in the phase where it would have cost something, and reactive in the phase after. ⚠️ A candidate who states that sequence has demonstrated the limits of patronage more effectively than any general claim about proxy wars, and has also explained why Egypt's president concluded that the Arabs needed outside help - a conclusion about his patron's unreliability, not about his own weakness.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the war's second result is the one that carries the region into the present and it must be stated in the account's own terms: about a million additional Arabs came under Israeli rule, many of them already refugees from 1948, in territories the Israeli government declined a UN order to return. ⚠️ That is the origin of the occupation question, and no Cold War category explains it.

### 4. 1973, the superpowers and the oil weapon

✅ **Fact:** several things combined to cause the renewed conflict. ✅ Pressure was brought to bear on the Arab states by the PLO under Yasser Arafat for further action; when very little happened, a more extreme group within the PLO, the Popular Front for the Liberation of Palestine, embarked on a series of terrorist attacks to draw world attention to the grave injustice being done to the Arabs of Palestine, hijacking airliners and flying three of them to Amman, where they were blown up in 1970; this was embarrassing for King Hussein of Jordan, who now favoured a negotiated peace, and in September 1970 he expelled all PLO members based in Jordan; terrorist attacks continued, reaching a horrifying climax when some members of the Israeli team were murdered at the 1972 Munich Olympics.

✅ **Fact:** Anwar Sadat, president of Egypt since Nasser's death in 1970, was becoming increasingly convinced of the need for a negotiated peace settlement with Israel; he was worried that PLO terrorism would turn world opinion against the Palestinian cause. ✅ He was prepared to work either with the USA or with the USSR, but hoped to win American support for the Arabs so that the Americans would persuade the Israelis to agree to a settlement; however, the Americans refused to get involved. ✅ Sadat, together with Syria, therefore decided to attack Israel again, hoping that this would force the Americans to act as mediators; the Egyptians were feeling more confident because they now had modern Russian weapons and their army had been trained by Russian experts.

✅ **Fact:** the war began on 6 October 1973, with Egyptian and Syrian forces attacking early on the feast of Yom Kippur, a Jewish religious festival, hoping to catch the Israelis off guard. ✅ After some early Arab successes the Israelis, using mainly American weapons, were able to turn the tables; they succeeded in hanging on to all the territory captured in 1967 and even crossed the Suez Canal into Egypt. ✅ In one sense Sadat's plan had been successful: both the USA and the USSR decided it was time to intervene to try to bring about a peace settlement, and acting with UN co-operation they organised a ceasefire which both sides accepted.

✅ **Fact:** the end of the war brought a glimmer of hope for some sort of permanent peace: Egyptian and Israeli leaders came together, though not in the same room, in Geneva, and the Israelis agreed to move their troops back from the Suez Canal, which had been closed since the 1967 war, enabling the Egyptians to clear and open it in 1975, though not to Israeli ships.

✅ **Fact:** an important development during the war was that the Arab oil-producing states tried to bring pressure to bear on the USA and on western European states friendly to Israel by reducing oil supplies; this caused serious oil shortages, especially in Europe. ✅ At the same time producers, well aware that oil supplies were not unlimited, looked on their action as a way of preserving resources, and with this in mind the Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries began to raise oil prices substantially; this contributed to inflation and caused an energy crisis in the world's industrial nations.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** 1973 is the war that most decisively refutes the proxy reading, and it does so through Sadat's stated purpose. ✅ He wanted an American-brokered settlement, was refused, and attacked in order to compel the mediation he had been denied - using Soviet weapons and Soviet training to produce an American diplomatic intervention. ⚠️ That is a client using one patron's material to force the other patron's engagement, which inverts the proxy relationship entirely. ⚠️ And it worked in the limited sense the account records: both superpowers decided it was time to intervene, and a ceasefire followed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the oil dimension is the point at which the region acted on the world economy rather than the reverse, and its two stated motives should be kept separate, because scripts habitually merge them. ✅ The supply reduction was political pressure on states friendly to Israel; ✅ the OPEC price rise was a conservation and revenue decision by producers aware that supplies were not unlimited. ⚠️ The first is a weapon in a regional conflict; the second is a structural change in the world economy, whose consequences - inflation and an energy crisis in the industrial nations - belong to the world-economy owner and are connected here only by date and by cause.

### 5. Camp David, and what peace did not settle

✅ **Fact:** Sadat had become convinced that Israel could not be destroyed by force and that it was foolish to keep wasting Egypt's resources in fruitless wars; but it took great courage to be the first Arab leader to meet the Israelis face to face, since even talking with Israeli leaders meant conceding that Egypt recognised the lawful existence of the state of Israel, and he knew that the PLO and the more aggressive Arab states, Iraq and Syria, would bitterly resent any approach. ✅ In spite of the dangers, Sadat offered to go to Israel and talk to the Knesset.

✅ **Fact:** the Israelis were suffering economic problems, partly because of their enormous defence expenditure and partly because of a world recession; the USA was pressing them to settle their differences with at least some of the Arabs. ✅ They accepted Sadat's offer; he visited Israel in November 1977 and Menachem Begin, the Israeli prime minister, visited Egypt the following month. ✅ President Carter played a vital role in setting up formal negotiations between the two sides, which began in September 1978 at Camp David.

✅ **Fact:** with Carter acting as intermediary, the talks led to a peace treaty signed in Washington in March 1979. ✅ The state of war that had existed between Egypt and Israel since 1948 was ended; Israel promised to withdraw its troops from Sinai; Egypt promised not to attack Israel again and guaranteed to supply her with oil from the recently opened wells in southern Sinai; and Israeli ships could use the Suez Canal.

✅ **Fact:** the treaty was condemned by the PLO and most other Arab states, except Sudan and Morocco, and there was clearly a long way to go before similar treaties could be signed by Israel with Syria and Jordan. ✅ World opinion began to move against Israel and to accept that the PLO had a good case; but when the USA tried to bring the PLO and Israel together in an international conference, the Israelis would not co-operate. ✅ In November 1980 Begin announced that Israel would never return the Golan Heights to Syria, not even in exchange for a peace treaty, and would never allow the West Bank to become part of an independent Palestinian state, which would be a mortal threat to Israel's existence. ✅ At the same time resentment mounted among West Bank Arabs at the Israeli policy of establishing Jewish settlements on land owned by Arabs.

✅ **Fact:** the peace also seemed threatened for a time when President Sadat was assassinated by some extremist Muslim soldiers while watching a military parade in October 1981; they believed that he had betrayed the Arab and Muslim cause by doing a deal with the Israelis. ✅ However, Sadat's successor, Hosni Mubarak, announced that he would continue the Camp David agreement.

✅ **Fact:** for most of the 1980s the Arab-Israeli feud was overshadowed by the Iran-Iraq War, which occupied much of the Arab world's attention. ✅ But beginning in December 1987 there were massive demonstrations by Palestinians living in the refugee camps of the Gaza Strip and the West Bank; the intifada, meaning "shaking off", was a long campaign of civil disobedience involving strikes, non-payment of taxes and an attempt to boycott Israeli products, protesting against repressive Israeli policies and the behaviour of Israeli troops in the camps and the occupied territories; an Israeli clampdown failed to quell it and it continued for over three years, with the methods used earning UN and worldwide condemnation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Camp David is the clearest demonstration in the region of the difference between ending a war and settling a conflict. ✅ It ended the state of war between the two strongest belligerents of 1948, 1956, 1967 and 1973, removed Sinai from the dispute, and opened the canal to Israeli shipping - substantial and durable achievements, sustained by Sadat's successor after his assassination. ⚠️ It left untouched every element of the question that generated the conflict: the occupied West Bank and Golan, the settlements, the refugees and Palestinian statehood. ⚠️ The intifada beginning in December 1987 in the camps of 1948 is the evidence that the unsettled element was the load-bearing one.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Cold War content of Camp David is thin and should be stated as thin. ✅ An American president acted as intermediary and American pressure moved the Israeli government; the Soviet Union does not appear in the account of the settlement at all. ⚠️ That absence is itself the finding: by 1978-79 the region's principal diplomatic process had one external sponsor, which is a measure of how far the competition of 1955-73 had already been decided in that theatre.

### 6. The sequel that outlasted the cycle

✅ **Fact:** the owner's table records the Iran-Iraq War of 1980-88 and the Gulf War of 1990-91 under a single heading: regional rivalry, oil security and outside intervention outlasted the classic Arab-Israeli war cycle.

✅ **Fact:** the core account's material used in Lesson 17 supplies the Cold War-facing detail on Iraq: from 1979 the USA financed and supplied Saddam Hussein, backing him against the new anti-American government in Iran; and after the long and inconclusive Iran-Iraq War of 1980-88, Saddam's forces invaded and conquered Kuwait in August 1990, only to be driven out again by UN forces, of which by far the largest contingent was the American one. ⚠️ **Dating correction, carried from Lesson 17:** the account's phrase that Saddam "became Iraqi leader in 1968" is not used here; the verified position is that he was prominent in the Ba'ath regime from its seizure of power in July 1968 and became **President of Iraq on 16 July 1979**.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sequence has a specific value for a Cold War answer and should be used only for that value. ⚠️ A state armed and financed by the United States from 1979 as an instrument against a regional adversary invaded a neighbour in 1990 and was expelled by a coalition led by its former supplier, at the moment the Cold War was ending. ⚠️ That is the clearest single illustration that the instruments created during the rivalry outlived it, and it belongs in any answer on the consequences of the Cold War's end - a theme the end-of-Cold-War owner develops and this session does not.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the four-layer model is a way of avoiding a judgement. Every complex conflict has multiple causes; saying so is not analysis. The candidate is being asked how important the Cold War was, and "one factor among four" is a non-answer.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is right that layering without weighting is evasion, so the weighting must be supplied - and the evidence supports a specific and defensible one. ⚠️ The Cold War explains the *armament* of the conflicts and much of their *management*: ✅ Egyptian weapons came from the eastern bloc from September 1955, Israeli weapons were mainly American by 1973, ✅ the Russians encouraged and propagandised before 1967, ✅ and the ceasefires of 1956 and 1973 were produced by superpower intervention. ⚠️ It does not explain their *occurrence*: ✅ the absence of a peace treaty after 1948-49 and the refusal of recognition, ✅ the refugee problem created in 1948, ✅ the Ba'athist and Nasserite programmes, ✅ the Syrian bombardment from the Golan, ✅ Israeli security calculations and the decision to strike first in 1967, and ✅ the occupation of a million additional Arabs after it are all regional in origin. ⚠️ The defensible verdict is therefore precise rather than balanced: superpower rivalry determined how the wars were fought and how they were stopped, and the region determined that they happened. ⚠️ That is a weighting, not a list.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why can the Arab-Israeli conflict not be explained as a Cold War proxy struggle alone?
> ⚠️ Assess the interaction of regional causes and superpower rivalry in the Middle East after 1948.
> ⚠️ Examine the significance of the oil weapon in international relations in the 1970s.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first two, use the four-layer table as the spine and the weighting in section 7 as the verdict; the two strongest individual pieces of evidence are the Soviet non-appearance in 1967 after encouraging the build-up, and Sadat's use of Soviet weapons in 1973 to compel American mediation. ⚠️ For the third, keep the two motives separate - political pressure on states friendly to Israel, and a producers' conservation-and-revenue decision - and hand the macroeconomic consequences to the world-economy owner in one clause.

### Traps

- Writing that the superpowers created the Arab-Israeli conflict. ✅ The refugee problem and the refusal of recognition date from 1948-49, before either had a client in the region.
- Saying the USSR fought alongside the Arab states in 1967. ✅ It encouraged, propagandised and hinted at help, sent none, and re-armed Egypt and Syria afterwards.
- Treating 1973 as a straightforward Arab defeat. ✅ Sadat's purpose was to compel American mediation, and both superpowers did decide it was time to intervene.
- Merging the oil embargo with the OPEC price rise. ✅ The first was political pressure; the second was a conservation and revenue decision by producers.
- Presenting Camp David as a comprehensive settlement. ✅ It ended the Egyptian-Israeli state of war and left the West Bank, the Golan, the settlements and Palestinian statehood unresolved.
- Omitting the intifada. ✅ It began in December 1987 in the camps of 1948 and ran for over three years.
- Treating the Iran-Iraq and Gulf wars as part of the Arab-Israeli cycle. ✅ The owner records them as a different rivalry that outlasted it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Four layers: Cold War rivalry, decolonisation and Arab nationalism, competing national projects, and oil and strategic routes - each with a stated blind spot.
2. ✅ 1948-49: Israel survives; the refugee problem and Arab-Israeli hostility become enduring; no peace treaty, no recognition, and the war regarded as a first round.
3. ✅ 1967 build-up: Ba'athist Iraq and Syria, Aref's June 1967 statement, Syrian support for Fatah and bombardment from the Golan, Nasser's domestic programme and the Aswan Dam completed in 1971, troops to Sinai and the closure of the Gulf of Aqaba.
4. ✅ Soviet conduct: encouragement, anti-Israeli propaganda and hints of help, aimed at increasing influence at American and Israeli expense.
5. ✅ 5-10 June 1967: Israeli pre-emptive air strikes; Gaza and Sinai from Egypt, the rest of Jerusalem and the West Bank from Jordan, the Golan from Syria; a UN ceasefire; success explained by the slow Arab build-up, Israeli air superiority and inadequate Arab preparation and communications.
6. ✅ Results: buffer zones and a UN order ignored; about a million extra Arabs under Israeli rule, many in the camps of 1948; humiliation for Nasser; no Soviet help, followed by modern Soviet weapons for Egypt and Syria.
7. ✅ 1970-72: PLO pressure, PFLP hijackings to Amman, King Hussein's expulsion of the PLO in September 1970, and the Munich murders of 1972.
8. ✅ Sadat wanted an American-brokered settlement, was refused, and attacked on 6 October 1973 with Syria to compel mediation, using modern Russian weapons and Russian-trained forces.
9. ✅ Israel recovered with mainly American weapons, held the 1967 territory and crossed the canal; both superpowers intervened and a ceasefire followed with UN co-operation; Geneva talks and the canal reopened in 1975, though not to Israeli ships.
10. ✅ The oil weapon: supply reductions against states friendly to Israel, and a substantial OPEC price rise motivated also by conservation; inflation and an energy crisis in industrial nations.
11. ✅ Camp David: Sadat's Knesset visit in November 1977, Begin's visit the following month, Carter's mediation from September 1978, the treaty of March 1979 ending the state of war, Israeli withdrawal from Sinai, an Egyptian guarantee of oil and canal access for Israeli ships.
12. ✅ Condemned by the PLO and most Arab states except Sudan and Morocco; Begin's November 1980 refusal on the Golan and the West Bank; settlements on Arab-owned land; Sadat assassinated in October 1981; Mubarak continues the agreement.
13. ✅ December 1987: the intifada begins in the camps of Gaza and the West Bank - strikes, non-payment of taxes, a boycott - and continues for over three years.
14. ✅ Iran-Iraq 1980-88 and the Gulf War of 1990-91: regional rivalry, oil security and outside intervention outlasting the Arab-Israeli cycle; American support for Saddam from 1979; the 1990 invasion of Kuwait reversed by UN forces with by far the largest contingent American.
15. ⚠️ The verdict: superpower rivalry determined how the wars were fought and stopped; the region determined that they happened.

### Concept check

**Question:** Use the Soviet role in 1967 and Sadat's decision in 1973 to show what "proxy war" gets wrong about the Middle East.

**Model answer:** Together they show the client directing the patron at least as often as the reverse. ✅ Before June 1967 the Russians encouraged Egypt and Syria, maintained a flow of anti-Israeli propaganda because Israel was supported by the USA, aimed to increase their influence at American and Israeli expense, and hinted that they would send help if war came. ✅ When war came they sent none, and Nasser's recorded conclusion was that the Arabs needed outside help if they were ever to free Palestine - a judgement about the unreliability of his patron; ✅ the Russians then supplied modern weapons to Egypt and Syria in order to repair the relationship. ✅ In 1973 Sadat, having failed to obtain American mediation because the Americans refused to get involved, attacked with Syria specifically in order to force the Americans to act as mediators, using modern Russian weapons and an army trained by Russian experts - and the account records that in one sense the plan succeeded, since both the USA and the USSR decided it was time to intervene and organised a ceasefire. ⚠️ A proxy war is a conflict fought by clients on behalf of patrons; here the client fought to compel his adversary's patron to act, with his own patron's equipment, against his own patron's preference for restraint. ⚠️ The accurate description is that superpower involvement supplied the weapons and shaped the endings, while the decisions to fight were taken in Cairo, Damascus and Jerusalem.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the superpowers were therefore marginal. Their arms transfers made the wars possible at the scale they were fought, their combined pressure ended two of them, and American mediation produced the only interstate peace of the period - which is why the correct verdict weights their role by function rather than dismissing it.

---

## Lesson 17 - The intervention record: Chile, south-east Asia, Iran and Iraq

Progress: 17/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the recorded technique set and the admitted breadth of American activity; the named authors who documented it; Chile from Frei to Allende to Pinochet, including the five causes of the overthrow and the CIA's role; Laos, Cambodia and Thailand; Iran and the restored Shah; Iraq from Kassem to Saddam; the evidential rules that make this material usable; and the comparison with Soviet conduct inside its own sphere
