*The right-hand column of the outcome box is the analytical payload. In three of the four cases the intervention produced, after a delay, the outcome it had been designed to prevent - which is a finding about the instrument, not a moral observation.*

### 1. What the account actually says, and why the wording matters

✅ **Fact:** Vietnam, Cuba and Chile were not the only countries in which the USA intervened during the first half of the Cold War. ✅ Working through the Central Intelligence Agency, the American State Department was active in an astonishing number of states in the cause of preserving freedom and human rights, and above all, preventing the spread of communism.

✅ **Fact:** often the regimes that were labelled as communist and targeted for removal were simply pursuing policies which went against American interests. ✅ US activities were carried out sometimes in secret, leaving the American people largely unaware of what was going on, or, as in the case of major military interventions, were presented as necessary surgical actions against the cancer of communism. ✅ Techniques included attempts to carry out assassinations, rigging of elections, organising and financing acts of terrorism, economic destabilisation and, in the last resort, full-scale military intervention.

✅ **Fact:** the account names its sources for this material: several former members of the State Department and the CIA, for example William Blum and Richard Agee, and a number of other writers, including the linguistics expert Noam Chomsky, have produced detailed accounts of how the leaders of the USA tried to build up their influence and power in the world by exercising control over such countries as Iran, Guatemala, Costa Rica, Indonesia, Guyana, Iraq, Cambodia, Laos, Ecuador, the Congo or Zaire, Brazil, the Dominican Republic, Ghana, Uruguay, Bolivia, East Timor, Nicaragua and many more. ✅ The account states that there is insufficient space to examine all these cases and offers a few examples.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** three features of that passage determine how it can be used in an examination, and getting them right is the difference between a strong answer and an unusable one. ⚠️ First, the list of states is a list of states *written about by named authors*, not a list of proven operations, and the account presents it that way; an answer should reproduce the attribution rather than convert the list into a set of findings. ⚠️ Second, the techniques are stated as a set and are therefore assertible as a set. ⚠️ Third, the criterion sentence - that the targeted regimes were often simply pursuing policies which went against American interests - is the analytical claim, and it is the one to build on, because it identifies a systematic slippage between the stated purpose of containment and its operational criterion.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** that slippage is the theme of this lesson and links it back to Lessons 11 and 14. Egypt in 1955 was not communist and was reclassified for buying arms from eastern Europe. Allende in 1970 was a Marxist elected under a democratic constitution who believed communism could succeed without violent revolution. ⚠️ In both cases the operative test was alignment with American interests rather than adherence to communism - which is precisely what the account says.

### 2. Chile: the fullest case the account gives

✅ **Fact:** in September 1970 Salvador Allende, a Marxist doctor of medicine from a middle-class background, won the presidential election as leader of a left-wing coalition of communists, socialists, radicals and social democrats called Unidad Popular. ✅ It was a narrow victory, with Allende winning 36 per cent of the votes against the 35 per cent of his nearest rival - but enough to make him president, the world's first Marxist leader to be voted into power through a democratic election. ✅ The account explains why it examines the case in detail: although it lasted only three years, Allende's government is still the only one of its kind, and it shows the sort of problems likely to be faced by a Marxist government trying to function within a democratic system.

✅ **Fact:** Chile, unlike most other South American states, had a tradition of democracy, with three main parties or groups - Unidad Popular on the left, the Christian Democrats also left-inclined, and the National Party, a liberal-conservative coalition. ✅ The army played little part in politics, and the democratic constitution, similar to that of the USA except that the president could not stand for re-election immediately, was usually respected.

✅ **Fact:** the 1964 election was won by Eduardo Frei of the Christian Democrats, who believed in social reform. ✅ Frei began vigorously: inflation was brought down from 38 per cent to 25 per cent, the rich were made to pay their taxes instead of evading them, 360 000 new houses were built, the number of schools was more than doubled, and limited land reform was introduced, with over 1200 inefficiently run private holdings confiscated and given to landless peasants; he also took over about half the holdings in the American-owned copper mines, with suitable compensation. ✅ The American government admired his reforms and poured in lavish economic aid.

✅ **Fact:** by 1967 the tide was turning: the left thought his land reforms too cautious and wanted full nationalisation of copper, Chile's most important export, while the right thought he had gone too far. ✅ In 1969 a serious drought lost a third of the harvest; large quantities of food had to be imported, causing inflation to soar; copper miners struck for higher wages and several were killed by government troops. ✅ Allende made skilful use of this in the 1970 campaign, his coalition had much better campaign organisation and could get thousands of supporters onto the streets, and Allende himself inspired confidence - elegant and cultured, the very opposite of the violent revolutionary. ✅ The account adds that appearances were not deceptive: he believed that communism could succeed without a violent revolution.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Frei paragraph is essential and is almost always omitted, because it establishes the baseline against which the American response to Allende has to be judged. ⚠️ A reforming government that reduced inflation, enforced taxation on the wealthy, built housing and schools, redistributed inefficient holdings and took half the American-owned copper with compensation received lavish American aid. ⚠️ A government that proposed to complete the same programme and nationalise the rest of the copper received, on the account's own evidence, an effort to make the economy scream. ⚠️ The difference between the two was not democratic legitimacy, since both were elected under the same constitution, and it was not violence, since Allende believed communism could succeed without a violent revolution. ⚠️ The difference was the scope of the nationalisation and the declared doctrine - which is the criterion problem of section 1, demonstrated within a single country across six years.

### 3. Allende's policies, problems and the five causes of his overthrow

✅ **Fact:** the problems facing the new government were enormous: inflation over 30 per cent, unemployment at 20 per cent, industry stagnating, and 90 per cent of the population living in such poverty that half the children under 15 suffered from malnutrition. ✅ Allende believed in a redistribution of income which would enable the poor to buy more and thereby stimulate the economy: all-round wage increases of about 40 per cent were introduced and firms were not allowed to increase prices; the remainder of the copper industry, textiles and banks were nationalised; Frei's land redistribution was speeded up; and the army was awarded an even bigger pay rise than anybody else to make sure of keeping its support. ✅ In foreign affairs, Allende restored diplomatic relations with Castro's Cuba, China and East Germany.

✅ **Fact:** whether Allende's policies would have succeeded in the long run is open to argument; certainly he retained his popularity sufficiently for Unidad Popular to win 49 per cent of the votes in the 1972 local elections and to slightly increase their seats in the 1973 elections for Congress. ✅ However, the experiment came to an abrupt and violent end in September 1973.

✅ **Fact:** the account gives five reasons for the overthrow. ✅ Land redistribution caused a fall in agricultural production, mainly because farmers whose land was due to be taken stopped sowing and often slaughtered their cattle, causing food shortages and further inflation. ✅ Private investors were frightened off and the government became short of funds to carry out social reforms as rapidly as it would have liked. ✅ Copper nationalisation was disappointing: there were long strikes for higher wages, production went down and the world price of copper fell suddenly by about 30 per cent, causing a further decrease in government revenue. ✅ Some communists who wanted a more drastic Castro-style approach grew impatient with Allende's caution, refused to make allowances for the fact that he did not have a stable majority in parliament, and formed the Movement of the Revolutionary Left, which embarrassed the non-violent Unidad Popular by seizing farms and evicting owners. ✅ And the USA disapproved strongly of Allende's policies and did everything in their power to undermine Chile's economy, while other South American governments were nervous in case the Chileans tried to export their revolution.

✅ **Fact:** looming above everything else was the question of what would happen in September 1976 when the next presidential election was due. ✅ Under the constitution Allende would not be able to stand, but no Marxist regime had ever let itself be voted out of power; the opposition feared, perhaps with justification, that Allende was planning to change the constitution, since any president finding his legislation blocked by Congress could appeal to the nation by referendum, and with sufficient support Allende might use that device to postpone the election. ✅ It was this fear, or so they afterwards claimed, which caused the opposition groups to draw together and take action before Allende did.

✅ **Fact:** they organised a massive strike, and having won the support of the army, the right staged a military coup, organised by leading generals, who set up a military dictatorship in which General Pinochet came to the fore. ✅ Left-wing leaders were murdered or imprisoned and Allende himself was reported to have committed suicide; however, the cause of death has been controversial, many of his supporters claiming that he was gunned down in the presidential palace, and in 2011 Chilean television reported that a newly discovered document proved beyond doubt that he had been assassinated.

✅ **Fact:** the American Central Intelligence Agency, helped by the Brazilian government, a repressive military regime, played a vital role in the preparations for the coup, as part of its policy of preventing the spread of communism in Latin America. ✅ There is evidence that the CIA had been considering a coup as soon as Allende won the election in 1970. ✅ There is no doubt that the Nixon administration had done its best to destabilise the Allende government over the next three years by undermining the economy, and Nixon himself was reported as saying that they must "make the Chilean economy scream".

✅ **Fact:** the new Chilean regime soon provoked criticism from the outside world for its brutal treatment of political prisoners and its violations of human rights. ✅ However, the American government, which had reduced its economic aid while Allende was in power, stepped up its assistance again. ✅ The Pinochet regime had some economic success and by 1980 had brought the annual inflation rate down from around 1000 per cent to manageable proportions. ✅ Pinochet was in no hurry to return the country to civilian rule; he eventually allowed presidential elections in 1989, when the civilian candidate he supported was heavily defeated, winning less than 30 per cent of the votes, and he permitted the winner, Christian Democrat leader Patricio Aylwin, to become president in 1990, though the 1981 constitution allowed Pinochet himself to remain Commander-in-Chief of the Armed Forces for a further eight years. ✅ During his seventeen years as president, around 3000 people were killed or disappeared, while tens of thousands were tortured, imprisoned or driven into exile.

✅ **Fact:** Pinochet duly stepped down in 1998, but his retirement did not work out as he had planned: on a visit to London later that year he was arrested and held in Britain for sixteen months after the Spanish government requested his extradition to face charges of torturing Spanish citizens in Chile; he was eventually allowed to return to Chile on medical grounds in March 2000. ✅ However, one of his most bitter opponents, Ricardo Lagos, had just been elected president in January 2000 - the first socialist president since Allende. ✅ Pinochet soon found himself facing over 250 charges of human rights abuses, but in July 2001 the Chilean Court of Appeal decided that the general, then aged 86, was too ill to stand trial; he died in 2006 at the age of 91.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the five causes are of two kinds and an answer that sorts them is immediately stronger. Three are internal policy consequences - the agricultural collapse caused by anticipatory disinvestment, the flight of private investors, and the disappointment of copper nationalisation compounded by a 30 per cent fall in the world price. ⚠️ One is internal political fragmentation: the Movement of the Revolutionary Left attacking the government from the left by seizing farms. ⚠️ One is external: an American effort to undermine the economy, with the CIA considering a coup from 1970 and playing a vital role in its preparation. ⚠️ The honest verdict is that the external cause operated *on* internal vulnerabilities rather than instead of them, and that the vulnerabilities were substantially generated by the government's own programme. ⚠️ The mechanism is precisely the one stated for Angola in Lesson 14: intervention made much worse a crisis that had domestic causes.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the detail about the copper price is the single most instructive economic fact in the lesson. A government whose revenue depended on one commodity nationalised that commodity and then lost about 30 per cent of its world price - which means the decisive economic blow came from a market movement no government controlled. ⚠️ That is a general finding about single-commodity economies in this period and it belongs in any answer that touches the economics of the Global South's Cold War, alongside the observation that the same dependence made economic destabilisation by an external power so much easier to accomplish.

### 4. South-east Asia: three states, three instruments, one outcome pattern

✅ **Fact:** in Laos after independence there was conflict between the right-wing government backed by the USA and various left-wing groups led by the Pathet Lao, a left-wing nationalist party which had fought in the struggle against the French. ✅ At first the Pathet Lao showed itself willing to take part in coalition governments in an attempt to bring about peaceful social change. ✅ The USA saw the Pathet Lao as dangerous communists: the CIA and the State Department between them arranged a series of interventions which by 1960 had removed all left-wingers from important positions. ✅ The left turned to armed force and the CIA responded by gathering an army of 30 000 anti-communists from all over Asia to crush the insurgents; between 1965 and 1973 the US air force carried out regular bombing raids over Laos, causing enormous casualties and devastation. ✅ The account's verdict: it was all to no avail - American intervention strengthened the resolve of the left, and following the American withdrawal from Vietnam and south-east Asia and the communist takeover in Cambodia, the Laotian right gave up the struggle and their leaders left the country; in December 1975 the Pathet Lao took control peacefully and the Lao People's Democratic Republic was proclaimed.

✅ **Fact:** in Cambodia there was American involvement in a coup that overthrew the regime of Prince Sihanouk in 1970; the bombing campaigns which preceded the coup left the Cambodian economy in ruins. ✅ American intervention was followed by five years of civil war, which ended when Pol Pot and the Khmer Rouge took power.

✅ **Fact:** during the Vietnam War of 1965-73 the USA used Thailand as a base from which the bombing of North Vietnam took place. ✅ Eventually the American presence in Thailand was so massive that they seemed to have taken the country over. ✅ There was considerable opposition from Thais who resented the way their country was being used, but all criticism was treated as communist-inspired; over 40 000 American troops were active in trying to suppress opposition guerrilla fighters and in training Thai government forces. ✅ In August 1966 the *Washington Post* reported that in US government circles there was a strong feeling that continued dictatorship in Thailand suited the United States, since it assured the continuation of American bases in the country, and that, as a US official put it bluntly, that was the real American interest in the place.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Laotian sequence is the clearest self-defeating chain in the topic and should be written out as one. A left-wing nationalist party willing to join coalition governments was classified as dangerously communist; intervention removed all left-wingers from important positions by 1960; the left, excluded from politics, turned to armed force; a CIA-recruited army and eight years of bombing failed to defeat it; and the party took power peacefully in December 1975. ⚠️ The intervention converted a party willing to share power into an insurgency and then lost to it. ⚠️ That is the strongest single illustration of the criterion problem, because the initial classification was not merely harsh but factually wrong about the party's willingness to operate inside a coalition.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Thai quotation is the most candid statement of motive in the whole source base and should be used carefully and exactly. ⚠️ It reports a feeling in American government circles, as carried by a newspaper, that continued dictatorship suited American interests because it secured the bases. ⚠️ Reproduced with that attribution it is powerful evidence that in at least some cases the preservation of freedom was not the operative criterion; reproduced as a statement of American policy it overstates the source.

### 5. Iran and Iraq

⚠️ **Declared evidence gap.** The page of the core text carrying the opening of the Iranian case - the account of the events that led to the restoration - is blank in the copy held here. **No detail of that episode, its date, its participants or its methods is asserted anywhere in this session.** Only the legible continuation is used.

✅ **Fact:** the legible text records that the Shah was restored to full control; that he remained in power for the next twenty-five years, fully backed and supported by Washington; and that he was forced out in January 1979.

✅ **Fact:** Iraq came in for constant attention from the USA. ✅ In 1958 General Abdul Kassem overthrew the Iraqi monarchy and proclaimed a republic; he was in favour of reform and modernisation, and although he himself was not a communist, the new atmosphere of freedom and openness encouraged the growth of the Iraqi Communist Party. ✅ This made Washington uneasy, and the State Department was further perturbed in 1960 when Kassem was involved in setting up the Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries, which aimed to break the control of western oil companies over the sale of Middle East oil. ✅ The CIA had been trying to destabilise the country for several years - by encouraging a Turkish invasion, financing Kurdish guerrillas who were agitating for more autonomy, and attempting to assassinate Kassem. ✅ In 1963 they were successful: Kassem was overthrown and killed in a coup backed by the CIA and Britain.

✅ **Fact:** from 1979 the USA financed and supplied Saddam Hussein, backing him against the new anti-American government in Iran. ⚠️ **Correction to the held wording, declared once and applied throughout.** The account states that Saddam "became Iraqi leader in 1968". The verified record is that the Ba'ath Party seized power in Iraq in **July 1968**, that Saddam was prominent in that regime from the outset as vice-president and its effective second figure under President Ahmed Hassan al-Bakr, and that **he became President of Iraq on 16 July 1979**. ⚠️ The correction matters for this topic rather than only for accuracy: the American decision to finance and supply him from 1979 coincides with his assumption of the presidency and with the arrival of the new government in Iran, so the corrected date tightens a causal sequence that the uncorrected one blurs. ✅ After the long and inconclusive Iran-Iraq War of 1980-88, Saddam's forces invaded and conquered Kuwait in August 1990, only to be driven out again by UN forces, of which by far the largest contingent was the American one. ✅ In 2003 the Americans, with British help, finally overthrew and captured Saddam.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Iraqi case is the most complete demonstration of the criterion problem in the source base, because the account states the disqualifying facts explicitly. ✅ Kassem was not a communist. ✅ What made Washington uneasy was, first, that freedom and openness allowed the Iraqi Communist Party to grow, and second - the item the account places on the same footing - his involvement in founding an organisation aimed at breaking the control of western oil companies over Middle East oil. ⚠️ An oil-policy grievance and a communism grievance appear in the same sentence as joint causes of a destabilisation campaign that included encouraging a foreign invasion, financing an ethnic insurgency and attempted assassination. ⚠️ That sentence, reproduced accurately, does more analytical work than any general claim about American motives.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and the twenty-five-year Iranian sequence completes the pattern of section 4's outcome box. Backing sustained a government for a quarter of a century and it was forced out in January 1979, to be replaced by the anti-American government that made the arming of Saddam from 1979 seem necessary - which in turn produced the 1990 invasion that American forces reversed. ⚠️ Stating that chain in two sentences is the clearest available demonstration that Cold War instruments generated the problems that later instruments were built to solve.

### 6. The evidential rules that make this material usable

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the section of the topic where a candidate is most likely to lose marks by overreaching, so the rules should be explicit.

| Rule | Why |
|---|---|
| **Reproduce attributions** | ✅ The long list of states is attributed to named authors - Blum, Agee, Chomsky and others; it is a list of what has been written about, not an inventory of established operations |
| **Assert only what the account asserts directly** | ✅ The CIA's vital role in preparing the Chilean coup, the 1963 Iraqi coup backed by the CIA and Britain, the Laotian interventions and the CIA army of 30 000 are stated as fact; the wider list is not |
| **Preserve the account's own hedges** | ✅ "There is evidence that the CIA had been considering a coup as soon as Allende won"; "or so they afterwards claimed"; "perhaps with justification"; Allende's death "has been controversial" |
| **State internal causes alongside external ones** | ✅ Chile's five causes include three internal policy failures and one internal political split |
| **Do not generalise from the cases to a universal claim** | ⚠️ The account offers "a few examples" from a longer list it does not examine; an answer should say so |
| **Use no casualty figures the sources do not give** | ✅ Chile's recorded figures are around 3000 killed or disappeared and tens of thousands tortured, imprisoned or exiled under Pinochet; ⚠️ for Laos, Cambodia and Thailand the account gives no totals and none is supplied here |

### 7. The comparison, stated honestly

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Lesson 7 found three asymmetries favouring the Western alliance in its internal conduct - origin, voice and sanction - and warned that they said nothing about American conduct outside the alliance. This lesson supplies the other half, and a strong answer holds both.

| Dimension | ✅ Soviet conduct, from this session | ✅ American conduct, from this session |
|---|---|---|
| **Inside the bloc** | Governments installed 1945-48; the Cominform's requirement of Russian-style communism; Yugoslavia expelled 1948; Hungary crushed by tanks in 1956 | Allies could dissent and did - over a hundred Labour MPs against Fulton, Britain and France at Suez against American opposition; no formal sanction followed |
| **Outside the bloc** | Aid to neutral states as an instrument of persuasion; arms to Egypt and Syria; encouragement without commitment in 1967; troops in Afghanistan from December 1979 | Assassination attempts, election rigging, financed terrorism, economic destabilisation and, in the last resort, full-scale military intervention, across an astonishing number of states |
| **Against a state's own population** | Hungary 1956 | ✅ Not directly recorded here; the Chilean, Laotian and Cambodian consequences were produced through local forces that American action supported or created |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the honest finding is that the two systems concentrated their coercion differently: Soviet coercion was heaviest inside its own bloc and American coercion was heaviest outside its alliance. ⚠️ Neither observation excuses the other, and an answer that offers only one has produced propaganda rather than analysis. ⚠️ The account's own language supports the symmetry of treatment: it describes the Soviet imposition of governments in eastern Europe without euphemism, and it describes the American technique set without euphemism.

### 8. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** this material is a moral indictment rather than history. Listing covert operations proves nothing about the Cold War as an international system, and the sources cited are partisan - a former CIA officer and a linguist are not historians of international relations.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection has two parts and they need different answers. ⚠️ On the sources, it is a reason for care rather than exclusion, and the care is already built into the account, which names Blum, Agee and Chomsky as authors of detailed accounts rather than adopting their conclusions. ⚠️ This session follows that: the list is attributed, and only the specific cases the account states directly are asserted. ⚠️ On the systemic claim, the objection fails, because these cases answer a question that no other part of the topic answers. ⚠️ The rest of this session shows the rivalry operating through alliances, deterrence, crises and aid; this lesson shows what containment consisted of in states that were neither members of either alliance nor theatres of a declared war - which is where most of the world's population lived. ✅ And it supplies the finding that the criterion in operation was frequently not communism but the pursuit of policies that went against American interests, ✅ demonstrated by a non-communist Iraqi general destabilised partly for founding an oil producers' organisation and by a Laotian party destabilised while willing to join coalitions. ⚠️ That is a claim about how the system worked, not a verdict about who was virtuous.

### 9. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Examine the methods by which the superpowers sought influence outside their formal alliances.
> ⚠️ "The Cold War penetrated the domestic politics of states far from Europe." Discuss.
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the Chilean experiment of 1970-73.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, the technique list is assertible and the case selection should be small: Chile in detail and one Asian case, with the evidential rules observed. ⚠️ For the second, Chile, Laos and Iraq are the three cases where the account records domestic political outcomes being shaped from outside, and the Thai quotation is the candid statement of motive. ⚠️ For the third, the structure is the account's own: the Frei baseline, Allende's programme, the five causes sorted into internal and external, and the Pinochet record with its figures - closing on the general finding that intervention operated on vulnerabilities it did not create.

### Traps

- Converting the list of states into a list of proven operations. ✅ It is a list of states written about by named authors.
- Omitting Frei. ✅ His reforms drew lavish American aid; the contrast with Allende is the argument.
- Presenting Allende's fall as simply an American coup. ✅ Three internal policy failures and one internal political split appear alongside the American destabilisation.
- Forgetting the copper price. ✅ A sudden fall of about 30 per cent in the world price cut revenue independently of any policy.
- Saying Allende was assassinated as an established fact. ✅ The account records a controversy and a 2011 Chilean television report of a newly discovered document.
- Treating the Pathet Lao as communists who were always committed to armed struggle. ✅ At first they were willing to take part in coalition governments to bring about peaceful social change.
- Describing Kassem as a communist. ✅ The account says explicitly that he was not one, and names the OPEC grievance alongside the communist-party grievance.
- Supplying casualty figures for Laos, Cambodia or Thailand. ⚠️ The account gives none and none is invented here.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The technique set - assassination attempts, election rigging, organising and financing acts of terrorism, economic destabilisation and, in the last resort, full-scale military intervention - together with the criterion problem: regimes labelled communist and targeted were often simply pursuing policies which went against American interests.
2. ✅ The sources: Blum, Agee and Chomsky among named authors of detailed accounts covering an extensive list of states.
3. ✅ Chile: a tradition of democracy, three party groups, an army playing little part, a constitution usually respected - and Frei from 1964, with inflation down from 38 to 25 per cent, taxes enforced on the rich, 360 000 houses, schools more than doubled, over 1200 holdings redistributed and about half the American-owned copper taken with compensation, drawing lavish American aid.
4. ✅ 1967-69: the left wanted full copper nationalisation, the right thought he had gone too far; a drought lost a third of the harvest; miners struck and several were killed. September 1970: Allende wins with 36 per cent against 35, the world's first Marxist leader voted into power democratically, believing communism could succeed without violent revolution.
5. ✅ Inherited conditions - inflation over 30 per cent, unemployment 20 per cent, 90 per cent in poverty, half of under-15s malnourished - met by wage rises of about 40 per cent with a price freeze, nationalisation of the rest of copper, textiles and banks, faster land redistribution, the largest pay rise of all to the army, and restored relations with Cuba, China and East Germany.
6. ✅ Support held: 49 per cent in the 1972 local elections and slightly more congressional seats in 1973.
7. ✅ Five causes of the fall: anticipatory disinvestment and cattle slaughter by farmers; private investors frightened off; copper strikes, falling production and a world price fall of about 30 per cent; the Movement of the Revolutionary Left seizing farms; and American economic undermining, with neighbouring governments nervous.
8. ✅ The 1976 election fear, the referendum device, the strike, army support and the generals' coup of September 1973; Pinochet to the fore; leaders murdered or imprisoned; Allende's death controversial, with a 2011 report of a document proving assassination.
9. ✅ The CIA, helped by the Brazilian military regime, played a vital role in preparing the coup; it had been considering one since 1970; Nixon's reported instruction to make the economy scream; American aid, reduced under Allende, stepped up again after.
10. ✅ Pinochet: inflation down from around 1000 per cent by 1980; elections in 1989 lost by his candidate with under 30 per cent; Aylwin president in 1990 while Pinochet remained Commander-in-Chief for eight more years; around 3000 killed or disappeared and tens of thousands tortured, imprisoned or exiled; arrest in London in 1998, return in March 2000, over 250 charges, unfit to stand trial in July 2001, died 2006.
11. ✅ Laos: a coalition-willing Pathet Lao classified as dangerous communists; all left-wingers removed from important positions by 1960; a CIA army of 30 000; bombing 1965-73; intervention strengthened the left; the Pathet Lao took control peacefully in December 1975.
12. ✅ Cambodia: American involvement in the 1970 coup against Sihanouk; preceding bombing left the economy in ruins; five years of civil war ended with Pol Pot and the Khmer Rouge.
13. ✅ Thailand: a base for bombing the North; over 40 000 American troops; all criticism treated as communist-inspired; the August 1966 *Washington Post* report on continued dictatorship suiting American interests because it secured the bases.
14. ✅ Iran: the Shah restored to full control, twenty-five years in power fully backed by Washington, forced out in January 1979.
15. ✅ Iraq: Kassem, not a communist, overthrew the monarchy in 1958; Washington uneasy at the growth of the Iraqi Communist Party and at his role in founding OPEC in 1960; CIA destabilisation by encouraging a Turkish invasion, financing Kurdish guerrillas and attempting assassination; the 1963 coup backed by the CIA and Britain; Saddam Hussein - prominent in the Ba'ath regime from July 1968 and **President from 16 July 1979**, correcting the account's "Iraqi leader in 1968" - financed and supplied by the USA from 1979 against Iran; Kuwait invaded in August 1990 and reversed by UN forces mostly American; Saddam overthrown in 2003.

### Concept check

**Question:** The account says that targeted regimes were "often simply pursuing policies which went against American interests". Prove it from two cases.

**Model answer:** Iraq and Laos both contain the disqualifying detail inside the account's own narrative. ✅ In Iraq, General Abdul Kassem overthrew the monarchy in 1958 and favoured reform and modernisation, and the account states explicitly that he himself was not a communist; what made Washington uneasy was that the new atmosphere of freedom and openness encouraged the growth of the Iraqi Communist Party, and the State Department was further perturbed in 1960 when he was involved in setting up OPEC, which aimed to break the control of western oil companies over the sale of Middle East oil. ✅ The response was a CIA campaign of several years - encouraging a Turkish invasion, financing Kurdish guerrillas agitating for autonomy, and attempting assassination - culminating in the 1963 coup, backed by the CIA and Britain, in which Kassem was overthrown and killed. ⚠️ An oil-policy grievance sits in the same sentence as the communism grievance, which is exactly the slippage the account names. ✅ In Laos, the Pathet Lao was a left-wing nationalist party which had fought against the French and which at first showed itself willing to take part in coalition governments to bring about peaceful social change; the USA saw it as dangerously communist, and CIA and State Department interventions had removed all left-wingers from important positions by 1960. ⚠️ The classification preceded and produced the armed struggle rather than describing it, and the account's verdict is that the intervention strengthened the resolve of the left, which took power peacefully in December 1975.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating this record as proof that anti-communism was merely a pretext. In both cases a genuine communist presence or possibility existed - the Iraqi Communist Party was growing, and the Pathet Lao was a left-wing party. The finding is not that the concern was fabricated but that the operative test slipped from communism to alignment, which is why non-communist reformers and coalition-willing left-wing parties were treated as adversaries.

---

## Lesson 18 - Détente: why rivals cooperate without ceasing to be rivals

Progress: 18/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the definition and dating; the five recorded reasons and the separate national motives; Ostpolitik and Brandt; the sequence of agreements from the hotline and the test ban through the outer-space ban to SALT I and Helsinki; what SALT I counted and what it left out; the Helsinki trade of frontiers against human rights; the wheat exports and summit diplomacy; the China dimension of détente; and the four recorded limits that prevented it from becoming peace

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - the core account's section on détente, covering the definition, the reasons including each side's separate motives, Ostpolitik under Willi Brandt, the 1963 hotline and test ban, the 1967 outer-space agreement, SALT I in 1972 and what it covered, the Brezhnev-Nixon summits, wheat exports, the Helsinki Agreement of July 1975, and the setbacks of 1979; and the advanced owner's five reasons for détente and its list of the limits
🔍 CA search: not run for this lesson - the single topic-level query is recorded in Lesson 1
📰 CA found: None in last 6 months

### Visual: five reasons, four instruments, and the ceiling they could not pass

```text
   DEFINITION: "a permanent relaxation of tensions between East and
   West". The first real signs appear in the early 1970s. With one or
   two blips, détente eventually led on to the end of the Cold War
                                |
   THE SHARED REASON
   as the nuclear arsenals built up, both sides became increasingly
   fearful of a catastrophic nuclear war in which there could be no real
   winner; both sides were sickened by the horrors of Vietnam
                                |
   THE SEPARATE NATIONAL MOTIVES
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | USSR     · the expense of keeping up with the Americans was    |
   |            crippling; defence spending had to fall so that     |
   |            resources could raise living standards at home and  |
   |            in the satellites, all suffering economic difficulty|
   |          · unrest: Prague 1968, Poland in the early 1970s      |
   |          · bad terms with China, and unwillingness to be left  |
   |            out when Chinese-American relations improved in 1971|
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | USA      · a better way of coping with communism than the one  |
   |            having so little success in Vietnam; clear limits   |
   |            to what military power could achieve; some in       |
   |            Congress talking of a return to isolationism        |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | CHINA    · anxious about isolation; nervous about American     |
   |            intentions in Vietnam after Korea; unhappy about    |
   |            worsening relations with the USSR                   |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
   | WESTERN  · in the front line if nuclear war broke out;         |
   | EUROPE     Willi Brandt, Chancellor of West Germany from 1969, |
   |            worked for better relations with eastern Europe -   |
   |            OSTPOLITIK                                          |
   +---------------------------------------------------------------+
                                |
   THE INSTRUMENTS, IN ORDER
   1963  the hotline telephone link
   1963  the agreement to carry out only underground nuclear tests
   1967  an agreement banning the use of nuclear weapons in outer space
   1972  SALT I - decided how many ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs each side could
         have. THERE WAS NO AGREEMENT ABOUT MIRVs
         "The agreement did not reduce the amount of armaments but it
          did slow down the arms race"
         + three Brezhnev-Nixon summits; negotiations opened for SALT 2;
           the USA began to export wheat to Russia
   1975  the HELSINKI AGREEMENT, July. The USA, Canada, the USSR and
         most European states accepted the European frontiers drawn up
         after the Second World War, thus recognising the division of
         Germany. The communist countries promised to allow their
         peoples 'human rights', including freedom of speech and
         freedom to leave the country
                                |
   THE CEILING
   · SALT I capped categories, did not reduce stocks, and left out the
     most destabilising system
   · Helsinki recognised a division rather than settling it
   · 1979: NATO nervous at 150 new Soviet SS-20s -> over 500 Pershing
     and Cruise missiles to be deployed in Europe by 1983; the US Senate
     decides not to accept a SALT 2 treaty limiting MIRVs; Soviet forces
     enter Afghanistan on Christmas Day
```

*The lower half of the diagram answers the lesson's question. Every instrument in the list manages risk or ratifies an existing position; none alters the distribution of power. That is why cooperation and rivalry were compatible - and why the cooperation did not survive a change in the distribution of power.*

### 1. The definition, and the dating problem it creates

✅ **Fact:** the word détente is used to mean a permanent relaxation of tensions between East and West. ✅ The first real signs of détente could be seen in the early 1970s. ✅ With one or two blips along the way, détente eventually led on to the end of the Cold War.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there is a tension inside that definition worth one sentence in a strong answer. "Permanent" is a strong word for a process the same account says was interrupted by a second Cold War from 1979. ⚠️ The way to handle it is to distinguish the *aspiration* from the *record*: the parties intended a durable relaxation, and the instruments they built - a hotline, verification-free test limits, category caps, frontier recognition - were designed for durability, while the political relationship that carried them was not. ⚠️ The dating tension is similar. The first real signs are placed in the early 1970s, yet three of the instruments in the visual date from 1963 and 1967. ⚠️ The resolution is that the earlier agreements were risk-management measures taken in the shadow of the Cuban crisis, and that what began in the early 1970s was something larger - a negotiated relationship with summits, arms limits, trade and a European settlement.

### 2. The reasons, shared and separate

✅ **Fact:** as the nuclear arsenals built up, both sides became increasingly fearful of a catastrophic nuclear war in which there could be no real winner, and both sides were sickened by the horrors of Vietnam. ✅ In addition, countries had their own individual motives for wanting détente.

✅ **Fact:** the USSR was finding the expense of keeping up with the Americans crippling; it was essential to reduce defence spending so that more resources could be devoted to bringing living standards up to western levels, both in the USSR and in the satellite states, all of which were suffering economic difficulties. ✅ In 1968 Russian troops were sent to Prague to deal with disturbing developments in Czechoslovakia, when Alexander Dubcek tried to introduce "communism with a human face"; there was unrest, especially in Poland in the early 1970s, which threatened to destabilise the communist bloc. ✅ At the same time the Russians were on bad terms with China, and did not want to be left out when relations between China and the USA began to improve in 1971.

✅ **Fact:** the Americans were beginning to realise that there must be a better way of coping with communism than the one which was having so little success in Vietnam; clearly there were limits to what their military power could achieve; and some Congressmen and Senators were even beginning to talk of a return to isolationism.

✅ **Fact:** the Chinese were anxious about their isolation, nervous about American intentions in Vietnam after what had happened in Korea, and not happy about their worsening relations with the USSR.

✅ **Fact:** the nations of western Europe were worried because they would be in the front line if nuclear war broke out. ✅ Willi Brandt, who became Chancellor of West Germany in 1969, worked for better relations with eastern Europe, a policy known as Ostpolitik.

✅ **Fact:** the advanced owner adds its own list of reasons, which overlaps and extends the above: fear of nuclear catastrophe; Soviet economic strain; American exhaustion and disappointment after Vietnam; West European desire for stability; and triangular pressure created by China-US rapprochement.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the structure of this explanation is unusual and is what makes détente examinable. ⚠️ It is not that two powers decided to get on better; it is that four distinct parties reached, for four different reasons, a preference for the same outcome. ⚠️ Moscow wanted resources and feared isolation from a Sino-American relationship; Washington wanted an alternative to a method that had failed; Beijing wanted out of isolation and away from Soviet pressure; western Europe wanted not to be the battlefield. ⚠️ An answer that lists "fear of nuclear war and economic strain" has given the shared reason and missed the convergence, and the convergence is why détente happened when it did rather than at any earlier point when nuclear fear and economic strain also existed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Prague in 1968 and Poland in the early 1970s belong in the answer for a reason that is not obvious. They are cited as *motives for détente* - unrest that threatened to destabilise the communist bloc and therefore made an easing of external pressure and a diversion of resources to living standards more urgent. ⚠️ So the internal weakness of the eastern bloc is present in the explanation of détente fifteen years before the collapse, which is a useful thread to carry into Lesson 20 and to hand over to the end-of-Cold-War owner.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Ostpolitik deserves more than its one line. A West German government seeking better relations with eastern Europe was, in practice, accepting the postwar settlement that its predecessors had refused to recognise - and Helsinki in 1975 is that acceptance generalised to the whole continent. ⚠️ The sequence therefore runs from a change of policy in Bonn in 1969 to a European instrument in 1975, which is an instance of a middle power setting the agenda of a superpower negotiation.

### 3. The instruments, and what each did

| ✅ Date and instrument | ✅ What it did | ⚠️ What it did not do |
|---|---|---|
| 1963, the hotline | A telephone link between Moscow and Washington allowing swift consultation | Nothing about weapons |
| 1963, the test ban | The USSR, USA and Britain agreed to carry out nuclear tests only underground, to avoid polluting the atmosphere further | Nothing about the number, type or deployment of weapons |
| 1967 | An agreement banned the use of nuclear weapons in outer space | Nothing about weapons on earth |
| 1972, SALT I | The first major breakthrough: it decided how many ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs each side could have. ✅ "The agreement did not reduce the amount of armaments but it did slow down the arms race" | ✅ There was no agreement about MIRVs |
| 1972-75, summitry and trade | Brezhnev and Nixon had three summit meetings; negotiations opened for SALT 2; the USA began to export wheat to Russia | No political settlement |
| 1975, Helsinki | The USA, Canada, the USSR and most European states accepted the European frontiers drawn up after the Second World War, thus recognising the division of Germany; the communist countries promised to allow their peoples human rights, including freedom of speech and freedom to leave the country | It recognised a division rather than resolving it, and the human-rights undertaking carried no enforcement mechanism in the account's description |

⚠️ **Note on the Helsinki dating, displayed rather than reconciled.** ✅ The core account and the canonical owners both give the Helsinki Agreement as **July 1975**. ✅ The verified record of the signature gives **1 August 1975** as the date on which the Final Act was signed at Helsinki, the conference itself having run to its close in the last days of July. ⚠️ Both are shown here and **neither is substituted for the other**: for an examination answer the year is what carries the mark, and a candidate who needs the month should write that the conference concluded at the end of July and the Final Act was signed on 1 August 1975, rather than reproducing a bare "July" as though it were the signature date. ⚠️ The same treatment is applied in Lesson 12 to the Nuclear Test Ban Treaty, where the account gives July 1963 and the verified record gives 25 July for the initialling and 5 August for the signature. ⚠️ These are the two dating discrepancies in this session; both are displayed, and no third is claimed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** SALT I is the centre of the lesson and its two halves must be held together. ⚠️ It was genuinely a breakthrough: for the first time the two powers agreed numerical limits on categories of strategic weapon, which required agreeing what counted and therefore accepting mutual observation of a kind. ⚠️ And it was, in the account's own words, an agreement that did not reduce armaments but slowed the race. ⚠️ Worse, on Lesson 8's analysis it capped the systems that were comparatively stabilising or neutral and omitted the one that most increased the incentive to strike first. ⚠️ The formulation that captures both halves: SALT I regulated the competition without reducing it, and left uncovered the technology that made the competition dangerous.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** Helsinki is the other instrument that repays close reading, because it was an exchange of two very different kinds of thing. ⚠️ The West conceded something concrete and immediate: recognition of the frontiers drawn after 1945, including the division of Germany, which had been the central unresolved European question since Lesson 6. ⚠️ The East conceded something declaratory and deferred: a promise to allow human rights including freedom of speech and freedom to leave the country. ⚠️ In 1975 that looked like an unequal bargain in Moscow's favour, and the sources here record no enforcement mechanism. ⚠️ The end-of-Cold-War owner holds what happened to those undertakings afterwards; what belongs here is the observation that the agreement inserted a standard into the eastern bloc's own signed commitments, and that inserting a documented standard against which a government can later be measured is the same mechanism this session met in Lesson 4, where a promise of free elections at Yalta became the West's indictment.

### 4. The China dimension

✅ **Fact:** the account treats Chinese-American relations as a part of détente rather than as a separate development, and Lesson 13 supplies the sequence: the table-tennis invitation of 1971, the UN seat in October 1971, Nixon's visit in February 1972, Ford's in 1975, Carter's withdrawal of recognition from Nationalist China in 1978 and formal recognition of the People's Republic in 1979, described by the account as the climax of détente between China and the USA.

✅ **Fact:** the advanced owner lists triangular pressure created by China-US rapprochement among the reasons for détente, and the core account records that the Russians did not want to be left out when relations between China and the USA began to improve in 1971.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the triangle changes what détente was. A bilateral relaxation between two adversaries is a bargain about risk; a triangular one is a competition for the third party's alignment conducted through conciliation rather than coercion. ⚠️ That explains an otherwise odd feature of the early 1970s - that the most intense period of Soviet-American negotiation coincided with the most rapid improvement in Chinese-American relations. ⚠️ They were not independent processes; each increased the value of the other to Washington and the urgency of the first to Moscow.

### 5. The limits, and why détente did not become peace

✅ **Fact:** the advanced owner states the limits directly: the SS-20 against Pershing and Cruise missile competition; the failure of SALT II ratification; Soviet intervention in Afghanistan; and the persistence of deep mutual suspicion. ✅ Its safe answer line is that détente managed rivalry; it did not abolish rivalry.

✅ **Fact:** the core account gives the sequence of the breakdown. ✅ Détente did not proceed without some setbacks, especially in 1979, when NATO became nervous at the deployment of 150 new Russian SS-20 missiles; ✅ NATO decided to deploy over 500 Pershing and Cruise missiles in Europe by 1983 as a deterrent to a possible Russian attack on western Europe; ✅ at the same time the US Senate decided not to accept a SALT 2 treaty which would have limited numbers of MIRVs; ✅ and when the Russians invaded Afghanistan on Christmas Day 1979 and replaced the president with one more favourable to them, all the old western suspicions of Russian motives revived.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the ordering of those four items is itself the explanation, and it is worth writing out. A regional deployment produced an alliance counter-deployment; the legislature of one party then declined to ratify the agreement that would have covered the technology at issue; and an intervention in a third country then removed the political basis for further negotiation. ⚠️ Note that the first two are about the same system - the MIRV, which SALT I had not covered and which SALT II was intended to cover. ⚠️ Détente therefore failed at precisely the point its first instrument had left open, which is the strongest possible argument for the view that an arms-control agreement omitting the destabilising system was building on an unsound foundation.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the deeper structural reason belongs in the answer too. Every instrument of détente managed risk or ratified an existing distribution; none changed the distribution. ⚠️ That is what allowed two adversaries to sign them - neither was giving anything up - and it is also why they provided no protection when the distribution began to change. ⚠️ Cooperation that costs nothing also secures nothing.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** détente was a failure. It produced one arms agreement that reduced no weapons, a European agreement that ratified a division the West had always refused to accept, and a decade of trade and summits that ended with Soviet troops in Afghanistan. A policy whose main achievement was legitimising the other side's position is not a success by any measure.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection scores against the strong claim and not against the defensible one, so the first move is to abandon the strong claim. ⚠️ Détente did not end the rivalry and did not intend to; the owner's own formulation is that it managed rivalry rather than abolishing it. ⚠️ Judged against that objective, three achievements stand. ✅ First, risk reduction: a hotline, an atmospheric test ban, a ban on weapons in outer space and agreed numerical limits on three categories of strategic weapon are all reductions in the probability of accidental or miscalculated catastrophe. ✅ Second, the relationship survived the largest American defeat of the Cold War: in 1975 both Russians and Chinese reacted to the fall of Saigon with restraint and did not boast about it, which the account reads as indicating that they wished to relax international tensions. ⚠️ Third, on Helsinki, the objection assumes the concession was one-sided, but the frontier recognition ratified a reality that had been unchanged for thirty years and had already been accepted in practice by Ostpolitik, while the human-rights undertaking introduced a standard into the eastern bloc's own commitments. ⚠️ The honest limit is the one the objection identifies correctly: a framework that regulated the competition but could not survive a change in its terms - and Lesson 19 is the demonstration.

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why was there a period of détente during the 1970s and 1980s, and in what ways did détente manifest itself?
> ⚠️ Explain the causes, course and consequences of détente.
> ⚠️ "Détente relaxed tension without ending rivalry." Assess.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first two, use the convergence structure rather than a list: one shared reason and four separate national motives, then the instrument table in order, then the limits. ⚠️ For the third, agree with the proposition and then earn the marks by explaining *why* it could not end rivalry - because every instrument managed risk or ratified an existing distribution, and none altered the distribution. ⚠️ In all three, the single most valuable specific point is that SALT I covered ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs and not MIRVs, and that SALT II, which would have covered MIRVs, was not accepted by the US Senate.

### Traps

- Defining détente as friendship or alliance. ✅ It is a permanent relaxation of tensions, and the owner's line is that it managed rather than abolished rivalry.
- Dating it from 1972 alone. ✅ The first real signs are in the early 1970s, but the hotline and test ban are 1963 and the outer-space agreement 1967.
- Saying SALT I reduced armaments. ✅ It did not reduce the amount of armaments but did slow down the arms race.
- Omitting the MIRV gap. ✅ SALT I covered ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs, and there was no agreement about MIRVs.
- Presenting Helsinki as a settlement of the German question. ✅ It recognised the frontiers drawn after the war, thus recognising the division of Germany.
- Giving Helsinki a confident month as the signature date. ✅ The account says July 1975; the verified record dates the signature of the Final Act to 1 August 1975, and §3 displays both.
- Ignoring Ostpolitik and Brandt. ✅ A West German policy from 1969 of better relations with eastern Europe is among the recorded reasons.
- Treating détente as bilateral. ✅ China's anxieties and the triangular pressure are among the recorded causes, and the Russians did not want to be left out when Chinese-American relations improved in 1971.
- Making Afghanistan the sole cause of the breakdown. ✅ The SS-20 and Pershing-Cruise competition and the Senate's refusal of SALT II precede it in the account's own order.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Definition: a permanent relaxation of tensions between East and West; first real signs in the early 1970s; it eventually led on to the end of the Cold War.
2. ✅ The shared reasons: fear of a catastrophic nuclear war with no real winner as arsenals built up; and being sickened by the horrors of Vietnam.
3. ✅ Soviet motives: crippling expense; the need to raise living standards at home and in the satellites; Prague 1968 and Polish unrest in the early 1970s; bad relations with China and unwillingness to be left out of the 1971 Sino-American improvement.
4. ✅ American motives: the failure of the Vietnam method; recognised limits to military power; talk of a return to isolationism in Congress.
5. ✅ Chinese motives: isolation, nervousness about American intentions after Korea, worsening relations with the USSR.
6. ✅ West European motives: the front line in any nuclear war; Brandt's Ostpolitik from 1969.
7. ✅ The advanced owner's five reasons: nuclear fear, Soviet economic strain, American exhaustion after Vietnam, West European desire for stability, and triangular pressure from Sino-American rapprochement.
8. ✅ 1963: the hotline, and the agreement to test only underground; 1967: nuclear weapons banned in outer space.
9. ✅ 1972: SALT I decided how many ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs each side could have; no agreement about MIRVs; it did not reduce armaments but slowed the race; three Brezhnev-Nixon summits; SALT 2 negotiations opened; American wheat exports to Russia began.
10. ✅ July 1975: Helsinki - acceptance by the USA, Canada, the USSR and most European states of the postwar frontiers, recognising the division of Germany; communist promises of human rights including freedom of speech and freedom to leave the country. ⚠️ The owners and the core account give July 1975; the verified record dates the signature of the Final Act to 1 August 1975, and both are displayed in §3.
11. ✅ 1979 setbacks: 150 new Soviet SS-20s; a NATO decision to deploy over 500 Pershing and Cruise missiles in Europe by 1983; the US Senate's refusal of a SALT 2 treaty limiting MIRVs; and the invasion of Afghanistan on Christmas Day, replacing the president with one more favourable.
12. ✅ The advanced owner's limits: the SS-20 against Pershing and Cruise competition, the failure of SALT II ratification, Afghanistan, and persistent deep mutual suspicion.
13. ⚠️ The structural reason for the ceiling: every instrument managed risk or ratified an existing distribution; none altered it.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why détente collapsed at exactly the point where its first agreement had been incomplete.

**Model answer:** Because the system SALT I omitted was the one that drove the next round of competition. ✅ SALT I in 1972 decided how many ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs each side could have, did not reduce the amount of armaments but did slow down the arms race, and contained no agreement about MIRVs - the multiple independently targetable re-entry vehicle, which could carry as many as fourteen separate warheads each programmed to a different target and which, on the arms-race analysis, made a first strike arithmetically attractive. ✅ The Soviet version of that technology, the SS-20, appeared in 1977 with three warheads targeted on western Europe; ✅ by 1979 NATO was nervous at the deployment of 150 new SS-20s and decided to deploy over 500 Pershing and Cruise missiles in Europe by 1983; ✅ and at the same time the US Senate decided not to accept a SALT 2 treaty which would have limited numbers of MIRVs. ⚠️ So the uncovered category produced a deployment, the deployment produced a counter-deployment, and the treaty designed to close the gap was refused by the legislature of the state that had originally left it open. ✅ The invasion of Afghanistan on Christmas Day 1979 then removed the political basis for negotiation, but the arms-control failure preceded it in the account's own sequence.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Afghanistan as the cause of détente's collapse. It revived all the old western suspicions, but the missile competition and the Senate's refusal of SALT II came first, which means the framework was already failing on its own central subject before the intervention occurred.

---

## Lesson 19 - 1979-85: Afghanistan, the missiles and the second Cold War

Progress: 19/22 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the invasion of December 1979 and the change of president; the revival of old suspicions; the SS-20 deployment and the NATO response; the Senate's refusal of SALT II; the arsenal build-up of the early 1980s; the Strategic Defence Initiative and what it was intended to do; the Soviet position in Afghanistan by 1985 and the four pressures that ended it; and why this phase is best understood as a demonstration that relaxation was reversible rather than as a return to 1949

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - the core account on the setbacks to détente in 1979, the SS-20 and Pershing-Cruise deployments, the Senate's refusal of SALT 2, the invasion of Afghanistan on Christmas Day 1979, the arsenal build-up of the first half of the 1980s and Reagan's Strategic Defence Initiative; and on the Soviet position in Afghanistan by 1985 with the four pressures that produced the withdrawal agreement; together with the advanced owner's designation of these years as a "second Cold War"
