### Visual: the reversal, item by item, with its dates

```text
   1977  the SS-20 enters service: the Soviet version of the MIRV,
         three warheads, targeted on western Europe, less sophisticated
         than the American system
              |
   1979  NATO becomes nervous at the deployment of 150 new Russian
         SS-20 missiles
              |
   1979  NATO decides to deploy OVER 500 PERSHING AND CRUISE MISSILES
         in Europe BY 1983, as a deterrent to a possible Russian attack
         on western Europe
              |
   1979  the US SENATE decides NOT to accept a SALT 2 treaty which would
         have limited numbers of MIRVs
              |
   25 December 1979  THE RUSSIANS INVADE AFGHANISTAN and replace the
         president with one more favourable to them
         -> "all the old western suspicions of Russian motives revived"
              |
   1979-85  "THE NEXT FEW YEARS ARE SOMETIMES REFERRED TO AS THE SECOND
            COLD WAR"
         · both sides spent the first half of the 1980s building up
           their nuclear arsenals
         · US President Reagan (1981-9) apparently gave the go-ahead for
           a new weapons system, the STRATEGIC DEFENCE INITIATIVE (SDI),
           also known as 'Star Wars', intended to use weapons based in
           space to destroy ballistic missiles in flight
              |
   BY 1985  THE SOVIET POSITION IN AFGHANISTAN
         · the USSR is "seriously embarrassed by its involvement"
         · over 100 000 Soviet troops in the country
         · "they found it impossible to subdue the ferocious Islamic
           guerrillas"
         · a drain on their resources and a blow to their prestige
         · the hostility of China
         · the suspicion of Islamic states all over the world
         · repeated condemnations by the UN
         -> these convinced Gorbachev it was time to pull out
         -> agreed: Russian withdrawal to begin on 15 MAY 1988, provided
            the Americans stopped sending military aid to the Afghan
            resistance movement
```

*Everything above the Afghan box is a reversal of Lesson 18; everything inside it is the beginning of Lesson 20. The phase has a single shape: the instruments of détente failed on the system they had not covered, a regional intervention removed the political basis for repair, and the intervention then became the reason for repairing it.*

### 1. What made these years a "second Cold War", and what makes the label useful

✅ **Fact:** the advanced owner marks the years 1979-85 as a second Cold War after Afghanistan and missile tensions, and the core account records that the next few years after 1979 are sometimes referred to as the second Cold War.

✅ **Fact:** the content of the phrase is given in one sentence: both sides spent the first half of the 1980s building up their nuclear arsenals.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the label is useful and can be over-used, so the qualification should be supplied in the same breath. ⚠️ What returned was the arms competition, the rhetoric and the mutual suspicion; what did not return was the structure of 1949. ⚠️ Three things were permanently different. ✅ China was no longer an ally of the Soviet Union but a state whose grievances against Moscow in 1984 were entirely about Soviet deployments, and which had exchanged ambassadors with Washington in 1979. ✅ The machinery of détente survived on the shelf - the hotline, the test ban, the outer-space agreement and SALT I were not repudiated, and negotiations resumed within six years. ✅ And the European frontier question had been settled in the sense that mattered, because Helsinki had recorded acceptance of the postwar frontiers. ⚠️ The precise formulation is that the second Cold War was a reversal of the *relationship* without a reversal of the *architecture*, which is exactly why it could end as quickly as it did.

### 2. The missile sequence, and why it came before the invasion

✅ **Fact:** détente did not proceed without setbacks, and this was especially true in 1979 when NATO became nervous at the deployment of 150 new Russian SS-20 missiles. ✅ NATO decided to deploy over 500 Pershing and Cruise missiles in Europe by 1983 as a deterrent to a possible Russian attack on western Europe. ✅ At the same time the US Senate decided not to accept a SALT 2 treaty which would have limited numbers of MIRVs.

✅ **Fact:** Lesson 8 supplies the technical background: the SS-20 of 1977 was the Soviet version of the MIRV, targeted on western Europe, less sophisticated than the American system and carrying three warheads; and the Cruise missiles developed by the Americans at the end of the 1970s were based in Europe and flew at low altitudes so as to penetrate under Russian radar.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the chronology within 1979 is the analytical point of this section, and it is worth spelling out because scripts routinely invert it. ⚠️ The missile competition and the Senate's refusal of SALT II both precede the invasion of Afghanistan in the account's own order. ⚠️ That matters for two reasons. ⚠️ First, it means the collapse of détente cannot be attributed to a single Soviet act: the central arms-control process had already failed, in a legislature, over a category of weapon. ⚠️ Second, it establishes that the failure was substantially *technical and institutional* - an uncovered system produced a deployment, a counter-deployment followed, and the treaty that would have covered the system was refused by a body that neither superpower's executive controlled.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** there is also a point about theatre here that is worth one clause. The SS-20 and the Pershing and Cruise missiles were *European* systems: weapons aimed at and based in the continent whose division had just been formally recognised at Helsinki. ⚠️ The second Cold War therefore re-militarised the one question détente had appeared to settle, which explains why the protest movements of the 1980s described in Lesson 8 were concentrated in western Europe - in West Germany, Holland, Britain at Greenham Common - and why their stated fear was that Britain could be almost destroyed by Russian nuclear retaliation if the missiles based there were ever fired.

### 3. Afghanistan: what the account says, and what it does not

✅ **Fact:** when the Russians invaded Afghanistan on Christmas Day 1979 and replaced the president with one more favourable to them, all the old western suspicions of Russian motives revived.

✅ **Fact:** by 1985 the USSR was seriously embarrassed by its involvement in Afghanistan. ✅ Although there were over 100 000 Soviet troops in the country, they found it impossible to subdue the ferocious Islamic guerrillas; it was a drain on their resources and a blow to their prestige.

✅ **Fact:** the hostility of China, the suspicion of Islamic states all over the world and repeated condemnations by the UN convinced Gorbachev it was time to pull out. ✅ It was eventually agreed that the Russians would begin withdrawing their troops from Afghanistan, provided the Americans stopped sending military aid to the Afghan resistance movement. ⚠️ **Date correction, declared.** The account gives 1 May 1988 for the start of the withdrawal; the verified record is that the Geneva Accords were signed on **14 April 1988** and that the Soviet withdrawal began on **15 May 1988**. This session uses 15 May 1988 throughout.

⚠️ **Declared boundary and evidence limit.** The internal politics of Afghanistan, the composition and organisation of the resistance, the channels and scale of external assistance to it, and the war's course and casualties are **not** recorded in the material used for this session, and **nothing about them is asserted here**. What the sources support is the international dimension: the date, the replacement of the president, the effect on Western perceptions, the troop figure, the four pressures and the withdrawal condition.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** within those limits the case is still highly examinable, because the four pressures form an unusually clear set. ⚠️ Two are material - a drain on resources and an inability to subdue the resistance. ⚠️ Two are diplomatic and are the ones usually missed: the hostility of China, which Lesson 13 shows listing Afghanistan first among its 1984 grievances, and the suspicion of Islamic states all over the world. ⚠️ The second of those is particularly important for this topic, because it identifies a constituency - the Islamic world - whose reaction was neither Western nor communist and which nonetheless constrained a superpower. ⚠️ Together with the repeated UN condemnations, it shows international opinion functioning as a cost even where it commanded no force, which is a finding this session has met once before, at Suez in 1956.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the condition attached to the withdrawal is the sharpest single fact in the section: the Russians would begin withdrawing on 15 May 1988 *provided the Americans stopped sending military aid to the Afghan resistance movement*. ⚠️ That clause states, in the account's own terms, that a third party's war was being supplied by the other superpower, and that ending the intervention required a bilateral bargain between the two principals. ⚠️ It is therefore the clearest documented instance in this session of the proxy mechanism operating in its strict sense - and it belongs beside the Angolan settlement of December 1988, where the resolution also came from a change in the international situation rather than from the local balance.

### 4. The Strategic Defence Initiative, and the problem it created

✅ **Fact:** US President Reagan, in office from 1981 to 1989, apparently gave the go-ahead for a new weapons system, the Strategic Defence Initiative, also known as "Star Wars", intended to use weapons based in space to destroy ballistic missiles in flight.

✅ **Fact:** the account records its later effect on negotiations: at Reykjavik in October 1986 the Americans responded to some extent to Gorbachev's proposals, though Reagan was not prepared to abandon his Star Wars project; and at the time of the INF Treaty in December 1987 there was still the stumbling block of Star Wars, which he was still not prepared to give up, even though it was only at the planning stage.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** SDI belongs in the same analytical category as the ABM in Lesson 8, and the connection is the most valuable thing a candidate can say about it. ⚠️ A system that destroys incoming missiles in flight reduces the attacker's confidence in retaliation - which, if it works, makes a first strike less costly for the side that possesses it. ⚠️ That is why a purely defensive-sounding programme was treated as destabilising by the other side and why it obstructed negotiations for the rest of the decade. ⚠️ The account's two qualifiers are also important and should be preserved: Reagan *apparently* gave the go-ahead, and the project was *only at the planning stage* when it was blocking agreement in 1987. ⚠️ A programme that existed mainly as a plan was nonetheless a real obstacle, which is a genuine finding about how expectations rather than capabilities drove this competition - the same finding Lesson 12 produced about the Cuban missiles not posing an entirely new threat.

### 5. The phase in one comparison

| Dimension | 1947-53, the first hardening | 1979-85, the second Cold War |
|---|---|---|
| **Trigger** | ✅ Soviet control of eastern Europe; the Truman Doctrine and Marshall Aid; Berlin; the Soviet bomb; China; Korea | ✅ The SS-20 deployment; the NATO counter-decision; the Senate's refusal of SALT 2; the invasion of Afghanistan |
| **Instruments** | ✅ Alliance-building, economic aid, rearmament, a war by proxy in Korea | ✅ Arsenal build-up on both sides; a planned space-based defence; a regional intervention |
| **Institutional machinery between the parties** | ⚠️ None; there was nothing to suspend | ✅ Hotline, test ban, outer-space agreement, SALT I and Helsinki all remained in place |
| **Third parties** | ✅ China allied to the USSR from February 1950 | ✅ China hostile to the USSR, its 1984 grievances all Soviet deployments; formal Chinese-American recognition from 1979 |
| **How it ended** | ✅ Stalin's death, the hydrogen-bomb balance, the discrediting of McCarthy, Panmunjom and the Austrian treaty | ✅ A new Soviet leadership from March 1985, Geneva, Chernobyl, Reykjavik and the INF Treaty - within six years |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third and fourth rows are why the second Cold War lasted six years and the first lasted more than a decade. ⚠️ There was standing machinery to resume rather than to invent, and there was no longer a second communist great power to consolidate the adversary's position. ⚠️ A candidate who makes that comparison has explained the phase's brevity rather than merely noting it.

### 6. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** calling 1979-85 a second Cold War concedes too much to the idea that the rivalry had ever stopped. Détente was always a tactical pause; the arms race continued through it, the interventions continued through it, and the label merely dramatises a fluctuation.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is partly right and its partial rightness is the best material for an answer. ⚠️ It is correct that competition continued during détente: ✅ the Russians continued to build up their nuclear armaments even during the thaw of the 1950s, ✅ SALT I did not reduce armaments, ✅ the SS-20 appeared in 1977 while détente was still running, ✅ and the American intervention record of Lesson 17 continued through the 1970s. ⚠️ But three changes between 1978 and 1981 are not fluctuations. ✅ The central arms-control process stopped, because the Senate refused SALT 2. ✅ Both sides built up their arsenals in the first half of the 1980s, which is a reversal of direction rather than a continuation. ✅ And a new weapons concept was authorised whose effect, planned or not, was to obstruct negotiation for the rest of the decade. ⚠️ The defensible position is that the rivalry never stopped and the *negotiated management of it* did, and that the second label refers to the management rather than to the rivalry.

### 7. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Why did détente break down at the end of the 1970s?
> ⚠️ "The Cold War of the early 1980s was a repetition of the Cold War of the late 1940s." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Assess the international consequences of the Soviet intervention in Afghanistan.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, keep the account's order: the SS-20, the NATO decision, the Senate refusal, and only then Afghanistan - and make the point that the arms-control failure preceded the intervention. ⚠️ For the second, the comparison table is the answer, with the third and fourth rows carrying the verdict. ⚠️ For the third, stay inside the evidence: the effect on Western perceptions, the four pressures of 1985, the withdrawal condition about American aid to the resistance, and the place of Afghanistan at the head of China's 1984 grievances. ⚠️ Do not narrate the war.

### Traps

- Making Afghanistan the beginning of the breakdown. ✅ The SS-20, the NATO counter-decision and the Senate's refusal of SALT 2 all precede it in 1979.
- Confusing the SS-20 and Cruise. ✅ The SS-20 is Soviet, three warheads, targeted on western Europe; Cruise is American, based in Europe, flying low under radar.
- Saying SALT II was signed and then broken. ✅ The US Senate decided not to accept it.
- Describing SDI as operational. ✅ It was still only at the planning stage in December 1987, and the account says Reagan *apparently* gave the go-ahead.
- Treating SDI as purely defensive and therefore harmless. ⚠️ Like the ABM, a system that intercepts retaliation lowers the cost of striking first.
- Narrating the Afghan war. ⚠️ The sources here support the international dimension only, and the limit is declared.
- Forgetting the withdrawal condition, or repeating the account's start date. ✅ The withdrawal was conditional on the Americans stopping military aid to the resistance, and it began on 15 May 1988 under the Geneva Accords of 14 April 1988, not on the 1 May the account gives.
- Treating the second Cold War as a full return to 1949. ⚠️ The machinery of détente survived, and China was now hostile to Moscow.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ 1977: the SS-20, the Soviet MIRV equivalent, three warheads, targeted on western Europe.
2. ✅ 1979: NATO nervous at 150 new SS-20s; NATO decides to deploy over 500 Pershing and Cruise missiles in Europe by 1983.
3. ✅ 1979: the US Senate decides not to accept a SALT 2 treaty which would have limited MIRVs.
4. ✅ 25 December 1979: the Russians invade Afghanistan and replace the president with one more favourable to them; all the old western suspicions revive.
5. ✅ The next few years are sometimes referred to as the second Cold War; both sides spent the first half of the 1980s building up their nuclear arsenals.
6. ✅ Reagan, 1981-89, apparently authorised the Strategic Defence Initiative, "Star Wars", to use weapons based in space to destroy ballistic missiles in flight.
7. ✅ By 1985 the USSR was seriously embarrassed in Afghanistan: over 100 000 troops, an inability to subdue the guerrillas, a drain on resources and a blow to prestige.
8. ✅ Four pressures convinced Gorbachev to pull out: the material drain, Chinese hostility, the suspicion of Islamic states all over the world, and repeated UN condemnations.
9. ✅ Withdrawal conditional on the Americans stopping military aid to the Afghan resistance movement; Geneva Accords 14 April 1988; withdrawal began 15 May 1988, correcting the account's 1 May.
10. ✅ SDI remained a stumbling block at Reykjavik in October 1986 and at the INF signature in December 1987, though only at the planning stage.
11. ⚠️ What did not return: the machinery of détente stayed in place, and China was now hostile to Moscow and recognised by Washington.
12. ⚠️ The formulation: the rivalry never stopped; the negotiated management of it did.

### Concept check

**Question:** Why did the second Cold War last six years when the first hardening lasted more than a decade?

**Model answer:** Because the machinery of negotiation survived it and the adversary's camp had changed. ✅ In 1947-53 there was nothing between the parties to resume: the confrontation was built from scratch through the Truman Doctrine, Marshall Aid, the Cominform, Berlin, NATO, the Warsaw Pact's precursors and Korea, and it eased only when Stalin died in March 1953, a hydrogen-bomb balance emerged by August 1953, McCarthy was discredited in 1954, and specific issues could be closed at Panmunjom and in the Austrian State Treaty of May 1955. ✅ In 1979-85 the hotline, the underground-testing agreement, the 1967 outer-space ban, SALT I and the Helsinki recognition of the postwar frontiers all remained in place; the breakdown was a refusal to ratify one further treaty, a build-up of arsenals and an intervention, not a dismantling of what existed. ✅ The second difference is China: in February 1950 a Sino-Soviet treaty of alliance had made Washington fear an advancing tide of communism, whereas by 1984 China's stated grievances against the USSR were Russian troops in Afghanistan, Soviet backing for Vietnamese troops in Kampuchea and the Soviet build-up on its frontiers, and ambassadors had been exchanged with Washington in 1979. ⚠️ So the resumption after March 1985 required a change of leadership and a reason - and the four pressures in Afghanistan supplied the reason - rather than the construction of a new relationship.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the second Cold War as proof that détente had achieved nothing. Its achievements were precisely what made the second phase short: agreements that had not been repudiated could be built on immediately once the political will returned, which is why negotiations resumed at Geneva within eight months of Gorbachev taking office.

### Visual: four years of negotiation, three years of collapse, one handover

```text

             |   missiles based in western Europe                       |
             | · STRICT VERIFICATION PROVISIONS so that both sides      |
             |   could check that the weapons were actually destroyed   |
             +---------------------------------------------------------+
             THE LIMITS, STATED IN THE SAME BREATH
             · at most only 4 PER CENT of existing stocks of nuclear
               weapons
             · the stumbling block of Star Wars, still not given up,
               though only at the planning stage
             · British and French weapons not included; Thatcher
               determined that Britain keep its own arsenal and planned

              |
   15 May 1988  Soviet withdrawal from Afghanistan begins
   Jun 1988  Reagan goes to Moscow to discuss the INF timetable
              |
   Aug 1988 - Dec 1991  THE COLLAPSE (stated here as outcome only)
   Poland: Solidarity's strikes force free elections, communists heavily
   defeated June 1989 · Hungary: free elections, communists defeated ·
   East Germany: Honecker overruled, government resigns by end 1989,
   the Wall breached, Germany re-united in the summer of 1990 ·
   Czechoslovakia, Bulgaria and Romania throw out their communist
   governments by the end of 1989 · multi-party elections in Yugoslavia
   1990 and Albania spring 1991 · by the end of December 1991 the USSR
   has split into separate republics and Gorbachev has resigned;
   communist rule in Russia is over after 74 years
              |
   >>>>>>>>>>  HANDOVER TO THE END-OF-COLD-WAR OWNER  <<<<<<<<<<
   the reasons behind the collapse, the transitions, the post-1991 order
```

*The diagram deliberately compresses the bottom band. This session teaches the ending phase of the rivalry - the negotiations that unwound the confrontation - and states the collapse as the outcome. The explanation of why communist systems fell belongs to the file that owns it.*

### 1. Why the resumption was possible within eight months

✅ **Fact:** détente gathered momentum again thanks to the determination of the new Soviet leader, Mikhail Gorbachev, in office from 1985 to 1991. ✅ In November 1985 he had a meeting with Reagan in Geneva; this went well and they issued a joint statement that "nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought". ✅ The signs were that détente was back on course.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the joint statement deserves a sentence because of what it does rather than what it says. ⚠️ It is a shared denial of the premise of the entire arms competition: if nuclear war cannot be won, then accumulating a war-winning capacity is irrational, and both governments had just spent five years doing exactly that. ⚠️ A jointly issued sentence of that kind is not a treaty and changes nothing materially; it establishes a shared vocabulary within which subsequent bargains become sayable. ⚠️ That is why it comes first in the sequence and why the substantive agreement follows two years later.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the speed is also explicable from Lesson 19's comparison. ⚠️ Gorbachev took office in March 1985 and met Reagan in November 1985 - eight months - because there was a standing agenda, a standing relationship of summitry established in the Brezhnev-Nixon years, and unrepudiated agreements to build on. ⚠️ The contrast with the decade required after 1947 is the measure of what détente had left behind.

### 2. Chernobyl: a domestic event with an international consequence

✅ **Fact:** in April 1986 there was a disastrous accident at the Chernobyl nuclear power station in the Ukraine. ✅ This caused a great wave of anti-nuclear feeling in the USSR, and Gorbachev decided that measures to reduce nuclear dangers were absolutely vital.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** this is the only place in the whole session where domestic opinion inside the Soviet Union is recorded as changing a Soviet leader's position on the central question of the rivalry, and it should be used for exactly that. ⚠️ Lesson 8 recorded protest movements in Britain, West Germany, Holland and the United States and the account's hedged verdict that they *perhaps* played a part; Lesson 15 recorded American public opinion forcing a change of policy in March 1968. ⚠️ Chernobyl supplies the eastern counterpart, and it did so through an accident rather than through a movement. ⚠️ The general observation available from all three is that nuclear weapons had a domestic political constituency on both sides of the division by the 1980s, which had not been true in 1949 - and that this is one of the underlying changes that made the ending phase possible.

### 3. Reykjavik and the obstacle

✅ **Fact:** in October 1986 Gorbachev invited Reagan to a summit meeting at Reykjavik and proposed a fifteen-year timetable for a "step-by-step process for ridding the earth of nuclear weapons". ✅ The Americans responded to some extent, though Reagan was not prepared to abandon his Star Wars project.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the proposal is the most radical arms-control offer recorded anywhere in this session and it is worth registering as such: a timetabled process for eliminating nuclear weapons entirely, from the leader of the state that had just spent five years building up its arsenal. ⚠️ The obstacle to it was a programme still at the planning stage. ⚠️ Whatever one concludes about the offer's feasibility - and the sources say nothing about that - the recorded fact is that the most far-reaching proposal of the period was blocked by an undeployed system, which is the strongest illustration in the topic of Lesson 8's finding that expectations about future capability drove this competition as powerfully as actual capability.

### 4. The INF Treaty: the first destruction of a class of weapons

✅ **Fact:** at the next summit, held in Washington in December 1987, a historic breakthrough was made: Reagan and Gorbachev formally signed the INF - Intermediate-Range Nuclear Forces - Treaty.

✅ **Fact:** all land-based intermediate-range nuclear weapons, defined as 300 to 3000 miles, were to be scrapped over the next three years. ✅ This meant 436 American and 1575 Soviet warheads, and would include all Russian missiles in East Germany and Czechoslovakia, and all American Cruise and Pershing missiles based in western Europe. ✅ There were strict verification provisions so that both sides could check that the weapons were actually being destroyed.

✅ **Fact:** however, all this amounted, at most, to only 4 per cent of existing stocks of nuclear weapons, and there was the stumbling block of Reagan's Star Wars, which he was still not prepared to give up even though it was only at the planning stage. ✅ Nor did the agreement include British and French weapons: the UK prime minister Margaret Thatcher was determined that Britain should keep its own nuclear arsenal, and planned to develop Trident missiles, which were more sophisticated than Cruise missiles.

✅ **Fact:** nevertheless, the account's verdict is emphatic: this INF Treaty was an important turning point in the nuclear arms race, since it was the first time any weapons had been destroyed.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the four qualifications and the verdict must be written together, because the examinable skill here is holding a small quantitative achievement and a large qualitative one in the same judgement. ⚠️ Quantitatively the treaty was minor: four per cent of stocks, with two nuclear arsenals excluded and a destabilising programme untouched. ⚠️ Qualitatively it was a break in kind, and the account identifies why: it was the first time any weapons had been destroyed. ⚠️ Every previous agreement in this session had capped, counted, relocated or restricted; SALT I in particular did not reduce the amount of armaments. ⚠️ A treaty that destroys warheads under mutual verification establishes that reduction is possible and observable, and both of those propositions had been open questions for forty years.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the strict verification provisions deserve their own clause in a strong answer. Verification requires each side to accept the other's inspection of its own military assets, which is a substantial surrender of secrecy by two states whose mutual suspicion is the subject of this entire topic. ⚠️ That is arguably the treaty's most significant feature, because it changes the information relationship between the parties rather than the balance between them - and an information relationship, once established, is available for later use.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** and the treaty's geography makes a point that closes the European arc of this session. ✅ The weapons to be destroyed included all Russian missiles in East Germany and Czechoslovakia and all American Cruise and Pershing missiles in western Europe. ⚠️ The first concrete disarmament of the Cold War was therefore the removal of nuclear weapons from the two halves of the continent whose division had begun the confrontation in Lessons 4 to 6 - and it happened two years before that division ended.

### 5. Afghanistan and the closing of the file

✅ **Fact:** it was agreed that the Russians would begin withdrawing their troops from Afghanistan, provided the Americans stopped sending military aid to the Afghan resistance movement; ⚠️ the account gives 1 May 1988 for the start, and the verified record gives the Geneva Accords of **14 April 1988** and a withdrawal beginning **15 May 1988**, which is the date used here. ✅ In June 1988 Reagan went to Moscow to discuss the timetable for implementing the INF Treaty.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the pairing of those two facts in the account's own sequence is the shape of the ending phase in miniature - a regional disengagement and a summit about implementation, in consecutive months. ⚠️ It also completes the reversal begun in Lesson 19: the intervention that destroyed détente in December 1979 was being unwound in the same period as the first treaty to destroy weapons was being implemented.

### 6. The collapse, stated as outcome, and the handover

✅ **Fact:** remarkable events happened in eastern Europe in the period August 1988 to December 1991: communism was swept away by a rising tide of popular opposition and mass demonstrations, far more quickly than anybody could ever have imagined.

✅ **Fact:** the process began in Poland in August 1988 when the Solidarity trade union organised huge anti-government strikes; these eventually forced the government to allow free elections, in which the communists were heavily defeated in June 1989, and revolutionary protests rapidly spread to all the other Russian satellite states. ✅ Hungary was the next to allow free elections, in which the communists again suffered defeat. ✅ In East Germany, communist leader Eric Honecker wanted to disperse the demonstrations by force but was overruled by his colleagues; by the end of 1989 the communist government had resigned, the Berlin Wall was breached, and, most astonishing of all, in the summer of 1990 Germany was reunited. ✅ Czechoslovakia, Bulgaria and Romania had thrown out their communist governments by the end of 1989, and multi-party elections were held in Yugoslavia in 1990 and in Albania in the spring of 1991. ✅ By the end of December 1991 the USSR itself had split up into separate republics and Gorbachev had resigned; communist rule in Russia was over after 74 years.

⚠️ **Boundary, stated once and observed.** ✅ The account itself refers the reasons behind the collapse of communism in eastern Europe to other sections. This session **states the sequence as the outcome of the rivalry it has been teaching and does not explain it**. The causes of the collapse, the internal transitions, the Soviet dissolution and the post-1991 order belong to the end-of-Cold-War owner. ⚠️ What belongs here, and what the next section supplies, is the effect of that outcome on international relations.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** one observation inside the boundary is permissible and valuable, because it concerns the rivalry rather than the transitions. ✅ Honecker wanted to disperse the demonstrations by force and was overruled by his colleagues; ✅ and the whole sequence proceeded without the Soviet military response that had ended the Hungarian rising in 1956 and the Czechoslovak reform of 1968. ⚠️ The owner's formulation of the ending is that Gorbachev did not use old-style Soviet force to preserve communist rule in eastern Europe. ⚠️ Given everything this session has taught about what maintained the eastern bloc - Lesson 4's secret police and Russian troops, Lesson 5's Cominform, Lesson 7's Warsaw Pact, Lesson 11's tanks in Budapest - the decision not to use force is the single fact that connects the negotiations of 1985-88 to the collapse of 1989-91, and it is the last thing this session says before handing over.

### 7. The six consequences for international relations

✅ **Fact:** the account records that many people in the West thought that with the collapse of communism the world's problems would miraculously disappear, but that nothing could have been further from the truth and a range of new problems surfaced. It then lists six.

| # | ✅ Consequence as recorded |
|---:|---|
| 1 | **The Cold War was over.** The former USSR and its allies were no longer seen by the West as the enemy. In November 1990 the countries of NATO and the Warsaw Pact signed a treaty agreeing that they were "no longer adversaries" and that none of their weapons would ever be used except in self-defence. Gorbachev must take much of the credit for bringing it to an end; his determination to work for disarmament broke the stalemate and impressed Reagan, who also deserves much credit for responding so positively |
| 2 | **New conflicts soon arose**, often caused by nationalism. During the Cold War the USSR and the USA kept tight control, by force if necessary, over areas where their vital interests might be affected; now a conflict which did not directly affect the interests of East or West would probably be left to find its own solution, bloody or otherwise. Nationalism suppressed by communism re-emerged: Czechoslovakia divided peacefully; war broke out between Azerbaijan and Armenia over disputed territory; there was fighting in Georgia; and most tragic of all was Yugoslavia, which broke into five states, with a complex civil war lasting almost four years until a ceasefire in November 1995 |
| 3 | **Supervision of nuclear weapons.** With the Russians and Americans less willing to act as policemen, there was a fear that countries with what the powers considered unstable or irresponsible governments might use nuclear weapons; one of the needs of the 1990s was better international supervision and control of nuclear, biological and chemical weapons |
| 4 | **Economic problems.** All the former communist states faced the collapse and intense poverty left by the command economies and the problem of changing to free-market economies; they needed a carefully planned and generous programme of financial help from the West, or nationalism and economic unrest could cause a right-wing backlash, especially in Russia, with the danger that Russia might sell nuclear weapons to unsuitable governments |
| 5 | **German reunification created problems.** The Poles were suspicious of a united and powerful Germany, fearing it might try to take back the former German territory east of the Oder and Neisse; Germany also found itself providing refuge for people fleeing disturbances elsewhere in Europe, with at least 16 000 refugees a month entering by October 1992, provoking violent protests from right-wing neo-Nazi groups |
| 6 | **Relations between the western allies changed.** The USA, western Europe and Japan had been held together by the need to stand firm against communism; now differences emerged over trade and over how far the USA and Japan were prepared to help solve eastern Europe's problems. During the war in Bosnia relations became strained when the USA refused to provide troops for UN peacekeeping forces. The overriding fact was that the USA was left as the world's only superpower, and it remained to be seen how Washington would choose to play its new role |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second consequence is the one that most deserves examination emphasis, because it states a mechanism rather than an event. ⚠️ Bipolar rivalry had suppressed certain conflicts by making every local dispute a matter in which one or both superpowers had an interest; when the interest was withdrawn, disputes that had been frozen were released, and there was no longer an actor with a reason to contain them. ⚠️ The account's phrase - left to find its own solution, bloody or otherwise - is the formulation to reproduce, and Yugoslavia, Azerbaijan-Armenia and Georgia are the named cases. ⚠️ It is also the direct answer to the commonest weak conclusion in this topic, which is that the end of the Cold War brought peace.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the sixth consequence closes the session's argument. ⚠️ The Western alliance had been held together by an adversary, and the adversary's disappearance changed it. ⚠️ Lesson 7 identified NATO's distinctive features as an advance commitment and a joint peacetime command; both were built against a specific threat, and the account records the strains that appeared when the threat vanished. ⚠️ That the United States was left as the only superpower is where this session stops, and the question the account attaches to it - how Washington would choose to play its new role - is the opening question of the topic that follows.

### 8. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** the ending phase is told here as a story about negotiations between two leaders, when the Cold War was actually ended by the people who came onto the streets in Poland, Hungary, East Germany and Czechoslovakia. The summits are the surface; the collapse is the event.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection identifies a real risk and is answered by the boundary rather than against it. ⚠️ This session does not claim that the summits caused the collapse; it teaches the summits because they are the ending of the *rivalry*, which is this topic's subject, and it states the collapse as an outcome whose explanation belongs to the owner that holds it. ✅ That owner's own account foregrounds exactly what the objection wants - Solidarity's strikes in August 1988, free elections that heavily defeated the communists in June 1989, and mass demonstrations across the satellites. ⚠️ Two points, though, must be conceded to the summits, and both are in the record. ✅ The negotiated phase produced a Soviet leadership that had publicly agreed nuclear war could not be won, had destroyed a class of weapons under mutual verification, and had withdrawn from Afghanistan - a government, in other words, that had already chosen negotiation over force in its external policy before it faced the choice internally. ✅ And Honecker's wish to disperse the demonstrations by force was overruled, without the Soviet response that had answered Budapest in 1956. ⚠️ The defensible formulation is that mass opposition supplied the force and the negotiated relationship supplied the absence of a Soviet veto on it, and that neither alone accounts for the outcome.

### 9. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Explain why the Cold War came to an end, and show how this affected international relations.
> ⚠️ Assess the significance of the INF Treaty.
> ⚠️ "The end of the Cold War solved fewer problems than it created." Discuss.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, the ending of the rivalry is the negotiated sequence from Geneva to the Afghan withdrawal, the outcome is the collapse, and the effects are the six consequences - and the boundary should be stated in one clause rather than crossed. ⚠️ For the second, hold the four qualifications and the verdict together: four per cent of stocks, two excluded arsenals, an untouched programme - and the first destruction of weapons, under strict verification. ⚠️ For the third, consequences two to six are the answer, and the phrase about conflicts being left to find their own solution, bloody or otherwise, is the hinge.

### Traps

- Crediting Gorbachev alone. ✅ The account gives him much of the credit and also much to Reagan for responding so positively.
- Saying the INF Treaty ended the arms race. ✅ It covered at most 4 per cent of existing stocks and excluded British and French weapons.
- Omitting the verification provisions. ✅ They were strict, and they allowed each side to check that weapons were actually destroyed.
- Treating Star Wars as irrelevant once the treaty was signed. ✅ It remained a stumbling block in December 1987 although only at the planning stage.
- Explaining the collapse of communism inside a Cold War answer. ⚠️ The end-of-Cold-War owner holds the reasons; this session states the sequence.
- Concluding that the end of the Cold War brought peace. ✅ New conflicts arose, often caused by nationalism, with Yugoslavia, Azerbaijan-Armenia and Georgia named.
- Forgetting the November 1990 NATO-Warsaw Pact treaty. ✅ The two alliances agreed that they were no longer adversaries.
- Missing the Afghan and INF pairing. ✅ Withdrawal from 15 May 1988; Reagan in Moscow in June 1988 on the INF timetable.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Gorbachev, Soviet leader 1985-91; Geneva, November 1985; the joint statement that nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought.
2. ✅ Chernobyl, April 1986: a great wave of anti-nuclear feeling in the USSR; Gorbachev decides measures to reduce nuclear dangers are absolutely vital.
3. ✅ Reykjavik, October 1986: a fifteen-year timetable proposed for a step-by-step process of ridding the earth of nuclear weapons; the Americans respond to some extent; Reagan will not abandon Star Wars.
4. ✅ Washington, December 1987: the INF Treaty signed.
5. ✅ Its terms: all land-based intermediate-range weapons of 300 to 3000 miles scrapped over three years; 436 American and 1575 Soviet warheads; all Russian missiles in East Germany and Czechoslovakia and all American Cruise and Pershing missiles in western Europe; strict verification provisions.
6. ✅ Its limits: at most 4 per cent of existing stocks; Star Wars still a stumbling block though only at the planning stage; British and French weapons excluded, with Thatcher planning Trident.
7. ✅ Its significance: an important turning point, since it was the first time any weapons had been destroyed.
8. ✅ Afghan withdrawal began 15 May 1988 under the Geneva Accords of 14 April 1988, correcting the account's 1 May; Reagan in Moscow in June 1988 on the INF timetable.
9. ✅ The collapse: Solidarity's strikes from August 1988; free elections and a heavy communist defeat in Poland in June 1989; Hungary next; Honecker overruled and the East German government resigned by the end of 1989, the Wall breached, Germany reunited in the summer of 1990; Czechoslovakia, Bulgaria and Romania by the end of 1989; Yugoslavia 1990 and Albania spring 1991; the USSR split and Gorbachev resigned by the end of December 1991, ending 74 years of communist rule in Russia.
10. ✅ Consequence 1: the Cold War over; the November 1990 NATO-Warsaw Pact treaty declaring them no longer adversaries; credit to Gorbachev and to Reagan.
11. ✅ Consequence 2: new conflicts, often nationalist - Czechoslovakia peacefully, Azerbaijan-Armenia, Georgia, and Yugoslavia's five states with a civil war to the ceasefire of November 1995.
12. ✅ Consequence 3: the need for better international supervision of nuclear, biological and chemical weapons.
13. ✅ Consequence 4: economic collapse and the transition to free-market economies; the need for Western help; the risk of a right-wing backlash and of nuclear sales.
14. ✅ Consequence 5: Polish suspicion of a united Germany over the Oder-Neisse territory; at least 16 000 refugees a month by October 1992; neo-Nazi protests.
15. ✅ Consequence 6: strains among the western allies over trade and over helping eastern Europe; the American refusal of troops for UN peacekeeping in Bosnia; the USA left as the world's only superpower.

### Concept check

**Question:** The INF Treaty covered at most four per cent of nuclear stocks. Why is it nevertheless described as an important turning point?

**Model answer:** Because it changed the category of what an arms agreement could do, and it changed the information relationship between the parties. ✅ Every earlier instrument in the rivalry had regulated rather than reduced: the 1963 hotline addressed communication, the test ban addressed the atmosphere, the 1967 agreement addressed outer space, and SALT I in 1972 decided how many ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs each side could have while, in the account's own words, not reducing the amount of armaments. ✅ The INF Treaty of December 1987 required all land-based intermediate-range weapons of 300 to 3000 miles - 436 American and 1575 Soviet warheads, including all Russian missiles in East Germany and Czechoslovakia and all American Cruise and Pershing missiles in western Europe - to be scrapped over three years, and the account's stated reason for calling it a turning point is that it was the first time any weapons had been destroyed. ⚠️ The strict verification provisions matter equally: two governments whose mutual suspicion is the subject of this whole topic accepted arrangements allowing each to check that the other's weapons were actually being destroyed, which is a surrender of secrecy rather than of advantage. ⚠️ The four-per-cent figure, the exclusion of British and French weapons and the unresolved Star Wars programme all limit the treaty's quantitative reach without touching either of those two changes of kind.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the treaty as the moment the arms race ended. It removed one class of weapon from one theatre; the stocks, the strategic systems and the planned space-based programme all remained, and what the treaty established was that verified destruction was possible - a precedent rather than a conclusion.
