### Visual: four non-military dimensions, and which lessons supply their evidence

| Dimension | ✅ Western instruments | ✅ Eastern instruments | Lessons supplying evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Economic** | Marshall Aid: over $13 billion to sixteen nations over four years, averaging about 2.5 per cent of their national income; Truman Doctrine aid to Greece and about $60 million to Turkey; wheat exports to Russia from 1972; economic destabilisation of targeted governments | The Molotov Plan of 1947 offering Russian aid to the satellites; Comecon co-ordinating their economic policies; aid to neutral states; the Czechoslovak arms deal with Egypt, September 1955; reduction of economic aid to China after 1956 | 5, 10, 11, 13, 14, 17, 18 |
| **Ideological** | Containment framed against communism as such; the domino theory; the defence of "free peoples" | Peaceful coexistence as a route to a communist-dominated world through recognised economic superiority; the Cominform's requirement of Russian-style communism; the revisionism charge against Khrushchev | 1, 5, 9, 10, 13, 15 |
| **Political and propagandistic** | Propaganda and a general policy of non-cooperation; the Fulton speech; McCarthy's charge that the State Department was "infested" with communists | Propaganda and non-cooperation; Stalin's February 1946 speech; the denunciation of Churchill as a "warmonger"; the denunciation of the UN as a tool of the capitalists after Korea | 1, 4, 9, 10 |
| **Societal and cultural** | CND from 1958, the Aldermaston marches, the 1980s demonstrations in West Germany, Holland and the USA, Greenham Common; American public opinion forcing the policy change of March 1968; the revulsion factors in Vietnam | Anti-nuclear feeling in the USSR after Chernobyl in April 1986; popular opposition and mass demonstrations across the satellites from August 1988 | 8, 10, 15, 19, 20 |

```text
   THE ASYMMETRY THAT RUNS THROUGH ALL FOUR ROWS
   WESTERN INSTRUMENTS operated mainly by TRANSFER and INCENTIVE:
   money moved to governments that then spent it under their own
   authority, and the political effect was expected to follow from
   prosperity
   EASTERN INSTRUMENTS operated mainly by PRESCRIPTION:
   aid came with a required economic model - industrialised,
   collectivised, centralised - and a required trading orientation
   inside the bloc
   >>> THE EXCEPTION THAT PROVES IT IS NOT A MORAL CONTRAST:
   outside its own alliance the Western instrument set included
   economic destabilisation, election rigging and worse (Lesson 17)
```

*The table is a consolidation rather than a new topic. Its function is to let a candidate answer the substantial minority of stems that ask about the Cold War as something other than a military and diplomatic contest, using evidence already taught.*

### 1. Why this dimension is examinable at all

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the definition in Lesson 1 already committed this session to it. ✅ The Cold War is defined by an absence of direct general war plus a list of substitutes, and three of the six substitutes - propaganda, economic pressure, and a general policy of non-cooperation - are not military at all, while a fourth, alliance-building, is only partly so. ⚠️ A candidate who has defined the topic by its instruments and then written a purely military and diplomatic answer has contradicted their own introduction. ⚠️ This lesson therefore does no new work of discovery; it reorganises taught evidence around the non-military substitutes so that they are available under pressure.

✅ **Fact:** the syllabus mapping reinforces the point from the other side. The General Studies Paper I clause names "political philosophies like communism, capitalism, socialism etc. - their forms and effect on the society", and the folder assigns that sub-clause to a different Core owner. ⚠️ The boundary is therefore firm and useful: the doctrines belong elsewhere; what belongs here is the record of two states that claimed them and the instruments through which the claim was pressed.

### 2. The economic dimension: two systems of instrument

✅ **Fact:** the Western instrument set is documented across this session. ✅ Truman Doctrine aid brought massive supplies to Greece, where the communists were defeated by 1949, and about $60 million to Turkey. ✅ The European Recovery Programme brought over $13 billion into western Europe over four years, to sixteen nations, fostering the recovery of agriculture and industry, with the account recording its humanitarian statement of purpose, its commercial benefit in lucrative markets for American exports, and its probable main political aim. ✅ During the same period communist parties in western Europe suffered electoral defeat, most notably in France and Italy.

✅ **Fact:** the Eastern instrument set is equally documented. ✅ The Cominform of September 1947 required Russian-style communism, with eastern Europe to be industrialised, collectivised and centralised, states expected to trade primarily with Cominform members, and all contacts with non-communist countries discouraged. ✅ The Molotov Plan of 1947 offered Russian aid to the satellites, and Comecon was set up to co-ordinate their economic policies.

✅ **Fact:** the competitive use of aid outside both blocs is also recorded. ✅ Khrushchev hoped to win neutral states over to communism by lavish economic aid. ✅ China offered friendship and aid to under-developed Third World countries in Asia, Africa and Latin America. ✅ And the cancellation of the $46 million Aswan grant in July 1956 shows aid being withdrawn as a coercive instrument.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the asymmetry in the code block above is the analytical payload and it is genuinely instructive rather than merely descriptive. ⚠️ A transfer instrument produces political effects indirectly - through prosperity, and therefore slowly and unreliably, which is why European historians could argue that the conditions for recovery were already in place and that the aid averaged about 2.5 per cent of national income. ⚠️ A prescriptive instrument produces political effects directly - through control of the economic model and the direction of trade - and therefore quickly, but at the cost of provoking exactly the resistance the Cominform met in Yugoslavia in 1948. ⚠️ Each instrument's weakness is the mirror of its strength, and stating that is a genuine comparison rather than a table.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the economic dimension also supplies the ending-phase link that Lesson 18 opened. ✅ The Soviet motive for détente included the crippling expense of keeping up with the Americans and the need to devote resources to raising living standards both at home and in the satellites, all of which were suffering economic difficulties. ⚠️ That is the rivalry's economic dimension acting on its diplomatic one, fifteen years before the collapse - and the deeper economic causes belong to the end-of-Cold-War owner, where this session leaves them.

### 3. The ideological dimension, kept inside its boundary

✅ **Fact:** the ideological content of the rivalry as this topic owns it consists of what each side claimed and how the claim shaped conduct. ✅ The basic cause of conflict lay in the differences of principle between communist states, whose system was based on collective ownership, central planning and state social policy, and capitalist or liberal-democratic states, operating on private ownership, private enterprise in the pursuit of profit and the preservation of the power of private wealth.

✅ **Fact:** the claim was operative on the Soviet side in a precise way. ✅ Khrushchev's peaceful coexistence did not mean abandoning the idea of a communist-dominated world; it would still come, but when the western powers recognised the superiority of the Soviet economic system rather than when they were defeated in war.

✅ **Fact:** it was operative on the Western side too. ✅ The Truman Doctrine committed the USA to supporting free peoples resisting subjugation, and to containing communism not just in Europe but throughout the world. ✅ The domino theory held that if one country in a region fell to communism it would quickly knock over all its neighbours.

✅ **Fact:** and the rivalry had an ideological dimension *inside* the communist world. ✅ The Chinese accused the Russians of revisionism - revising or reinterpreting the teachings of Marx and Lenin to suit their own needs - over peaceful coexistence and the claim that communism could be achieved by methods other than violent revolution.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the Sino-Soviet material is what prevents this section from collapsing into "two ideologies opposed each other". ⚠️ Ideology in this topic did not only divide the two blocs; it divided one of them, and it divided it precisely over the doctrine's implications for the conduct of the rivalry. ⚠️ The formulation available from that is worth having ready: ideological commitment did not produce unity even among states that shared the ideology, because what was contested was the doctrine's *strategic consequence*, and on that the two largest communist powers reached opposite conclusions.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the second general point concerns the relation between ideology and behaviour, and Lesson 2 supplies the formula: ideology determined the content and the interpretation; the security dilemma determined the dynamic. ⚠️ The evidence for the first half is that the instruments of each side reflected its own system - American aid to private economies, Soviet prescription of a planned model - and the evidence for the limits of ideology is the territorial quarrel that survived the doctrinal one in Lesson 13, and the alignment of a Marxist Vietnam with the USSR against a communist China.

### 4. Propaganda, non-cooperation and the two domestic fronts

✅ **Fact:** the account's definition includes propaganda and a general policy of non-cooperation among the means by which the rival powers attacked each other. ✅ Specific instances are recorded: Stalin's widely reported February 1946 speech; Churchill's Fulton reply and Stalin's denunciation of him as a warmonger; Molotov's characterisation of Marshall Aid as dollar imperialism; the communist world's denunciation of the UN as a tool of the capitalists after Korea; and the Russian flow of anti-Israeli propaganda before 1967.

✅ **Fact:** the domestic front inside the United States is documented through McCarthy. ✅ Senator Joseph McCarthy claimed that the State Department was "infested" with communists who were, in effect, working for the USSR, and Truman was anxious to show that this claim was preposterous - one of his four recorded reasons for intervening in Korea. ✅ Anti-communist feelings in the USA which had been stirred up by McCarthy began to moderate when he was discredited in 1954: it had become clear that he was something of a fanatic, and when he began to accuse leading generals of having communist sympathies he had gone too far; the Senate condemned him by a large majority and he foolishly attacked President Eisenhower for supporting the Senate. ✅ Republican feeling that the USA had lost an opportunity to destroy communism in China after Korea contributed towards some of the later excesses of McCarthyism.

✅ **Fact:** the domestic front inside the eastern bloc is documented differently. ✅ The Cominform required Russian-style communism and discouraged all contacts with non-communist countries. ✅ Across eastern Europe by the end of 1947 elections were rigged, non-communist ministers expelled, many arrested and executed and all other political parties dissolved, under the watchful eyes of secret police and Russian troops. ✅ Helsinki in 1975 recorded communist promises to allow their peoples human rights, including freedom of speech and freedom to leave the country.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the McCarthy episode is worth two sentences in any answer on the rivalry's domestic dimension, and the second sentence is the important one. ⚠️ The first records the phenomenon: a Cold War inside American domestic politics, with consequences for foreign policy that the account states explicitly in Truman's reasons for Korea. ⚠️ The second records its termination by an institution - the Senate condemned him by a large majority - and the trigger, which was that he extended the accusation to the generals. ⚠️ That is an institutional limit operating, and it is a genuine asymmetry with the eastern bloc, where the account records no comparable mechanism in this period. ⚠️ The asymmetry should be stated without triumph, because Lesson 17 records what the same system did outside its own borders.

### 5. The societal dimension: who could object, and what happened when they did

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** four episodes in this session record populations acting on the rivalry, and setting them side by side produces a genuine finding.

| ✅ Episode | ✅ What happened | ⚠️ Effect on policy |
|---|---|---|
| CND and the Aldermaston marches from 1958; 1980s demonstrations in West Germany, Holland, the USA and at Greenham Common | Mass demonstrations, rallies and annual marches for unilateral disarmament; no British government dared take the risk, preferring multilateral disarmament | ✅ The account's hedged verdict: perhaps the enormity of it all and the protest movements did play a part in bringing both sides to the negotiating table |
| American opinion on Vietnam, 1968 | Great pressure on the government to withdraw after the Tet offensive convinced many Americans of the hopelessness of the struggle | ✅ On 31 March 1968 Johnson suspended bombing, froze troop levels and sought a negotiated peace |
| Anti-nuclear feeling in the USSR after Chernobyl, April 1986 | A great wave of feeling following the accident | ✅ Gorbachev decided that measures to reduce nuclear dangers were absolutely vital |
| Popular opposition across eastern Europe, August 1988 onwards | Solidarity's strikes, mass demonstrations, free elections heavily defeating communists | ✅ Communism swept away far more quickly than anybody could have imagined |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the pattern is that societies could act on the rivalry in both blocs, and that the channel differed completely. ⚠️ In the Western bloc the channel was continuous and institutional - elections, legislatures, demonstrations tolerated over decades - and its effect was correspondingly incremental: a hedged contribution to negotiations, one reversal of a bombing policy. ⚠️ In the eastern bloc there was no continuous channel, and the effects when they came were discontinuous: an accident changed a leader's priorities in 1986, and mass opposition removed the governments themselves from 1988. ⚠️ A candidate who can state that contrast has a genuine comparative point about the two systems that is drawn entirely from this topic's own evidence and does not require importing claims from elsewhere.

### 6. Technology and prestige

✅ **Fact:** the technological competition is documented through the weapons sequence of Lesson 8 - the hydrogen bomb, ICBMs, SLBMs, ABMs, MIRVs, the SS-20, Cruise and the Strategic Defence Initiative - and through the satellite episode, whose detail is declared illegible in the copy held here and of which only one clause is used: the Americans again felt that they dared not be left behind and within a few months had launched an earth satellite of their own.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the prestige dimension is visible in that clause and in several others, and it is worth naming as a distinct mechanism. ⚠️ A state that must not be seen to be behind will invest in capabilities whose value is demonstrative rather than operational. ⚠️ The same logic appears in Lesson 12, where later historians noted that Soviet long-range missiles could already reach the United States so that the Cuban missiles did not pose an entirely new threat, and in Lesson 19, where a programme at the planning stage obstructed negotiations for years. ⚠️ Prestige and expectation were therefore not decorations on the strategic competition; they were among its drivers, and a strictly materialist account of the arms race cannot explain why undeployed or redundant systems mattered so much.

### 7. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** this lesson repackages material already taught. Economic instruments were covered with the Marshall Plan, ideology with the causes, propaganda with the 1946 speeches and protest with the arms race; a separate thematic lesson adds nothing.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection is right that the evidence is not new and wrong that the organisation adds nothing, and the reason is about examination performance rather than about history. ⚠️ Evidence learned inside a chronological narrative is retrievable only through that narrative: a candidate who has learned Marshall Aid as part of 1947 will produce it when asked about 1947 and may not produce it when asked about the economic dimension of the Cold War. ⚠️ Reorganisation by dimension creates a second retrieval route to the same facts, which is why this lesson exists and why it adds no new evidence. ⚠️ There is also one substantive product that the chronological lessons could not deliver: the transfer-versus-prescription asymmetry in section 2 and the continuous-versus-discontinuous channel contrast in section 5 are both comparisons across the whole period, and neither could be stated inside a lesson about a single decade.

### 8. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ "The Cold War was fought with economic rather than military instruments." Critically examine.
> ⚠️ Examine the ideological dimension of the Cold War and its limits as an explanation.
> ⚠️ Assess the effect of the Cold War on the societies of the states that waged it.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the first, do not simply agree: the correct answer concedes the enormous economic component, then insists that the military dimension set the boundary within which economic competition was safe, and closes with the transfer-versus-prescription asymmetry. ⚠️ For the second, the limits are the argument - the Sino-Soviet split over doctrine, the territorial claim that outlasted it, Vietnam's alignment with the USSR against communist China - and the boundary with the doctrine owner should be observed in a clause. ⚠️ For the third, the four-episode table is the plan, and the continuous-versus-discontinuous contrast is the verdict.

### Traps

- Defining communism, capitalism or socialism at length. ⚠️ The doctrine owner holds the sub-clause; this topic owns the geopolitical record.
- Treating Marshall Aid as purely economic or purely political. ✅ The account records a humanitarian statement of purpose, a commercial benefit and a probable main political aim.
- Presenting Comecon as the eastern Marshall Plan. ✅ Aid was the Molotov Plan; Comecon co-ordinated economic policies; the Cominform imposed the model.
- Treating ideology as a sufficient explanation. ⚠️ It divided the communist bloc as well as the two blocs, and a territorial claim outlasted the doctrinal quarrel.
- Claiming the protest movements produced détente. ✅ The account offers only a hedged perhaps.
- Omitting the eastern societal dimension. ✅ Chernobyl changed Gorbachev's priorities and popular opposition from August 1988 removed the governments.
- Treating McCarthyism as marginal. ✅ It is among Truman's four recorded reasons for intervening in Korea, and Republican feeling after Korea fed its later excesses.
- Supplying satellite details. ⚠️ That passage is illegible here; only the recorded American response is used.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Three of the six defining substitutes are non-military: propaganda, economic pressure and a general policy of non-cooperation.
2. ✅ The syllabus's doctrines sub-clause has a different Core owner; this topic owns the geopolitical record.
3. ✅ Western economic instruments: Greek supplies and about $60 million to Turkey; over $13 billion to sixteen nations over four years; wheat exports from 1972; economic destabilisation of targeted governments.
4. ✅ Eastern economic instruments: the Cominform's required model of industrialisation, collectivisation, centralisation and intra-bloc trade; the Molotov Plan; Comecon.
5. ✅ Competitive aid outside the blocs: Khrushchev's lavish aid to neutral states; China's friendship-and-aid offer; the cancelled Aswan grant of July 1956.
6. ⚠️ The asymmetry: Western instruments worked by transfer and incentive; Eastern instruments by prescription of model and trade direction - with the Lesson 17 qualification for conduct outside the Western alliance.
7. ✅ The ideological content: collective ownership, central planning and state social policy against private ownership, private enterprise for profit and the power of private wealth.
8. ✅ Peaceful coexistence aimed at a communist-dominated world through recognised Soviet economic superiority; containment aimed at supporting free peoples and containing communism worldwide; the domino theory.
9. ✅ Ideology divided the communist world: the revisionism charge against peaceful coexistence and non-violent routes to communism.
10. ✅ Propaganda instances: Stalin's February 1946 speech; Fulton and the warmonger reply; dollar imperialism; the UN denounced as a capitalist tool after Korea; anti-Israeli propaganda before 1967.
11. ✅ The American domestic front: McCarthy's charge of an infested State Department; Truman's anxiety to refute it as a reason for acting in Korea; McCarthy discredited in 1954 after accusing leading generals, condemned by the Senate by a large majority.
12. ✅ The eastern domestic front: rigged elections, expelled ministers, arrests and executions, parties dissolved under secret police and Russian troops; Helsinki's human-rights promises in 1975.
13. ✅ Societal action: CND and the 1980s demonstrations with a hedged effect; American opinion and 31 March 1968; Chernobyl and Gorbachev's decision; popular opposition from August 1988.
14. ⚠️ The channel contrast: continuous and institutional in the West with incremental effects; absent in the East with discontinuous effects.
15. ⚠️ Prestige and expectation drove investment in demonstrative capability - the satellite response, the Cuban missiles that added little, and a planning-stage programme that blocked negotiations.

### Concept check

**Question:** Using the aid instruments of both sides, explain why each bloc's economic strength was also its economic weakness.

**Model answer:** Because each instrument's mode of operation created its own characteristic failure. ✅ The Western instrument worked by transfer: over $13 billion of Marshall Aid to sixteen nations over four years, spent by recipient governments under their own authority, with the political effect expected to follow from prosperity - and the account records that communist parties in France and Italy were defeated electorally in the same period. ⚠️ Its weakness is that the causal link is indirect and therefore deniable, which is exactly the ground of the European historians' objection that recovery was so rapid that its conditions must already have been in place and that the aid averaged only about 2.5 per cent of the sixteen countries' national income. ✅ The Eastern instrument worked by prescription: the Cominform of September 1947 required not merely communism but Russian-style communism, with eastern Europe to be industrialised, collectivised and centralised, states expected to trade primarily with Cominform members, and contacts with non-communist countries discouraged, while the Molotov Plan supplied aid and Comecon co-ordinated policy. ⚠️ Its strength is directness - it could reshape an economy rather than hoping prosperity would do political work - and its weakness is that prescription invites refusal, which is why Yugoslavia was expelled in 1948 and why the same requirement of doctrinal conformity produced the charge of revisionism from China after 1956. ⚠️ In each case the property that made the instrument effective is the property that limited it.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the contrast as a moral comparison between generosity and control. The same Western system that transferred resources to allied governments used economic destabilisation against governments outside its alliance, and Nixon's reported instruction about the Chilean economy is in the same source base as the Marshall Plan.

---

## Lesson 22 - Periodisation, demand families, thesis templates and the conversion procedure

Progress: 22/22 | Stage: Synthesis | Subtopic: the five-phase periodisation and the mechanism that defines each; the seven demand families with their tests and prohibitions; five thesis templates; mark-scaled structures for 10, 15 and 20; the six handovers stated as one clause each; the complete factual-risk control list; the five-step procedure for converting a cold stem into a structure; and the five items to rehearse given that this topic owns no verified question

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
📚 Book context: queried - the advanced owner's five-phase periodisation and its instruction that answers become stronger when they show the Cold War as a sequence of differently structured confrontations; and the basic owner's answer architecture, comprising the seven-row directive and demand map, the qualified thesis templates, the mark-scaled structures, the verdict scaffolds and the factual-risk controls
🔍 CA search: not run for this lesson - the single topic-level query is recorded in Lesson 1
📰 CA found: None in last 6 months
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five phases, each with its own mechanism

```text
   PHASE 1  1945-53  HARDENING BLOCS: YALTA/POTSDAM TO KOREA
   MECHANISM: each side's precaution reads to the other as aggression;
   instruments are built, and each instrument produces a counter-
   instrument
   MARKERS: Yalta, Potsdam, Sovietisation, Truman Doctrine, Marshall
   Aid, Cominform, Czechoslovakia, Berlin, NATO, two Germanies, the
   Soviet bomb, NSC-68, Korea
              |
   PHASE 2  1953-62  PARTIAL THAW; BERLIN AND CUBA KEEP CONFRONTATION
                     ALIVE
   MECHANISM: relaxation is available where an issue can be closed;
   rigidity where the issue is who controls what
   MARKERS: Stalin's death, Panmunjom, McCarthy discredited, Austrian
   State Treaty, Warsaw Pact, Hungary, peaceful coexistence, U-2, the
   Wall, Cuba
              |
   PHASE 3  the 1970s  DETENTE AND TRIANGULAR DIPLOMACY
   MECHANISM: rivals cooperate where cooperation manages risk or
   ratifies the existing distribution, and only there
   MARKERS: Ostpolitik, the China opening, SALT I, summitry, wheat,
   Helsinki
              |
   PHASE 4  1979-85  THE SECOND COLD WAR
   MECHANISM: the uncovered system drives a new competition; the
   negotiated management stops while the architecture survives
   MARKERS: SS-20, Pershing and Cruise, the Senate and SALT 2,
   Afghanistan, arsenal build-up, SDI
              |
   PHASE 5  1985-91  GORBACHEV, ARMS CONTROL AND THE ENDING
   MECHANISM: a leadership concludes that the costs exceed the benefits
   and declines to use force to defend the system
   MARKERS: Geneva, Chernobyl, Reykjavik, INF, the Afghan withdrawal,
   1989-91
              |
   >>> BOUNDARY: the reasons for the collapse belong to the
       end-of-Cold-War owner
```

*A phase is defined by its mechanism, not by its dates. That is what the advanced owner means when it says answers become stronger by showing that the Cold War was not one frozen condition but a sequence of differently structured confrontations - and it is the single most reliable way to convert recall into analysis in this topic.*

### 1. The seven demand families, with their tests and their prohibitions

✅ **Fact:** the basic owner supplies a directive and demand map. Reproduced here with the session's lesson routes added:

| ✅ If the question says | ✅ It is really testing | ✅ Do **not** write | Session route |
|---|---|---|---|
| *What caused the Cold War?* | The relative weight of ideology, security and power, plus the security-dilemma logic | A blame verdict | Lessons 2, 3, 4 |
| *Assess the relative role of ideology and power politics* | Explicit weighting, not a list of both | "Both mattered" | Lessons 2, 3, 21 |
| *Discuss a crisis - Berlin, Cuba, Korea* | What the crisis revealed about how bipolarity worked | A crisis narrative | Lessons 6, 9, 12 |
| *Explain détente* | Why rivals cooperate without ceasing to be rivals | "Tensions reduced" | Lessons 12, 18, 19 |
| *Why did the Cold War end?* | Multi-causality: structural, economic, agency and choice | "Gorbachev ended it" | Lessons 19, 20 |
| *The Cold War and the decolonised world* | Local agency, non-alignment and the limits of the proxy label | Treating new states as pawns | Lessons 11, 14, 16, 17 |
| *Was it only a European conflict?* | Asia, Latin America, the Middle East and Africa as autonomous theatres | Europe only | Lessons 9, 13, 14, 15, 16, 17 |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the third column is the most valuable and the least used. Each prohibition names the default answer a candidate produces under time pressure, which means the map is a list of the seven most likely ways to write a mediocre script on this topic. ⚠️ Reading it before an attempt is worth more than reading any single lesson, because it converts a set of traps into a checklist.

### 2. Five thesis templates

✅ **Fact:** the basic owner supplies qualified thesis templates. Each is a defensible opening sentence for its family.

1. ⚠️ **Origins.** "The Cold War arose less from a single aggressor than from a security dilemma between two powers whose defensive measures were structurally indistinguishable from offensive ones - ideology determined how each read the other's moves."
2. ⚠️ **Ideology against power.** "Ideology supplied the interpretation, power supplied the stakes: without ideological difference the rivalry would have been negotiable; without the power vacuum in central Europe it would not have arisen at all."
3. ⚠️ **Détente.** "Détente was risk management, not reconciliation: it regulated the arms competition precisely because neither side expected the rivalry to end."
4. ⚠️ **The ending.** "The Cold War ended because Soviet economic stagnation, reformist choices in Moscow, mobilised societies in Eastern Europe and the decision not to use force converged - no single one of these was sufficient."
5. ⚠️ **The Global South.** "Newly independent states were neither pawns nor bystanders: they used superpower competition as leverage, and where they miscalculated, they paid for it in civil war."

✅ **Fact:** the owner also supplies verdict scaffolds for the close of an answer, including: on causes, a security dilemma read through incompatible ideologies, with Soviet defensive expansion producing Western containment, which confirmed Soviet suspicion, and the atomic asymmetry making every step feel irreversible; on ideology against power, that power politics created the confrontation while ideology made it non-negotiable and global; on geography, European in origin, global in operation, and decided in part by the refusal of the decolonised world to be organised by either bloc; on bloc unity, that it held only on the Western side, since by 1979 the two largest communist powers were arming each other's enemies and fighting each other's allies, which is why the American opening to China in 1972 was made possible by a quarrel inside the bloc containment was designed to face; and on the ending, that a reforming Soviet leadership concluded that the costs of the system exceeded its benefits and, decisively, declined to use force to defend it.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a template is only worth using if the body of the answer can prove it, so each should be paired with its minimum evidence. ⚠️ Template 1 requires two cases read from both capitals - the best pair is Marshall Aid and the Cominform. ⚠️ Template 2 requires one case where ideology changed an interpretation and one where power set the stakes - the February 1946 speech and the atomic asymmetry. ⚠️ Template 3 requires SALT I's coverage and its MIRV omission. ⚠️ Template 4 requires at least three of its four elements, and the Afghan pressures plus Honecker's being overruled are the cheapest two. ⚠️ Template 5 requires the SEATO count and the Angolan verdict.

### 3. Mark-scaled structures

✅ **Fact:** the basic owner's mark-scaled structures are:

| Marks | ✅ Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Thesis → **two** causes or **two** crisis cases with what each proved → one qualification → verdict |
| **15** | Thesis → causes → escalation 1945-53 → crisis phase → détente → verdict |
| **20** | All of the above → **plus** a non-European theatre with local agency, non-alignment, and the multi-causal end → verdict |

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the 20-mark row carries an instruction that is easy to miss: the additional content at 20 marks is not *more of the same* but a *different kind* of material - a non-European theatre, agency, non-alignment and multi-causality. ⚠️ A script that answers a 20-marker by writing the 15-mark structure at greater length has misread the scale. ⚠️ The practical rule is that the extra five marks are earned by a change of register, not by additional narrative.

### 4. The six handovers, one clause each

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a script that trespasses into another owner's territory loses time and gains nothing. Each of these should be executed in a single clause and then dropped.

1. **The Second World War.** "The war left two powers with capacities no other state possessed, and the terms of the settlement in dispute." → then move on.
2. **The United Nations.** "The organisation acted in Korea because of a Soviet absence, was effective at Suez and ineffective over Hungary." → its structure and performance belong elsewhere.
3. **China's revolution.** "A communist government was proclaimed in China in October 1949, and its alliance with the USSR in February 1950 shaped American threat perception." → the revolution itself belongs elsewhere.
4. **Decolonisation.** "A large array of new states emerged in the 1960s, most of which declined the bloc choice." → routes, cases and regional variation belong elsewhere.
5. **India's foreign policy.** "India was among the states that refused alignment, and the 1954 agreements placed the coexistence principles in a treaty preamble." → the doctrine and its evolution belong elsewhere.
6. **The collapse.** "Communist systems fell between August 1988 and December 1991, without the Soviet force used in 1956." → the reasons belong to the end-of-Cold-War owner.

### 5. The complete factual-risk control list

✅ **Fact:** the basic owner supplies factual-risk controls; this session's own additions from its lessons are marked as such.

| # | ❌ Do not write | ✅ Write instead |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | Détente ended the Cold War | It relaxed tension without ending rivalry |
| 2 | Korea was a pure civil war, or a pure proxy war | It was both: local initiative, external approval and materials, no direct patron involvement |
| 3 | The Berlin Wall solved the German question | It froze one aspect and symbolised continuing division |
| 4 | One summit ended the Cold War | It ended through longer structural and political change |
| 5 | Every Middle Eastern war was superpower rivalry | Local nationalism, territory, refugees, state security and oil had autonomous importance |
| 6 | Warhead counts, aid figures or casualty totals not in the sources | Only the finite list of quantities recorded in this session |
| 7 | Loose dates for the alliances, the Wall, Cuba, SALT I, Helsinki or INF | 1949, 1955, 1961, 1962, 1972, 1975, 1987 |
| 8 | India-specific non-alignment content in a world-history answer | One instance, then the cross-link |
| 9 | The communist world as a single bloc | The deterioration after 1956, the revisionism charge, the territorial claim, February 1979 |
| 10 | Definitions of communism, capitalism or socialism | The geopolitical record; the doctrine owner holds the rest |
| 11 | *(this session)* That Stalin ordered the Korean invasion | Kim pressed the idea; the USSR and China approved and supplied without wanting direct involvement |
| 12 | *(this session)* That Truman dropped the bombs to intimidate Stalin | Some historians hold this; reproduce the attribution |
| 13 | *(this session)* That the Marshall Plan saved western Europe | About 2.5 per cent of national income; communist popularity already declining |
| 14 | *(this session)* That the Cuban crisis was an unprovoked Soviet gamble | The Jupiter agreement with Turkey was signed in 1959, before any top-level Castro-Soviet contacts |
| 15 | *(this session)* That the settlement of 1962 was an unconditional Soviet retreat | Kennedy also promised not to invade and undertook to disarm the Jupiters, undisclosed |
| 16 | *(this session)* That Nixon initiated the opening to China | The Chinese invited a table-tennis team in 1971; the American act was to withdraw a UN veto |
| 17 | *(this session)* That Vietnam was one American war | Two wars, with a cancelled election in July 1956 between them |
| 18 | *(this session)* That Kassem or Nasser was a communist | Neither was; the operative criterion was alignment |
| 19 | *(this session)* That SALT I reduced armaments | It did not reduce them; it slowed the race, and omitted MIRVs |
| 20 | *(this session)* That the INF Treaty ended the arms race | At most 4 per cent of stocks, but the first destruction of weapons, under strict verification |
| 21 | *(this session)* That the end of the Cold War brought peace | New conflicts arose, often nationalist, with Yugoslavia, Azerbaijan-Armenia and Georgia named |
| 22 | *(this session)* That the Soviet Union helped the Arab states militarily in 1967 | It encouraged, propagandised and hinted; it sent none; it re-armed afterwards |
| 23 | *(this session)* Details of the Afghan war, the satellite episode, the Yalta opening, the 1973 ceasefire or the Iranian restoration | Those passages are illegible or blank in the copy used here and are declared |
| 24 | *(corrected here)* That the war with Japan ended on 10 August 1945 | 10 August is the surrender offer; acceptance announced 14-15 August; Instrument of Surrender signed 2 September 1945 |
| 25 | *(corrected here)* That Khrushchev signed the Austrian State Treaty | Signed at Vienna on 15 May 1955; Molotov signed for the Soviet Union |
| 26 | *(corrected here)* That Saddam Hussein became Iraqi leader in 1968 | Prominent in the Ba'ath regime as vice-president from July 1968; President of Iraq from 16 July 1979 |
| 27 | *(corrected here)* That the Soviet withdrawal from Afghanistan began on 1 May 1988 | Geneva Accords 14 April 1988; withdrawal began 15 May 1988 |
| 28 | *(corrected here)* That about 48 000 is the total of American deaths in Vietnam | That figure matches the hostile-death category; the verified official total is 58 220, with 47 434 hostile and 10 786 non-hostile |
| 29 | *(corrected here)* That the Helsinki Final Act was signed in July 1975 | The account says July 1975; the signature was on 1 August 1975, the conference having closed in the last days of July |
| 30 | *(corrected here)* Dating Comecon to 1947 | The held sources give no founding date for Comecon; leave it undated |
| 31 | *(corrected here)* That sixteen countries contributed troops in Korea | The USA and fourteen other named countries - fifteen contributing states in all |

### 6. The five-step conversion procedure

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** the following should be executable in ninety seconds from a cold stem.

1. **Sort the stem into one of the seven families.** The directive word usually decides it; if two families apply, the noun in the stem decides - "crisis" for episodic, "role" or "relative" for causal weighting, "world" or "Third World" for the decolonised family.
2. **Take the prohibition first.** Write the third column of the demand map in the margin. It tells you what the average script will do.
3. **Choose the thesis template and its minimum evidence.** One sentence, plus the two or three items that prove it.
4. **Choose the phase or phases.** A causal question needs phase 1; a crisis question needs the phase containing the crisis and the mechanism that defines it; an ending question needs phases 4 and 5. Name the mechanism, not only the dates.
5. **Plan the qualification and the verdict before writing the body.** The qualification is usually the counter-consideration column of an evidence table; the verdict is the scaffold that answers the directive, graded and, where possible, given a time-signature.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** step 5 is where most marks are won and lost. ⚠️ A verdict planned in advance shapes the selection of evidence; a verdict improvised at the end is usually a summary, and a summary is not a judgement.

### 7. What to rehearse, given that this topic owns no verified question

✅ **Fact:** the repository's routing ledgers return no General Studies question owned by this topic, as the verified-question section below records in full.

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a topic with no owned question is prepared by structure rather than by recall, and the following five items are the rehearsal set.

1. **The five thesis templates, verbatim.** They are the fastest available openings and each one already contains a qualification.
2. **The seven demand rows with their prohibitions.** Practise producing a correct structure from a cold stem using the five-step procedure.
3. **The five phases with their mechanisms.** A phase named with its mechanism converts chronology into analysis in one sentence.
4. **Six evidence clusters.** Causes and the security dilemma; containment's instruments; the crisis triad of Berlin, Korea and Cuba; the bloc's internal division; the decolonised world's refusal and its limits; and the détente-to-ending sequence.
5. **The thirty-one factual-risk controls.** These are what a first attempt gets wrong, and five of them - the Korean origins, the Turkish missiles, the SALT I omission, the SEATO count and the post-1991 conflicts - account for most avoidable losses.

### 8. Criticism and reply

⚠️ **Objection:** a synthesis lesson of templates and procedures teaches examination technique rather than history, and a candidate who has understood the topic does not need it.

⚠️ **Reply:** the objection would be right if the templates were substitutes for understanding, and they are not; each is a compressed statement of a finding that the preceding lessons established with evidence. ⚠️ The origins template states the security-dilemma finding of Lesson 2; the détente template states the risk-management finding of Lesson 18; the Global South template states the agency finding of Lesson 14. ⚠️ A candidate who has not done the work cannot defend any of them for more than a sentence. ⚠️ What the lesson adds is *retrievability under time pressure*, which is a real constraint and not a trivial one - and the second product, the phase-mechanism pairing, is a genuine analytical claim that the chronological lessons could only demonstrate and not state.

### 9. Examination use

**Probable framings this lesson serves**

> ⚠️ Any of the seven families, since this lesson is the conversion apparatus for all of them.
> ⚠️ "The Cold War was not one conflict but several." Examine.
> ⚠️ Trace the changing character of superpower relations between 1945 and 1991.

**Answer-use guidance.** ⚠️ For the last two, the five phases with their mechanisms are the whole answer, and the marks come from the mechanism sentences rather than from the markers. ⚠️ For any of the seven families, execute the five-step procedure and resist the pull of narrative.

### Traps

- Treating periodisation as a list of dates. ⚠️ A phase is defined by its mechanism.
- Writing the 15-mark structure at greater length for a 20-marker. ⚠️ The additional marks require a different register: a non-European theatre, agency, non-alignment and multi-causality.
- Using a thesis template without the evidence that proves it. ⚠️ Each template has a minimum evidence set.
- Improvising the verdict at the end. ⚠️ Plan it before the body and let it shape the selection.
- Crossing a handover boundary. ⚠️ One clause each, then move on.
- Answering the crisis family with a narrative. ✅ The demand is what the crisis revealed about how bipolarity worked.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Five phases: 1945-53 hardening; 1953-62 partial thaw with Berlin and Cuba; the 1970s détente and triangular diplomacy; 1979-85 the second Cold War; 1985-91 Gorbachev, arms control and the ending.
2. ⚠️ Each phase has its own mechanism, and naming the mechanism is what converts chronology into analysis.
3. ✅ Seven demand families with their tests and prohibitions, from the basic owner's directive map.
4. ⚠️ Five thesis templates: origins, ideology against power, détente, the ending, the Global South.
5. ✅ Verdict scaffolds on causes, on ideology against power, on geography, on bloc unity and on the ending.
6. ✅ Mark structures: at 10, thesis plus two items plus a qualification plus a verdict; at 15, add causes, escalation, crisis and détente; at 20, add a non-European theatre with agency, non-alignment and multi-causality.
7. ⚠️ Six handovers, one clause each: the Second World War, the UN, China's revolution, decolonisation, India's foreign policy, and the collapse.
8. ⚠️ Thirty-one factual-risk controls: ten from the owner, thirteen from this session's own teaching, and eight verified corrections and clarifications.
9. ⚠️ The five-step conversion procedure: sort the family, take the prohibition, choose the template and its evidence, choose the phase and name its mechanism, plan the qualification and the verdict.
10. ⚠️ The five rehearsal items, since this topic owns no verified question: templates, demand rows, phases, evidence clusters and risk controls.

### Concept check

**Question:** Convert this cold stem into a structure in five steps: "Assess the view that the Cold War was a European conflict with global consequences."

**Model answer:** Step one: this is the seventh demand family - was it only a European conflict - and the directive is "assess", so an explicit judgement is required rather than a survey. ✅ Step two: the prohibition is a Europe-only answer, so the margin note is "must carry Asia, Latin America, the Middle East and Africa as autonomous theatres". ⚠️ Step three: the template is the geography scaffold - European in origin, global in operation, and decided in part by the refusal of the decolonised world to be organised by either bloc - and its minimum evidence is the Truman Doctrine's worldwide formula naming Korea and Vietnam, the SEATO count of three Asian members, and the Angolan verdict. ⚠️ Step four: phases one and two carry the European origin and its export, and the mechanism to name is that a commitment defined by the type of threat rather than by a region had no geographical stopping point. ⚠️ Step five: the qualification is that the proposition's second half understates the theatres, because Korea, Vietnam and the Middle East had their own causes and their own initiators, so the consequences were not merely consequential; and the verdict, graded and time-signed, is that the conflict was European in origin to 1949, genuinely global in operation from Korea onward, and in several theatres shaped more by local actors than by either superpower. ⚠️ The plan is therefore: thesis, European origin, the doctrine's unlimited formula, three non-European theatres with their local causes, the refusal of the uncommitted, and the graded verdict.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "assess" as an invitation to agree with the proposition and illustrate it. The examinable work is locating what the proposition gets wrong - here, the word "consequences", which makes the non-European world passive - and the answer that scores is the one that corrects the stem's implicit claim while conceding what is true in it.
