# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - Origins and the hardening, Lessons 1 to 5

### Cumulative check A1 - assembling the causes into a weighted answer

**Question:** Assemble the explanation of the Cold War's origins in five ranked components, and state precisely what the atomic asymmetry contributed that the other four could not.

**Model answer:** The first component is the incompatibility of principle. ✅ The communist system rested on collective ownership, central planning and state social policy, the capitalist on private ownership, private enterprise for profit and the preservation of the power of private wealth; capitalist mistrust dates from the first communist government in 1917, and in 1918 the USA, Britain, France and Japan sent troops to help the anti-communist forces in the Russian civil war. The second is Soviet security policy. ✅ Stalin's aim was to use the military situation to strengthen Russian influence, taking as much German territory as he could and land from Finland, Poland and Romania, after a war in which he had expected and received a capitalist attack. The third is the Western reading of those acts. ✅ The West was alarmed at what it took to be Soviet aggression and believed Stalin was committed to spreading communism over as much of the globe as possible, and Truman toughened American policy after Roosevelt's death in April 1945. The fourth is the wartime legacy: ✅ the Second Front delayed to June 1944, the concealment of the bomb until shortly before its use, and the refusal to let Russia share the occupation of Japan. ⚠️ The atomic asymmetry is in a different category from all four. ✅ The account's own summarising clause is that above all, the West had the bomb and the USSR did not. ⚠️ The others are dispositions or completed grievances; the monopoly was a continuing condition that operated on every negotiation between 1945 and September 1949, making any Soviet concession a concession to the holder of an unanswerable weapon. ⚠️ That is why the owner's formulation is that it made every conventional concession look like a strategic surrender, and why the arms race began in earnest only once the monopoly ended.

**Misconception to avoid:** Adding the atomic monopoly to the list as a fifth grievance. It belongs to a different causal category - a standing structural condition rather than an attitude or a completed act - and treating it as one more item makes the ranking impossible.

### Cumulative check A2 - the instruments, taught as a chain

**Question:** Show that the containment sequence of 1947-49 was self-propelling by tracing one continuous chain from Greece to the completion of the iron curtain.

**Model answer:** ✅ Greek communists, helped from Albania, Bulgaria and Yugoslavia, threatened a monarchy that British troops could no longer afford to support, and Bevin appealed to Washington. ✅ Truman announced in March 1947 that the USA would support free peoples resisting subjugation by armed minorities or by outside pressures; Greece received massive supplies and the communists were defeated by 1949, Turkey received about $60 million, and the commitment was to containing communism not just in Europe but throughout the world, including Korea and Vietnam. ✅ In June 1947 Marshall's European Recovery Programme offered help wherever it was needed; sixteen nations drew up a joint plan by September and over $13 billion flowed in over four years. ✅ Molotov denounced it as dollar imperialism, the USSR refused, and the satellites and an interested Czechoslovakia were forbidden to accept. ✅ In September 1947 the Cominform required Russian-style communism and intra-bloc trade; the Molotov Plan offered Russian aid and Comecon co-ordinated policy; ✅ Yugoslavia objected and was expelled in 1948. ✅ In Czechoslovakia the refusal of aid prolonged food shortages, the communists were blamed, defeat was expected in the May elections, and in February 1948 they seized power using the unions and the police they already held. ⚠️ Each step is a response to the previous one, and no new cause is required after 1946 - which is the security dilemma of the origins lesson operating as a visible sequence rather than as a formula.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Truman Doctrine, Marshall Aid, the Cominform and the Czechoslovak coup as four separate developments to be listed. They are one chain, and the marks are in the links.

## Block B - Crisis management and the limits of the thaw, Lessons 6 to 12

### Cumulative check B1 - the operating rule, demonstrated three times

**Question:** State the operating rule of Cold War crisis management and demonstrate it from Berlin in 1948, Korea in 1951 and Cuba in 1962.

**Model answer:** The rule is that each side chose the option which maximised pressure while leaving the other side responsible for the first shot, and that this held even at extreme risk. ✅ In Berlin the Russians closed all road, rail and canal links to force a Western withdrawal by starvation; the West flew two million tons in over ten months, rightly judging that the Russians would not risk shooting down transport planes, while positioning B-29 bombers on British airfields. ⚠️ Neither side fired; each left the escalation decision to the other. ✅ In Korea, MacArthur argued after the Chinese counter-offensive of over 300 000 troops that the way to defeat communism was to attack Manchuria, with atomic bombs if necessary; Truman judged this would provoke a large-scale war the USA did not want, settled for merely containing communism, and removed the commander rather than the policy. ✅ At Cuba, advisers urged air strikes and General Maxwell Taylor a full-scale invasion; Kennedy alerted troops, blockaded the island against 25 Russian ships and demanded dismantling - and Khrushchev ordered the ships to turn back. ⚠️ In all three the side with local advantage declined the option that would itself have constituted an act of war. ✅ The underlying reason is the one the sources give for the whole period: the Americans were reluctant to use the bomb again unless attacked directly, and the Russians dared not risk such an attack.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading this as evidence of moderation or goodwill. It is a structural feature of confrontation under a nuclear ceiling, and the same governments were simultaneously conducting interventions elsewhere in which no comparable restraint operated.

### Cumulative check B2 - the boundary of the thaw

**Question:** Explain why Austria was settled in 1955, Hungary crushed in 1956 and Berlin unresolved throughout, using one principle.

**Model answer:** The principle is that relaxation was available wherever an issue could be closed without altering the distribution of control, and unavailable wherever the issue *was* the distribution of control. ✅ Austria could be closed: all occupying troops withdrawn, independence within the 1937 frontiers, no union with Germany, strictly limited forces and treaty neutrality barring both NATO and the European Economic Community - a settlement that removed a Soviet liability without creating a Western asset, and which the Russians accepted partly from fear of a West German-Austrian merger. ✅ Hungary was inside the perimeter that settlement protected: Khrushchev had no intention of relaxing Russia's grip on the satellite states, and the rising in Budapest was ruthlessly crushed by Russian tanks. ✅ Berlin was neither closable nor concedable: the city sat a hundred miles inside the eastern zone, two currencies could not coexist in it, and a Western withdrawal was read in Washington as the prelude to an attack on West Germany - so Khrushchev's demands of 1958 and 1961 were refused and the Wall followed the second refusal, after about 200 000 refugees a year and over three million since 1945. ⚠️ One principle, three outcomes, and the same leader in all three.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding from Hungary that the thaw was insincere. The Austrian withdrawal was irreversible and the lifting of the veto on sixteen new UN members could not be undone; a bounded relaxation with a rigid limit is a coherent policy, not a contradiction.

## Block C - The global rivalry and the refusal to align, Lessons 13 to 17

### Cumulative check C1 - dismantling the two-bloc picture

**Question:** Produce the four strongest pieces of evidence against the proposition that the Cold War was a contest between two unified blocs, and say what each one establishes.

**Model answer:** ✅ First, the Sino-Soviet deterioration after 1956: the Chinese accused the Russians of revisionism over peaceful coexistence and non-violent routes to communism, the Russians reduced economic aid, and the nineteenth-century territorial claim north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang became, once China softened towards the USA, the main bone of contention. ⚠️ This establishes that classical state interest outlasted ideology inside an ideological alliance. ✅ Second, February 1979: China attacked Vietnam, partly in retaliation for Vietnam's December 1978 invasion of Kampuchea which removed the Chinese protégé Pol Pot, and withdrew after three weeks, while Vietnam supported Russia. ⚠️ This establishes that communist states fought each other. ✅ Third, SEATO in 1954, where only Pakistan, Thailand and the Philippines joined, with the account concluding that many states wanted to keep clear of the Cold War and remain uncommitted. ⚠️ This establishes that the blocs could not recruit where local preferences ran the other way. ✅ Fourth, Suez in 1956, where Britain and France went to war against explicit American preference and were compelled to stop by American and Soviet pressure together, leaving Britain unable to follow a foreign policy independently of the USA. ⚠️ This establishes both that Western members could act independently and that the cost of doing so was prohibitive. ⚠️ The composite finding is the owner's: bipolarity described the distribution of weapons, not the distribution of loyalty.

**Misconception to avoid:** Over-correcting into a claim that the blocs were fictions. Soviet control of eastern Europe was enforced with troops and police, NATO's advance commitment and joint command were real, and the Warsaw Pact existed; the finding is about cohesion, not about existence.

### Cumulative check C2 - the criterion problem

**Question:** Using Egypt, Iraq and Laos, show that the operative test in American policy towards uncommitted states frequently slipped from communism to alignment, and state the limit of that finding.

**Model answer:** ✅ In Egypt, Nasser bought arms from Czechoslovakia in September 1955 while outside both alliances, and the recorded criterion was that any country not part of the Western alliance which bought arms from eastern Europe was, in American eyes, just as bad as a communist country; the $46 million Aswan grant was cancelled in July 1956 to force him away from his new links. ✅ In Iraq, the account states explicitly that General Kassem was not a communist; what made Washington uneasy was that freedom and openness encouraged the growth of the Iraqi Communist Party and that in 1960 he was involved in setting up OPEC, which aimed to break the control of western oil companies - and the CIA campaign included encouraging a Turkish invasion, financing Kurdish guerrillas and attempting assassination, culminating in the 1963 coup backed by the CIA and Britain. ✅ In Laos, the Pathet Lao was a left-wing nationalist party which at first showed itself willing to take part in coalition governments to bring about peaceful social change; it was classified as dangerously communist, all left-wingers were removed from important positions by 1960, and the intervention strengthened the resolve of the left, which took power peacefully in December 1975. ⚠️ The account's own generalisation covers all three: regimes labelled communist and targeted for removal were often simply pursuing policies which went against American interests. ⚠️ The limit is that the concern was not fabricated - the Iraqi Communist Party was growing and the Pathet Lao was a left-wing party - so the finding is about the slippage of the test, not about the invention of the threat.

**Misconception to avoid:** Turning this into a general claim that anti-communism was a pretext throughout. The same session records genuine communist advances in China in 1949, in Korea in 1950 and in Indo-China, and a finding about criterion slippage in particular cases cannot be generalised into a statement about motive across the period.

## Block D - Détente, reversal and the ending, Lessons 18 to 22

### Cumulative check D1 - why cooperation and rivalry were compatible

**Question:** Explain, with reference to at least four instruments, why the two powers could sign agreements throughout the 1960s and 1970s without either reducing its hostility.

**Model answer:** Because every instrument either managed risk or ratified an existing distribution, and none altered the distribution. ✅ The 1963 hotline established swift consultation between Moscow and Washington and touched no weapon. ✅ The 1963 test ban restricted testing to underground to avoid further atmospheric pollution and touched no arsenal. ✅ The 1967 agreement banned nuclear weapons in outer space, which neither side had deployed there. ✅ SALT I in 1972 decided how many ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs each side could have and, in the account's own words, did not reduce the amount of armaments but did slow down the arms race - and left MIRVs uncovered. ✅ Helsinki in July 1975 recorded acceptance by the USA, Canada, the USSR and most European states of the frontiers drawn after the Second World War, thus recognising the division of Germany, against communist promises of human rights including freedom of speech and freedom to leave the country. ⚠️ Not one of these required either party to surrender a position it held. ⚠️ That is why they were signable by adversaries and also why they provided no protection when the distribution began to change: the uncovered MIRV produced the SS-20, the SS-20 produced the NATO decision to deploy over 500 Pershing and Cruise missiles by 1983, the US Senate refused the SALT 2 treaty that would have limited MIRVs, and the invasion of Afghanistan on Christmas Day 1979 revived all the old suspicions.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that détente was therefore worthless. It reduced the probability of accidental catastrophe, survived the fall of Saigon in 1975 without either communist power boasting about it, and left an architecture that allowed negotiations to resume within eight months of Gorbachev's accession.

### Cumulative check D2 - the multi-causal ending

**Question:** Explain the end of the Cold War in four converging elements, and state why no single one of them is sufficient.

**Model answer:** ✅ The first is the negotiated sequence: Geneva in November 1985 with the joint statement that nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought; Reykjavik in October 1986 with a fifteen-year proposal for ridding the earth of nuclear weapons; and the INF Treaty of December 1987 scrapping all land-based intermediate-range weapons of 300 to 3000 miles - 436 American and 1575 Soviet warheads - under strict verification, the first time any weapons had been destroyed. ✅ The second is the material and diplomatic cost of Afghanistan: over 100 000 Soviet troops unable to subdue the guerrillas, a drain on resources and prestige, Chinese hostility, the suspicion of Islamic states all over the world and repeated UN condemnations, producing a withdrawal that began on 15 May 1988, conditional on the Americans ceasing military aid to the resistance. ✅ The third is mobilised society in eastern Europe: Solidarity's strikes from August 1988, free elections heavily defeating the communists in June 1989, and the rapid spread of protest across the satellites. ✅ The fourth is the decision not to use force: Honecker was overruled when he wished to disperse the demonstrations, and the owner records that Gorbachev did not use old-style Soviet force to preserve communist rule in eastern Europe. ⚠️ None is sufficient alone: negotiation without a domestic challenge would have produced arms control inside an unchanged system, as it had in 1972; a domestic challenge without the decision not to use force would have produced Budapest in 1956; and the Afghan cost without the other three would have produced a withdrawal and nothing more. ⚠️ The deeper economic causes belong to the end-of-Cold-War owner and are cross-linked rather than duplicated.

**Misconception to avoid:** Writing that Gorbachev ended the Cold War. He is credited by the account with much of the credit, and Reagan with much for responding positively, but a single-agency explanation cannot accommodate Solidarity, the Afghan pressures or the mobilisation of populations across six states.

### Cumulative check D3 - the whole topic in one judgement

**Question:** In a single argued paragraph, say what the Cold War was and what determined its course.

**Model answer:** ✅ It was a period in which two states that could destroy each other did not fight each other directly in an open general war, and instead confronted each other through propaganda, arms competition, alliance-building, economic pressure, a general policy of non-cooperation and limited wars fought by others. ⚠️ Three things determined its course. ✅ The first is the double reading: each side interpreted its own acts as defensive and the other's as aggressive, so that every instrument produced a counter-instrument without any new cause being required after 1946. ✅ The second is the nuclear ceiling: the Americans were reluctant to use the bomb again unless attacked directly and the Russians dared not risk such an attack, which raised the threshold for direct conflict and lowered the cost of conflict conducted through others - Korea, Vietnam and Afghanistan are inside the period, not exceptions to it. ⚠️ The third is that neither the blocs nor the world outside them behaved as bipolarity predicts: ✅ the two largest communist powers quarrelled from 1956 and fought each other's allies by 1979, ✅ only three Asian states joined SEATO in 1954, ✅ and in Angola, outside interference made much worse a conflict that would have produced fighting anyway. ⚠️ The defensible summary is that the rivalry was European in origin, global in operation, restrained at its centre by the weapons that defined it, and shaped at its edges by states that refused to be organised by either side.

**Misconception to avoid:** Ending on the proposition that the Cold War kept the peace. The principals avoided direct war; the wars they conducted through others killed on a scale that the account records in Korea and Vietnam, and a judgement that omits that has described only half of what the definition contains.

---

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ These seven questions are this session's own. **None is a previous-year question**, none is represented as one, and no owned verified question exists for this topic. Each model answer is written inside the stated word ceiling and carries a scoring note identifying the single feature that earns the marks.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ Examine why the Berlin blockade of 1948-49 did not become a war.

### Model answer

Because both sides chose instruments that left the first shot to the other. The dispute was structural: three western zones merged into one economic unit prospering on Marshall Aid, a Soviet zone drained as a satellite, and a new western currency in June 1948 inside a city a hundred miles within the eastern zone. Moscow closed road, rail and canal links to force withdrawal by starvation rather than seizing the city. Washington, convinced retreat would precede an attack on West Germany, flew in two million tons over ten months for 2.5 million Berliners, judging correctly that transport aircraft would not be shot down, while positioning B-29 bombers on British airfields. Each option maximised pressure without constituting an act of war. The outcome confirmed the period's operating rule and produced NATO, because victory had exposed western military unreadiness rather than concealing it.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from naming the decision rule shared by both governments, and from the closing inversion that a crisis the West won increased rather than reduced its insecurity.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

> ⚠️ "Non-alignment was indecision rather than policy." Comment.

### Model answer

The evidence refuses the proposition. Newly emerging states wished to remain neutral or non-aligned in the struggle between communism and capitalism, and at Algiers in 1973 their leaders declared themselves a Third World, suspicious of the motives of both systems and resentful of economic dependence on wealthy powers. The refusal is countable: when SEATO was created in 1954 only Pakistan, Thailand and the Philippines joined, and the obvious conclusion was that many states wanted to keep clear of the Cold War. Nor was it neutrality: Austria's treaty neutrality carried legal disabilities barring NATO and the European Economic Community, whereas non-alignment reserved independent judgement case by case. Its cost was exposure - an Egyptian arms purchase from Czechoslovakia in 1955 produced an American classification treating such states as equivalent to communist ones, and the Aswan grant was cancelled. It was an effective instrument of sovereignty and a weak instrument of protection.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from converting an abstract claim into a counted refusal, and from the neutrality distinction that most scripts collapse.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ Assess the relative role of ideology and power politics in the origins of the Cold War.

### Model answer

Power created the confrontation; ideology made it non-negotiable and global. The material position is straightforward. Two states emerged with capacities no other possessed: the USA with the largest navy and air force and the bomb, the USSR with the largest army. Central Europe was a vacuum, and Stalin used the military situation to strengthen Russian influence, taking German territory and land from Finland, Poland and Romania. A vacuum between unequal victors produces rivalry without any doctrine.

Ideology determined how that rivalry was read. Capitalist mistrust dated from 1917 and had produced intervention in 1918; Stalin expected another attempt to destroy communism and was invaded in 1941. The West believed he was committed to spreading communism as widely as possible. Each therefore read its own precautions as defensive and the other's as aggressive, and every act could be read two ways - Marshall Aid as recovery or as dollar imperialism.

The atomic monopoly belongs to the power column and weighted it decisively: above all, the West had the bomb and the USSR did not, which made every conventional concession resemble a strategic surrender until September 1949.

Two qualifications follow. Ideology had not prevented the wartime alliance, so it cannot be sufficient. And the account records an asymmetry of method: it was Stalin's means of gaining control in eastern Europe, not the existence of a sphere Churchill had conceded in 1944, that upset the West. Remove ideology and one gets a negotiable great-power rivalry; remove the vacuum and one gets an argument.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from weighting rather than listing, from placing the atomic monopoly inside the power column, and from conceding the wartime alliance as the limit on the ideological claim.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ How far did the Korean War change the character of the Cold War?

### Model answer

It changed its geography, its membership and its instruments, while leaving its frontier where it began.

Before June 1950 containment was a European policy with a global formula. The Truman Doctrine had committed the USA to supporting free peoples resisting subjugation by armed minorities or outside pressures, and to containing communism throughout the world; the instruments, however, were Greek supplies, Turkish aid, Marshall money and NATO. Korea made the formula operational. American troops were ordered from Japan before the UN had decided; the Security Council acted only because the Russian delegation was boycotting over Chinese representation; fifteen states contributed, though most troops were American.

Membership changed next. The Chinese counter-offensive of over 300 000 troops after UN forces reached the Yalu prevented unification under American influence and established China as a world power, permanently straining American relations with Beijing as well as Moscow.

The instruments changed last and most durably. Alliance-building appeared in Asia: agreements with Australia and New Zealand in 1951 and SEATO in 1954, which only three Asian states joined. Simultaneously China offered friendship and aid to under-developed countries and signed peaceful coexistence agreements with India and Burma in 1954, so a competition for the uncommitted began that year.

Two limits qualify the verdict. The armistice restored roughly the pre-war line, so nothing territorial changed; and Truman's refusal to attack Manchuria, with MacArthur removed rather than the policy, showed the nuclear ceiling constraining escalation as before. The war globalised the rivalry without altering its central restraint.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from separating three dimensions of change from the unchanged frontier, and from using SEATO's recruitment failure and China's counter-offer as one paired piece of evidence.

## Question 5 - 15 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ "Détente relaxed tension without ending rivalry." Critically examine.

### Model answer

The statement is accurate and incomplete; it describes the outcome without explaining why no other was available.

Détente was real. From fear of a catastrophic nuclear war in which there could be no winner, and from motives peculiar to each party - crippling Soviet expense and unrest after Prague in 1968 and in Poland, American recognition that military power had limits after Vietnam, Chinese fear of isolation, West European fear of the front line and Brandt's Ostpolitik - four parties converged on the same preference. The instruments followed: the hotline and the underground-testing agreement in 1963, the outer-space ban of 1967, SALT I in 1972, three Brezhnev-Nixon summits, wheat exports and Helsinki in 1975.

The limitation is structural rather than accidental. Every instrument either managed risk or ratified an existing distribution. SALT I decided how many ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs each side could have and, in the account's words, did not reduce armaments though it slowed the race. Helsinki recognised the postwar frontiers, and therefore the division of Germany, against declaratory human-rights promises. Agreements costing neither party a position could be signed by adversaries; they could not survive a change in the positions.

The collapse proves it. SALT I had omitted MIRVs; the SS-20 followed in 1977; NATO decided in 1979 to deploy over 500 Pershing and Cruise missiles by 1983; the Senate refused SALT 2, which would have limited MIRVs; and Afghanistan revived the old suspicions in December 1979. Détente failed precisely where its central instrument had been incomplete.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from explaining the compatibility of cooperation and rivalry structurally, and from tracing the collapse back to the specific omission in the 1972 agreement.

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ Critically examine the proposition that the Cold War was a bipolar conflict between two unified blocs.

### Model answer

Bipolarity described the distribution of weapons, not of loyalty.

Two powers dominated militarily, and the alliance architecture was real: NATO in April 1949 converted an attack on one into an attack on all under a joint peacetime command, and the Warsaw Pact followed in 1955 after West Germany joined NATO.

Four bodies of evidence refuse the rest. First, the eastern bloc divided early and permanently. Yugoslavia's communist government was legally elected in 1945 on resistance prestige and was expelled from the Cominform in 1948 while remaining communist. Sino-Soviet relations deteriorated steadily after 1956, with the revisionism charge over peaceful coexistence, reduced Soviet aid, and a territorial claim north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang which became the main bone of contention. By February 1979 China attacked Vietnam, which supported Russia, three months after Vietnam removed the Chinese protégé Pol Pot from Kampuchea.

Second, the quarrel reshaped the whole rivalry: a Chinese invitation in 1971 produced the UN seat, Nixon's visit in 1972 and, by the decade's end, both communist powers vying for American support against each other.

Third, the uncommitted world refused recruitment. Only Pakistan, Thailand and the Philippines joined SEATO in 1954, and at Algiers in 1973 Third World leaders declared themselves non-aligned between the two worlds.

Fourth, Western unity was conditional. At Suez Britain and France acted against American preference and were stopped by American and Soviet pressure together, after which Britain could not follow an independent foreign policy.

The rivalry was bipolar in capability and plural in politics.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from conceding the military bipolarity precisely before dismantling the political claim, and from using February 1979 as a single decisive fact rather than as an illustration.

## Question 7 - 20 marks, 250 words

> ⚠️ To what extent did the Cold War create the conflicts of the decolonised world? Discuss with reference to at least three cases.

### Model answer

Superpower rivalry internationalised conflicts it did not create, and the distinction is measurable case by case.

In Egypt the causal arrow ran outward. Nasser's objectives were the end of the British base when the 1936 agreement expired, support for Algerian independence, opposition to the Baghdad Pact and pressure on Israel. He bought arms from Czechoslovakia in September 1955, and the recorded American criterion was that a state outside the Western alliance buying eastern-bloc arms was just as bad as a communist country. The Aswan grant was cancelled; nationalisation and war followed; Russian aid replaced American aid - the outcome the policy had been designed to prevent.

In Angola the account's verdict is decisive: there would have been fighting and bloodshed anyway between the MPLA, UNITA and the FNLA after independence in 1975, but outside interference and the extension of the Cold War certainly made the conflict much worse. American backing for the FNLA, Cuban troops, South African invasion via Namibia and Zairean intervention prolonged rather than caused it, and settlement came in December 1988 only when the international situation changed.

In Vietnam the second war began with a cancelled election. Geneva had divided the country temporarily at the 17th parallel pending all-Vietnam elections in July 1956; Ngo refused to prepare them and the USA did not press him for fear of a communist victory, which the account calls a violation of the agreement.

Superpower involvement supplied weapons, duration and intensity. Local nationalism, land, religion and state formation supplied the conflicts.

⚠️ **Scoring note:** the mark comes from the three-part structure - causal direction, amplification and legitimacy - and from quoting the Angolan verdict's concession before its claim.

---

# REMEDIATION

## Error map

⚠️ Each row is an error observed in scripts on this topic, the correction with its evidence, and the lesson that repairs it.

| # | The error | ✅ The correction | Repair |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | "The Cold War was a war" | No direct general war between the principals; propaganda, arms competition, alliances, economic pressure, non-cooperation and limited wars instead | L1 |
| 2 | "The Cold War was a long peace" | Korea, Vietnam and Afghanistan are inside the period, not exceptions to it | L1, L9, L15, L19 |
| 3 | "It began in 1945" / "in 1947" without qualification | State which marker is used and why; the periodisation is a choice | L1 §5, L22 |
| 4 | Defining communism and capitalism at length | The doctrine sub-clause has a different Core owner; this topic owns the geopolitical record | L1 §3, L21 §1 |
| 5 | "Britain was a superpower" | Two only: the USA with the largest navy and air force and the bomb; the USSR with the largest army | L1 §2 |
| 6 | "Containment meant destroying communism" | Kennan advised containment by political, economic and diplomatic means; Truman settled for merely containing in Korea | L1, L9 §5 |
| 7 | Listing five causes without weighting | The directive is almost always "assess" or "how far"; a list scores as narration | L2 §5 |
| 8 | "Ideological hostility began in 1945" | Capitalist mistrust dates from 1917 and produced intervention in 1918 | L2 §1 |
| 9 | "Truman dropped the bombs to intimidate Stalin" | Attributed by the account to some historians; reproduce the attribution | L2 §3 |
| 10 | "Stalin had a blueprint for world revolution" | He took as much as he could get away with - opportunism, on the post-revisionist reading | L2 §2, L3 §3 |
| 11 | "The security dilemma means both sides were equally to blame" | It is a causal claim; the account separately records an asymmetry of method | L2 §7 |
| 12 | Post-revisionism as "both sides were to blame" | Two specific claims: deliberate political purpose in American aid; opportunism not programme in Soviet expansion | L3 §3 |
| 13 | "Revisionism was a Soviet position only" | Some American historians of the 1960s and early 1970s held it; Williams is named | L3 §2 |
| 14 | "Kennan was a historian" | Embassy official in the mid-1940s, ambassador 1952-53, author of the containment prescription | L3 §1 |
| 15 | Treating Stalin's February 1946 speech as settled evidence of intent | Russian historians allege biased Western reporting, naming Kennan | L3 §4, L4 §5 |
| 16 | "Yalta failed" | It agreed the UN, zones, free elections and Soviet entry against Japan; it failed on Poland's western frontier | L4 §1 |
| 17 | "Churchill attended all of Potsdam" | Replaced by Attlee after the British election result | L4 §2 |
| 18 | "Stalin was told nothing about the bomb" | Not told its exact nature; Churchill was told | L4 §2 |
| 19 | Treating Sovietisation as uniform | Hungary's free election, Yugoslavia's elected communists and Czechoslovakia to February 1948 are three distinct cases | L4 §3, §4 |
| 20 | Forgetting the 1944 spheres agreement | Churchill had agreed much of eastern Europe should be a Russian sphere; the objection was to methods | L4 §6 |
| 21 | "Fulton was official Western policy" | Churchill was leader of the opposition; over a hundred Labour MPs signed a motion criticising him | L4 §5 |
| 22 | Confusing the Doctrine and the Plan | March 1947 and June 1947 respectively | L5 §2, §3 |
| 23 | "The Marshall Plan saved western Europe" | About 2.5 per cent of national income; communist popularity was already declining | L5 §4 |
| 24 | "Marshall Aid was purely humanitarian" / "purely political" | A declared humanitarian purpose, a commercial benefit and a probable main political aim | L5 §3 |
| 25 | "The Cominform was an aid body" | Aid was the Molotov Plan; co-ordination was Comecon; the Cominform imposed Russian-style communism | L5 §5 |
| 26 | "Yugoslavia left the communist bloc in 1948" | Expelled from the Cominform; remained communist | L5 §5 |
| 27 | "Czechoslovakia was invaded in 1948" | An internal armed seizure by a party already holding unions and police, with Soviet involvement judged likely but unproven | L5 §6 |
| 28 | Omitting Masaryk | Officially suicide; documents found after 1989 proved murder | L5 §6 |
| 29 | "The airlift broke the blockade by force" | It worked because the West judged the Russians would not shoot down transport planes | L6 §3 |
| 30 | Reversing the two German states | West Germany August 1949; East Germany October 1949 in reply | L6 §5 |
| 31 | "The Wall began the Berlin crisis" | It is the third episode, after 1948-49 and 1958, and follows two refused demands | L6 §6 |
| 32 | Explaining the Wall by ideology alone | About 200 000 refugees a year and over three million since 1945 | L6 §6 |
| 33 | "The Wall solved the German question" | It froze one aspect and symbolised continuing division | L6, L22 |
| 34 | Omitting the U-2 affair | 1960, over a thousand miles inside Russia; no apology; Khrushchev left the Paris summit | L6 §6 |
| 35 | Dating NATO's origins to 1949 alone | The Brussels Defence Treaty of March 1948 is its core | L7 §1 |
| 36 | Missing NATO's "in advance" clause | The Americans pledged themselves in advance to military action for the first time | L7 §1 |
| 37 | "The Warsaw Pact was NATO's twin" | Six years later, and read by the West as a gesture against West German membership | L7 §2 |
| 38 | "SEATO organised Asian security" | Only Pakistan, Thailand and the Philippines joined | L7 §3, L14 §2 |
| 39 | "Austria chose neutrality freely and without cost" | A treaty obligation barring NATO and the European Economic Community | L7 §4, L10 §4 |
| 40 | "Nuclear weapons prevented war" | The general claim is attributed; the specific claim concerns direct general war between the principals | L8 §1 |
| 41 | Dating the arms race from 1945 | It developed from the Soviet bomb of September 1949 | L8 §2 |
| 42 | Using NSC-68 as evidence of Soviet intentions | It is evidence of American threat perception | L8 §2 |
| 43 | Treating all weapons as equally destabilising | SLBMs reduced the first-strike temptation; ABMs and MIRVs increased it | L8 §4, §5 |
| 44 | Confusing MIRV and SS-20 | Up to fourteen warheads against three; the SS-20 targeted western Europe | L8 §4 |
| 45 | Confusing unilateral and multilateral disarmament | One state alone against general agreement among the major powers | L8 §6 |
| 46 | "Stalin ordered the Korean invasion" | The most widely accepted view: Kim pressed it; the USSR and China approved and supplied without direct involvement | L9 §2 |
| 47 | "Korea was purely a civil war" | Approval, war materials and the Chinese intervention are recorded | L9 §2 |
| 48 | "The UN authorised the American deployment" | Troops were ordered from Japan before the UN decided | L9 §3 |
| 49 | Forgetting the Soviet boycott | The Council acted in the Russian delegation's absence during a protest over Chinese representation | L9 §4 |
| 50 | Missing the October 1950 change of war aims | The original UN objective had been achieved by the end of September | L9 §5 |
| 51 | "MacArthur was sacked for insubordination alone" | He proposed attacking Manchuria with atomic bombs if necessary; Truman judged this would provoke a large-scale war | L9 §5 |
| 52 | Omitting Korea's human cost | About four million killed and five million homeless | L9 §6 |
| 53 | "The thaw was continuous improvement" | The account says it was not a consistent development | L10 §1 |
| 54 | "Peaceful coexistence abandoned the communist objective" | A change of route: recognition of Soviet economic superiority rather than defeat in war | L10 §2 |
| 55 | Omitting McCarthy's discrediting | 1954, after accusing leading generals; condemned by the Senate by a large majority | L10 §1, L21 §4 |
| 56 | Presenting 1955 as a year of thaw only | The Austrian State Treaty and the Warsaw Pact are both 1955 | L7 §2, L10 §4 |
| 57 | "Nasser nationalised the canal without compensation" | Compensation was promised, and access for the ships of all nations except Israel | L11 §2 |
| 58 | "The Israeli invasion surprised Britain and France" | It was the first step of a plan agreed in secret talks | L11 §3 |
| 59 | "The superpowers cooperated at Suez" | They "for once agreed"; the interests were convergent, not coordinated | L11 §4 |
| 60 | Forgetting Omega | An Anglo-American plan to remove Nasser by political and economic pressure already existed | L11 §3 |
| 61 | Starting the Cuban story in October 1962 | It begins with Castro's seizure of power in 1959 and the nationalisations | L12 §1 |
| 62 | "Castro was a communist when he took power" | He announced he was a Marxist later in 1961, after the Bay of Pigs | L12 §1 |
| 63 | Omitting the Jupiter missiles in Turkey | Agreed in 1959, before any top-level Castro-Soviet contacts | L12 §3 |
| 64 | "The 1962 settlement was an unconditional Soviet retreat" | Kennedy also promised not to invade and undertook to disarm the Jupiters, undisclosed | L12 §5 |
| 65 | Omitting Castro's reaction | Furious at being deserted apparently without consultation; relations cool for several years | L12 §5 |
| 66 | Reporting only the praise of Kennedy | Later historians criticised him from both directions | L12 §7 |
| 67 | Dating the Sino-Soviet split vaguely | Steady deterioration after 1956; the charge targets Khrushchev's positions of that year | L13 §1, §2 |
| 68 | Explaining "revisionism" loosely | Revising or reinterpreting Marx and Lenin to suit their own needs, over coexistence and non-violent revolution | L13 §2 |
| 69 | Omitting the territorial claim | North of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang; it became the main bone of contention | L13 §3 |
| 70 | Crediting Nixon with initiating the China opening | The Chinese invited a table-tennis team in 1971; the American act was to withdraw a veto | L13 §4 |
| 71 | Missing February 1979 | China attacked Vietnam and withdrew after three weeks; Vietnam supported Russia | L13 §5 |
| 72 | Using "neutral" and "non-aligned" interchangeably | Treaty obligation with disabilities against reserved independent judgement | L14 §5 |
| 73 | Presenting the choice as binary | China's 1954 friendship-and-aid offer, with agreements signed with India and Burma | L14 §2 |
| 74 | Reducing African and Asian conflicts to proxy wars | Angola: fighting anyway, made much worse by outside interference | L14 §4 |
| 75 | Duplicating India's foreign policy in a world-history answer | One instance, then the cross-link | L14 §5 |
| 76 | Treating Vietnam as one American war | Two wars, 1946-54 and 1961-75, with a cancelled election between them | L15 §1, §2 |
| 77 | "The Vietminh was a communist organisation" | Communist-led and an alliance of all shades of opinion wanting an end to foreign control | L15 §1 |
| 78 | Crediting Soviet supply with the French defeat | The decisive external factor was probably Chinese supply from 1950 | L15 §1 |
| 79 | Omitting the cancelled election of July 1956 | The account calls the American attitude a violation of the Geneva Agreement | L15 §2 |
| 80 | "The NLF was the communist party" | The communists were only one section of it | L15 §3 |
| 81 | Reporting Johnson as a simple hawk | Recent assessments see him as wracked with uncertainty and afraid of Chinese entry | L15 §4 |
| 82 | "Tet was a Vietcong military victory" | Much ground was lost later; its effect was on American opinion | L15 §4 |
| 83 | "The USSR fought alongside the Arabs in 1967" | It encouraged and propagandised, sent none, and re-armed afterwards | L16 §3 |
| 84 | Merging the oil embargo with the OPEC price rise | Political pressure against a conservation-and-revenue decision | L16 §4 |
| 85 | "Camp David settled the conflict" | It ended the Egyptian-Israeli state of war and left the West Bank, Golan, settlements and statehood unresolved | L16 §5 |
| 86 | Converting the intervention list into proven operations | It is a list of states written about by named authors | L17 §1, §6 |
| 87 | Omitting Frei | His comparable reforms drew lavish American aid; the contrast is the argument | L17 §2 |
| 88 | "Allende fell because of an American coup alone" | Three internal policy failures and one internal political split appear alongside it | L17 §3 |
| 89 | "Kassem was a communist" | The account states explicitly that he was not; OPEC is named beside the communist-party grievance | L17 §5 |
| 90 | Defining détente as friendship | A permanent relaxation of tensions; it managed rather than abolished rivalry | L18 §1 |
| 91 | "SALT I reduced armaments" | It did not reduce them; it slowed the race, and omitted MIRVs | L18 §3 |
| 92 | "Helsinki settled the German question" | It recognised the postwar frontiers, and therefore the division | L18 §3 |
| 93 | Ignoring Ostpolitik | A West German policy from 1969 and a recorded reason for détente | L18 §2 |
| 94 | Making Afghanistan the sole cause of the breakdown | The SS-20, the NATO decision and the Senate's refusal precede it in 1979 | L19 §2 |
| 95 | "SALT II was signed and broken" | The US Senate decided not to accept it | L19 §2 |
| 96 | Treating SDI as operational or harmless | Still at the planning stage in December 1987, and destabilising in the same way as the ABM | L19 §4 |
| 97 | Narrating the Afghan war | The sources used support the international dimension only; the limit is declared | L19 §3 |
| 98 | Crediting Gorbachev alone for the ending | Much credit to him and much to Reagan for responding positively | L20 §7 |
| 99 | "The INF Treaty ended the arms race" | At most 4 per cent of stocks; but the first destruction of weapons, under strict verification | L20 §4 |
| 100 | Explaining the collapse of communism inside a Cold War answer | The end-of-Cold-War owner holds the reasons; this session states the sequence | L20 §6 |
| 101 | "The end of the Cold War brought peace" | New conflicts arose, often nationalist - Yugoslavia, Azerbaijan-Armenia, Georgia | L20 §7 |
| 102 | Treating periodisation as a list of dates | A phase is defined by its mechanism | L22 |
| 103 | Writing the 15-mark structure longer for a 20-marker | The extra marks require a non-European theatre, agency, non-alignment and multi-causality | L22 §3 |
| 104 | Attributing conduct to nations or peoples | Governments, parties, ministries, agencies, alliances and named individuals only | Throughout |
| 105 | Supplying figures the sources do not carry | Every quantity must come from a held source and be used in that source's sense | Throughout |
| 106 | "The war with Japan ended on 10 August 1945" | 10 August is the surrender offer; the acceptance was announced on 14-15 August; the Instrument of Surrender was signed on 2 September 1945 | L4 §2 |
| 107 | "Khrushchev signed the Austrian State Treaty" | Signed at Vienna on 15 May 1955, with Molotov signing for the Soviet Union | L10 §4 |
| 108 | "Saddam Hussein became Iraqi leader in 1968" | Prominent in the Ba'ath regime as vice-president from July 1968; President of Iraq from 16 July 1979 | L17 §5 |
| 109 | "The Soviet withdrawal from Afghanistan began on 1 May 1988" | The Geneva Accords were signed on 14 April 1988 and the withdrawal began on 15 May 1988 | L19 §3, L20 §5 |
| 110 | "About 48 000 Americans died in Vietnam" given as the total | That figure matches the hostile-death category; the verified official total is 58 220, comprising 47 434 hostile and 10 786 non-hostile deaths | L15 §6 |
| 111 | "The Helsinki Agreement was signed in July 1975" | The account says July 1975; the Final Act was signed on 1 August 1975 after the conference closed in the last days of July | L18 §3 |
| 112 | Dating Comecon to 1947 because the Molotov Plan is dated 1947 | The held sources give no founding date for Comecon; leave it undated | L5 §5 |
| 113 | "Sixteen countries contributed troops in Korea" | The USA and fourteen other named countries - fifteen contributing states in all | L9 §4 |

## Drill set A - date discrimination

⚠️ Give the year, and then the distinction that makes the pair examinable.

1. Truman Doctrine against Marshall Plan. → ✅ March 1947 and June 1947; a security commitment against its economic extension.
2. NATO against the Warsaw Pact. → ✅ April 1949 and 1955; a founding alliance against a response to West German membership.
3. West Germany against East Germany. → ✅ August 1949 and October 1949; the second was explicitly a reply.
4. The Berlin blockade against the airlift's end. → ✅ June 1948 and May 1949; ten months, two million tons.
5. The Berlin Wall against the Cuban missiles crisis. → ✅ August 1961 and October 1962; a refused demand answered by a barrier, then a deployment answered by a blockade.
6. The Austrian State Treaty against the Warsaw Pact. → ✅ Both May 1955 and 1955; settlement and formalisation in the same year.
7. SALT I against Helsinki. → ✅ 1972 and July 1975; weapon categories against frontiers and human rights.
8. Nixon in China against SALT I. → ✅ February 1972 and 1972; the triangle and the arms agreement in the same year.
9. The Soviet entry into Afghanistan against the INF Treaty. → ✅ December 1979 and December 1987; eight years from rupture to the first destruction of weapons.
10. The start of the Sino-Soviet deterioration against the Chinese attack on Vietnam. → ✅ After 1956 and February 1979.
11. Geneva against Reykjavik against Washington. → ✅ November 1985, October 1986, December 1987.
12. Solidarity's strikes against the Soviet dissolution. → ✅ August 1988 and the end of December 1991.

## Drill set B - one-line corrections

⚠️ Each stem below is wrong. Produce the correction in one sentence without looking back.

1. "The Cold War began when Churchill described an iron curtain."
2. "Stalin refused Marshall Aid because the offer excluded the eastern bloc."
3. "NATO was created after a Soviet attack on western Europe."
4. "The Korean War ended with the unification of the peninsula."
5. "Khrushchev abandoned the goal of a communist world in 1956."
6. "Nasser was a Soviet ally by 1956."
7. "Kennedy forced Khrushchev to back down without conceding anything."
8. "China and the USSR were allies throughout the Cold War."
9. "Non-aligned states took no aid from either bloc."
10. "The Americans lost Vietnam because they were outnumbered."
11. "Détente ended the arms race."
12. "The Cold War ended because the Soviet economy collapsed in 1991."

✅ **Answers, in order:** the phrase was used at Fulton in March 1946, after Sovietisation was already under way and after Stalin's February 1946 speech; aid was in theory available to eastern Europe and Molotov rejected it as dollar imperialism, forbidding the satellites and Czechoslovakia to accept; NATO followed the Berlin blockade, which exposed western military unreadiness, not an attack; the armistice of July 1953 restored a frontier roughly along the 38th parallel; peaceful coexistence changed the route, not the objective, which was to be reached through recognised Soviet economic superiority; he bought arms from Czechoslovakia while remaining outside both alliances and was reclassified by an American criterion rather than by alignment; Kennedy promised not to invade Cuba again and undertook to disarm the Jupiter missiles in Turkey, though he would not allow that to be announced; relations deteriorated steadily after 1956 and China attacked Vietnam in February 1979; Egypt bought Soviet-bloc arms and Khrushchev courted neutrals with lavish aid, because non-alignment meant independent judgement rather than equidistance; in early 1969 the American side had roughly a two-to-one advantage in personnel, and the failure was political rather than numerical; SALT I did not reduce armaments and left MIRVs uncovered; the ending was multi-causal, and the collapse of 1989-91 is the outcome whose causes belong to the end-of-Cold-War owner.

## Drill set C - demand sorting

⚠️ For each stem, name the demand family, the prohibition and the phase or phases to use. Target: ninety seconds for all six.

1. "Examine the view that the Cold War was an unavoidable consequence of the Second World War."
2. "Discuss the significance of the Cuban missiles crisis."
3. "How far did the communist world act as a single bloc?"
4. "Assess the effect of the Cold War on newly independent states."
5. "Explain why détente failed."
6. "Account for the end of the Cold War."

✅ **Answers, in order:** causal family, prohibition against a blame verdict, phase 1 with the security-dilemma mechanism; episodic family, prohibition against narrative, phase 2 with the deterrence mechanism and the détente consequence; structural family, prohibition against a Europe-only or two-bloc reading, phases 2 to 4 with the Sino-Soviet chain; decolonised-world family, prohibition against treating new states as pawns, phases 2 and 3 with the SEATO count and the Angolan verdict; structural family on détente, prohibition against "tensions reduced", phases 3 and 4 with the MIRV omission; terminal family, prohibition against "Gorbachev ended it", phases 4 and 5 with the four converging elements and the handover.

## Drill set D - the map work

⚠️ This topic has a spatial dimension that is easily lost in prose. Be able to place, from memory, each of the following and to say in one clause why it matters.

1. The Oder-Neisse line. → ✅ The disputed Polish-German frontier that Yalta left unresolved and Potsdam found already occupied, with about five million Germans expelled.
2. The four occupation zones of Germany and of Berlin. → ✅ Berlin lay a hundred miles inside the Russian zone, which is the whole of the Berlin problem.
3. Stettin and Trieste. → ✅ The two ends of Churchill's iron curtain in the Fulton formulation.
4. The 38th parallel. → ✅ The 1945 administrative division of Korea and the 1953 armistice frontier.
5. The 17th parallel. → ✅ The 1954 temporary division of Vietnam pending elections that were never held.
6. The Yalu river. → ✅ The Korea-China frontier reached by UN forces in October 1950, triggering the Chinese counter-offensive.
7. Cuba's distance from the United States. → ✅ Less than a hundred miles at the nearest point; missiles of up to 2000 miles would have threatened New York, Washington, Chicago and Boston.
8. Turkey. → ✅ Site of the 1959 Jupiter deployment and a Truman Doctrine aid recipient; the undisclosed half of the 1962 bargain.
9. The Suez Canal. → ✅ Nationalised in July 1956; blocked during the war; reopened to Egyptian control in 1975 and to Israeli ships after 1979.
10. Sinai, Gaza, the West Bank, East Jerusalem and the Golan Heights. → ✅ Occupied by Israel in June 1967; Sinai returned under the 1979 treaty.
11. The area north of Vladivostok, and Sinkiang. → ✅ The nineteenth-century Russian acquisitions claimed by China and, once Beijing softened towards Washington, the main bone of contention.
12. East Germany and Czechoslovakia. → ✅ Where the Russian missiles destroyed under the INF Treaty were based, matched by Cruise and Pershing in western Europe.

## Answer spines

⚠️ Four skeletons, each executable in about a minute of planning.

```text
SPINE 1 - ORIGINS (causal family)
thesis: power created it, ideology made it non-negotiable and global
-> the material position: two powers, one vacuum
-> ideology as interpretation: 1917, 1918, 1941, the two readings of
   Marshall Aid
-> the atomic asymmetry as the weighting factor
-> qualification 1: ideology did not prevent the wartime alliance
-> qualification 2: an asymmetry of method in eastern Europe
-> verdict, time-signed: to 1947 Soviet methods did more; after 1947
   Western instruments did more to make the division permanent

SPINE 2 - A CRISIS (episodic family)
thesis: what the crisis revealed about how the system worked
-> the structural problem that produced it
-> the precipitant
-> each side's options, and why the chosen one left the first shot to
   the other
-> the settlement, including any concealed element
-> what it produced institutionally
-> verdict: the crisis as demonstration rather than as turning point

SPINE 3 - THE DECOLONISED WORLD (agency family)
thesis: internationalised, not created
-> the countable refusal: SEATO's three Asian members
-> the reclassification mechanism: the Egyptian criterion
-> one amplification case with the concession stated first: Angola
-> one case where the causal arrow ran outward: Suez or Vietnam
-> the neutrality/non-alignment distinction
-> verdict: an effective instrument of sovereignty, a weak instrument
   of protection

SPINE 4 - THE ENDING (terminal family)
thesis: four converging elements, none sufficient
-> the negotiated sequence, Geneva to INF
-> the Afghan costs, material and diplomatic
-> mobilised societies from August 1988
-> the decision not to use force
-> the boundary clause: the reasons for the collapse belong elsewhere
-> verdict: the rivalry ended by withdrawal of one side's system, not
   by victory in war; and new conflicts followed
```

## Map 2 - The five phases and their mechanisms

| Phase | ✅ Span | ⚠️ Defining mechanism | ✅ Decisive markers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Hardening | 1945-53 | Instrument produces counter-instrument under the double reading | Yalta, Potsdam, Sovietisation, 1947 doctrines, Cominform, Czechoslovakia, Berlin, NATO, two Germanies, Soviet bomb, NSC-68, Korea |
| Partial thaw | 1953-62 | Settlement where an issue can be closed; rigidity where the issue is control | Stalin's death, Panmunjom, McCarthy discredited, Austrian treaty, Warsaw Pact, Hungary, coexistence, U-2, Wall, Cuba |
| Détente | the 1970s | Cooperation only where it manages risk or ratifies the existing distribution | Ostpolitik, the China opening, SALT I, summits, wheat, Helsinki |
| Second Cold War | 1979-85 | The uncovered system drives new competition; management stops, architecture survives | SS-20, Pershing and Cruise, the Senate and SALT 2, Afghanistan, build-up, SDI |
| Ending | 1985-91 | A leadership concludes costs exceed benefits and declines to use force | Geneva, Chernobyl, Reykjavik, INF, Afghan withdrawal, 1989-91 |

## Map 3 - Crisis comparison: what each crisis proved about the system

| Crisis | ✅ What happened | ⚠️ What it proved |
|---|---|---|
| Eastern Europe, 1945-48 | Pro-communist governments installed; Hungary's free election overridden in cabinet; Czechoslovakia seized in February 1948 | Spheres would be enforced, not negotiated - and enforcement ran through electoral institutions rather than simply abolishing them |
| Truman Doctrine and Marshall Aid, 1947 | Support for free peoples resisting subjugation; $13bn to sixteen states over four years | Economic power was a Cold War instrument from the outset, and a threat-type commitment had no geographical limit |
| Berlin blockade, 1948-49 | Links closed; two million tons airlifted over ten months | Both sides would risk confrontation while leaving the first shot to the other - the founding rule |
| NATO 1949, Warsaw Pact 1955 | Advance commitment and joint command; a mutual defence agreement six years later | Division institutionalised, asymmetrically: one alliance built on an existing initiative, one created around installed governments |
| Korea, 1950-53 | Local initiative, external approval and materials, Chinese entry, armistice on the old line | Rivalry could become armed conflict by proxy, and Asia became a primary theatre |
| Hungary, 1956 | The rising crushed by Russian tanks; the UN blocked and ignored | The West would not contest the Soviet sphere militarily |
| Suez, 1956 | Britain, France and Israel compelled to withdraw by American and Soviet pressure together | Each bloc's leader could discipline its own; neither could discipline the other |
| Berlin Wall, 1961 | 28 miles, after two refused demands and 200 000 refugees a year | Division stabilised by being made physical - and unresolved by being stabilised |
| Cuba, 1962 | Blockade, compromise, concealed Turkish undertaking | Nuclear risk itself forced restraint - the origin of détente |
| Vietnam, 1961-75 | Safe villages, bombing exceeding the tonnage dropped on Germany, Vietnamisation, withdrawal | Military superiority did not produce a political outcome that survived withdrawal |
| Chile, 1970-73 | An elected Marxist government undermined and overthrown, with the CIA playing a vital role | The rivalry penetrated domestic politics far beyond either bloc |
| Angola, 1975-88 | Three movements, four external interveners, a settlement in December 1988 | External involvement made much worse a conflict it did not create |
| Afghanistan, 1979-88 | Over 100 000 troops, four pressures, a withdrawal conditional on American aid ceasing | Détente was reversible - and a proxy war's ending required a bilateral bargain |

## Map 4 - The bloc-cohesion argument, as an argument tree

```text
   CLAIM: "The Cold War was a contest between two unified blocs."
        |
        +-- CONCEDED: military bipolarity was real
        |     · USA and USSR alone had the relevant capacities
        |     · NATO's advance commitment and joint command
        |     · the Warsaw Pact's mutual defence agreement
        |

        |     · territorial claim north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang
        |       becomes the main bone of contention

        |       after Vietnam removes Pol Pot from Kampuchea
        |     · 1984 Chinese grievances: all three are Soviet
        |       deployments
        |
        +-- QUALIFIED on the western side
        |     · Labour MPs against Fulton; Suez against American
        |       preference
        |     · BUT no formal sanction, and Britain afterwards could
        |       not act independently of the USA
        |
        +-- REFUTED from outside both blocs
        |     · SEATO 1954: three Asian members only
        |     · Algiers 1973: Third World self-description
        |     · Austria 1955: negotiated neutrality in Europe itself
        |
        v
   VERDICT: bipolar in capability, plural in politics. The most
   consequential diplomatic move of the period - the American opening
   to China in 1972 - was made possible by a quarrel inside the bloc
   that containment was designed to face.
```

## Map 5 - Evidence discipline: what may and may not be asserted

| Category | ✅ Assertible from this session | ❌ Not assertible here |
|---|---|---|
| **Quantities** | The finite list declared in the opening section | Warhead totals by year, aid by country, casualty figures for the interventions, defence budgets |
| **Motives** | Stated motives and the account's own weightings, with hedges preserved | Motive claims the sources attribute to "some historians" as if settled |
| **Covert operations** | The technique set; the Chilean, Laotian, Iraqi and Cambodian cases the account states directly | The wider list of states, which is attributed to named authors |
| **Damaged passages** | Nothing | The Yalta opening, the satellite paragraph, the Vietnam ceasefire and collapse pages, the Iranian restoration, the post-revisionist successor passage, the Oslo page |
| **Question wording** | Nothing, since no verified question is owned here | Any stem, option or key, including the adjacent International Relations items |
| **Attribution of conduct** | Governments, parties, ministries, agencies, alliances, congresses, named individuals | Nations, peoples, or whole social or religious categories |

## Map 6 - The two alliance systems compared

| Dimension | ✅ NATO and the Western alliance | ✅ The Cominform, Comecon and the Warsaw Pact |
|---|---|---|
| **Origin** | A west European treaty of March 1948 that the Americans joined in April 1949 | Created by the USSR among governments installed between 1945 and 1948 |
| **Core obligation** | An attack on one regarded as an attack on all; forces under a joint peacetime command | A mutual defence agreement signed in 1955 after West Germany entered NATO |
| **Economic arm** | Marshall Aid: transfer to governments spending under their own authority | The Molotov Plan and Comecon: aid plus a prescribed model and trade direction |
| **Doctrinal requirement** | None recorded | To be communist was not enough; it must be Russian-style communism |
| **Dissent** | Labour MPs against Fulton; Britain and France at Suez against American preference | Yugoslavia expelled in 1948; Hungary crushed in 1956 |
| **Recruitment outside Europe** | ANZUS 1951 and SEATO 1954, which only three Asian states joined | Friendship and aid offers to neutrals; arms to Egypt and Syria |
| **Conduct outside the alliance** | Assassination attempts, election rigging, financed terrorism, economic destabilisation, full-scale intervention | Aid as persuasion; arms transfers; encouragement without commitment in 1967; Afghanistan from December 1979 |
