# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

> These are compressed recall notes, organised by the topic's own structure rather than by a fixed template. They assume the teaching and exist to be revised against.

## Definition, vocabulary and boundaries

- ✅ **The Cold War:** a period in which the USA and USSR did not fight each other directly in an open general war, but confronted each other through propaganda, arms competition, alliance-building, economic pressure and limited wars.
- ✅ **The account's version:** no actual fighting directly between the camps; hostility expressed through propaganda, economic measures and a general policy of non-cooperation.
- ✅ **The restraint mechanism:** the Americans were reluctant to use the bomb again unless attacked directly; the Russians dared not risk such an attack.
- ✅ **Exam line:** the Cold War was not peace; it was organised hostility under the shadow of nuclear weapons.
- ✅ **Superpower content:** USA - largest navy and air force, control of the atomic bomb. USSR - severely weakened, still the largest army.
- ✅ **Containment:** Kennan's prescription of political, economic and diplomatic means; after March 1947 a worldwide commitment.
- ✅ **Détente:** a permanent relaxation of tensions, first visible in the early 1970s.
- ⚠️ **Proxy war:** describes superpower involvement, not the causes of local conflicts.
- ✅ **Syllabus:** the General Studies Paper I world-history clause; the doctrines sub-clause is owned by the Enlightenment-and-revolutions file; no separate Prelims clause.
- ⚠️ **Five boundaries:** the Second World War owner, the UN owner, the China owner, the decolonisation owner and the end-of-Cold-War owner.

## Origins: five causes and one mechanism

- ✅ Differences of principle: collective ownership, central planning and state social policy against private ownership, private enterprise for profit and the power of private wealth.
- ✅ Mistrust from 1917; intervention by the USA, Britain, France and Japan in 1918; Stalin's expectation of another attempt, met by the German invasion of 1941.
- ✅ The alliance was formed because self-preservation caused the partners to forget - not resolve - their differences.
- ✅ Stalin's aim: to use the military situation to strengthen Russian influence; German territory and land from Finland, Poland and Romania.
- ✅ Truman more suspicious than Roosevelt from April 1945; the bomb's demonstrative purpose asserted by *some historians* only.
- ✅ Soviet grievances: the Second Front to June 1944; the bomb concealed; no share in the occupation of Japan.
- ✅ The summarising clause: above all, the West had the bomb and the USSR did not.
- ✅ The mechanism: every act interpretable two ways; self-defence to one side read as aggression by the other.
- ⚠️ The weighting: ideology determined content and interpretation; the security dilemma determined the dynamic; an asymmetry of method persists.

## The blame historiography

- ✅ **Orthodox:** 1950s western historians, Kennan named - Stalin's motives sinister, intending to spread communism through Europe and Asia; NATO and Korea as self-defence.
- ✅ **Revisionist:** Soviet historians and some Americans of the 1960s and early 1970s; enormous wartime losses, purely defensive motives, no real threat; Williams on the atomic monopoly and industrial strength in a drive for world hegemony; the Vietnam War as the political context of the turn.
- ✅ **Post-revisionist:** popular in the 1980s, using newly opened archives - both sides share blame; Marshall Aid deliberately designed to increase American political influence; Stalin an opportunist without long-term plans.
- ✅ **Disputed item:** Stalin's February 1946 speech, with the Russian counter-claim of biased Western reporting naming Kennan.
- ⚠️ **Declared gap:** the passage naming later historians on the Cold War's outcome is illegible here and is used nowhere.

## 1945-47: settlement, rupture and Sovietisation

- ✅ **Yalta agreed:** the United Nations; zones for Germany and Austria with Berlin split inside the Russian zone; free elections in eastern Europe; Soviet entry against Japan for Sakhalin and Manchurian territory.
- ✅ **Yalta unresolved:** the Oder-Neisse frontier; the Lublin-London compromise added non-communist ministers to a communist government.
- ✅ **Potsdam:** a distinctly cooler atmosphere; Truman for Roosevelt, Attlee for Churchill; disarmament, the Nazi party disbanded, war-crimes trials, reparations mostly to the USSR on a food-for-goods exchange; German unity and the Oder-Neisse unresolved, with five million Germans expelled.
- ✅ The bomb's nature withheld from Stalin though told to Churchill; Soviet declaration of war 8 August; the war with Japan ended without Russian help; south Sakhalin annexed; no occupation share. ⚠️ 10 August is the surrender offer, 14-15 August the acceptance and announcement, 2 September the signed Instrument of Surrender.
- ✅ **Sovietisation:** Poland, Hungary, Bulgaria, Albania and Romania; opponents imprisoned or murdered; Hungary's free election won by communists with under 20 per cent yet a communist cabinet majority; by the end of 1947 every state but Czechoslovakia fully communist; the Russian zone of Germany treated as Russian territory.
- ✅ **Yugoslavia:** Tito legally elected in 1945 on resistance prestige; his forces liberated the country; he resented Soviet interference.
- ✅ **February and March 1946:** Stalin on the impossibility of peaceful coexistence; Fulton's iron curtain from Stettin to Trieste; Stalin's "warmonger"; over a hundred Labour MPs objecting.
- ✅ **The concession already made:** Churchill had agreed in 1944 that much of eastern Europe should be a Russian sphere; it was Stalin's methods that upset the West.

## Containment's instruments

- ✅ **Truman Doctrine, March 1947:** support for free peoples resisting subjugation by armed minorities or outside pressures; Greek supplies and communist defeat by 1949; about $60 million to Turkey; no return to isolation; containment worldwide, including Korea and Vietnam.
- ✅ **Marshall Plan, June 1947:** the European Recovery Programme against hunger, poverty, desperation and chaos; sixteen nations by September; over $13 billion over four years; western European communist parties defeated, notably in France and Italy.
- ✅ **The purposes:** recovery, lucrative markets for American exports, and a probable main political aim.
- ✅ **The qualification:** aid averaged about 2.5 per cent of the sixteen countries' national income; communist popularity was already declining; Stalin was more concerned with Soviet security than with invading western Europe.
- ✅ **Molotov:** dollar imperialism; the USSR refused and forbade the satellites and Czechoslovakia.
- ✅ **Cominform, September 1947:** Russian-style communism required; industrialisation, collectivisation, centralisation, intra-bloc trade; Yugoslavia expelled 1948 but still communist. **Molotov Plan 1947** for aid; **Comecon** for co-ordination.
- ✅ **Czechoslovakia:** 1946 coalition freely elected, communists 38 per cent and 114 of 300 seats; May elections expected to go badly after the aid refusal; armed coup in February 1948 using unions and police; Masaryk's death, later proved murder; single-list May elections; Gottwald president; Soviet involvement unproven but judged likely, with divisions moved up from Austria.

## Germany and Berlin

- ✅ Four zones each for Germany and Berlin, with Berlin a hundred miles inside the Russian zone; western reconstruction against Soviet extraction.
- ✅ Early 1948 the three western zones merged; June 1948 a new currency and the end of price controls; Soviet objections to an island of capitalism, two currencies in one city and the visible contrast in living standards.
- ✅ Blockade of all road, rail and canal links to force withdrawal by starvation; airlift of two million tons over ten months for 2.5 million Berliners; B-29s on British airfields; blockade lifted May 1949.
- ✅ Three results: a psychological boost with relations at their worst ever; the formation of NATO; German division for the foreseeable future.
- ✅ German Federal Republic August 1949 under Adenauer; German Democratic Republic October 1949 in reply; division until 1989-90.
- ✅ 1958 Khrushchev withdraws recognition of western rights and does not press it; 1960 the U-2 shot down and the Paris summit abandoned; 1961 a second demand refused, then the 28-mile Wall in August after refugee flows of about 200 000 a year and over three million since 1945.

## Alliances and the uncommitted

- ✅ Brussels Defence Treaty March 1948; North Atlantic Treaty April 1949 with the Brussels five plus the USA, Canada, Portugal, Denmark, Iceland, Italy and Norway; attack on one is attack on all; joint peacetime command; the first American pledge in advance to military action.
- ✅ Warsaw Pact 1955, after West Germany joined NATO.
- ✅ ANZUS 1951; SEATO 1954 to encircle China with bases; only Pakistan, Thailand and the Philippines joined.
- ✅ Austria 1955: troops withdrawn, independence within 1937 frontiers, no union with Germany, limited forces, treaty neutrality barring NATO and the European Economic Community; the South Tyrol left with Italy.

## Nuclear weapons and the arms race

- ✅ Hiroshima and Nagasaki demonstrated horrifying powers of destruction; the deterrent claim is attributed, not asserted.
- ✅ September 1949 the Soviet bomb; an arms race develops; a hydrogen bomb authorised; both have one by August 1953.
- ✅ NSC-68, April 1950: the Russians regarded as fanatics who would stop at nothing; armaments spending to be more than tripled.
- ✅ October 1949 the Chinese communist government; February 1950 the Sino-Soviet treaty; then Korea.
- ✅ Systems: ICBMs; SLBMs (Polaris, later Poseidon, from submarines in the eastern Mediterranean); ABMs destroying incoming missiles; MIRVs with as many as fourteen warheads; the SS-20 of 1977 with three, targeted on western Europe; Cruise flying low under radar; SDI from the early 1980s, space-based, to destroy missiles in flight.
- ✅ The Soviet motive for parity: to persuade the Americans to limit and reduce the build-up; the opportunity came as American spending went to Vietnam, and by the early 1970s the USSR had overtaken in ICBMs and SLBMs.
- ✅ The danger: enough weaponry to destroy the world many times over, and the temptation to strike first and destroy the other side's weapons before retaliation.
- ✅ CND from 1958, Aldermaston, unilateral against multilateral disarmament, the 1980s demonstrations and Greenham Common; the account's verdict is a hedged perhaps.

## Korea

- ✅ Japanese occupation since 1910; division at the 38th parallel in August 1945, not intended to be permanent; all-Korea elections proposed and never agreed.
- ✅ Republic of Korea August 1948 under Syngman Rhee at Seoul; Democratic People's Republic September 1948 under Kim Il Sung at Pyongyang; both occupiers withdrawn 1949.
- ✅ June 1950 invasion; four suggested origins - Kim's own idea encouraged by Acheson's omission; Chinese encouragement; Stalin testing Truman with Soviet tanks supplied; the communist claim that the South began it.
- ✅ Most widely accepted: Kim pressed it, the USSR and China approved and promised materials but did not want direct involvement.
- ✅ Truman's four reasons: conviction it was Stalin's doing; the 1930s appeasement analogy; support for the UN where the League failed; and McCarthy's charge of an infested State Department.
- ✅ Troops ordered from Japan before the UN decided; the Council acted in the Soviet delegation's absence during the boycott over Chinese representation; the USA and fourteen other states contributed, fifteen in all, under MacArthur.
- ✅ Inchon 15 September, 200 miles behind the lines; the South cleared by the end of September; Truman then invades the North with UN approval; Zhou Enlai's warning ignored; Pyongyang taken; the Yalu reached.
- ✅ November: over 300 000 Chinese "volunteers"; by mid-January 1951 the UN is out of the North and Seoul has fallen; MacArthur proposes attacking Manchuria with atomic bombs if necessary; Truman refuses, settles for containment and removes him; Panmunjom talks run two years to July 1953 on roughly the 38th parallel.
- ✅ Results: about four million Korean dead and five million homeless; containment claimed and rollback mourned, feeding McCarthyism; the UN's authority exerted and denounced as a capitalist tool; China established as a world power still excluded from the UN; Asian alliance-building against a Chinese friendship-and-aid offer, with peaceful coexistence agreements signed with India and Burma in 1954.

## The thaw and its limits

- ✅ Improvement from 1953, but not a consistent development.
- ✅ Stalin's death March 1953; Malenkov, Bulganin and Khrushchev; the hydrogen-bomb balance of August 1953 making relaxation necessary.
- ✅ McCarthy discredited in 1954 after accusing leading generals; Senate condemnation by a large majority; Eisenhower on friendship with the Soviet people.
- ✅ February 1956: only two ways, peaceful coexistence or the most destructive war in history; there is no third way. **But** a communist-dominated world still to come, through recognised Soviet economic superiority, with neutral states won by lavish aid.
- ✅ First signs: Panmunjom July 1953; the end of the Indo-China war the following year.
- ✅ 1955 concessions: Finnish bases given up; the veto lifted on sixteen new UN members; the Yugoslav quarrel healed by a visit to Tito; the Cominform abandoned.
- ✅ Austrian State Treaty May 1955 - the most important development, and the thaw's one completed settlement.
- ✅ Four limits: the Warsaw Pact 1955; Hungary crushed 1956; the continued nuclear build-up; Berlin; and, most provocative of all, Cuba 1962.
- ⚠️ The boundary principle: settlement where an issue can be closed; rigidity where the issue is who controls what.

## 1956: Suez and Hungary

- ✅ Nasser's irritants; the Czechoslovak arms deal of September 1955; the American criterion converting Egypt into a Cold War problem; the $46 million Aswan grant cancelled July 1956; nationalisation with compensation promised.
- ✅ Eden's reading; the Thomas, Churchill and Gaitskell remarks; the secret plan with France and Israel; the Omega alternative; MI6 and Macmillan urging force; French pressure probably decisive.
- ✅ 29 October the Israeli invasion, Sinai taken in a week; British and French bombing and landings at Port Said; Americans and Russians "for once agreed" on an immediate ceasefire; the Council resolution vetoed by Britain and France; the Assembly condemning 64 to 5; a UN force of 5000 from ten countries.
- ✅ Outcomes: humiliation for Britain and France and triumph for Nasser; the canal blocked and oil supplies reduced with petrol rationing; Russian aid replacing American; Nuri-es-Said murdered 1958; Britain unable to act independently of the USA; Algerians encouraged; Israel gaining a breathing space and turning to the USA.
- ✅ Hungary simultaneously: Soviet troops crushed the revolt; the Russians vetoed the Council resolution and refused to co-operate with the Assembly's committee; Britain and France bowed to international pressure while the Russians simply ignored the UN.

## Cuba

- ✅ 1959 Castro seizes power from Batista and nationalises American-owned estates and factories; January 1961 relations broken and Soviet aid increased.
- ✅ April 1961 the Bay of Pigs - about 1400 men, CIA deeply involved, crushed by Castro's forces and two jet planes; later in 1961 Castro declares himself a Marxist; November 1961 Operation Mongoose.
- ✅ Five reasons for the deployment: the lost ICBM lead; the Jupiter agreement with Turkey of 1959, before any top-level Castro-Soviet contacts, with Khrushchev's memoir quotation; solidarity with Castro; testing Kennedy; and possible bargaining over European missiles or Berlin.
- ✅ Range up to 2000 miles threatening New York, Washington, Chicago and Boston; spy-plane photographs in October 1962.
- ✅ Advisers urged air strikes, Maxwell Taylor invasion; Kennedy chose the blockade against 25 Russian ships plus a demand for dismantling; U Thant appealed for restraint.
- ✅ Compromise: the ships turned back, missiles removed and sites dismantled; a promise not to invade again and an undisclosed undertaking to disarm the Jupiters; Castro furious at desertion without consultation.
- ✅ Products: the hotline; the Nuclear Test Ban Treaty of 1963 limiting tests to underground. ⚠️ The account gives July 1963; the standard record gives 25 July for initialling and 5 August for signature.
- ✅ Later criticism from both flanks; and the observation that Soviet long-range missiles could already reach the USA.
- ✅ In private the Russians admitted their main aim had failed; the Turkish concession was worthless because of Polaris and Poseidon.

## The Sino-Soviet quarrel and the triangle

- ✅ 1950 treaty of mutual assistance and friendship; steady deterioration after 1956.
- ✅ The charge of revisionism over peaceful coexistence and non-violent routes to communism; anger at the soft line towards the USA; Soviet economic aid reduced.
- ✅ Territorial claim north of Vladivostok and in Sinkiang; once China softened towards the USA it became the main bone of contention.
- ✅ 1971 the table-tennis invitation; the American veto called off; UN entry in October 1971; Nixon's visit February 1972 with Mao's reported remark; Ford in 1975; Carter's withdrawal of recognition from Nationalist China in 1978 and formal recognition in 1979.
- ✅ Both powers vying for American support against each other at the end of the 1970s.
- ✅ December 1978 Vietnam invades Kampuchea and removes Pol Pot; February 1979 China attacks Vietnam and withdraws after three weeks.
- ✅ 1984 grievances: troops in Afghanistan; backing for Vietnamese troops in Kampuchea; the Mongolian and Manchurian build-up.
- ✅ July 1985 five-year trade and economic agreements; May 1989 formal reconciliation in Beijing; Vietnam withdraws from Kampuchea the same year.

## Decolonisation, non-alignment and agency

- ✅ The war encouraged decolonisation; Japanese defeats of Britain, Holland and France destroyed the tradition of European superiority and invincibility; a large array of new states in the 1960s.
- ✅ Algiers 1973: a Third World wishing to remain neutral or non-aligned between communism and capitalism; suspicious of the motives of both; resentful of economic dependence on wealthy powers.
- ✅ Countable refusal: three Asian SEATO members in 1954. ✅ Third option: China's friendship and aid, with agreements signed with India and Burma in 1954.
- ✅ The 1954 India-China agreement on Tibet placed the five Panchsheel principles in its preamble.
- ⚠️ Non-alignment is independent judgement, not legal neutrality, isolation or equidistance; Austria's treaty neutrality is the contrast.
- ✅ Angola: three movements after 1975; American, Cuban, South African and Zairean involvement; the verdict that outside interference made much worse a conflict that would have produced fighting anyway; the December 1988 deal trading Namibian withdrawal against 50 000 Cuban troops.
- ⚠️ The conditional verdict: effective for sovereignty, weak for protection.

## Vietnam

- ✅ Two wars: 1946-54 against France and 1961-75 with American intervention.
- ✅ The Vietminh - communist-led, an alliance of all shades of opinion wanting an end to foreign control; Ho Chi Minh trained in Russia; Dien Bien Phu May 1954; Chinese arms from 1950 probably decisive; American money to the French.
- ✅ Geneva 1954: Laos and Cambodia independent; division at the 17th parallel; all-Vietnam elections in July 1956, never held; Ngo refused and the USA did not press him for fear of a communist victory - a violation of the agreement.
- ✅ Causes of the civil war: the cancelled election with the domino theory behind it; Ngo's Catholic elite against a Buddhist peasant majority with no land reform, plus corruption and nationalist resentment; the NLF of 1960, of which the communists were only one section, with Buddhist self-immolations; Ngo overthrown and murdered November 1963; then Thieu to 1975; and the American decision to escalate.
- ✅ Kennedy's safe villages failed because most Vietcong were peasants; 1964 reports of about 40 per cent of villages under Vietcong and NLF control; Johnson's bombing from 1965 exceeding in tonnage what fell on Germany, with over half a million troops; Tet in February 1968 taking something like 80 per cent of towns and villages; 31 March 1968 bombing suspended and negotiation sought; Paris talks from May running five more years.
- ✅ Early 1969: half a million Americans, 50 000 South Koreans, 750 000 South Vietnamese against 450 000 Vietcong and perhaps 70 000 North Vietnamese; Vietnamisation with about half the Americans home by mid-1971; renewed bombing of the North and of the Trail through Laos and Cambodia; the Vietcong holding the entire western half by the end of 1972.
- ✅ Revulsion: the bombing; defoliants and napalm with birth defects among the after-effects; civilian deaths.
- ✅ 1973 withdrawal; 1975 collapse and unification under a communist government; Cambodia and Laos communist before the year's end.
- ✅ Costs: the account's about 48 000 American dead, which matches the hostile-death category, against the verified official total of 58 220 U.S. military fatal casualties - 47 434 hostile and 10 786 non-hostile; 300 000 wounded; about a third of the South severely damaged; concentration camps and no freedom of speech under the new government; a profound effect on American society compounded by Watergate; restraint from Moscow and Beijing.

## The Middle East

- ✅ Four layers: Cold War rivalry, decolonisation and Arab nationalism, competing national projects, oil and strategic routes - each with a stated blind spot.
- ✅ 1948-49: Israel survives; the refugee problem and hostility become enduring; no peace treaty and no recognition, the war regarded as a first round.
- ✅ 1967 build-up: Ba'athist Iraq and Syria, Aref's statement, Syrian support for Fatah and bombardment from the Golan, Nasser's socialist programme and the Aswan Dam completed 1971, troops to Sinai and the closure of Aqaba; Soviet encouragement and propaganda with hints of help.
- ✅ 5-10 June 1967: pre-emptive Israeli air strikes; Gaza and Sinai, the rest of Jerusalem and the West Bank, and the Golan taken; a UN ceasefire; buffer zones, a UN order ignored, about a million extra Arabs under Israeli rule; no Soviet help, then modern Soviet weapons.
- ✅ 1970-72: PLO pressure, PFLP hijackings, the Jordanian expulsion, Munich.
- ✅ 6 October 1973: Sadat, refused American mediation, attacks with Syria to compel it; Israel recovers with mainly American weapons and crosses the canal; both superpowers intervene and a ceasefire follows; the canal reopened 1975.
- ✅ The oil weapon: supply reductions against states friendly to Israel; an OPEC price rise also motivated by conservation; inflation and an energy crisis.
- ✅ Camp David: Sadat in the Knesset November 1977; Begin in Egypt the following month; Carter's mediation from September 1978; the March 1979 treaty ending the state of war, Israeli withdrawal from Sinai, Egyptian oil and canal access; condemned by the PLO and most Arab states; Begin's November 1980 refusals; settlements; Sadat assassinated October 1981; Mubarak continuing.
- ✅ December 1987: the intifada in the camps of Gaza and the West Bank, running over three years.
- ✅ Iran-Iraq 1980-88 and the Gulf War of 1990-91 as a rivalry outlasting the Arab-Israeli cycle.

## The intervention record

- ✅ Techniques: assassination attempts, election rigging, organising and financing acts of terrorism, economic destabilisation, and full-scale military intervention in the last resort.
- ✅ The criterion problem: targeted regimes were often simply pursuing policies which went against American interests; the wider list of states is attributed to Blum, Agee, Chomsky and others.
- ✅ Chile: Frei's reforms drawing lavish American aid; Allende's 36-to-35 victory in September 1970, the world's first Marxist elected head of government, believing communism could succeed without violent revolution; wage rises with a price freeze, nationalisation of copper, textiles and banks, faster land redistribution, the army's pay rise, relations with Cuba, China and East Germany; 49 per cent in the 1972 local elections.
- ✅ Five causes of the fall: anticipatory disinvestment and cattle slaughter; investors frightened off; copper strikes and a world price fall of about 30 per cent; the Movement of the Revolutionary Left seizing farms; American economic undermining, with Nixon's reported instruction to make the economy scream.
- ✅ The September 1973 coup, the CIA's vital role with Brazilian help, consideration of a coup from 1970, Allende's contested death; Pinochet's seventeen years with around 3000 killed or disappeared and tens of thousands tortured, imprisoned or exiled; inflation down from around 1000 per cent by 1980; the 1989 defeat of his candidate, Aylwin president in 1990, the London arrest of 1998 and the 2001 unfitness ruling.
- ✅ Laos: a coalition-willing Pathet Lao classified as dangerous communists; left-wingers removed by 1960; a CIA army of 30 000; bombing 1965-73; the left strengthened; peaceful Pathet Lao control in December 1975.
- ✅ Cambodia: involvement in the 1970 coup against Sihanouk, preceded by bombing that ruined the economy; five years of civil war ending with Pol Pot.
- ✅ Thailand: a bombing base, over 40 000 American troops, all criticism treated as communist-inspired, and the August 1966 press report on dictatorship suiting American interests.
- ✅ Iran: the Shah restored, twenty-five years fully backed by Washington, forced out January 1979.
- ✅ Iraq: Kassem, explicitly not a communist, overthrown and killed in 1963 in a coup backed by the CIA and Britain, after destabilisation including an encouraged Turkish invasion, financed Kurdish guerrillas and attempted assassination, with the OPEC grievance named beside the communist-party grievance; Saddam Hussein - prominent in the Ba'ath regime from July 1968, President from 16 July 1979 - financed and supplied from 1979 against Iran; Kuwait invaded August 1990 and reversed by UN forces mostly American; Saddam overthrown 2003.

## Détente, reversal and ending

- ✅ Reasons: nuclear fear and revulsion at Vietnam shared; Soviet expense, living standards, Prague 1968 and Polish unrest, and unwillingness to be left out of the 1971 Sino-American improvement; American recognition of military limits and talk of isolationism; Chinese isolation and Soviet pressure; West European exposure and Brandt's Ostpolitik from 1969.
- ✅ Instruments: hotline and underground testing 1963; outer space 1967; SALT I 1972 covering ABMs, ICBMs and SLBMs but not MIRVs and not reducing armaments; three Brezhnev-Nixon summits; SALT 2 negotiations; wheat exports; Helsinki July 1975 accepting the postwar frontiers, and therefore the division of Germany, against human-rights promises.
- ✅ 1979 reversal: 150 new SS-20s; NATO's decision on over 500 Pershing and Cruise missiles by 1983; the Senate's refusal of SALT 2; Afghanistan on Christmas Day.
- ✅ Second Cold War 1979-85: arsenals built up; SDI authorised, space-based, to destroy missiles in flight.
- ✅ By 1985 in Afghanistan: over 100 000 troops, an inability to subdue the guerrillas, a drain on resources and prestige, Chinese hostility, Islamic suspicion, repeated UN condemnations; withdrawal conditional on an end to American aid to the resistance, Geneva Accords 14 April 1988 and withdrawal begun 15 May 1988.
- ✅ Geneva November 1985: nuclear war cannot be won and must never be fought. ✅ Chernobyl April 1986 and Gorbachev's decision. ✅ Reykjavik October 1986: a fifteen-year timetable proposed; Star Wars not abandoned.
- ✅ INF December 1987: all land-based 300-3000 mile weapons scrapped over three years; 436 American and 1575 Soviet warheads; all Russian missiles in East Germany and Czechoslovakia and all American Cruise and Pershing in western Europe; strict verification; at most 4 per cent of stocks; British and French weapons excluded with Trident planned; the first time any weapons had been destroyed.
- ✅ Collapse August 1988 to December 1991: Solidarity's strikes; free elections and heavy communist defeats in Poland in June 1989 and in Hungary; Honecker overruled and the East German government resigned by the end of 1989, the Wall breached, Germany reunited in the summer of 1990; Czechoslovakia, Bulgaria and Romania by the end of 1989; Yugoslavia 1990, Albania spring 1991; the USSR split and Gorbachev resigned by the end of December 1991, ending 74 years of communist rule.
- ✅ Six consequences: the Cold War over with the November 1990 NATO-Warsaw Pact treaty; new, often nationalist conflicts; the need for better weapons supervision; transition economies needing Western help; problems from German reunification; and strains among the western allies, with the USA left as the only superpower.

## Non-military dimensions

- ✅ Three of the six defining substitutes are non-military: propaganda, economic pressure, non-cooperation.
- ⚠️ The instrument asymmetry: Western transfer and incentive against Eastern prescription of model and trade direction - qualified by Western conduct outside its own alliance.
- ✅ Propaganda instances: the 1946 speeches, dollar imperialism, the UN denounced as a capitalist tool, anti-Israeli propaganda before 1967.
- ✅ Domestic fronts: McCarthy's charge and his Senate condemnation in 1954; rigged elections, expelled ministers, dissolved parties, secret police and Russian troops in the east; Helsinki's human-rights promises.
- ✅ Societal action: CND and the 1980s demonstrations with a hedged effect; American opinion and 31 March 1968; Chernobyl and Gorbachev's decision; popular opposition from August 1988.
- ⚠️ Channels: continuous and institutional in the West with incremental effects; absent in the East with discontinuous effects.
- ⚠️ Prestige and expectation drove investment in demonstrative capability.

## Answer architecture

- ⚠️ Five phases with their mechanisms; seven demand families with their prohibitions; five thesis templates; mark-scaled structures at 10, 15 and 20; six handovers; thirty-one factual-risk controls; the five-step conversion procedure.
- ✅ Zero verified questions owned by this topic in any ledger or span; prepare by structure rather than by recall.

2. **Period control on the book.** Only those chapters and the named preceding sections were used. Nothing from the book's chapters on the peace settlements, interwar diplomacy, the fascist regimes, the military course of the Second World War, the Russian revolutions, the post-1953 Soviet Union, European integration, the world economy or the post-1991 order appears anywhere in this session, because all of it belongs to other owners.

3. **Bounded-research control.** No second book was read for this topic. Under the bounded History research rule the substantive base is the complete basic owner, the complete advanced owner and this one core OCR history source, and no optional corroboration, alternative textbook, monograph or supplementary dossier was consulted. Where the bounded base leaves a point unresolved, it is recorded as an open gap rather than filled from outside.

4. **Quotation control.** Every quoted phrase in this session is taken verbatim from the extracted text of the book named above, from the canonical owners, or - in the single current linkage of Lesson 6 - from the official pages identified in the live-source table. Participant quotations are confined to Truman on free peoples, Marshall on hunger, poverty, desperation and chaos, Churchill on the iron curtain and on Nasser, Molotov on dollar imperialism, Khrushchev on peaceful coexistence and on enemy missiles, Mao's reported remark to Zhou Enlai, Aref on wiping Israel off the map, Nixon's reported instruction about the Chilean economy, Beijing on teaching Vietnam a lesson, Gorbachev's Reykjavik formula and the Geneva joint statement. Historians are quoted only where the core account itself quotes or reports them - Kennan, Williams, Behrman, Mills, Hugh Thomas, Kevin Ruane, Gaitskell as a political figure and the post-revisionists collectively - and each is reproduced as the account records it. The *Washington Post* report of August 1966 is reproduced as the account reports it, with its attribution intact.

5. **Extraction-damage control.** Six defects in the copy held here are declared rather than worked around. The passage immediately following the post-revisionist paragraph, which names later historians writing on the Cold War's outcome, is degraded beyond sentence-level legibility, so **no position, name, date, book or argument from it is asserted**. The opening paragraph of the Yalta section is illegible, so **no conference date, location or attendance detail is asserted from it**, and the agreed points are taken from the readable list that follows. The page carrying the opening of the arms-race section, including the satellite paragraph, is largely illegible, so **no date, name, designation or specification of any satellite is stated**, and only the legible clause about the American response is used. Two pages covering the end of the Vietnam war are blank, so **no detail of the 1968 incident beyond the legible clause, and no ceasefire terms or military detail of the 1975 collapse, are asserted**; the outline comes from the chapter summary. The page carrying the opening of the Iranian case is blank, so **no detail of the events preceding the restoration is asserted**. And a page in the 1993 accord material is degraded and is **not used at all**.

6. **Declared evidence boundary.** The internal politics of Afghanistan, the composition and organisation of the Afghan resistance, the channels and scale of external assistance to it, and the war's course and casualties are not carried by the material used for this session, and **nothing about them is asserted**. Lesson 19 states this boundary at the point of use and confines itself to the international dimension.

7. **Attribution control.** Responsibility is stated about governments, parties, ministries, intelligence agencies, alliances, congresses, legislatures and named individuals throughout. Where a held source uses a collective formulation, it is reported as that source's wording and is not adopted as this session's own. The account's own list of states in which American agencies were active is reproduced as a list attributed to named authors, and is not converted into a set of established operations.

8. **Sensitive-material control.** Three bodies of material in this topic involve mass death - the Korean war's recorded toll, the Vietnam war's recorded costs, and the Chilean repression figures. **No casualty total is stated anywhere in this session that a held source does not state; no held total is presented as settled; and no total is supplied for Laos, Cambodia, Thailand, Afghanistan or Angola, because the sources used here give none.**

## The bounded material-fact pass, as declared

⚠️ **Why the pass was necessary and how it was bounded.** The two owners and the core book supply the chronology, the institutions, the statistics, the historiography and the complete answer architecture. Before drafting, one bounded material-fact pass was declared and confined to three categories: a short enumerated list of standard connecting dates whose absence or imprecision in the held sources would create a real sequencing gap; the routing status and wording availability of any verified question relevant to this topic; and one current linkage. **Within that original pre-drafting pass no second pass was opened, no category was extended, and each item was verified once and not reopened.** ⚠️ That statement is scoped to the pre-drafting pass alone; the separately declared and separately bounded extension recorded below was opened afterwards, on its own enumerated terms.

| Cluster verified | Result carried |
|---|---|
| **Cluster 1: standard connecting dates** | ✅ Yalta, 4-11 February 1945; Potsdam, 17 July-2 August 1945; the Truman Doctrine address, 12 March 1947; Marshall's speech, 5 June 1947; the North Atlantic Treaty signed 4 April 1949; the Warsaw Pact signed 14 May 1955; the Berlin Wall begun 13 August 1961; the Cuban crisis, October 1962; SALT I signed 26 May 1972; Nixon in China, February 1972; the Helsinki Final Act signed 1 August 1975; the Korean armistice signed 27 July 1953 at Panmunjom; the Partial Test Ban Treaty initialled 25 July and signed 5 August 1963; the INF Treaty signed 8 December 1987; the Bandung Conference of April 1955; and the first Non-Aligned Movement summit at Belgrade, September 1961. ⚠️ Of these, the items actually carried into the teaching are only those needed to hold a sequence together or to display a discrepancy: the month-level datings already present in the held sources are used as the sources give them, the Helsinki and test-ban datings are **displayed as discrepancies rather than substituted**, and **Bandung and Belgrade were verified and then deliberately not used**, because the held sources carry the Algiers conference of 1973 and the SEATO refusal as their own evidence for the non-alignment argument and no gap remained |
| **Cluster 2: question routing and wording availability** | ✅ Routing status: **zero** direct topic-only routes in the 2018-2025 Mains ledgers; **five** World-History-tagged items across that span, none routed here; **two** adjacent International Relations Mains items and **two** adjacent International Relations Prelims items, none routed here; **nil** owned return in all three Prelims ledgers; **nil** in all six 2026 ledgers; and none of the four pre-2018 recurring demands routed here. ✅ Wording availability: the folder README holds two World-History wordings verbatim and neutral renderings for three; the repository holds neutral renderings only for the four adjacent International Relations items. **No wording is reproduced for any of them in this session** |
| **Cluster 3: one current linkage** | ✅ The U.S. Department of State's National Museum of American Diplomacy pages on the Berlin Wall "Signature Segment" and on visiting the museum, retrieved 29 September 2026 - tabulated below and used in Lesson 6 section 8 only |

### Bounded correction extension, declared and closed

⚠️ **Why an extension was opened, and how it was bounded.** Independent review of the frozen draft identified a finite set of points at which the held sources are imprecise, mis-worded or silent in ways that would put a factual error into an examination answer. One extension to the material-fact pass was therefore opened, **restricted in advance to the eight enumerated items below and to nothing else**. Each item was verified once against the minimum reliable source, the result was carried into the teaching with the held wording displayed alongside it, and the extension was then closed. **No new narrative, no new statistical series, no new interpretive claim and no new current linkage entered the session through it.**

| # | Item enumerated before verification | ✅ Result carried, and where |
|---:|---|---|
| 1 | Saddam Hussein's position in 1968 and the date he became head of state | ✅ The Ba'ath Party took power in July 1968 with Saddam prominent in the regime as vice-president under President Ahmed Hassan al-Bakr; he became **President of Iraq on 16 July 1979**. **This is a correction of the core account's "became Iraqi leader in 1968"**, applied in Lesson 17 §5 with the held wording displayed, and propagated to Lesson 16 §6, both register-note echoes and the remediation set |
| 2 | American military fatalities in the Vietnam war, and the category each published figure counts | ✅ The U.S. Defense Casualty Analysis System file held by the National Archives records **58 220 U.S. military fatal casualties**, comprising **47 434 hostile** and **10 786 non-hostile** deaths. ⚠️ The core account's "about 48 000" corresponds to the hostile-death category, not to the total. Both are displayed in Lesson 15 §6 with their objects named, neither is averaged, and the session instructs the learner to name the category |
| 3 | The signatory of the Austrian State Treaty for the Soviet Union | ✅ Signed at Vienna on **15 May 1955**; **Vyacheslav Molotov** signed for the Soviet Union. The draft's concept-check wording had attributed the signature to Khrushchev; corrected in Lesson 10 §4 and its concept check, trap list and register note |
| 4 | The end of the war against Japan | ✅ **10 August 1945** is Japan's conditional surrender offer; the Allied acceptance was announced on **14-15 August 1945**; the **Instrument of Surrender was signed on 2 September 1945**. **This is a correction of the core account's "the war ended quickly on 10 August"**, applied in Lesson 4 §2 with the held wording displayed |
| 5 | The date on which the Soviet withdrawal from Afghanistan began | ✅ The **Geneva Accords were signed on 14 April 1988** and the withdrawal **began on 15 May 1988**. **This is a correction of the core account's "1 May 1988"**, applied in Lessons 19 §3 and 20 §5 with the held date displayed, and propagated to the dependency map, the Lesson 20 visual, both trap lists, both register-note echoes and the cumulative check |
| 6 | The founding date of Comecon | ✅ The sources used here give **none**, and the extension therefore records this as an **open gap rather than filling it**: Lesson 5 §5 states explicitly that Comecon is undated in the held sources and is left undated, and that the 1947 attached to the Molotov Plan is not a date for Comecon |
| 7 | The signature date of the Helsinki Final Act | ✅ **1 August 1975**, the conference having run to its close in the last days of July. The owners and the core account give "July 1975"; **both are now displayed at the point of use** in Lesson 18 §3 rather than only in this ledger, which the frozen draft had failed to do |
| 8 | The number of troop-contributing states in Korea | ✅ The core account gives the USA and fourteen other named countries - **fifteen contributing states in all**. The frozen draft's Lesson 9 progress line had said "sixteen"; corrected there and made explicit in every echo |

⚠️ **Control on the pass and its extension.** The original pass produced only dates and routing status and overrode nothing. ⚠️ **The extension does override the core account at three points** - items 1, 4 and 5 - and each of those is recorded in this session as a declared correction with the held wording shown beside it, so that a reader can see exactly what was changed and why. ⚠️ Items 2, 3, 6, 7 and 8 correct or complete this session's own handling rather than the source: item 2 supplies a category distinction the account does not make, item 3 repairs a misattribution introduced in drafting, item 6 converts a silence into a declared gap, item 7 moves an already-verified discrepancy from the ledger into the teaching, and item 8 repairs an internal inconsistency. ⚠️ Where the extension and a held source address the same point, **both are displayed rather than reconciled by preference**, and the learner is told which is which.

⚠️ **Control on the original pass.** No item from the original pass overrides an owner or the core book at any point. It produced **no historical narrative, no statistical series, no casualty figure and no interpretive claim**; it produced dates and routing status.

Also retrieved: the museum's official visiting page, recording that it is located at the 21st Street entrance of the Harry S Truman building in Washington and is open to the public on Tuesdays and Thursdays with free timed-entry passes | Lesson 6 section 8 only - the single current linkage in this session. ## Endpoints identified but not retrieved in this pass

Nothing in the teaching depends on text that was not read. The following were identified and **not** fetched, in accordance with the bounded History research rule: any second history textbook, monograph or survey on the Cold War, its origins, its crises, détente, its ending, the Korean or Vietnam wars, the Middle East, Latin America or the decolonised world; the works of Kennan, Williams, Gaddis, Westad, Behrman, Mills, Hugh Thomas, Kevin Ruane, Blum, Agee and Chomsky, all of which are known to this session only through the core account's report of their positions; the texts of the North Atlantic Treaty, the Warsaw Pact, the Geneva Agreement, SALT I, the Helsinki Final Act, the INF Treaty, the Camp David accords or any United Nations resolution; the Truman, Marshall, Churchill and Khrushchev speeches, used only as the core account reports them; NSC-68 and any other declassified planning document; the archives on which the post-revisionist historians drew; any statistical series for warheads, defence spending, aid disbursements or casualties beyond the figures the sources used here record; the repository's own complete-topic package for this topic beyond its recorded PYQ-integrity and live-link findings; and any material behind the current linkage beyond the two official pages themselves. In every case the canonical owners or the core OCR history source supplied the teaching content.

## Evidence and limitation controls

- No content from any derived or generated file in this repository - complete-topic packages, learner-v2 exports, quick-glance charts, manifests or trackers - is reproduced as evidence. The package file listed under the layered/complete session row was consulted for its recorded PYQ-integrity and live-link findings only.
- No claim in this session rests on a source that was not read in full or, where damaged, declared.
- Six extraction gaps, one declared evidence boundary and one declared open gap - the absence of any Comecon founding date in the held sources - are recorded at the point of use and tabulated in the coverage matrix.
- Four internal tensions are displayed rather than resolved: the core account's July 1963 dating of the Nuclear Test Ban Treaty against the verified 25 July initialling and 5 August signature; its July 1975 dating of the Helsinki Agreement against the verified signature of the Final Act on 1 August 1975; the definition of détente as a *permanent* relaxation against the same account's record of a second Cold War from 1979; and the account's hedge that the Marshall Plan's main aim was *probably* political, preserved rather than converted into a finding.
- Three corrections are made against the core account, each declared at the point of use with the held wording shown beside it: Saddam Hussein's position in 1968 and his accession to the presidency on 16 July 1979; the compression of Japan's 10 August surrender offer, the 14-15 August acceptance and the 2 September 1945 Instrument of Surrender into "the war ended quickly on 10 August"; and the date on which the Soviet withdrawal from Afghanistan began, given as 1 May 1988 and verified as 15 May 1988 under the Geneva Accords of 14 April 1988.
- One category distinction is supplied where the account gives a figure without one: the Vietnam fatality count, where the account's approximately 48 000 matches the hostile-death category and the verified official total is 58 220.
- One verification limitation is disclosed: the locally held 2026 official papers are scanned images without an extractable text layer in this environment.
- Zero objective items appear anywhere in this file, and no answer key is asserted or implied.
- The seven original Mains questions are this session's own and are labelled as such; none is represented as an examination item.
- This session teaches the rivalry to the collapse of 1989-91 and hands over there.
